Namo tassa bhagavato arahato sammāsambuddhassa.

Homage to the Blessed One, the Arahant, the Perfectly Self-Enlightened One.

Rasavāhinī

The Stream of Nectar

Paṇāmādikathā

Introductory Homage

1.

1.

Satthuppasatthacaraṇaṃ [M 0.0001] saraṇaṃ janānaṃ,

Brahmādimoḷi maṇiraṃsi samāvahantaṃ,

Paṅkeruhābhamudukomalacāruvaṇṇaṃ;

Vandāmi cakkavaralakkhaṇamādadhānaṃ.

I pay homage to the One whose conduct is praised by the Teacher, the refuge of beings, bearing the rays of jewels from the crowns of Brahmā and other gods, whose complexion is lotus-like, soft, tender, and beautiful, and who bears the excellent mark of the wheel.

2.

2.

Siddhaṃ jinena cirakālamatanditena,

Yaṃ bhāvako samadhigacchati khemamaggaṃ;

Yaṃ kapparukkha rucidāna maṇiva bhāti,

Taṃ dhammamagga masamaṃ paṇamāmi niccaṃ.

I constantly revere the incomparable Dhamma path, which the Victor, untiring over a long time, realized; the path of safety that the meditator attains, shining like a wish-fulfilling tree, like a splendid jewel.

3.

3.

Santindriyaṃ sugatasūnuvaraṃ visuddhaṃ,

Yaṃ dakkhiṇeyyamatadaṃ sucipuññakhettaṃ;

Tāṇesinaṃ saraṇamujjhitasabbadukkhaṃ,

Vandāmi saṅgha managhaṃ sirasā mahagghaṃ.

I bow my head in reverence to the Sangha, faultless and greatly valuable: the excellent disciples of the Well-Gone One, with senses calmed, pure, worthy of offerings, an unsurpassed, pure field of merit, a refuge for those seeking protection, who have abandoned all suffering.

4.

4.

Yampattamettha ratanattayathomanena,

Puññena tena duritaṃ sakalaṃ paṇujja,

Vakkhāmahaṃ sumadhuraṃ rasavāhinintaṃ,

Bho bho suṇantu sujanā bhimudāvahā sā.

By the merit attained here through praising the Triple Gem, having dispelled all misdeeds by that merit, I shall explain the very sweet Rasavāhinī. O good people, listen, for it brings great delight.

5.

5.

Tatthatatthūpapannāni, [M 0.0002] vatthūni arahā pure;

Abhāsuṃ dīpabhāsāya, ṭhapesuṃ taṃ purātanā.

The Arahants of old told the stories that arose in various places in the language of the island; the ancients established them.

6.

6.

Mahāvihāre taṅgutta, vaṅkapariveṇavāsiko;

Raṭṭhapāloti nāmena, sīlācāra guṇākaro.

A resident of the Vaṅka Pariveṇa in the Mahāvihāra, self-controlled, was one named Raṭṭhapāla, a mine of the qualities of virtue and good conduct.

7.

7.

Hitāya parivattesi, pajānaṃ pāḷibhāsato;

Punaruttādidosehi, tamāsi sabbamākulaṃ;

Anākulaṃ karissāmi, taṃ suṇātha samāhitā.

For the welfare of the people, he rendered it into the Pāli language. But due to faults such as repetition, it all became confused. I shall make it unconfused; listen attentively.

8.

8.

Vitarāgā pure vocuṃ, yasmā tasmā hi bhāsitaṃ;

Etamādaraṇīyañhi, sādhu sādhūhi sabbadāti.

Since those free from passion spoke this of old, this utterance is indeed worthy of reverence, and is always acclaimed by the virtuous.

Jambudīpuppatti vatthūni.

Stories Originating in Jambudīpa

Dhammasoṇḍakavaggo

The Chapter on Dhammasoṇḍaka

1. Dhammasoṇḍakassa vatthumhi ayamānupubbīkathā

1. This is the connected account regarding the story of Dhammasoṇḍaka.

Tattha tesaṃ vatthūna muppattiyo dvidhā bhavanti jambudīpe sīhaḷadīpecāti, tattha jambudīpe tāḷīsa, sīhaḷadīpe tesaṭṭhi, tesu tāva jambudīpuppattivatthūni āvi bhavissanti, tatopi dhammasoṇḍakassa vatthu ādi, kathaṃ, amhākaṃ kira bhagavato pubbe imasmiṃyeva bhaddakappe kassaponāma satthā loke udapādi, tassa kho pana bhagavato sāsanantaradhānato na cireneva kālena amhākaṃ bodhisatto bārāṇasīrañño aggamahesiyā kucchismiṃ nibbatti, tassu ppatti kālasamanantarameva sabbasattānaṃ manasi dhammasaññā udapādi, tasmāssa dhammasoṇḍotināma makaṃsu, so paneso kumāro mahantena parivārena vaḍḍhento sabbasippesu nipphattiṃ patvā pitarā uparajjena pūjito hutvā dānādayo dasakusalakammapathe pūrento pituaccayenāmaccehi [M 0.0003] rajjenābhisiñcito ahosi, so panesa dhammasoṇḍakamahārājā devanagarasadise bārāṇasīnagare cakkavattisadisaṃ bārāṇasīrajjaṃ kārento māsaddhamāsaccayena sirisayanagato evaṃ cintesi, mamevaṃ rajjasirimanubhavanaṃ na sobhati dhammaviyogena, divākara virahito nabho viyātiādinā nānākāraṇaṃ cintesi, tenettha.

Herein, the origins of those stories are twofold: in Jambudīpa and in Sīhaḷadīpa. Of these, forty are in Jambudīpa, and sixty-three in Sīhaḷadīpa. First, the stories originating in Jambudīpa will be made manifest. Among them, the story of Dhammasoṇḍaka comes first. How so? It is said that before our Blessed One, in this very Fortunate Eon, a teacher named Kassapa arose in the world. Not long after the disappearance of that Blessed One’s dispensation, our Bodhisatta was reborn in the womb of the chief queen of the king of Bārāṇasī. At the very moment of his birth, the perception of the Dhamma arose in the minds of all beings. Therefore, they gave him the name Dhammasoṇḍaka. This prince, growing up with a great retinue, having attained proficiency in all the arts, and being honored by his father as viceroy, while fulfilling the ten wholesome courses of action beginning with generosity, upon his father’s death, was consecrated as king by his ministers. Then, the great king Dhammasoṇḍaka, ruling the kingdom of Bārāṇasī like a wheel-turning monarch in the city of Bārāṇasī, which resembled a divine city, after a month or half a month had passed, while lying on his royal couch, reflected thus: “For me to enjoy this royal splendor while separated from the Dhamma is not fitting, like the sky without the sun,” and so on, he pondered for various reasons. Therefore, herein...

1.

1.

Puññena sīlādimayena pubbe,

Katena pattosmi atanditena,

Masakkasāre viya devarājā;

Rājattamiddhe puramuttamamhi.

By merit made previously and diligently, consisting of virtue and the like, I have attained prosperous kingship in this most excellent city, like the king of the gods in Masakkasāra.

2.

2.

Rūpena hārīnayanussavena,

Saddena sammā savaṇāmatena,

Gandhena ghānussavasobhanena,

Rasaññapuññena rasenacāpi.

By form, a captivating festival for the eyes; by sound, truly ambrosia for the ears; by scent, a beautiful festival for the nose; and also by taste, a taste for one with the merit of discernment.

3.

3.

Phassena gattassa suphassadena,

Samiddhipattosmi mahiddhikohaṃ,

Nevettakeneva pamādabhāva,

Mā pajjituṃ yuttarūpanti ñatvā.

By touch that gives pleasant contact to the body, I have attained prosperity and am one of great power. Yet, knowing it is not proper to fall into a state of heedlessness for this reason alone...

4.

4.

Dassāmi aṅgaapi jīvitañca,

Dhaññaṃ dhanaṃ cāpi pasannacitto,

Sossāmi dhammaṃ sivamādadhānaṃ,

Jineritaṃ jātijarāpahāṇaṃ.

With a serene mind, I will give even my limbs and life, and also grain and wealth. I will listen to the Dhamma that brings security, spoken by the Conqueror, which destroys birth and aging.

5.

5.

Na sobhati yathākāsaṃ, jalaṃ dhāmapatiṃvinā,

Rajjakaraṇaṃ tathā mayhaṃ, vinā dhammā na sobhati.

Just as the sky does not shine without the lord of light, so too my ruling of the kingdom does not shine without the Dhamma.

6.

6.

Na sobhati hathā ratti, nisānāthaṃ vinā sadā,

Rajjakaraṇaṃ tathā mayhaṃ, vinā dhammā na sobhati.

Just as the night never shines without the lord of the night, so too my ruling of the kingdom does not shine without the Dhamma.

7.

7.

Alaṅkatopi [M 0.0004] ce hatthī, vinā dāṭhā na sobhati,

Rajjakaraṇaṃ tathā mayhaṃ, vinā dhammā na sobhati.

Even though adorned, an elephant does not shine without its tusks; so too, my ruling of the kingdom does not shine without the Dhamma.

8.

8.

Yathā kallolamālīyaṃ,

Vinā velā na sobhati,

Rajjakaraṇaṃ tathā mayhaṃ,

Vinā dhammā na sobhati.

Just as the ocean garlanded with waves does not shine without the shore, so too my ruling of the kingdom does not shine without the Dhamma.

9.

9.

Yathā sumaṇḍito rājā,

Kupaṭo neva sobhati,

Rajjakaraṇaṃ tathā mayhaṃ,

Vinā dhammā na sobhati.

Just as a well-adorned king, if he is of poor conduct, does not shine, so too my ruling of the kingdom does not shine without the Dhamma.

10.

10.

Dhammameva suṇissāmi, dhamme me ramatī mano,

Na hi dhammā paraṃ atthi, dhammamūlaṃ tisampadanti.

I will listen only to the Dhamma; my mind delights in the Dhamma. Indeed, there is nothing higher than the Dhamma; the Dhamma is the root of the three accomplishments.

Evaṃ cintetvā pātova sirigabbhā nikkhamma susajjite samussitasetacchatte rājapalleṅke amaccagaṇa parivuto nisīdi devarājāviya virocamāno, nisinno pana rājā amacce evamāha, yo panettha bhonto buddhabhāsitesu dhammesu kiñcidhammaṃ jānāti, so bhāsatu, sotumicchāmidhammanti, te sabbepi mayaṃ deva na jānāmāti āhaṃsu, taṃ sutvā a na tta ma no rājā evaṃ cintesi, yannūnāhaṃ hatthikkhandhe sahassaṃ ṭhapetvā nagare bheriṃcarāpeyyaṃ, yaṃ appevanāma koci dhanalobhena cātuppadikāyapi gāthāya dhammaṃ deseyya. Taṃ me dīgharattaṃ hitāya sukhāya bhavissatīti, tato so tathā katvāpi dhammadesakaṃ alabhanto puna dvisahassaṃ ticatupañcasahassantiyāvakoṭippakoṭiṃdammīti, tato gāma nigama janapade, tato seṭṭhiṭṭhānaṃ senāpati uparājaṭṭhānā dayopi, puna setacchattaṃ dammi, rājavesaṃ pahāya attānaṃ dāsaṃ sāvetvā dhammadesentassa dammiti vatvā bheri carāpetvāpi dhamma desakamalabhitvā saṃviggo [M 0.0005] kimme dhammaviyogena rajjenāti amaccānaṃ rajjaṃ nīyyātetvā saddhammagavesako dhammasoṇḍakamahārājā mahāvanaṃ pāvisi gāma nigama rājadhāni paramparāya, tenettha.

Having reflected thus, early in the morning he emerged from the royal chamber and, under a raised white parasol, seated himself on the well-prepared royal throne surrounded by a group of ministers, shining like a king of gods. Seated there, the king addressed the ministers: “Sirs, whoever among you knows any teaching spoken by the Buddha, let him speak. I wish to hear the Dhamma.” They all replied, “O King, we do not know.” Hearing this, the king, being displeased, thought, “What if I were to place a thousand coins on an elephant's back and have the drum beaten in the city, so that perhaps someone, out of greed for wealth, might teach the Dhamma even with a four-line verse? That would be for my long-term welfare and happiness.” Then, having done so and still not finding a Dhamma teacher, he declared, “I will give two thousand, then three, four, five thousand, up to a crore of crores!” Then he had this proclaimed in villages, towns, and districts, and at the residences of merchants, generals, and the viceroy. Again he declared, “I will give the white parasol; having abandoned the royal attire and announced myself a slave, I will give myself to one who teaches the Dhamma.” Yet, even after having the drum beaten with this proclamation, he did not find a Dhamma teacher. Stirred with urgency, he thought, “What use is this kingdom to me, separated from the Dhamma?” Entrusting the kingdom to his ministers, the great king Dhammasoṇḍaka, a seeker of the true Dhamma, entered the great forest, proceeding from village to town to capital city. Therefore, herein...

11.

11.

Pure bheriṃ carāpetvā,

Dhammasoṇḍo narādhipo;

Saddhammajjhesakaṃ satthu,

Aladdhā dhanakoṭihi.

Having had the drum beaten in the city, King Dhammasoṇḍaka, not having found a teacher of the true Dhamma even for crores of wealth...

12.

12.

Dāso homi pahāyāhaṃ,

Rājattaṃ desakassa me;

Iccāha so mahīpālo,

Aho dhammesu lolatā.

“I will become a slave to one who teaches me, having relinquished my kingship.” Thus spoke that ruler of the land. Oh, what longing for the Dhamma!

13.

13.

Rajjaṃ nīyyātayitvāna, amaccānaṃ manoramaṃ,

Vanaṃ pāvisi so rājā, gavesaṃ dhammamuttamanti.

Having entrusted the delightful kingdom to his ministers, that king entered the forest, seeking the supreme Dhamma.

Mahāvanaṃ paviṭṭhakkhaṇe pana mahāsattassa puññatejena sakkassāsanaṃ uṇhākāraṃ dassesi, atha devarājā cintesi akāmaṃ me paṇḍukambala silāsanaṃ uṇhamahosi, kinnukho kāraṇanti lokaṃ olokento sakko devarājā dhammasoṇḍaka mahārājānaṃ sakalajambudīpaṃ vicinitvā saddhammajjhesakaṃ alabhitvā vanaṃ paviṭṭhabhāvaṃ addasa, dhammasoṇḍakamahārājā saddhammatthāya rajja dhana bandhu jīvitampi pahāya araññaṃ paviṭṭho, na so vatāyaṃ yovā sovā satto, imasmiṃyeva kappe buddho bhavissati, buddhabodhisatto cāyaṃ ajjeva mahāraññaṃ paviṭṭho saddhammaṃ aladdhā mahādukkhaṃ pāpuṇeyya, na cetaṃ yuttaṃ, ajja mayā tattha ganthabbaṃ dhammāmatarasena tamabhisiñcitvā rajje pahiṭṭhāpetunti cintetvā attabhāvaṃ vijahitvā bhayānakaṃ mahantaṃ rakkhasavesaṃ nimmiṇitvā mahāsattābhimukho avidūre attānaṃ dassesi, tenettha.

As the Great Being entered the great forest, Sakka's seat showed the characteristic of heat due to the power of his merit. Then the king of the gods reflected, 'My Paṇḍukambala stone seat has become hot, though I did not wish it. What could be the reason?' Surveying the world, Sakka, the king of the gods, saw that the great king Dhammasoṇḍaka, having searched all of Jambudīpa but not finding one who taught the true Dhamma, had entered the forest. 'This person is not indeed an ordinary being. In this very aeon, he will become a Buddha. This Bodhisatta, who today entered the great forest, would attain great suffering if he does not attain the true Dhamma. This is not fitting. Today I must go there, anoint him with the nectar of the Dhamma, and establish him in kingship.' Having resolved this, he abandoned his own form, created a terrifying, great demonic guise, and showed himself not far from the Great Being. Thus it is said:

14.

14.

Byagghacchasīhamahiso [M 0.0006] ragahatthidīpi,

Migākulaṃ kaṇṭakaselarukkhaṃ;

Narānamindo pavisitvakānanaṃ,

Itocito vibbhami dhammakāmo.

Into a forest thronged with deer, with tigers, bears, lions, buffaloes, rutting elephants, and leopards, with thorny rocks and trees—the lord of men, desiring the Dhamma, entered, wandering here and there.

15.

15.

Tassānubhāvena purindadassa,

Silāsanaṃ uṇhamahosi kāmaṃ;

Teneva so lokamudikkhamāno,

Addakkhi dhīraṃ vipine carantaṃ.

By his power, Purindada's stone seat became exceedingly hot. For that very reason, surveying the world, he saw the wise one wandering in the wilderness.

16.

16.

Mayajja taṃ dhammarasena sammā,

Santappayitvā gamanaṃ varanti;

Mantvā subhīmañjanakūṭavaṇṇaṃ,

Mahāmukhaṃ niggata bhīmadāṭhaṃ.

Thinking, 'Today, having rightly satisfied him with the nectar of the Dhamma, it is best that I go,' he fashioned a most terrifying guise: the color of an antimony mountain peak, with a huge mouth and protruding, terrifying fangs.

17.

17.

Dittaggisaṅkāsa visālanettaṃ,

Majjhena bhaggaṃ cipiṭagganāsaṃ;

Kharatambadāṭhiṃ ghanamassuvantaṃ,

Nīlodaraṃ gajjitabhīmaghosaṃ.

With eyes wide, resembling a blazing fire; a nose broken in the middle, with a flattened tip; with rough, copper-colored fangs and a thick beard; a blue belly, and a dreadful, roaring sound.

18.

18.

Karoruhaṃ tikkhasalohitāyataṃ,

Visāladhotāyatakhaggahatthaṃ;

Gadāyudhenaṅkitamaññabāhuṃ,

Daṭṭhoṭṭhabhīmaṃ savalīlalāṭaṃ.

With long, sharp, blood-red claws; one hand holding a broad, polished, long sword; the other arm marked with a mace as a weapon; frightful with a bitten lip, and with a wrinkled forehead.

19.

19.

Manussamaṃsādanarattapānaṃ,

Bhayānakaṃ kakkhalayakkhavaṇṇaṃ;

Sumāpayitvāna vanantarasmiṃ,

Dassesi attaṃ sa narādhipassāti.

Eating human flesh, drinking blood, a fearsome, harsh yaksha appearance—having thus fashioned himself in the midst of the forest, he revealed himself to that lord of men.

Atha mahāsatto attano avidūre ṭhitaṃ rakkhasaṃ addakkhi, taṃ disvānāssa bhayaṃvā chambhitattaṃvā citthutrāsamattaṃvā nāhosi, kimatra cintesi, apināma evarūpo pirakkhaso dhammaṃ [M 0.0007] jāneyya, yannūnāhaṃ tassa santike dhammaṃ suṇissāmi, tamme dīgharattaṃ hitāya sukhāya bhavissatīti cintesi. Cintetvā capana ajja mayā tamupasaṅkamma pucchitaṃ vaṭṭatīti gantvā rakkhasena saddhiṃ sallapanto āha.

Then the Great Being saw the ogre standing not far from him. Upon seeing him, he felt no fear, trembling, or dread. He thought, "Could it be that even such an ogre knows the Dhamma? Indeed, I should listen to the Dhamma from him. That would be for my long-term welfare and happiness." Having reflected thus, he thought, "Today, it is fitting that I should approach and ask him." So he went and, conversing with the ogre, said:

20.

20.

Asmiṃ vanasmiṃ tarusaṇḍa maṇḍite;

Suphullitānekalatākulākule;

Adhiggahīto si mahānubhāva,

Pucchāmi taṃ deva vadehi kaṅkhaṃ.

In this forest, adorned with groves of trees, densely intertwined with many flowering creepers, you preside, O mighty one. I ask you, O deva, speak and dispel my doubt.

21.

21.

Dhammaṃ gavesaṃ vanamāgatomhi,

Pahāya rajjaṃ api ñātisaṅghaṃ;

Jānāsi ce samma vadehi mayhaṃ,

Ekampi gāthaṃ sugatena desitaṃ.

Seeking the Dhamma, I came to the forest, having abandoned my kingdom and even the company of my relatives. If you know, friend, speak to me even a single verse taught by the Sugata.

Tato yakkho āha.

Then the yakkha said:

22.

22.

Dhammaṃ pajānāmahamekadesaṃ,

Jineritaṃ sādhutaraṃ rasānaṃ,

Desemi cehaṃ tava dhamma maggaṃ,

Tuvañhi kiṃ kāhasi desakassāti.

I know a part of the Dhamma, taught by the Conqueror, the finest of essences. If I teach you the path of the Dhamma, what will you do for the teacher?

Atha mahāsatto āha.

Then the Great Being said:

23.

23.

Rajje ṭhito assamahaṃ sace bho,

Anapparūpaṃ pakaromi pūjaṃ;

Idāni eko vanamajjhapatto,

Karomi kiṃ dehamimaṃ ṭhapetvā.

If I were established in my kingdom, sir, I would make an unsurpassed offering; but now, alone, having come to the midst of the forest, what can I do, apart from this body?

24.

24.

Yadicchasi tvaṃ mama maṃsalohitaṃ,

Karomahaṃ saṅgahamajja tena,

Na catthi aññaṃ tava accanīyaṃ,

Desehi dhammaṃ sugata ppasatthanti.

If you desire my flesh and blood, I shall make a gift of them today. There is nothing else to offer you. Teach the Dhamma praised by the Sugata.

Tato [M 0.0008] yakkho āha.

Then the yakkha said:

25.

25.

Bhutvāna maṃsaṃ suhitova santo,

Hantvā pipāsaṃ rudhiraṃ pivitvā;

Dhammaṃ kathetuṃ pabhavāmi tuyhaṃ,

Vattuṃ na sakkomi khudāparetoti.

Having eaten flesh and been well sated, having quenched my thirst by drinking blood, I am able to teach the Dhamma to you; but I am unable to speak, being overcome by hunger.

Atha mahāsatto āha.

Then the Great Being said:

26.

26.

Bhutvā tuvaṃ maṃ pathamañhi yakkha,

Pacchā tu desessasi kassa dhammaṃ;

Dhammassa mayhaṃ tava maṃsalābhaṃ,

Tvameva jānāhi yathā bhaveyyāti.

First you shall eat me, O Yakkha, but afterwards, to whom will you teach the Dhamma? You yourself must know how my attainment of the Dhamma and your gain of flesh might come to be.

Evaṃ vutte sakko devānamindo sādhu mahārāja ahameva yuttaṃ jānāmīti vatvā tassāvidūre tigāvutubbedhaṃ añjanavaṇṇaṃ mahantaṃ pabbataṃ māpetvā mahārāja imamāruyha pabbatamuddhaniṭṭhito mama mukhe patatu, ahaṃ patanthassa te dhammaṃ desessāmi, evaṃ sante tuyhaṃ dhammapaṭilābho, mayhaṃca maṃsapaṭilābho bhavissatīti. Taṃ sutvā mahāsatto anamatagge saṃsāre saṃsarato me sīhabyagghacchamacchakacchapavihaṅgādīnaṃ bhakkhabhūtassa jātīsu pamāṇaṃ natti, ajja mayā sambuddhassa dhammatthāya jīvitaṃ pariccajituṃ vaṭṭatīti cintetvā evamāha.

When this was said, Sakka, the lord of the devas, replied: "Good, great king, I myself know what is fitting." Having spoken thus, he created a great mountain the color of antimony, three gāvutas in height, not far from him. "Great king, climb this mountain, stand on its summit, and fall into my mouth. I will teach you the Dhamma as you are falling. In this way, there will be your attainment of the Dhamma, and my attainment of flesh." Hearing this, the Great Being reflected: "For me, wandering in beginningless saṃsāra, there is no measure to the births where I have been food for lions, tigers, bears, fish, tortoises, birds, and so on. Today, it is fitting for me to sacrifice my life for the sake of the Sambuddha’s Dhamma." Having thought thus, he spoke as follows:

27.

27.

Saṃsāravaṭṭesu vivaṭṭamānā,

Papponti dukkhaṃ janatā anekā;

Etañhi bho attano vā parassa,

Atthāya nāhosi ahosi tucchaṃ.

Revolving in the cycles of saṃsāra, many people attain suffering. This, indeed, whether for oneself or another, was of no benefit; it was empty.

28.

28.

Tvamadinnahārī tica pāradāriko,

Pāṇātipātīsi musā abhāsi;

Tva majjapāyīti akāsi dosaṃ,

Paggayha dukkhaṃ bahuso dadanti.

‘You take what is not given,’ ‘you are an adulterer,’ ‘you kill living beings,’ ‘you speak falsely,’ ‘you drink intoxicants’—thus one commits offenses, and taking up suffering, brings about much of it.

29.

29.

Etañhi [M 0.0009] bho attano vā parassa,

Atthāya nāhosi ahosi tucchaṃ;

Rukkhā papātā papatitvakeci,

Dubbandhiyā duggavisādanena.

This, indeed, whether for oneself or another, was of no benefit; it was empty. Some die, having fallen from trees or precipices, by hanging themselves, or through the misery of poison.

30.

30.

Byādhīhi nānākharavedanāhi,

Maranti sattā utuvedanāhi;

Etañhi bho attano vā parassa,

Atthāya nāhosi ahosi tucchaṃ.

Beings die from various diseases and sharp pains, from suffering caused by the seasons. This, indeed, whether for oneself or another, was of no benefit; it was empty.

31.

31.

Byagghacchamaccho ragakucchiyañhi,

Matassa me natthi pamāṇasaṅkhā;

Etañhi bho attano vā parassa,

Atthāya nāhosi ahosi tucchaṃ.

Having been in the belly of tigers, bears, and fish, for me who has died so, there is no numerical measure. This, indeed, whether for oneself or another, was of no benefit; it was empty.

32.

32.

Etajja me ducca ja mattadānaṃ,

Na hoti devissariyādikāya;

Sabbaññubhāvaṃ pana pāpuṇitvā,

Saṃsārato nittaraṇāya satte.

This difficult gift of myself today is not for the sake of lordship over the gods or the like, but for delivering beings from saṃsāra, having myself attained the state of omniscience.

33.

33.

Tvaṃ samma mayhaṃ bahuso pakārī,

Tasmā tavetaṃ vacanaṃ karomi;

Asaṃkito desaya mayhadhammaṃ,

Samijjhate dāni manoratho teti.

You, friend, are doing me a great service; therefore, I will do as you say. Without hesitation, teach me the Dhamma; may your wish now succeed.

Evañca pana vatvā mahāsatto pabbatamāruyha ṭhito āha, ahamajja rajjena saddhiṃ jīvitañca sarīramaṃsañca saddhammatthāya dammīti somanassappatto hutvā samma dhammaṃ desehīti vatvā tasmiṃ mahādāṭhaṃ mahāmukhaṃ vivaritvā ṭhite tassābhimukho upapati. Atha sakko devānamindo somanasso acchariyappatto attabhāvaṃ vijahitvā alaṅkatadibbattabhāvaṃ māpetvā ākāse [M 0.0010] taruṇasuriyo viya obhāsamāno ākāsato patantaṃ mahāsattaṃ ubhohi hatthehi daḷhaṃ patigaṇhitvā devalokaṃ netvā paṇḍukambala silāsane nisīdāpetvā dibbamayehi gandhamālādīhi pūjetvā sayaṃ dhammaṃ sutvā pasanno pasannākāraṃ katvā kassapadasabalena desitāya aniccādiparidīpikāya.

Having spoken thus, the Great Being ascended the mountain and stood there, saying, "Today, I give my kingdom, along with my life and the flesh of my body, for the sake of the true Dhamma." Filled with joy, he said, "Friend, teach the Dhamma," and then, facing that one who stood with great fangs and a wide-open mouth, he leapt towards him. Then, Sakka, the lord of the devas, overjoyed and astonished, relinquished his own form and manifested an adorned divine form, shining in the sky like the morning sun. Catching the Great Being as he fell from the sky firmly with both hands, he took him to the heavenly realm. Seating him on the Paṇḍukambala stone seat, he honored him with divine perfumes, garlands, and other offerings. Having heard the Dhamma himself, Sakka was pleased and showed his pleasure because of the discourse revealing impermanence and so forth, spoken by Kassapa the Ten-Powered One:

34.

34.

Aniccāva ta saṅkhārā, uppādavayadhammino;

Uppajjitvā nirujjhanti, tesaṃ vūpasamo sukhoti.

Impermanent, indeed, are conditioned formations, subject to arising and passing away. Having arisen, they cease; their stilling is bliss.

Gāthāya dhammadesanena tassa manorathaṃ matthakaṃ pāpetvā devaloke mahantaṃ sirivibhavaṃ dassetvā ānetvā sakarajjeyeva patiṭṭhāpetvā appamatto hoti mahārājāti ovaditvā devalokameva agamāsīti.

By teaching the Dhamma in verse, having brought his heartfelt wish to fulfillment, showing him great glory and splendor in the heavenly realm, bringing him back, and establishing him firmly in his own kingdom, then admonishing him, "Great king, be diligent," he returned to the heavenly realm.

35.

35.

Iti amitasiriṃ vā jīvitaṃ vāpi santo,

Na sumariya pasatthaṃ dhammamevā caranti;

Tanutara vibhavānaṃ appamāyūnamambho,

Iha kusalapamādo ko nu tumhādisānanti.

Thus the wise, not considering boundless glory or even life, practice only the praised Dhamma. Alas! For those with meager resources and a short lifespan, what person like you would be heedless of the wholesome in this world?

Dhammasoṇḍakavatthuṃ paṭhamaṃ.

The Story of Dhammasoṇḍaka, the First.

2. Migaluddakassa vatthumhi ayamānupubbikathā

2. This is the sequential narrative concerning the Deer Hunter.

Ito kira eka tiṃsatime kappe sikhīnāma sammāsambuddho samatiṃsa pāramiyo pūretvā paramātisambodhiṃ patvā sadevakaṃ lokaṃ saṃsārakantārā uttārento dhammaratanavassaṃ vassāpento dhammabheriṃpaharanto dhammaketuṃ ussāpento ekasmiṃ samaye viveka manubrūhanto araññāyatanaṃ pāvisi, [M 0.0011] pavisitvā capana supupphitanāgapunnāgādinānātarusaṇḍamaṇḍite suphullasumanamālatippabhutinānālatākule anekavidhadipadacatuppadasaṅghanisevite ramaṇīye sītalasilātale catugguṇaṃ saṅghāṭiṃpaññapetvā nisīdi chabbaṇṇaraṃsīhi disaṃ pūrayanto, tadā tattha devabrahmanāgasupaṇṇādayo sannipatitvā dibbamayehi gandhamālādīhi bhagavantaṃ pūjayamānā thomayamānā namassamānā aṭṭhaṃsu, tasmiṃ panasamāgame bhagavā madhurassaraṃ nicchārento brahmaghosena catusaccapaṭisaṃyuttaṃ dhammaṃ deseti amatavassaṃ vassāpentoviya. Tadā eko migaluddako vanaṃ paviṭṭho migasūkare hantvā maṃsaṃ khādanto taṃ ṭhānaṃ patvā addasa bhagavantaṃ dhammaṃ desentaṃ. Disvā ekamantaṃ ṭhito dhammaṃ sutvā cittaṃ pasādetvā tato cuto devaloke nibbattitvā chasu kāmasaggesu manussesuca aparāparaṃ issariyaṃ anubhavanto imasmiṃ buddhuppāde sāvatthiyaṃ kulagehe nibbattitvā viññutaṃ patto bhagavato sāsane pabbajitvā ekadivasaṃ dhammaṃ desentassa bhagavato catusaccapati saṃyuttaṃ dhammakathaṃ sutvā catupaṭisambhidāhi arahattaṃ patvā ekadivasaṃ bhikkhusaṅghamajjhagato attano katakammappakāsanena pītivācamudāhari.

It is said that in the thirty-first aeon from now, the Fully Self-Enlightened One named Sikhī, having fulfilled the thirty perfections and attained supreme perfect enlightenment, was rescuing the world with its devas from the wilderness of saṃsāra, showering the rain of the Dhamma-gem, beating the drum of the Dhamma, and raising the banner of the Dhamma. On one occasion, cultivating solitude, he entered a forest dwelling. Having entered, he sat down on a delightful, cool stone slab—adorned with various trees such as blossoming nāga and punnāga, filled with various creepers like fully bloomed jasmine and mālati, and frequented by many kinds of bipeds and quadrupeds—and having spread his fourfold robe, he filled the directions with his six-colored rays. Then devas, Brahmās, nāgas, supaṇṇas, and others gathered there and stood honoring the Blessed One with divine perfumes, garlands, and so forth, praising and paying homage to him. In that assembly, the Blessed One, emitting a sweet voice with a Brahmā-like tone, taught the Dhamma connected with the Four Noble Truths, as if showering the rain of the Deathless. At that time, a certain deer hunter, having entered the forest, killing deer and boars and eating their flesh, arrived at that place and saw the Blessed One teaching the Dhamma. Having seen him, he stood to one side, listened to the Dhamma, and clarified his mind. Having passed away from there, he was reborn in the deva realm. Repeatedly experiencing dominion in the six sense-sphere heavens and among humans, in this Buddha-era he was reborn in a good family in Sāvatthi. Having attained maturity, he went forth in the Blessed One's Dispensation. One day, having listened to a discourse on the Dhamma connected with the Four Noble Truths from the Blessed One as he was teaching, he attained arahantship together with the four analytical knowledges. One day, amidst the community of bhikkhus, he uttered a joyful declaration, revealing his past deeds.

1.

1.

E ka tiṃ se ito kappe,

Loke uppajji nāyako;

Pattiṃsa lakkhaṇātiṇṇo,

Sambuddho sa sikhīvhayo.

Thirty-one aeons ago from now, / The Leader arose in the world; / Endowed with the thirty-two marks, / The Buddha, named Sikhī.

2.

2.

Jalanto dīparukkhova, sūriyova nabha muggato;

Merurājāva sambuddho, janesaggo patāpavā.

Like a burning lamp-tree, / like the sun risen in the sky, / like Mount Meru, the king of mountains, the Fully Enlightened One, / foremost among people, is glorious.

3.

3.

Pūretvā dhammanāvāyaṃ, sa nātho sakalaṃ pajaṃ;

Patiṭṭhapento saṃsāra, kantārā santibhūmiyaṃ.

Having filled the Dhamma-boat, / that Protector establishes all people / from the wilderness of saṃsāra / in the land of peace.

4.

4.

Dhammaketuṃ [M 0.0012] samussento, hananto dhammadundubhiṃ;

Satte dukkhā pamocento, vasī tattha jino vasī.

Raising the banner of Dhamma, / sounding the drum of Dhamma, / freeing beings from suffering, / the Victor, the Master, dwelt there.

5.

5.

Ekasmiṃ samaye nātho,

Lokapajjotako jino;

Vivekakāmo sambuddho,

Surammaṃ kānanaṃ gato.

On one occasion, the Protector, / the Enlightener of the world, the Victor, / the Fully Enlightened One, desiring solitude, / went to a delightful forest.

6.

6.

Punnāganāgapūgādi, nānāpādapasaṃkulaṃ;

Latā liṅgitasākhāhi, sā modakusumāyutaṃ.

Crowded with various trees, / such as punnāga, nāga, and pūga, / with branches entwined by creepers, / it abounded with delightful flowers.

7.

7.

Kusumā modasammatta, chappadāli nisevitaṃ;

Nānāmiga gaṇākiṇṇaṃ, mayūragaṇa naccitaṃ.

Frequented by swarms of bees / intoxicated by the fragrance of flowers, / teeming with herds of various animals, / danced upon by flocks of peacocks.

8.

8.

Sītalacchodikāsādhu, supatitthajalāsayaṃ;

Āsāra sāradhārāhi, nijjharāsata saṃkulaṃ.

With good, cool, clear water, / and reservoirs with fine bathing places, / filled with torrents of autumn showers, / thronged with a hundred waterfalls.

9.

9.

Gantvāna so mahāraññaṃ,

Sītalaṃ sikatātalaṃ;

Silātale nisinnosi,

Vissajjento charaṃsiyo.

Having gone to that great forest, / cool with its sandy ground, / he sat upon the rocky slab, / emitting his six-colored rays.

10.

10.

Devā tattha samāgantvā, pūjesuṃ dvipaduttamaṃ;

Dibbehi gandhamālāhi, naccehi turiyehica.

The devas, gathering there, / honored the foremost of bipeds / with divine perfumes and garlands, / with dances and musical instruments.

11.

11.

Devadevo tadā deva, saṅghamajjhe nisīdiya;

Catusacca madesesi, niccharaṃ madhuraṃ giraṃ.

Then the God of gods, / sitting amidst the assembly of devas, / taught the Four Truths, / emitting a sweet voice.

12.

12.

Tadāhaṃ luddako āsiṃ, migasūkaramārako;

Migamaṃsena jivāmi, tena posemi dārake posentoputtadārake itikatthaci.

At that time I was a hunter, / a killer of deer and boars; / I lived on the flesh of deer, / with that I nourished my children.

13.

13.

Tadāhaṃ [M 0.0013] migavaṃ yāto,

Sabāṇo sasarāsano;

Addasaṃ virajaṃ buddhaṃ,

Devasaṅghapurakkhataṃ.

Then I went hunting, / with arrows and a bow; / I saw the stainless Buddha, / honored by a host of devas.

14.

14.

Candaṃva tārakākiṇṇaṃ, meruṃvaṇṇavamajjhagaṃ;

Virocamāna māsīnaṃ, catusaccappakāsakaṃ.

Like the moon surrounded by stars, / like Meru in the ocean's midst, / shining, seated, / proclaiming the Four Truths.

15.

15.

Ekapasse ṭhito tattha, assosiṃ dhammamuttamaṃ;

Tattha cittaṃ pasādetvā, somanassaṃ pavedayiṃ.

Standing to one side there, / I heard the supreme Dhamma; / having clarified my mind there, / I expressed my joy.

16.

16.

Ekatiṃse ito kappe,

Yaṃ puññaṃ pasutaṃ mayā;

Tenāhaṃ puññakammena,

Jātosiṃ devayoniyaṃ.

Thirty-one aeons ago from now, / whatever merit I produced, / by that meritorious deed, / I was born in a divine realm.

17.

17.

Sampattimanubhutvāna, chakāmagge parāparaṃ;

Devasaṅghaparibbūḷho, vimāne ratanāmaye.

Having experienced prosperity / again and again in the six sense-sphere heavens, / surrounded by hosts of devas, / in a mansion made of gems.

18.

18.

Manussesuca yaṃ aggaṃ, tassa bhāgī bhavāmahaṃ;

Bhoge me ūnatā natthi, saddhammasavaṇe phalaṃ.

Whatever is supreme among humans, / I was a partaker of that; / I have no lack of possessions— / such is the fruit of listening to the true Dhamma.

19.

19.

Imasmiṃ bhaddake kappe,

Sāvattipuramuttame;

Aḍḍhe mahaddhane sāle,

Jātohaṃ udite kule.

In this auspicious aeon, / in the excellent city of Sāvatthī, / in a wealthy, prosperous household, / I was born in a prominent family.

20.

20.

Mahatā parivārena, patto vuddhiṃca viññutaṃ;

Cārikaṃ caramānohaṃ, patto jetavanaṃ varaṃ.

With a great retinue, / having attained growth and maturity, / while wandering on a journey, / I reached the excellent Jetavana.

21.

21.

Addasaṃ saha sissehi, nisinnaṃ sugataṃ tadā;

Assosiṃ madhuraṃ dhammaṃ, catusaccappakāsakaṃ.

I saw the Fortunate One / seated then with his disciples; / I heard the sweet Dhamma, / proclaiming the Four Truths.

22.

22.

Sutvāna madhuraṃ dhammaṃ, pabbajitvāna sāsane;

A jarā maraṃ sītibhūtaṃ, patto nibbāṇamuttamaṃ.

Having heard the sweet Dhamma, / and gone forth into the Dispensation, / I attained the supreme Nibbāna, / the ageless, deathless, tranquil state.

23.

23.

Sutaṃ [M 0.0014] ekamuhuttaṃ me, tadā dhammaṃ sudesitaṃ;

Tenamhi caturāpāye, na jāto na kutobhayaṃ.

For a single moment I heard / the Dhamma well taught then; / because of that, in the four lower realms / I was not born, nor is there fear from anywhere.

24.

24.

Karamukkhippa vakkhāmi, karothe kagiraṃ mama;

Mamo pamaṃ karitvāna, dhammaṃ suṇātha sādhukanti.

Raising my hand, I will speak; / listen carefully to my words. / Having made me your example, / listen well to the Dhamma.

Evañca pana vatvā satte dhammasavaṇe niyojesīti.

And having said this, he exhorted beings to listen to the Dhamma.

25.

25.

Iti tanutarakālaṃ sādhu dhammaṃ suṇitvā,

Adhigatavibhavānaṃ ānubhāvaṃ suṇitvā;

Bhavavibhavasukhaṃ bho patthayantā kusītaṃ,

Jahatha suṇatha dhammaṃ dullabhaṃ dullabhassāti.

Thus, having well heard the good Dhamma in this brief time, having heard the power of those who have attained greatness; O you who desire the happiness of existence and prosperity, abandon sloth; listen to the Dhamma, rare and difficult to obtain.

Migaluddakassa vatthuṃ dutiyaṃ.

The Story of the Deer Hunter, the Second.

3. Tiṇṇaṃjanānaṃ vatthumhi ayamānupubbīkathā

3. This is the gradual discourse concerning the story of three individuals.

Jambudīpasmiṃ kira pubbe mahānidāgho ahosi, tadā nidāghasuriyena sakiraṇakarā vāpi pokkharaṇi nadī girikandaranijjharādīsu udakaṃ nissesaṃ katvā pītamiva udake parikkhīṇe macchakacchapādayo yebhuyyena vināsaṃ pattā. Atha mahāraññabhūmiyaṃ rukkhatiṇalabhādayo atīva milātā ahesuṃ. Migapakkhinopighammābhitattā pipāsito marīciṃ toyantimaññamānā itocitoca dhāvantā mahādukkhappattā ahesuṃ. Tadā eko suvapotako pipāsito tattha tattha pānīyaṃ pariyesanto mahāraññe ekasmiṃ pūtipādape saṭṭhiratane narakāvāṭe pānīyagandhaṃ ghāyitvā lobhena pātuṃ otiṇṇo atipānena bhāro tattheva patitvā uggantuṃ nāsakkhi. Athāparopi sappoca manussocāti dve janā tattheva patiṃsu, sappānāma vivekaṃ laddhāva attaṃ vijahanti. Tasmāyaṃ aladdhā [M 0.0015] vivekattaṃ uggantuṃ nāsakkhi. Anālambattā manussopi. Te uggantuṃ asakkontā maraṇabhayabhītā aññamañña maviheṭhentā tattheva vasiṃsu. Atheko barāṇasīvāsiko manusso vanaṃ paviṭṭho tatheva pānīyaṃ pariyesamāno taṃ ṭhānaṃ patvā te tayopi disvā kampamānahadayo valliyā piṭakaṃ bandhitvā sikkāya pakkhipitvā otāretvā te tayopi uddhari, athānena te amhākaṃ jīvitaṃ dinnanti somanassā tassevamāhaṃsu, sāmi mayaṃ tumhe nissāya jīvitaṃ labhimha, tumhe ito paṭṭhāya amhākaṃ sahāyo, mayampi te sahāyā, amhākaṃ vasanaṭṭhānāni āgantukāmāti vatvā tesu tāva suvapotako āha, sāmi bārāṇasiyaṃ dakkhiṇadvāre mahānigrodho atthi, tatthāhaṃ vasāmi, tava tathārūpe kicce sati mama santikamāgamma suvāti saddaṃkarohīti vatvā mettithiraṃ katvā pakkāmi, sappopi sammāhaṃ tasseva nigrodhassā vidūre mahantaṃ vammikaṃ atthi, tattha vasāmi, tavatthe sati tatthāgantvā dīghātisaddaṃ karohīti vatvā tathevapakkāmi, manussopi bārāṇasiyaṃ asukāyanāma vīthiyā asukagehe vasāmi, tavatthe sati mama santikaṃ āgacchāti vatvā pakkāmi, athā parabhāge so upakārako puriso attano kicce sañjāte mama sahāyānaṃ santikaṃ gamissāmīti saṅketānusārena gantvā nigrodhamūle ṭhito suvassa saddamakāsi, taṃ sutvā suvapotako vegenāgantvā tena saddhiṃ paṭisammoditvāsamma cirenā gatosi, āgatakāraṇaṃ me ācikkhāti āha. Sopāha sammāhaṃ jīvitu masakkonto puttadārake ñātīnaṃ paṭipādetvā tava santikamāgatomhiti, suvapotakopi sādhu samma tayā kataṃ mama santikamāgacchantena, tayā mama jīvitaṃ dinnaṃ, mayāpi tava jīvanupāyaṃ kātuṃ vaṭṭati, yāvāhaṃ āgacchāmi, tāvettha thokaṃ vissamāti vatvā pakkāmi jīvanupāyaṃ pariyesamāno, [M 0.0016] tasmiṃ kira samaye bārāṇasīrājā nagarato nikkhamma susajjituyyānaṃ pavisitvā sapariso kīḷitvā majjhantikasamaye suphullitaṃ pañcapadumasañchanvaṃ maṅgalapokkharaṇiṃ disvā nahāyitukāmo sabbābharaṇāni omuñcitvā rājapurise paṭipādetvā nahāyituṃ otari, tadā suvapotako taṃ ṭhānaṃ patto sākhantare nilīno rājapurisānaṃ pamādaṃ disvā rañño muttāhāraṃ ḍasitvā ākāsaṃ pakkhanditvā vegenāgantvā attano sahāyassa datvā appamatto imaṃ valañjehi sammāti vatvā adāsi, tato so naṃ gahetvā imaṃ kuhiṃ paṭisāmessāmīti cintonto mameko sahāyako antonagare vasati, tasmiṃ ṭhapessāmīti cintetvā yathāsaṅketamupagamma taṃ disvā paṭisanthāraṃ katvā suvapotakena kato pakāraṃ pakāsetvā imaṃ muttāhāraṃ sādhukaṃ ṭhapehīti vatvā adāsi, taṃkhaṇe rājā nahātvānulitto ābharaṇāni pilandhento muttāhāraṃ nāddasa. Tato rājapurisā antoca bahica parijane upaparikkhitvā muttāhāraṃ apassantā nagare bheriṃ carāpesuṃ, yo muttāhāraṃ passati, tassa rājā mahantaṃ yasaṃ dassatīti. Taṃ sutvā so mittadūbhī evaṃ cintesi, ahaṃcamhi dukkhito, yannūnāhaṃ muttāhāraṃ rañño dassetvā sukhena vaseyyaṃ, kimme etenāti tena kataṃ tathārūpaṃ upakāraṃ asallakkhento mahāmittadūbhī puriso rājapurise upasaṅkamma muttahāraṃ attano santike ṭhapitabhāvaṃ kathesi, bho mama santike eko puriso muttahāraṃ ṭhapesīti. Evaṃ asappurisasaṃsaggoti, tathāhi.

It is said that formerly in Jambudīpa there was a great drought. At that time, the scorching sun, with its rays, completely dried up the water in ponds, lakes, rivers, mountain streams, and so on, as if it had drunk it. When the water was exhausted, most of the fish, turtles, and other creatures met with destruction. Then, in the great forest, trees, grass, creepers, and other vegetation withered exceedingly. Animals and birds, tormented by heat and thirst, mistaking mirages for water, ran here and there, experiencing great suffering. At that time, a young parrot, suffering from thirst, searched everywhere for water in the great forest. Smelling the scent of water near a rotten tree in a pit sixty ratanas deep, it descended out of greed to drink. Heavy from excessive drinking, it fell right there and could not get out. Then, a snake and a man also fell into the same pit. Snakes, it is said, only abandon themselves when they have found solitude; therefore, this one, not having found solitude, could not get out. The man, too, because of having no support, could not get out. Unable to get out and terrified of death, they remained there, not harming one another. Then, a man from Varanasi entered the forest searching for water. Coming upon that place and seeing the three of them, his heart trembled. He tied a basket with a vine, put it in a carrying-sling, lowered it, and pulled all three out. Then they, joyful that he had given them their lives, said to him, 'Master, we have gained our lives thanks to you. From now on, you are our friend, and we too are your friends. Please come to our dwelling places.' Among them, the young parrot spoke first, 'Master, near the southern gate of Varanasi, there is a great banyan tree. I live there. If you have any need, come to my presence and make the sound "Suva!"' Having established a firm friendship, it departed. The snake also said, 'Friend, not far from that same banyan tree, there is a large anthill. I live there. If you have need, come there and make the sound "Dīgha!"' And so it departed. The man also said, 'In Varanasi, on such-and-such street, in such-and-such house, I live. If you have need, come to my presence.' And so he departed. Later, when a need arose for that helpful man, he thought, 'I will go to my friends.' Following the agreed-upon sign, he went and stood at the foot of the banyan tree and made the sound for the parrot. Hearing it, the young parrot came swiftly, and after exchanging greetings with him, said, 'Friend, you have come after a long time. Tell me the reason for your coming.' He replied, 'Friend, being unable to make a living, I have entrusted my children and wife to my relatives and have come to you.' The young parrot said, 'It is good, friend, that you have come to me. You gave me my life, and it is right for me to provide a means of livelihood for you. Wait here for a little while until I return.' And it departed in search of a means of livelihood. At that time, it is said, the king of Varanasi had left the city and entered a well-adorned park. After playing with his retinue, at midday, he saw an auspicious pond covered with blooming lotuses of five colors and wished to bathe. Removing all his ornaments, he entrusted them to his royal attendants and entered the water to bathe. Then the young parrot, having reached that place and hiding among the branches, saw the negligence of the royal attendants, seized the king’s pearl necklace with its beak, flew up into the sky, and coming swiftly, gave it to its friend, saying, 'Friend, use this with care!' Then he, taking it, thought, 'Where shall I keep this safe?' Thinking, 'I have a friend who lives inside the city, I will leave it with him,' he went according to the agreement, and seeing him, made a friendly greeting, explained the deed done by the young parrot, and saying, 'Keep this pearl necklace safely,' he gave it to him. At that moment, the king, having bathed and anointed himself, while putting on his ornaments, did not see the pearl necklace. Then the royal attendants, having searched among the retinue both inside and outside and not seeing the pearl necklace, had a drum beaten in the city, proclaiming, 'Whoever finds the pearl necklace, the king will give him great honor.' Hearing this, that treacherous friend thought thus, 'I am afflicted with suffering. Why should I not show the pearl necklace to the king and live in happiness? What is this to me?' Not recognizing the great help that had been done, that most treacherous man approached the king’s men and told them that the pearl necklace had been left in his presence, saying, 'Sirs, a man left a pearl necklace with me.' Thus is association with the wicked. Indeed.

1.

1.

Yathā saṃvaḍḍhito nimbo, madhukhīro dasiñcanā;

Na yāti madhuraṃ taṃ vo, pakāra masataṃ kataṃ.

As a neem tree, though grown and watered with honey and milk, does not become sweet, so is kindness done to the wicked.

2.

2.

Sīsenu daka mādāya, vaḍḍhitopi nuhītaru;

Na yāti madhuraṃ taṃvo, pakāramasataṃ kataṃ.

Though the nuhī tree is grown by drawing up water to its crown, it does not become sweet; so is kindness done to the wicked.

3.

3.

Niccaṃ [M 0.0017] khīrodapānena, vaḍḍhito siviso yathā;

Visaṃva parivatteti, tathā nīcopakārakaṃ.

Just as a poisonous snake, though constantly nurtured with milk and water, produces only poison, so does a base person repay kindness.

4.

4.

Yathāttanā kato aggi, sītalaṃ na dade khalu;

Tathā nīce kataṃ kāraṃ, aggīva dahate tanuṃ.

As a fire made by oneself truly does not give coolness, so too a deed done to a base person, like fire, burns the body.

5.

5.

Tasmā upaparikkhitvā, hāvabhāvena buddhiyā;

Kātabbā metti jantūhi, nāmitto labhatesukhanti.

Therefore, having carefully examined with wisdom and discernment, friendship should be made with beings; one who has an enemy does not obtain happiness.

Athassa mittadubhino vacanena rājapurisā muttāhāraṃcataṃca gahetvā sabhaṇḍakaṃ purisaṃ dassesuṃ. Atha rājā sabhaṇḍakaṃ coraṃ disvā kuddho imaṃ netvā dakkhiṇadvāre jīvasūle uttāsethāti āṇāpesi, rājapurisā tassa rājāṇaṃ karonto agamaṃsu, tehi nīyamāno puriso dakkhiṇadvārā nikkhamma sappasahāyaṃ saritvā appevanāme tassa santikā kiñci sotthi bhaveyyāti pubbe vuttasaṅketā nusārena vammikaṃ disvā samma dīghāti saddamakāsi, so vammikā nikkhammataṃ tathā niyamānaṃ disvā saṃviggo dukkhappatto sahāyassame ajja avassayena upatthambhaṃ bhavituṃ vaṭṭatīti taṃ samassāsetvā attabhāvaṃ vijahitvā aññataravesena rājapurise upasaṅkamma imaṃ purisaṃ muhuttaṃmā mārethāti daḷhaṃ vatvā muhuttena rañño aggamahesiyā vasanaṭṭhānaṃ gantvā sappavaṇṇena deviṃ ḍasitvā tāya visena mucchitakāle manussavaṇṇena vajjhappatto visosadhaṃ jānātitī vatvā taṃkhaṇeva sahāyassa santikaṃ gantvā raññā tava pakko sitakāle gantvā udakappasatena deviyā sarīre paharitvā nibbisaṃ karāhīti vatvā pakkāmi, atha rājā visavejja pariyesanto taṃ pavattiṃ sutvā vajjhappattaṃ ānethāti āṇāpetvā deviyā nibbisaṃ karothāti āha, so nāgarājena vuttanayena nibbisamakāsi, sā sukhitā arogā ahosi, rājā taṃ disvā tuṭṭho tassa khettavatthuyānavāhanādidānena [M 0.0018] mahāsakkāramakāsi, atha so rājānaṃ upasaṅkamma attanā kataṃ sabbaṃ pakāsesi. Tena vuttaṃ.

Then, by the word of that treacherous friend, the king’s men seized the man along with his goods and the pearl necklace, and presented him. When the king saw the thief with the goods, he became furious and ordered, 'Take this man to the southern gate and impale him alive on a stake!' The king’s men, carrying out the king’s command, took him away. As he was being led out through the southern gate, the man remembered his serpent friend and thought, 'Perhaps some safety might come from him.' Following their previously arranged signal, he saw the anthill and cried out, 'Friend Dīgha!' The serpent, emerging from the anthill and seeing him being led away thus, was deeply agitated and distressed, and thought, 'Today, it is absolutely necessary for me to be a support for my friend.' Comforting him, the serpent abandoned his own form and, taking another guise, approached the king’s men. He firmly said, 'Do not kill this man for a moment!' Then, in an instant, he went to the chief queen’s chamber, bit the queen in the form of a snake, and when she fainted from the venom, he assumed a human guise and declared, 'The condemned man knows the antidote!' Immediately, he returned to his friend’s side and told him, 'When the king summons you, go and sprinkle the queen’s body with a handful of water and make her free from poison.' Then he departed. Meanwhile, the king, searching for a poison-doctor, heard of this matter and ordered, 'Bring the condemned man!' He then said, 'Make the queen free from poison!' The man, following the method told by the Nāga king, made her free from poison, and the queen became well and healthy again. Seeing this, the king was delighted and honored him greatly with gifts of fields, property, carriages, vehicles, and so on. Then, the man approached the king and revealed everything that he had done. Therefore it is said:

6.

6.

Ekadāhaṃ mahārāja, vanaṃ kammena kenaci;

Gatoddasaṃ mahāvāṭe, patitaṃ suvapotakaṃ.

Once, great king, I went to the forest for some work; / In a great pit, I saw a young parrot that had fallen.

7.

7.

Atho ragaṃ manussaṃca;

Dukkhappatte khudāpare;

Ukkhipiṃ karuṇāyāhaṃ;

Te me vocuṃ tadā tayo.

Then a snake and a man, / Suffering and afflicted by hunger, / I lifted up out of compassion; / Then those three spoke to me.

8.

8.

Adāsi jīvamamhākaṃ, upakārosi no tuvaṃ;

Tava kicce samuppanne, amhākaṃ ehi santikaṃ.

‘You gave us our life, you have been our benefactor; / When a need arises for you, come into our presence.’

9.

9.

Evaṃ tehi pavuttohaṃ, agañchiṃ suvasantikaṃ;

Tena katūpakārohaṃ, manussassāpi santikaṃ.

Thus spoken to by them, I went to the parrot's presence; / Having been helped by him, I went also to the man's presence.

10.

10.

Tenāhaṃ maraṇappatto, addasaṃ uragādhipaṃ;

Sodāsi jīvitaṃ mayhaṃ, alatthaṃ aladdha itisabbattha vipulaṃ dhanaṃ.

Through him I was brought to the point of death; I saw the serpent king. / He gave me my life, and I obtained vast wealth.

11.

11.

Sujano nāvamantabbo, khuddakoti narādhipa;

Suvoca uragocete, mittadhamme patiṭṭhitā.

A good person should not be despised, thinking, ‘He is lowly,’ O king of men; / The parrot and the snake, these two, were established in the principles of friendship.

12.

12.

Kāraṇaññū manusseso,

Amhehi samajātiko;

Katūpakāro evampi,

Diso jāto narādhamo.

This man, who should have been grateful, / Is of the same species as us; / Even though kindness was done to him, / This base man became an enemy.

13.

13.

Akasmā deva kuppanti, pasīdantinimittato;

Sīlaṃ hetamasādhūnaṃ, bālānamavijānataṃ.

For no reason they become angry, and without cause they are pleased; / This is the nature of the wicked, of fools who do not understand.

14.

14.

Manussāpi mahārāja, keci vissāsiyā na ve;

Tiracchānāpi honteva, ajimhamanasāsaṭhāti.

O great king, some humans are indeed not to be trusted; / While even animals can be of straight mind and not deceitful.

Evaṃ [M 0.0019] so attano pavattiṃ kathesi, rājā taṃ sutvā pasanno imassa purisassa mahantaṃ gehaṃ katvā mahāparihāraṃ karothāti āṇāpesi, so pana mama gehaṃ nigrodhassa ca vammikassa ca antare karothāti vatvā tathā kāretvā tattha vasanto rājūpaṭṭhānaṃ karonto tehi sahāyehi saddhiṃ sammodamāno yāvajīvaṃ vasitvā āyu pariyosāne tehi saddhiṃ yathākammaṃ gatoti.

Thus he recounted his own story. The king, hearing it, was pleased and ordered, 'Have a great house built for this man and provide him with great honors.' But he said, 'Build my house between the banyan tree and the anthill.' Having spoken thus, he had it built and lived there, attending on the king and rejoicing with his friends. Having lived for the extent of his life, at the end of his lifespan, he passed on according to his kamma, together with them.

15.

15.

Iti patitasukhamhā aṅgato vā dhanamhā,

Paramatarapatiṭṭhā honti mittā sakhānaṃ;

Virahitasakhinaṃ bho natthi yasmābhivuddhi,

Ciṇutha kusaladhammaṃ mittavantā mahantaṃ.

For one fallen from happiness, from limb, or from wealth, friends are the supreme support for their companions; / Since for those bereft of friends there is truly no prosperity, cultivate the great wholesome quality of being rich in friends.

Tiṇṇaṃ janānaṃ vatthuṃ tatiyaṃ.

The third story, of the three persons.

4. Buddheniyā vatthumhi ayamānupubbīkathā

4. In the story of Buddhenī, this is the sequential account:

Jambudīpe kira pubbe pāṭaliputtanagare sattāsītīkoṭinihitadhanaṃ ekaṃ seṭṭhikulaṃ ahosi, tassa pana seṭṭhino ekāyeva dhītā ahosi nāmena buddhenināma, tassā sattavassikakāle mātāpitaro kālamakaṃsu, tasmiṃ kule sabbaṃ sāpateyyaṃ tassāyeva ahosi, sā kira abhirūpā pāsādikā paramāya vaṇṇapokkharatāya samannāgatā devaccharapaṭibhāgā piyāca ahosi manāpā, saddhā pasannā ratanattayamāmikā paṭivasati, tasmiṃ pana nagare seṭṭhisenāpatiuparājādayo taṃ attano pādaparicārikaṃ kāmayamānā manusse pesesuṃ paṇṇākārehi saddhiṃ, sā taṃ sutvā cintesi, mayhaṃ mātāpitaro sabbaṃ vibhavaṃ pahāya matā, mayā pana tathā agantabbaṃ, kiṃ me patikulena, kevalaṃ [M 0.0020] vittavināsāya bhavati, mayā pani maṃ dhanaṃ buddhasāsaneyeva nidahituṃ vaṭṭatīti cintesi, cintetvā ca pana tesaṃ na mayhaṃ patikulenatthoti paṭikkhipi, sā tato paṭṭhāya mahādānaṃ pavattentī samaṇabrahmaṇe santappesi, tenettha.

In Jambudīpa, it is said that formerly in the city of Pāṭaliputta, there was a merchant family possessing eighty-seven koṭis of buried wealth. This merchant had only one daughter, named Buddhenī. When she was seven years old, her parents passed away, leaving all the family’s property solely to her. She was said to be very beautiful, pleasing, endowed with supreme loveliness, like a celestial nymph, and was beloved and agreeable. She dwelt there, faithful, serene, and devoted to the Triple Gem. In that city, wealthy merchants, generals, viceroys, and others, desiring her as their wife, sent messengers with gifts. Hearing this, she thought: 'My parents have died, abandoning all their wealth; I too must go in that same way. What need have I of a husband's family? It only leads to the destruction of wealth. It is fitting for me to invest this wealth in the Buddha’s Dispensation.' Having thought thus, she rejected them, saying, 'I have no need of a husband’s family.' From then on, instituting great giving, she satisfied ascetics and brahmins. Therefore it is said:

1.

1.

Catuddisāyātajinatrajānaṃ,

Āpānabhūtaṃ gharamāsi tassā;

Yadicchita ppaccayalābha hetu,

Devaddumovāsi mahānubhāvo.

For the sons of the Jina who came from the four directions, her house was a place of refreshment; / Because they could obtain whatever requisites they desired, she was like a divine tree of great power.

2.

2.

Pupphūpahārādi vitānalaṅkatā,

Padīpa paññatta subhāsanāvalī;

Sukhāsanāsīna vasīhilaṅkatā,

Tassāsi tasmiṃ varadānasālā.

Adorned with offerings of flowers and with canopies, / With rows of fine seats prepared and lamps lit; / Adorned by the masters seated in comfort, / Such was her excellent hall of giving.

3.

3.

Sudhotahatthā sucipuññacittā,

Sadādarā rakkhitapañcasīlā;

Buddhenināmā karuṇā guṇaggā,

Adā mahādānavaraṃ pasatthanti.

With hands well-washed and a mind pure in merit, ever respectful, guarding the five precepts; / Buddhenī by name, compassionate, highest in virtue, gave the great, excellent, and praised gift.

Athāparabhāge eko assavāṇijako assa-vāṇijjāya pubbantāparantaṃ gacchanto āgamma imissā gehe nivāsaṃ gaṇhi, atha so vāṇijo taṃ disvā dhītusinehaṃ pati ṭṭhā pe tvā gandhamālavatthālaṅkārādīhi tassā upakārako hutvā gamanakāle amma etesu assesu tava ruccanakaṃ assaṃ gaṇhāhīti āha, sāpi asse oloketvā ekaṃ sindhavapotakaṃ disvā etaṃ me dehīti āha, vāṇijo amma eso sindhavapotako, appamattā hutvā paṭijaggāhīti vatvā taṃ paṭipādetvā agamāsi. Sāpi taṃ paṭijaggamānā ākāsagāmibhāvaṃ ñatvā sammā paṭijaggantī evaṃ cintesi, puññakaraṇassa [M 0.0021] me sahāyo laddhoti, agatapubbāca me bhagavato samāraṃ mārabalaṃ vidhametvā buddhabhūtassa jayamahābodhibhūmi, yannūnāhaṃ tattha gantvā bhagavato jayamahābodhiṃ vandeyyanti cintetvā ba hū rajatasuvaṇṇamālādayo kārāpetvā ekadivasaṃ assa mabhiruyha ākāsena gantvā bodhimālake ṭhatvā āgacchantuayyā suvaṇṇamālā pūjetuṃti ugghosesi. Tenettha.

Then, on a later occasion, a horse trader, traveling back and forth for his horse trade, arrived and took up residence in her house. Seeing her, the merchant established a fatherly affection for her and, having been helpful to her with perfumes, garlands, clothes, ornaments, and so on, at the time of his departure, he said, 'My dear, among these horses, take whichever one pleases you.' She, looking over the horses, saw a young Sindhu colt and said, 'Give me this one.' The merchant replied, 'My dear, this is a Sindhu colt—be diligent and tend to it well.' Having entrusted it to her, he departed. She, while tending to it, realized its ability to travel through the sky. Tending to it well, she thought, 'I have gained a companion for making merit. I have never before gone to the ground of the great Bodhi of victory, where the Blessed One, having shattered the army of Māra together with Māra, became a Buddha. Surely I should go there and pay homage to the Blessed One’s great Bodhi of victory?' Having thought thus, she had many silver and golden garlands and other things made. One day, mounting the horse, she went through the sky and stood on the terrace of the Bodhi tree, proclaiming, 'Let the noble ones come to offer golden garlands!' Therefore it is said:

4.

4.

Yato paṭṭhāyahaṃ buddha, sāsane suddhamānasā;

Pasannā tena saccena, mamānuggahabuddhiyā.

Since I, with a pure mind, have been devoted to the Buddha's Dispensation, / By that act of truth, and with the intention of favoring me,

5.

5.

Āgacchantu namassantu, bodhiṃ pūjentu sādhukaṃ;

Soṇṇamālāhi sambuddha, puttā ariyasāvakā.

Let the noble disciples, sons of the Perfectly Enlightened One, come! / Let them pay homage and worship the Bodhi well with golden garlands.

6.

6.

Sutvā taṃ vacanaṃ ayyā, bahū sīhaḷavāsino;

Āgamma nabhasā tattha, vandiṃ suca mahiṃsucāti.

Hearing those words, many noble ones, residents of Sīhaḷa, / Arriving there through the sky, paid homage and honored her.

Tato ppatuti sā kumārikā buddhasāsane atīva pasannā niccameva assa mabhiruyha āgantvā ariyehi saddhiṃ mahābodhiṃ suvaṇṇamālāhi pūjetvā gacchati, atha pāṭaliputtanagaropavane vanacarā tassā abhiṇhaṃ gacchantiyā ca āgacchantiyā ca rūpasampattiṃ disvā rañño kathesuṃ. Mahārāja evarūpā kumārikā assa mabhiruyha āgantvā nibaddhaṃ vanditvā gacchati. Devassānurūpā aggamahesī bhavitunti, rā jā taṃ sutvā tenahi bhaṇe gaṇhatha naṃ kumāriṃ, mama aggamahesiṃ karomīti purise payojesi, tena payuttapurisā bodhipūjaṃ katvā āgacchantiṃ gaṇhāmāti tattha nilīnā gahaṇasajjā aṭṭhaṃsu, tadā sā kumārikā assa mabhiruyha mahābodhimaṇḍaṃ gantvā vītarogehi saddhiṃ pupphapūjaṃ katvā vanditvā nivatti, atha tesu eko dhammarakkhita ttheronāma tassā eva māha, bhagini taṃ antarāmagge corā gaṇhitukāmā ṭhitā, asukaṭṭhānaṃ patvā appamattā [M 0.0022] sīghaṃ gacchāti, sāpi gacchantī taṃ ṭhānaṃ patvā corehi anubandhitā assassa paṇhiyā saññaṃ datvā pakkāmi, corā pacchato pacchato anubandhiṃsu. Asso vegaṃ ja ne tvā ākāsa mullaṅghi, kumārikā vegaṃ sandhāretuṃ asakkontī assassa piṭṭhito parigalitvā patantī mayā katūpakāraṃ sara puttāti āha, so patantiṃ disvā vegenā gantvā piṭṭhiyaṃ nisīdāpetvā ākāsato netvā sa ka ṭṭhā ne yeva patiṭṭhāpesi. Tasmā.

From that time, that maiden, being exceedingly devoted to the Buddha’s dispensation, would always come riding an ass to worship the great Bodhi tree with golden garlands alongside the noble ones, and then depart. Then the forest-dwellers in the grove near the city of Pāṭaliputta, seeing her perfection of form as she frequently came and went, reported to the king: 'O great king, such a maiden rides an ass, comes regularly, pays homage, and departs. She would be a fitting chief queen for Your Majesty.' Hearing this, the king dispatched men, saying, 'Then go, seize that maiden. I will make her my chief queen.' The men he dispatched lay in wait there, ready to capture her as she returned after worshipping the Bodhi tree. At that time, the maiden rode her ass to the great Bodhi precinct, made floral offerings with those free from passion, paid homage, and turned back. Then one of them, the elder named Dhammarakkhita, said to her, 'Sister, on the path, thieves are waiting, intending to seize you. When you reach a certain place, be vigilant and go swiftly.' As she traveled, upon reaching that place, she was pursued by the thieves. She gave the ass a signal with her heel and sped away, and the thieves pursued her from behind. The ass, generating speed, leaped into the sky. Unable to withstand the speed, the maiden slipped from its back and, as she was falling, cried out, 'Remember the help I have given you, my son!' Seeing her falling, the ass swiftly went, seated her on its back, brought her through the sky, and set her down in her own place. Therefore...

7.

7.

Tiracchānagatāpevaṃ, sarantā upakārakaṃ;

Na jahantīti mantvāna, kataññū hontu pāṇinoti.

Even animals, remembering a benefactor, do not abandon them; knowing this, may all beings be grateful.

Tato sā kumārikā sattā sītikoṭidhanaṃ buddhasāsaneyeva cajitvā yāvajīvaṃ sīlaṃ rakkhitvā uposathakammaṃ ka tvā ta to cutā sutta ppabuddho viya devaloke nibbattīti.

Then that maiden, having given up eighty-seven crores of wealth for the Buddha's dispensation, observed the precepts for as long as she lived and performed the Uposatha observance. After passing away from there, she was reborn in the deva world, like one awakened from sleep.

8.

8.

Atitaruṇavayā bho mātugāmāpi evaṃ,

Vividhakusalakammaṃ katva saggaṃ vajanti;

Kusalaphalamahantaṃ maññamānā bhavantā,

Bhavatha katha mupekkhā dānamānādikamme.

O women, even those of very tender age, by performing various wholesome deeds, thus go to heaven. You, considering the great fruit of wholesome action, how can you be indifferent to deeds such as giving and honoring?

Buddheniyā vatthuṃ catutthaṃ.

The fourth story, concerning Buddhenī.

5. Ahituṇḍikassa vatthumhi ayamānupubbīkathā

5. In the story of the snake charmer, this is the connected account.

Imasmiṃ kira bhaddakappamhi amhākaṃ kira bhagavato pubbe kassaponāma satthā loke uppajjitvā sadevakaṃ lokaṃ saṃsārasāgarā tāretvā sabbabuddhakiccāni niṭṭhapetvā atthaṃ gato divasakaroviya setabyamhi anupādisesāya nibbāṇadhātuyā parinibbāyi, tadā sakalajambudīpavāsino manussā sannipatitvā [M 0.0023] ekekaṃ suvaṇṇiṭṭhakaṃ koṭiagghanakaṃ ratanavicittaṃ bahicinanatthāya ekekaṃ aḍḍhakoṭiagghanakaṃ abbhantarūpapūraṇatthaṃ manosilāya mattikākiccaṃ karontā yojanabbedhaṃ thūpaṃ katvā mahantaṃ sakkāraṃ karonti. Tadā eko ahituṇḍiko gāmanigamarājadhānīsu sappe kīḷāpetvā jīvikaṃ kappento ekaṃ gāmakaṃ patvā tattha sappe kīḷāpetvā santuṭṭhehi gāmavāsīhi dinnavividhopāyano khādanīyabhojanīyaṃ khā di tvā bhuñjitvā tattheva nivāsaṃ gahetvā nisīdi. Tasmiṃ kira gāmake manussā yebhuyyena ratanattayamāmakā. Tasmā te rattibhāge sayantā “namo buddhāyāti” evamādiṃ vadanti. So pana ahituṇḍiko micchādiṭṭhiko tiṇṇaṃ ratanānaṃ guṇaṃ na jānāti. Tasmā tesaṃ taṃ vacanaṃ sutvā sayampi ke ḷiṃ kurumāno parihāsavasena “namo buddhāyāti” vadati, athekadivasaṃ so attano kīḷāpanasamatthaṃ ekaṃ sappaṃ bhattha tattha pariyesamāno āhiṇḍati. Tadā eko nāgarājā kassapadasabalassa thū paṃ ga ntvā vanditvā ekaṃ vammikaṃ pavisati. Taṃ di svā ahituṇḍiko vegenā gantvā nāgarājaṃ gaṇhituṃ mantaṃ parijapi, so mantaṃ sutvā kujjhitvā taṃ māretukāmo anubandhi, taṃ di svā ahituṇḍiko ve ge na palāyanto ekasmiṃ pāsāṇe pakkhalitvā patamāno pubbevuttaparihāsa vacanaparicayena “namo buddhāyāti” vadanto pati. Tassa taṃ vacanaṃ anubandhantassa nāgarañño sotapathe amataṃviya pati. Atha so ratanattayagāravena tasmiṃ kodhaṃ nibbāpetvā samma mā bhāyi. Ahaṃ ratanattayamantānubhāvapāsena baddho. Tasmā tu vaṃ ḍasituṃ mayhaṃ ananurūpaṃ. Ajja ta yi pasannomhi, paṇṇākāraṃ tedammi, gaṇhāti tīṇi suvaṇṇapupphāni adāsi. Evaṃ ratanattayaṃ nāma ghorāsivisānampi sappānaṃ manaṃ pīṇeti. Honti cettha.

It is said that in this auspicious eon, before our Blessed One, a Teacher named Kassapa arose in the world. Having ferried the world with its devas across the ocean of saṃsāra and completed all the duties of a Buddha, he went to his setting like the sun and, in Setabyā, attained final Nibbāna with the Nibbāna-element without residue. At that time, the people of the entire Jambudīpa gathered together. For the outer construction, they used golden bricks, each worth a crore and adorned with jewels; for the inner filling, they used bricks worth half a crore each, doing the work of mortar with red arsenic. They built a stupa one yojana in height and performed great acts of honor. At that time, a certain snake charmer, making a living by having snakes dance in villages, towns, and royal capitals, arrived at a certain village. There, he had the snakes dance, and the pleased villagers gave him various gifts of food. Having eaten, he took up residence there and sat down. Now, in that village, the people were mostly devoted to the Triple Gem. Therefore, when lying down at night, they would say, 'Homage to the Buddha,' and so forth. But that snake charmer, being of wrong view, did not know the virtues of the Three Jewels. Therefore, hearing their words, he himself, making sport and in jest, would say, 'Homage to the Buddha.' Then one day, he was wandering about, searching for a snake suitable for his performance. At that time, a nāga king, having gone to the stupa of Kassapa, the one of Ten Powers, paid homage and entered an anthill. Seeing this, the snake charmer quickly went and recited a spell to capture the nāga king. Hearing the spell, the nāga became angry and pursued him, intending to kill him. Seeing this, the snake charmer fled swiftly, but stumbling on a rock, he fell. Through his familiarity with the previously spoken words of jest, he fell crying out, 'Homage to the Buddha!' As the nāga king was pursuing him, these words fell upon his ear like nectar. Then, out of reverence for the Triple Gem, he extinguished his anger and said, 'Friend, do not fear. I am bound by the snare of the power of the Triple Gem. Therefore, it is not fitting for me to bite you. Today I am pleased with you; I will give you a gift.' Saying, 'Take these,' he gave him three golden flowers. Thus, the Triple Gem pleases the minds even of snakes as fearsome as deadly vipers. And here are the verses:

1.

1.

Buddhoti [M 0.0024] vacanaṃ seṭṭhaṃ, buddhoti pada muttamaṃ;

Natthi tena samaṃ loke, aññaṃ sotarasāyanaṃ.

The word ‘Buddha’ is supreme, the term ‘Buddha’ is the highest; there is nothing in the world equal to it, no other elixir for the ear.

2.

2.

Dhammotivacanaṃ seṭṭhaṃ, dhammoti padamuttamaṃ;

Natthi tena samaṃ loke, aññaṃ sotarasāyanaṃ.

The word ‘Dhamma’ is supreme, the term ‘Dhamma’ is the highest; there is nothing in the world equal to it, no other elixir for the ear.

3.

3.

Saṅghoti vacanaṃ seṭṭhaṃ, saṅghoti padamuttamaṃ;

Natthi tena samaṃ loke, aññaṃ sotarasāyanaṃ.

The word ‘Saṅgha’ is supreme, the term ‘Saṅgha’ is the highest; there is nothing in the world equal to it, no other elixir for the ear.

4.

4.

Tassa mukhaṃ mukhaṃ nāma, yaṃ vattati mukhe sadā;

Dullabhaṃ buddhavacanaṃ, sabbasampattidāyakaṃ.

That is truly a mouth, in which there is always present the rare word of the Buddha, the bestower of all accomplishments.

5.

5.

Tassa mano mano nāma, yaṃ ce manasi vattati;

Dullabhaṃ buddhavacanaṃ, sabbasampattidāyakaṃ.

That is truly a mind, in which there is always present the rare word of the Buddha, the bestower of all accomplishments.

6.

6.

Tameva kavacaṃ dehe, tameva maṇi kāmado;

Tameva surabhī dhenu, tameva surapādapo.

That alone is the armor for the body, that alone is the wish-fulfilling gem; that alone is the wish-granting cow, that alone is the celestial tree.

7.

7.

Tasseva sotaṃ sotaṃva, yaṃ suṇāti jano ayaṃ

Dullabhaṃ buddhavacanaṃ, sabbasampattidāyakaṃ.

That is truly an ear, with which this person hears the rare word of the Buddha, the bestower of all accomplishments.

8.

8.

Evaṃ vidho rago ghoro, haḷāhaḷaviso sadā;

Buddhoti vacanaṃ sutvā, santuṭṭho dāsi jīvitaṃ.

Such was his terrible anger, ever like a deadly poison; yet hearing the word ‘Buddha,’ he was pleased and gave him his life.

9.

9.

Soṇṇapupphattayaṃcāpi, mahagghaṃ bahalaṃ adā;

Passa buddhoti vācāya, ānubhāvamahantatanti.

He also gave three golden flowers, precious and abundant. Behold the great power of the utterance, ‘Buddha’!

Atha nāgarājā tassa tāni suvaṇṇapupphāni datvā evamāha. Samma etesu ekaṃ tava puññattāya ekaṃ mama puññatthāya pūjehi. Itarena yāvajīvaṃ sukhena jīvanto puttadāre posento dānādīsu appamajjanto jīvikaṃ kappehi. Mā hīnakamme byavaṭo hohi, micchādiṭṭhiñca pajahāti ovaditvā pakkāmi. Ahituṇḍikopi somanassappatto tena vuttanayeneva dvihi pupphehi cetiyaṃ pūjetvā ekena sa ha ssaṃ labhitvā te na puttadāre [M 0.0025] posento kapaṇaddhikavaṇibbakādīnaṃ dānaṃ dento ahituṇḍikakammaṃ pahāha kusalameva upacinanto āyupariyosāne saggaparāyano ahosi.

Then the nāga king, giving him those golden flowers, said this: 'Friend, offer one of these for your own merit and one for my merit. With the other, live happily for as long as you live, support your wife and children, and make your living while being diligent in giving and other such deeds. Do not engage in base actions, and abandon wrong view.' Having advised him thus, he departed. The snake charmer, filled with joy, worshipped the cetiya with two flowers in the manner instructed. With the remaining one, he obtained a thousand, and with that he supported his wife and children. Giving alms to the poor, the destitute, and beggars, he abandoned the trade of a snake charmer and accumulated only what was wholesome. At the end of his life, he was bound for heaven.

10.

10.

Iti aviditasatto kiñci buddhānubhāvaṃ,

Labhati dhanavisesaṃ yassa nāmappakāsā;

Viditajananikāyo kinnu tassānubhāvaṃ,

Na lapati jinanāmaṃ kicca maññappahāyāti.

Thus, an ignorant being, knowing nothing of the Buddha’s power, obtains special wealth merely by proclaiming his name; why then would the assembly of the wise not proclaim the Conqueror’s name and his power, abandoning other concerns?

Ahituṇḍikassa vatthuṃ pañcamaṃ.

The Story of the Snake Charmer, the Fifth.

6. Saraṇattherassa vatthumhi ayamānupubbīkathā

6. Regarding the story of Elder Saraṇa, this is the connected account.

Sāvatthiyaṃ kira sumano nāme ko gahapati ahosi. Tassa bhariyā sujampatikā nāma. Te agāraṃ ajjhāvasantā aparabhāge puttaṃca dhītaraṃca labhiṃsu. Atha tesaṃ daharakāleyeva mātāpitaro kālaṃ karontā jeṭṭhakaṃpakkositvā mayaṃ putta tuvaṃ patirūpe ṭhāne nivesituṃ nāsakkhimha, yaṃ no ghare vibhavaṃ, sabbaṃ taṃ gaṇha. Imāyaca te kaṇiṭṭhikāya vuddhiṃtvameva jānāhīti vatvā jeṭṭhakassa hatthe kaṇiṭṭhikāya hatthaṃ ṭhapetvā kālamakaṃsu. Atha so mātāpitunnaṃ accayena āḷāhanakiccaṃ katvā vasanto kālantarena kaṇiṭṭhikaṃ patirūpena kulena sambandhitvā sayampi dārapariggahamakāsi. Athāparabhāge tassa kaṇiṭṭhikā gabbhinī hutvā ekadivasaṃ sāmikaṃ āha, sāmi mama bhātaraṃ daṭṭhukāmāmhīti. Sopi sādhu bhaddeti anurūpena paṇṇākārena tāya saddhiṃ nikkhami. Tadā pana bhagavā sunivattho supāruto bhikkhusaṅghaparivuso piṇḍāya nagaraṃ pāvisi chabbaṇṇaghanabuddharaṃsiyo vissajjento, ta to te bhagavantaṃ disvā pasannacittā pañcapatiṭṭhitena [M 0.0026] vanditvā aṭṭhaṃsu, atha sattā tesaṃ jayampatikānaṃ upanissayasampattiṃdisvā te saraṇesuca sīlesuca patiṭṭhāpetvā evamāha, kadāci vo dukkhe uppanne tathāgato anussaritabboti. Tathāhi.

It is said that in Sāvatthī there was a householder named Sumana. His wife was named Sujampatikā. Living in their house, they later had a son and a daughter. Then, while the children were still young, their parents, while dying, called their elder child and said, “Son, we were not able to settle you in a suitable position. Take all the wealth in our house. And as for your younger sister, you alone must see to her upbringing.” Having said this, they placed the younger sister’s hand in the elder brother’s hand and died. After his parents’ passing, the elder brother performed the funeral rites and continued living. In time, he arranged a suitable marriage for his younger sister and also took a wife himself. Later, his younger sister became pregnant. One day, she said to her husband, “Husband, I wish to see my brother.” He agreed, saying, “Very well, dear,” and set out with her with suitable gifts. At that time, the Blessed One, well-robed and with his outer robe draped, surrounded by the Sangha of monks, entered the city for alms, emitting the six-hued Buddha-rays. Seeing the Blessed One, they were filled with joy, paid homage with fivefold prostration, and stood aside. Then the Teacher, seeing the spiritual maturity of this couple, established them in the refuges and precepts, saying, “Whenever suffering arises, the Tathāgata should be recollected.” Indeed.

1.

1.

Yaṃkiñci bhayamuppannaṃ, rājacorādisambhavaṃ;

Tadā sareyya sambuddhaṃ, nicchantota dupaddavaṃ.

Whatever fear arises, born of kings, thieves, or the like, then one should recollect the Perfectly Awakened One, wishing to be free from that calamity.

2.

2.

Yaṃ ve upaddavaṃ hoti, yakkhapetā disambhavaṃ;

Tadā sareyya sambuddhaṃ, nicchanto tadupaddavaṃ.

Whatever calamity arises, born of yakkhas, petas, and so forth, then one should recollect the Perfectly Awakened One, wishing to be free from that calamity.

3.

3.

Sīhabyagghataracchādi, puṇḍarīkādisambhavaṃ;

Tadā sareyya sambuddhaṃ, nicchanto tadupaddavaṃ.

Whatever danger arises from lions, tigers, hyenas, and other such creatures, then one should recollect the Perfectly Awakened One, wishing to be free from that calamity.

4.

4.

Yamātapaggi vātādi, udakāsanisambhavaṃ;

Tadā sareyya sambuddhaṃ, nicchanto tadupaddavaṃ.

Whatever danger arises from heat, fire, wind, and so forth, or from water and lightning, then one should recollect the Perfectly Awakened One, wishing to be free from that calamity.

5.

5.

Pajjarādīhi rogehi, visamo tuhisambhavaṃ;

Tadā sareyya sambuddhaṃ, nicchanto tadupaddavaṃ.

Whatever danger arises from fevers and other diseases, or a severe affliction born of cold, then one should recollect the Perfectly Awakened One, wishing to be free from that calamity.

6.

6.

Maccunā ce yadā yuddhaṃ, kare tenāpi jantunā;

Saritabbo tadā buddho, patthentenattano jayanti.

And when a being must fight a battle with Death, then too the Buddha should be recollected by one who longs for one's own victory.

Tato te bhagavato vacanaṃ paṭinanditvā vanditvā agamaṃsu. Atha jeṭṭhako āgate te disvā yathānurūpaṃ sakkāra makāsi. Tassā sāmiko katipāhaṃ tattha vasitvā bhariyaṃ jeṭṭhakassa paṭipādetvā mama gāme kiccaṃ atthīti vatvā pakkāmi. Athassā bhātā bhariyaṃ pakkositvā āha bhadde imissā sabbaṃ kattabbaṃ karohīti. Sā tato paṭṭhāya tassā udakanna pānā dinā veyyāvaccaṃ kurumānā etissā hatthapādagīvūpagesu ābharaṇesu lobhaṃ uppādetvā taṃ vūpasametuṃ asakkontī āhārū pacchedaṃ katvā gilānāviya mañcakaṃ upagūhitvā nipati. Atha so gehaṃ gantvā taṃ tathā sayitaṃ [M 0.0027] disvā mañcake nisinno kiṃ bhadde aphāsukanti pucchi, sā tuṇhī hutvā katipayavāre pucchitā na sakkā kathetunti āha. Puna sāmikena gāḷhaṃ nibandhite sā cintesi, ujukaṃ mayā tassā piḷandhanaṃ patthemīti vutte na ppatirupaṃ, tassā pañcamadhura maṃsaṃ patthemīti vutte taṃ māressati, tadā piḷandhanāni mayhamevāti, tato sāmi tava kaṇiṭṭhikāya pañcamadhuramaṃsaṃ patthemi, alabhamānāya me jīvitaṃ natthīti āha, taṃ sutvā so anekapariyāyena manussamāraṇaṃ nāma bhāriyanti vatvā nivārentopi nivāretuṃ nāsakkhi, atha tāya paṭibaddhacitto kāmamucchito mohamūḷho hutvā sādhu labhissasīti tassā vacanaṃ sampaṭicchi. Tathāhi.

Then, having rejoiced in the Blessed One’s words and paid homage to him, they departed. Then, when they arrived, the elder brother, seeing them, showed appropriate hospitality. After staying there for a few days, her husband, having entrusted his wife to the elder brother, said, “I have business in my village,” and left. Then her brother summoned his wife and said, “Dear, you must do everything for her.” From then on, while providing her with water, food, and drinks, and attending to her needs, she developed greed for the ornaments on her hands, feet, and neck. Unable to subdue it, she cut off her food and, as if sick, lay down on a bed. Then he returned home, and seeing her lying like that, sat on the bed and asked, “Dear, what ails you?” She remained silent. Though asked several times, she said she could not speak. Again, when her husband pressed her earnestly, she thought, “If I say directly that I want her ornaments, it would not be fitting. If I say I want her five delicacies of flesh, he will kill her. Then the ornaments will be mine.” Then she said, “Husband, I desire the five delicacies of flesh from your younger sister. If I do not get it, I will die.” Hearing this, he tried in many ways to dissuade her, saying, “Killing a human being is a terrible thing!” but he could not restrain her. Then, his mind bound by her, infatuated with desire and deluded by ignorance, he agreed to her words, saying, “Very well, you shall have it.” Indeed.

7.

7.

Hāyanti idhalokatthā, hāyanti pāralokikā;

Hāyanti mahatā atthā, ye itthīnaṃ vasaṅgatā.

Benefits in this world decline, benefits in the next world decline; great aims decline for those who have come under the sway of women.

8.

8.

Esā mātā pitā eso, bhaginī bhātaro ime;

Garutabbe na jānanti, ye itthīnaṃ vasaṅgatā.

This is mother, this is father, these are sisters and brothers; those who have come under the sway of women do not know who is worthy of respect.

9.

9.

Kāraṇākāraṇantetaṃ, kattabbaṃvā na vā idaṃ;

Kāmandhattā na jānanti, ye itthīnaṃ vasaṅgatā.

This is a reason, this is not a reason; whether this should be done or not; blinded by desire, they do not know, those who have come under the sway of women.

10.

10.

Pāṇaṃ vā atipātenti, honti vā pāradārikā;

Bhāsanti alikaṃ vācaṃ, ye itthīnaṃ vasaṅgatā.

They destroy life, or they become adulterers; they speak false speech, those who have come under the sway of women.

11.

11.

Sandhicchedādikaṃ theyyaṃ, majjapānaṃca pesunaṃ;

Karonti sāhasaṃ sabbaṃ, ye itthīnaṃ vasaṅgatā.

They commit theft such as housebreaking, drink intoxicants, and slander, and every kind of violent act, those who have come under the sway of women.

12.

12.

Aho acchariyaṃ loke, sarantānaṃ bhayā vahaṃ;

Bhariyāya vasaṃ gantvā, sodariṃhantumicchatīti.

Oh, how astonishing in the world, bringing fear to those who are mindful! Having come under the sway of a wife, he wishes to kill his own sister.

Atha so sāhasiko puriso bhagini eva māha, ehi amma amhākaṃ mātāpitunnaṃ iṇaṃ sādhessāma, appevanāma no disvā iṇāyikā iṇaṃ dassantīti, taṃ sutvā tāya sampaṭicchite [M 0.0028] sukhayānake nisīdāpetvā iṇāyikānaṃ gāmaṃ gacchanto viya mahāaṭaviṃpatvā yānaṃ maggā okkamma ṭhapetvā viravantimeva naṃ hatthe gahetvā chindissāmīti cintetvā kese gahetvā bhūmiyaṃ pātesi, tasmiṃ khaṇe tassā kammajavātā caliṃsu. Sā bhātulajjāya sāmi kammajavātā me caliṃsu, yā vā haṃ vijāyāmi, tāva upadhārehīti vadantīpi apanetuṃ asakkontī puttaṃ vijāyi, atha so taṃ samīpe vaṭarukkhamūle māressāmīti cikure gahetvā ākaḍḍhi, tasmiṃ kāle sā sāmi tava bhāgineyyassa mukhaṃ oloketvā tassa sinehenāpi maṃ na mārehīti vadantī yāci, atha so kakkhaḷo tassā taṃ kāruṇikavacanaṃ asuṇanto viya māretuṃ ussahateva, tato sā kumārikā attano asaraṇā cintesi, mama saddenā gantvā yo koci mama bhātu anayaṃ kareyya, taṃ na ppatirūpanti bhātusinehena nissaddā attanā gatitasaraṇaṃ āvajjamānā nipajji, athassā bhā ta ri mettānubhāvenaca anussaritasaraṇānubhāvenaca tasmiṃ nigrodhe adhivatthā devatā evarūpo mātugāmo ettha māritā abhavissā, addhāhaṃ devasamāgamaṃ pavisituṃ na labhissāmīti cintetvā etissā sā mi ko viya taṃ ta jje tvā palāpetvā tvaṃ mā bhāyīti samassāsetvā yā na ke sa pu ttaṃ kumāriṃ nisīdāpetvā taṃ divasameva sāvatthimāgamma antonagare sā lā ya naṃ nipajjāpetvā antaradhāyi. Tathāhi.

Then that reckless man said to his sister, “Come, dear, let us settle our parents’ debt. Perhaps seeing us, the creditors will give us the money.” Hearing this, she agreed. He made her sit in a comfortable carriage and, pretending to go to the creditors’ village, entered a great forest, turned the carriage off the road, and stopped it. Thinking, “I will seize her by the hand and kill her while she is crying out,” he seized her by the hair and threw her to the ground. At that moment, her labor pains began. Out of shame before her brother, she said, “Sir, my labor pains have begun. Wait until I give birth.” But she could not hold back and gave birth to a son. Then he thought, “I will kill her right here at the foot of this banyan tree,” and dragged her by the hair. At that time, she pleaded, “Sir, look at your nephew’s face and do not kill me out of love for him.” But the cruel man, as if not hearing her compassionate words, was determined to kill her. Then the young woman, being without refuge, thought, “If anyone were to come at the sound of my cries and harm my brother, that would not be proper.” Out of love for her brother, she remained silent, reflecting on the refuge she had taken, and lay down.
