Then, through the power of her loving-kindness and the power of recollecting the refuge, the deity dwelling in that banyan tree thought, “If such a woman were killed here, surely I will not be able to enter the divine assembly.” So, appearing like her husband, the deity terrified the brother and made him flee. Comforting her, saying, “Do not fear,” the deity made the young woman and her son sit down in the carriage and, bringing them to Sāvatthī that very day, had her lie down in a hall within the city, and then disappeared. Indeed.

13.

13.

Sabbasampattidātāraṃ, sabbalokekanāyakaṃ;

Manasāpi yo vibhāveti, taṃ ve pālenti devatā.

The deities protect one who even mentally contemplates the giver of all attainments, the unique leader of all the world.

14.

14.

Muhuttampica yo mettaṃ, bhāveti yadi sādhukaṃ;

Taṃ ve pālenti devāpi, tosayanti upāyanāti.

If one cultivates loving-kindness skillfully, even for just a moment, even the deities protect that person and please them with gifts.

Tato [M 0.0029] tassā pana sāmiko nagarā nikkhamma gacchanto attano bhariyaṃ disvā tvaṃ kadā āgatā, kenānītāsīti pucchi. Sā devatāya ānītabhāvaṃ ajānantī kiṃ tvaṃ bhaṇasi, nanu tayā ānītāmhīti, sopi kiṃbhoti bhaṇasi, tava bhātugāme diṭṭhakālato ppatuti ajja cattāro māsā jātā, ettakaṃ kālaṃ tvaṃ na diṭṭhapubbā, kathaṃ tvaṃ mayā saddhiṃāgatāti pucchi. Sā taṃ sutvā tenahi māññassa imaṃ rahassaṃ kathehi sāmīti vatvā bhā ta rā attano kataṃ sabbaṃ vitthārena kathesi. Taṃ sutvā tassa sāmiko saṃviggo bhayappatto hutvā taṃ attano gehaṃ pāpesi, tato katipāhaṃ tāya vissamite te ubhopi satthāraṃ nimantetvā mahādānaṃ datvā vanditvā ekamante nisīdiṃsu, atha sā bhagavato saraṇasīlānubhāvena attano jīvitapaṭilābhaṃ pakāsetvā attano puttaṃ bhagavantaṃ vandāpetvā saraṇotināmamakaṃsu, satthā te saṃ ajjhāsayaṃ ñatvā tadanurūpaṃ dhammaṃ desesi, desanāvasāne ubhopi sotāpannā ahesuṃ, athassā putto saraṇakumāro vīsatime vasse buddhasāsane pabbajitvā vipassanaṃ vaḍḍhetvā arahattaṃ patto saraṇattheronāma paññāyīti.

Then her husband, going forth from the city and seeing his wife, asked, “When did you come? Who brought you here?” Not knowing she had been brought by the deity, she replied, “What are you saying? Surely you brought me here?” He, in turn, said, “What is this you are saying? From the time I saw you in your brother’s village until today, four months have passed. For such a long time you were not seen. How did you come here with me?” Hearing this, she said, “Then, husband, do not tell this secret to anyone,” and she related in detail everything her brother had done to her. Upon hearing this, her husband was deeply shaken and terrified. He brought her to their home. Then, after a few days, when she had rested, they both invited the Teacher and gave a great offering. After paying homage, they sat to one side. Then she revealed how she had regained her life through the power of the Blessed One's refuge and precepts. She had her son pay homage to the Blessed One, and they gave him the name Saraṇa. The Teacher, knowing their disposition, taught them the Dhamma accordingly. At the end of the teaching, both attained stream-entry. Later, their son, the youth Saraṇa, in his twentieth year, went forth in the Buddha’s dispensation. Developing insight, he attained arahantship and became known as the Elder Saraṇa.

15.

15.

Khaṇamapi manasevaṃ devadevaṃ sarantā,

Paramatarapatiṭṭhaṃ pāpuṇantīti mantvā;

Bhavagati guṇarāsiṃ jānamānā janā bho;

Bhajatha saraṇasīlaṃ sabbathā sabbakālanti.

O people, thinking that by recollecting the God of gods in mind even for a moment, one attains the supreme foundation, and knowing the mass of virtues in the course of existence, cultivate refuge and morality in every way, at all times.

Saraṇattherassa vatthuṃ chaṭṭhamaṃ.

The Story of the Elder Saraṇa, the sixth.

7. Vessāmittāya vatthumhi ayamānupubbīkathā

7. This is the introductory discourse to the story of Vessāmittā.

Jambudīpe [M 0.0030] kira kosambinagare kosambirañño vessāmittānāma aggamahesī ahosi. Tadā bhagavā kosambiyaṃ paṭivasati mahatā bhikkhusaṅghena saddhiṃ cārikaṃ caramāno. Tasmiṃ samaye sā raññā saddhiṃ vihāraṃ gantvā anopamāya buddhalīḷāya madhurena sarena desentassa bhagavato dhammaṃ sutvā pasannā saraṇesu patiṭṭhāya buddhamāmikā hutvā viharati. Athā parabhāge tassa rañño rajjatthāya paccantarājā yuddhasajjo rajjaṃ vā detu, yuddhaṃvāti paṇṇaṃ pahiṇi. Taṃ sutvā rājā mahatiyā senāya parivuto yuddhabhūmiṃ gacchanto mahesiyā saddhiṃ gantvā khandhāvāraṃ nivāsetvā tassā evamāha. Bhadde saṅgāmasīse jayaparājayo nāma na sakkā viññātuṃ. Sace me parājayo abhavissa, puretarameva rattapatākaṃ ussāpessāmi, tena abhiññāṇena tvaṃ kosambimeva gacchāhīti anusāsitvā saṅgāma maṇḍalaṃ gantvā mahāraṇaṃ karonto attano parājayabhāvaṃ ñatvā mātugāmaṃ saritvā rattaddhajaṃ ussāpetvā yujjhanto raṇe pati. Atha sā rattapatākaṃ disvā parājito nūna me sāmikoti bhayena palāyitumārabhi. Atha taṃ corarañño manussā disvā nūnāyaṃ rañño aggamahesīti ñatvā attano rājānaṃ dassesuṃ, rājā taṃ disvā paṭibaddhacitto mametaṃ abhisekaṃ karothāti amacce āṇāpesi. Amaccā taṃ abhisekatthāya yāciṃsu, sā na me bhaṇe abhisekenatthoti na icchi. Amaccā tamatthaṃ rañño ārocesuṃ. Rājā naṃ pakkosāpetvā kasmā na icchasīti pucchi. Sā evamāha.

In Jambudīpa, it is said that in the city of Kosambī, the king of Kosambī had a chief queen named Vessāmittā. At that time, the Blessed One was dwelling in Kosambī, wandering with a great assembly of monks. During that time, she went to the monastery with the king and, upon hearing the Dhamma taught by the Blessed One with his incomparable Buddha-like grace and sweet voice, she became devoted, established herself in the refuges, and lived as a female lay follower of the Buddha. Later, a neighboring king, seeking the kingdom, prepared for war and sent a message to that king demanding, “Either give up the kingdom or give battle.” Hearing this, the king, surrounded by a large army, set out for the battlefield. Taking the queen with him, he had a camp established and said to her: “Dear one, in the forefront of battle, victory or defeat cannot be known. If I am to be defeated, I will raise a red flag beforehand. By that sign, you must return to Kosambī.” Having instructed her thus, he entered the battlefield. Engaging in fierce combat and realizing his own defeat, he remembered the woman, raised the red flag, and fell in battle while fighting. When she saw the red flag, she thought, “Surely my lord has been defeated,” and began to flee in fear. Then the men of a robber king saw her and, recognizing her—“Surely this is the king’s chief queen”—they presented her to their own king. Upon seeing her, the king, his heart captivated, ordered his ministers, “Consecrate this one for me.” The ministers requested her for the consecration, but she, not wanting it, said, “Sirs, I have no need for a consecration.” The ministers reported this matter to the king. The king had her summoned and asked, “Why do you not want it?” She replied thus:

1.

1.

Suṇohi sādhukaṃ deva, bhāsamānāya me vaco;

Bhattā mayhaṃ mato ajja, sabbasampattidāyako.

Listen well, O king, to my words as I speak; my husband, the giver of all prosperity, is dead today.

2.

2.

Katvāna [M 0.0031] sobhisekaṃ maṃ, attano hadayaṃ viya;

Pāleti taṃ sarantassā, sokaggi dahate manaṃ.

Having performed my splendid coronation, he protected me as if I were his own heart; for me, remembering him, the fire of sorrow burns my mind.

3.

3.

Mahārāja sacaññassa, assa maggamahesikā;

Tamhā dukkhā na muccāmi, tenāhaṃ taṃ na patthaye.

Great king, if I were to become the chief queen of another, I would not be freed from this suffering; therefore, I do not desire it.

4.

4.

Sokagginā padittāhaṃ, soke sokaṃ kathaṃ khipe;

Jalantaggimhī ko nāma, palālaṃ pakkhipe budho.

I am ablaze with the fire of sorrow; how can I cast sorrow upon sorrow? What wise person, indeed, would throw straw into a blazing fire?

5.

5.

Piyavippayogadukkhaṃ, taṃ cintayantī punappunaṃ;

Tamhā dukkhā na muccāmi, tasmāhaṃ taṃ na patthayeti.

Thinking again and again of the suffering of separation from my beloved, I am not freed from that suffering; therefore, I do not desire this.

Taṃ sutvā rājā kodhenābhibhūto sace nābhisiñcissasi, aggimhi taṃ pakkhipissāmīti vatvā mahantaṃ dārucitakaṃ kārāpetvā aggiṃ datvā ekapajjote jāte etta pavisāti āha. Atha sā yācantī rājānaṃ āha.

Hearing that, the king, overcome by anger, said, “If you will not be consecrated, I will throw you into the fire.” Having spoken thus, he had a large pyre of wood prepared, set it alight, and when a single flame arose, he said, “Enter this.” Then she, pleading, spoke to the king.

6.

6.

Pāpo nippāpinaṃ rāja, pātanaṃ khalu pāvake;

Hoti pāpaphalaṃ tassa, paccatteca paratthaca.

O king, for a wicked person to cast an innocent one into the fire truly has an evil fruit for him, both in this life and in the next.

7.

7.

Purātanehi bhūpāla, samaṇabrahmaṇesuca;

Mātāpitusu bālesu, rogenā turaitthisu;

Nappasattho vadho deva, tasmāhaṃ na vadhārahāti.

O king, by the ancients, the killing of ascetics and brahmins, of mothers and fathers, of children, and of women afflicted by disease was never praised; therefore, O king, I am not one who deserves to be killed.

Taṃ sutvāpi rājā asaddahanto manusse āṇāpesi. Etāya hatthapāde gahetvā aggimhi pakkhipathāti te tathā kariṃsu, atha sā aggimhi pakkhipamānā natthettha me koci paṭisaraṇoti saraṇameva saraṇaṃ karomīti cintetvā “buddhaṃ saraṇaṃ gacchāmi, dhammaṃ saraṇaṃ gacchāmi, saṅghaṃ saraṇaṃ gacchāmīti” vadantī manasāca anussarantī aggimhi pati, tathāvidhopi aggi tassā sarīre lomakūpamattampi uṇhākāraṃ kātuṃ nāsakkhi. Padumagabbhaṃ paviṭṭhā viya sītibhūtasarīrā ahosi. Rājā taṃ acchariyaṃ [M 0.0032] disvā saṃviggo lomahaṭṭhajāto vegena taṃ upasaṅkamitvā ubhohi hatthehi paggayha ure nipajjāpetvā rājāsane nisīdāpetvā añjaliṃ gaggayha ṭhito kasmā te taṃ aggi sarīraṃ mā paridahīti pucchi. Sā taṃ kāraṇaṃ kathentī eva māha.

Hearing this, the king, still disbelieving, commanded his men: “Seize her by the hands and feet and throw her into the fire!” They did so. As she was being thrown into the fire, she thought, “There is no other refuge for me here. I will make the Refuges themselves my refuge,” and repeating, “I go to the Buddha for refuge, I go to the Dhamma for refuge, I go to the Sangha for refuge,” while also recollecting it in her mind, she fell into the fire. Even such a fire was not able to produce the slightest sensation of heat on her body, not even to the extent of a single pore. Her body became cool, as if she had entered the heart of a lotus. Seeing this marvel, the king, agitated and with his hair standing on end, quickly approached her, lifted her with both hands, placed her upon his breast, seated her on the royal throne, and, standing with his hands folded in reverence, asked, “Why did the fire not burn your body?” She explained the reason, saying thus:

8.

8.

Mātā pitāca ñātīca, parivārāca sohadā;

Manto sadhādayocāpi, mahesakkhāca devatā.

Mother and father, relatives, attendants, and friends; mantras, those with faith and so forth, and also powerful deities—

9.

9.

Eteca ññeca bhūpāla, sattānaṃ bhaya māgate;

Rakkhituṃ neva sakkonti, hitvāna saraṇattayaṃ.

These and others, O king, when fear comes upon beings, are not able to offer protection—apart from the Three Refuges.

10.

10.

Agāhaṃ buddhaṃ saraṇaṃ, buddho me saraṇaṃ iti;

Tena tejena maṃ rāja, jalanto aggi no dahi.

I went to the Buddha for refuge, thinking, ‘The Buddha is my refuge’; by that power, O king, the burning fire did not burn me.

11.

11.

Agāhaṃ dhammaṃ saraṇaṃ, dhammo me saraṇaṃ iti;

Tena tejena maṃ rāja, jalanto aggi no dahi.

I went to the Dhamma for refuge, thinking, ‘The Dhamma is my refuge’; by that power, O king, the burning fire did not burn me.

12.

12.

Agāhaṃ saṅghaṃ saraṇaṃ, saṅgho me saraṇaṃ iti;

Tena tejena maṃ rāja, jalanto aggi no dahi.

I went to the Sangha for refuge, thinking, ‘The Sangha is my refuge’; by that power, O king, the burning fire did not burn me.

13.

13.

Evaṃ mahānubhāvantaṃ, paccakkhaṃ ehipassikaṃ;

Nāno paddava viddhaṃsiṃ, nānāsampattidāyakaṃ.

Thus it is greatly powerful, directly visible, inviting one to come and see; the destroyer of various misfortunes, the giver of various attainments.

14.

14.

Saraṇattayañhi yo satto, na samādāya gaṇhati;

Idhavā paratthavā loke, so sukhaṃ nānubhossati.

For whatever being does not properly undertake the Three Refuges, that one will not experience happiness, either in this world or in the next.

15.

15.

Saraṇattayañhi yo satto, susamādāya gaṇhati;

Idhavāparatthavā loke, so sukhā na vihāyati.

But whatever being firmly undertakes the Three Refuges, that one will not be separated from happiness, either in this world or in the next.

16.

16.

Tasmā tuvampi bhūpāla, gaṇhāhi saraṇattayaṃ;

Taṃ te bhavati sabbattha, tāṇaṃ leṇaṃ parāyaṇanti.

Therefore, O king, you too, take the three refuges; that will be for you everywhere a protection, a shelter, and a supreme refuge.

Taṃ sutvā rājā ativiya pasannamānaso taṃ khamāpetvā mahantaṃ sakkārasammānaṃ katvā ajjappaṭṭhāya tvaṃ mama mātāti taṃ mātuṭṭhāne ṭhapetvā saraṇa magamāsi. Tasmiṃ sannipatitvā ṭhitamahājanā taṃ pāṭihāriyaṃ disvā saraṇesuca sīlesuca patiṭṭhāya dānādīni puññakammāni katvā yathākammaṃ gatāti.

Hearing this, the king, with a heart exceedingly pleased, asked her forgiveness and, having bestowed great honor and respect upon her, from that day forward placed her in the position of his mother, saying, 'You are my mother,' and took refuge. The great multitude gathered there, witnessing this marvel, became established in the refuges and precepts, performed meritorious deeds such as giving, and departed according to their deeds.

17.

17.

Iti [M 0.0033] saraṇavaraṃ sā kevalaṃ uggahetvā,

Jalitadahanamajjhe sītibhāvaṃ alattha;

Paramasaraṇasīlaṃ pālayantā kathaṃ vo,

Na labhatha bhavabhogaṃ nibbutiñcāpi anteti.

Thus, having taken up only that excellent refuge, she attained coolness amidst the blazing fire. You who protect the supreme refuge and virtue, how could you not obtain both the enjoyments of existence and, in the end, final extinguishing?

Vessāmittāpa vatthuṃ sattamaṃ.

The seventh story of Vessāmittā.

8. Mahāmandhātuvatthumhi ayamānupubbīkathā

8. This is the sequential narrative in the Story of Mahāmandhātu.

Ito kira ekanavutikappamatthake vipassīnāma sammāsambuddho loke uppajjitvā pavattavaradhammacakko sadevakehi lokehi pūjiyamāno bandhumatīnagare paṭivasati. Tadā so mandhātā tasmiṃ nagare tunnakāro hutvā nibbatti. Tunnakārakammena jīvikaṃ kappento viharati. Tadā sakalanagaravāsino buddhapamukhaṃ bhikkhusaṅghaṃ nimantetvā mahādāna madaṃsu. Atha so evaṃ cintesi, sabbepime nagaravāsino dānaṃ dadanti. Ahamekova seso duggatattā, yajjāhamajja bījaṃ na ropemi, imamhā dukkhā na parimuccissāmīti. So vegena tunnakārakammaṃ pariyesitvā kiñcimūlaṃ labhitvā tena ekassāpi dānaṃ dātuṃ okāsa maladdhā āpaṇaṃ gantvā rājamāsake gahetvā caṅkoṭakaṃ pūretvā ādāya buddhapamukhassa bhikkhusaṅghassa bhattaggaṃ gantvā ṭhito evaṃ cintesi, natthi dāni okāsaṃ ekassāpi bhikkhuno patte okirituṃ, addhāhaṃ ime ākāse vikirissāmīti appevanāma patamānānaṃ ekassāpi bhikkhuno patte ekampi pateyya, taṃ me bhavissati dīgharattaṃ hibhāya sukhāyāti pasannamānaso uddhaṃ khipi, ta to patamānā te parivārikadevatānañca bhagavato ānubhāvenaca bahi apatitvā bhagavantamādiṃ katvā sabbesaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ [M 0.0034] patteyeva patiṃsu. Atha so taṃ acchariyaṃ disvā pasannamānaso sirasi añjaliṃ paggayha ṭhito evaṃ patthanamakāsi.

It is said that ninety-one eons ago, the Perfectly Enlightened One named Vipassī arose in the world. Having set in motion the supreme Wheel of the Dhamma, he was revered by the world with its devas and resided in the city of Bandhumatī. At that time, this Mandhātā was born there as a tailor. He made his living by tailoring. Then, all the city’s inhabitants invited the Sangha of bhikkhus headed by the Buddha and gave a great offering of alms. Then he thought thus: 'All these city-dwellers are giving alms. Only I remain, on account of my poverty. If I do not plant a seed today, I shall not be released from this suffering.' Quickly seeking tailoring work, he obtained some capital, but not finding an opportunity to give an offering to even a single person, he went to the market. Taking royal māsaka coins, he filled a small basket and, carrying it, went to the dining hall of the Sangha of bhikkhus headed by the Buddha. Standing there, he thought thus: 'Now there is no opportunity to sprinkle these into the bowl of even a single bhikkhu. Surely I will scatter these into the air—perhaps, as they fall, even one may land in a bhikkhu’s bowl. That will be for my long-term welfare and happiness.' With a mind full of faith, he threw them upward. Then, as they were falling, by the power of the attendant devas and the majesty of the Blessed One, they did not fall outside but fell only into the bowls of all the bhikkhus, beginning with the Blessed One. Seeing that marvel, with a mind full of faith, he raised his hands in reverence to his head, and standing there, he made this aspiration:

1.

1.

Iminā me adhikārena, pasādena yatissare;

Kāmabhogīnahaṃ aggo, bhaveyyaṃ jātijātiyaṃ.

By this my meritorious deed, and by my faith in the Lord of ascetics, may I, in birth after birth, be foremost among those who enjoy sensual pleasures.

2.

2.

Paharitvā yadā pāṇiṃ, olokemi nabhotalaṃ;

Sattaratanasampannaṃ, vassaṃ vassatu sabbadāti.

When I clap my hand and gaze at the sky, may a rain endowed with the seven treasures always fall.

So tato paṭṭhāya devamanussesu saṃsaranto mahantaṃ dibbasampattiṃ anubhavitvā imasmiṃ bhaddakappe ādimhi mahāsammato nāma rājā ahosi. Tassa putto rojo nāma. Tassa putto vararojo nāma, tassa putto kalyāṇo nāma, tassa putto varakalyāṇo nāma. Varakalyāṇassa putto uposatho nāma. Uposathassa putto mandhātā nāma hutvā nibbatti. So sattahi ratanehi catūhi ca iddhīhi samannāgato cakkavattirajjaṃ kāresi, tassa vāmahatthaṃ sammiñjitvā dakkhiṇahatthena appoṭṭhite ākāsato dibbameghā viya jaṇṇuppamāṇaṃ sattaratanavassaṃ vassati. Evarūpo acchariyo ahosi. So caturāsītivassasahassāni kumārakīḷaṃ kīḷi. Caturāsītivassasahassāni oparajjaṃ kāresi. Caturāsītivassasahassāni cakkavattirajjaṃ kāresi, āyu panassa asaṅkhyeyyaṃ ahosi. So ekadivasaṃ kāmataṇhaṃ pūretuṃ asakkonto ukkaṇṭhitākāraṃ dassesi, amaccā deva kinnakho ukkaṇṭhā sīti pucchiṃsu. So bhaṇe mayhaṃ puññakamme olokiyamāne imaṃ rajjaṃ nappahoti, katarannu kho ṭhānaṃ ramaṇīyanti. Devaloko mahārājāti. So cakkaratanaṃ abbhukkiritvā parisāya saddhiṃ cātumahārājikadevalokaṃ agamāsi, athassa cattāro mahārājāno dibbamālagandha- hatthā [M 0.0035] devagaṇaparivutā paccuggamanaṃ katvā taṃ ādāya cātumahārājikadevalokaṃ gantvā rajjaṃ adaṃsu, tassa parisāya parivutassa tasmiṃ rajjaṃ karontassa dīgho addhā vītivatto. So tatthapi taṇhaṃ pūretu masakkonto ukkaṇṭhitā kāraṃ dassesi. Tato cattāro mahārājāno kinnukho mahārāja ukkaṇṭhitoti pucchiṃsu. Imamhā devalokā katarannu kho ṭhānaṃ ramaṇīyanti, deva paresaṃ upaṭṭhākamanussasadisā mayaṃ. Tāvatiṃsadevaloko ito sataguṇena ramaṇīyoti. Mandhātā cakkaratanaṃ abbhukkiritvā attano parisaparivuto tāvatiṃsābhimukho pāyāsi, athassa sakko devarājā dibbamālagandhahattho devagaṇaparivuto paccuggamanaṃ katvā taṃ hatthe gahetvā ito ehi mahārājāti āha. Tato rañño devagaṇaparivutassa gamanakāle pariṇāyakaratanaṃ cakkaratanaṃ ādāya saddhiṃparisāya manussapathaṃ otaritvā attano gharaṃ pāvisi. Sakko mandhātuṃ sakkabhavanaṃ netvā devatā dve koṭṭhāse katvā attano rajjaṃ majjhe bhinditvā adāsi. Tato paṭṭhāya dvepi rājano rajjaṃ kāresuṃ. Evaṃ kāle gacchante sakko saṭṭhisatasahassādhikāni tissoca vassakoṭiyo āyuṃ khepetvā cavi. Añño sakko nibbatti, sopi tatheva devarajjaṃ kāretvā āyukkhayena cavi, etenu pāyena chattiṃsa sakkā caviṃsu, mandhātā pana manussaparihārena devarajjaṃ kāresiyeva, tassevaṃ kāle gacchante bhīyyoso mattāya kāmataṇhā uppajji, so kimme upaḍḍharajjena. Sakkaṃ māretvā ekarajjaṃ karissāmīti cintesi. Sakkaṃ pana māretuṃ na sakkā, kāmataṇhā panesā vipattimūlā. Tathāhi.

From that time onward, wandering among devas and humans, he experienced great divine prosperity. In this auspicious eon, at the beginning, he became a king named Mahāsammata. His son was named Roja. His son was named Vararoja. His son was named Kalyāṇa. His son was named Varakalyāṇa. His son was named Uposatha. Uposatha’s son was born as Mandhātā. Endowed with the seven treasures and the four supernormal powers, he ruled as a wheel-turning monarch. When he clenched his left hand and clapped with his right, a rain of the seven treasures, knee-deep like a divine cloud, fell from the sky. Such was his marvel. For eighty-four thousand years he enjoyed princely sport. For eighty-four thousand years he ruled as crown prince. For eighty-four thousand years he reigned as a wheel-turning monarch. His lifespan, however, was incalculable. One day, unable to satisfy his craving for sensual pleasures, he showed signs of discontent. His ministers asked, 'Your Majesty, what is the cause of your discontent?' He replied, 'Considering my meritorious deeds, this kingdom is not enough. What place is more delightful?' 'The world of the devas, great king.' He then directed his wheel-treasure upwards and, with his retinue, went to the deva world of the Four Great Kings. The Four Great Kings, holding divine garlands and perfumes and surrounded by a host of devas, came out to welcome him. Taking him with them, they went to their deva world and gave him their kingdom. Surrounded by his retinue, he ruled there for a long time. Yet even there, unable to satisfy his craving, he showed signs of discontent. Then the Four Great Kings asked, 'Great king, what is the cause of your discontent?' 'What place is more delightful than this deva world?' 'Your Majesty, we are like attendants to others. The Tāvatiṃsa deva world is a hundred times more delightful than this.' Mandhātā directed his wheel-treasure upwards and, surrounded by his retinue, set out for Tāvatiṃsa. Then Sakka, king of the devas, holding divine garlands and perfumes and surrounded by a host of devas, came out to welcome him. Taking him by the hand, he said, 'Come, great king!' Then, as the king was departing with the host of devas, his counselor-treasure, together with the retinue, descended to the human world and entered his own palace. Sakka led Mandhātā to his own abode, divided the devas into two groups, and splitting his kingdom in half, gave it to him. From then on, both kings ruled. As time passed, Sakka, having exhausted his lifespan of thirty-six million years, passed away. Another Sakka arose. He too ruled the deva kingdom in the same way and passed away at the end of his lifespan. In this manner, thirty-six Sakkas passed away. But Mandhātā, with his human form, continued to rule the deva kingdom. As time went on in this way, his craving for sensual pleasures arose to an even greater degree. He thought, 'What is this half-kingdom to me? I will kill Sakka and rule the entire kingdom alone.' But it is not possible to kill Sakka. This craving for sensual pleasures is indeed the root of ruin.

3.

3.

Varamatra sukhantyatra, atricchāvihato naro;

Idhavā paratthavā kiñci, na sātaṃ vindate sadā.

A person overcome by excessive craving never finds any satisfaction, whether in this world or the next.

4.

4.

Taṇhāya [M 0.0036] jāyate soko,

Taṇhāya jāyate bhayaṃ;

Taṇhāya vippamuttassa,

Natthi soko kuto bhayaṃ.

From craving, sorrow is born; from craving, fear is born. For one who is completely free from craving, there is no sorrow, so how could there be fear?

5.

5.

Taṇhādāso naro ettha, rājacorādisambhavaṃ;

Hattacchedādikaṃ dukkhaṃ, pāpuṇāti vihaññati.

Here, a person who is a slave to craving undergoes suffering, such as the cutting off of hands, which arises from kings, thieves, and others, and is tormented.

6.

6.

Yena lobhena jātena, sadā jīyanti pāṇino;

Khettaṃ vatthuṃ hiraññaṃca, gavāssaṃ dāsaporisaṃ;

Sabbatthāmena so lobho, pahātabbova viññunāti.

By that greed which arises, beings are always overcome in their desire for fields, property, gold, cattle, horses, slaves, and servants. That greed should be utterly abandoned by the wise.

Tato atricchāvihatassa tassa āyusaṅkhāro parihāyi, jarā sarīraṃ pahari, manussasarīrañhi nāma na devalokebhijjati, tatha so devalokā bhassitvā bandhumatīnagaruyyānaṃ pāvisi, uyyānapālo tassa āgatabhāvaṃ rājakulaṃ nivedesi. Rājā rājakulā āgantvā uyyāneyeva āsanaṃ paññāpesi. Tato mandhātā uyyāne paññattavarāsane nipanno anuṭṭhānaseyyaṃ kappesi. Tato amaccā deva tumhākaṃ parato kinnu kathesāmāti pucchiṃsu, mama parato tumhe imaṃ sāsanaṃ mahājanassa katheyyātha, mandhātumahārājā dvisahassadīpaparivāresu catūsu mahādīpesu cakkavattirajjaṃ kāretvā cātumahārājikesu rajjaṃ kāretvā chattiṃsasakkānaṃ āyuparimāṇena devaloke rajjaṃ kāretvā kālamakāsīti. So evaṃ vatvā kālaṃkatvā yathākammaṃ gatoti imamatthaṃ pakāsetuṃ bhagavā catumahāparisamajjhe imā gāthāyo āha.

Then, his life-force, afflicted by excessive craving, declined, and old age struck his body. For a human body does not endure in the deva realms. Thus, having fallen from the deva world, he entered the royal garden of Bandhumatī city. The park-keeper announced his arrival to the royal court. The king came from the palace and had a seat prepared for him in the garden itself. Then, Mandhātā, lying down on the excellent seat prepared in the garden, took to his bed from which he would not rise. Then the ministers asked, "Lord, what should we say after your passing?" He replied, "After my passing, you should tell this to the people: 'King Mandhātā, having exercised universal sovereignty over the four great continents with two thousand surrounding islands, having reigned in the realm of the Four Great Kings, and having reigned in the deva world for the lifespan of thirty-six Sakkas, has passed away.'" Having said this, he passed away and went according to his deeds. To reveal this matter, the Blessed One spoke these verses in the midst of the fourfold assembly:

7.

7.

Yāvatā candimasuriyā, pariharanti disā bhanti virocanā;

Sabbeva dāsā mandhātā, ye pāṇā pathavinissitā.

As far as the moon and sun revolve, illuminating the directions, shining brightly—all beings who dwell on earth are slaves to Mandhātā.

8.

8.

Na [M 0.0037] kahāpaṇavassena, titti kāmesu vijjati;

Appassadā dukhā kāmā, iti viññāya paṇḍito.

Not by a rain of coins is satisfaction found in sensual pleasures; sensual pleasures are of little enjoyment and are suffering—knowing this, the wise one.

9.

9.

Api dibbesu kāmesu, ratiṃso nādhigacchati;

Taṇhākkhayarato hoti, sammāsambuddhasāvakoti.

Even in divine sensual pleasures, he finds no delight; he delights in the destruction of craving, a disciple of the Perfectly Enlightened One.

Taṃ sutvā bahū sotāpattiphalādīni pāpuṇiṃsūti.

Hearing this, many attained the fruit of stream-entry and the other fruits.

10.

10.

Iti gatiniyatānaṃ bodhiyā uttamānaṃ;

Sakavasamupanetvā deti dukkhātitaṇhā;

Aniyatagatikānaṃ kā kathā mādisānaṃ;

Jahatha tamiti mantvā bho bhajavho tivatthuṃti.

Thus, for those whose destiny is fixed on supreme Awakening, craving, having brought them under its own power, gives suffering. What to say of those like me, whose destiny is unfixed? Thinking, "Abandon that!" O friends, resort to the Triple Gem!

Mahāmandhātuvatthuṃ aṭṭhamaṃ.

The Story of the Great Mandhātu, the Eighth.

9. Buddhavammavāṇijakavatthumhi ayamānupubbīkathā

9. Herein, the sequential account in the story of Buddhavamma the Merchant.

Jambudīpe kira pāṭaliputtanagare buddhavammo nāma vāṇijako ahosi vāṇijakakammena jīvamāno. So aparabhāge satthavāhehi saddhiṃ gāmanigamajanapada rājadhānīsu vāṇijjaṃ payojayamāno vicarati, tasmiṃ samaye bhagavānekabhikkhusahassaparivuto janapadacārikaṃ carati bahū devamanusse saṃsārakantārā uttārento. Tadā so bhagavantaṃ addasa dvattiṃsalakkhaṇānubyañjanapatimaṇḍitaṃ jalamānasuvaṇṇameruṃ viyavirocamānaṃ mahābhikkhusaṅghaparivutaṃ, disvā paramapītiyā phuṭasarīro añjaliṃ paggayha bhagavantaṃ upasaṅkamitvā vanditvā sāyaṇhe bhagavantaṃ bhattena nimantesi buddhasāsane aparicitabhāvena. Athassa bhagavā vikālabhojanā paṭiviratā tathāgatāti āha. Atha so bhagavantaṃ vanditvā kiṃ bhante bhagavantā vikāle bhuñjissantīti, athassa kathaṃ paṭicca bhagavā [M 0.0038] aṭṭhavidhaṃ pānaṃ tathāgatānaṃ vikāle bhuñjituṃ kappati. Seyyathidaṃ, ambapānaṃ jambupānaṃ cocapānaṃ mocapānaṃ phārusakapānaṃ madhupānaṃ muddikapānaṃ sālūkapānanti. Taṃ sutvā vāṇijo sahasakkārārasehi muddikapānaṃ katvā buddhapamukhassa bhikkhusaṅghassa adāsi, sabhikkhusaṅgho satthā paribhuttapānīyaraso tassa dhammaṃ desetvā janapadacārikaṃ pakkāmi. Sopi pasannamānaso hutvā nivatto saddhiṃ vāṇijakehi tesu tesu janapadesu vāṇijjaṃ payojento mahāvattanīyaṃnāma kantāraṃ pāpuṇi, tatta tesaṃ sabbesuyeva sakaṭesu pānīyaṃ parikkhayamagamāsi. Tattha sabbamanussānaṃca balīvaddānaṃca pānīyaṃ nāhosi. Atha so vāṇijo pipāsābhibhūto tesu tesu sakaṭesu pānīyaṃ pariyesanto vicarati. Athekasmiṃ sakaṭe manussā taṃ disvā kāruññena etthāgaccha, imasmiṃ koḷambe thokaṃ pānīyaṃ atthi pivāti vadiṃsu. Ta to so gantvā pānīyaṃ pivi. Tassa taṃ rasaṃ muddikapānarasasadisaṃ ahosi, pivantova so evaṃ cintesi. Sammāsambuddhassa tadā me dinnamuddikapānassa nissando ajja sampatto bhavissatīti accherabbhutacitto somanassajāto gantvā sayameva cāṭiyā pidhānaṃ vivari. Sakalāpi sā cāṭi muddikapānena paripuṇṇā ahosi. Tato so ra sava ntaṃ ojavantaṃ aparikkhayaṃ dibbapānasadisaṃ pānīyaṃ disvā paramāya pītiyā phuṭasarīro ugghosesi sabbe pānīyaṃ pivantūti. Taṃ sutvā sabbe sannipatitvā pānīyaṃ disvā abbhutacittā jātā. Vāṇijo tesaṃ majjhe buddhānubhāvaṃ pakāsento āha.

It is said that in Jambudīpa, in the city of Pāṭaliputta, there was a merchant named Buddhavamma who made his living through trade. Later, he wandered, engaging in trade with caravans through villages, towns, districts, and royal cities. At that time, the Blessed One, surrounded by many thousands of monks, was wandering through the countryside, leading many gods and humans across the wilderness of saṃsāra. Then he saw the Blessed One, adorned with the thirty-two marks and the minor characteristics, shining forth like a blazing golden Mount Meru, surrounded by a great assembly of monks. Seeing him, his body pervaded with supreme joy, he raised his hands in veneration, approached the Blessed One, paid homage, and in the evening invited the Blessed One for a meal, being unfamiliar with the Buddha’s discipline. Then the Blessed One said to him, "The Tathāgatas abstain from eating at an improper time." Then he, having paid homage to the Blessed One, asked, "Venerable sir, what do the Blessed Ones consume at an improper time?" In response to his question, the Blessed One said that eight kinds of drinks are permissible for Tathāgatas to consume at an improper time: mango juice, rose-apple juice, banana juice, mocapāna, phārusaka juice, honey drink, grape juice, and lotus-root juice. Hearing that, the merchant, with great respect and delight, made grape juice and gave it to the Sangha headed by the Buddha. After the Teacher and the entire Sangha had partaken of the juice, he taught him the Dhamma and departed on his journey through the countryside. He, too, with a gladdened mind, returned and, engaging in trade with his fellow merchants in various regions, reached a wilderness called Mahāvattanīya. There, the water in all their carts ran out. There was no water for any of the people or the oxen. Then the merchant, overcome by thirst, wandered about, searching for water in those carts. Then, in one cart, some people, seeing him, said out of compassion, "Come here, there is a little water in this pot; drink it." So he went and drank the water. Its taste was like the taste of grape juice. As he drank, he thought, "Surely, the result of the grape juice I offered to the Perfectly Enlightened One has ripened today!" Filled with wonder and amazement, and with joy arisen, he went and opened the lid of the pot himself. The entire pot was filled with grape juice. Then, seeing that nourishing, potent, inexhaustible, divine-like drink, his body pervaded with supreme joy, he exclaimed, "Everyone, drink the water!" Hearing that, all gathered and, seeing the water, were filled with amazement. The merchant, proclaiming the Buddha's power among them, said:

1.

1.

Passathedaṃ bhavanto bho, ānubhāvaṃ mahesino;

Acintanīyamaccheraṃ, sandiṭṭhika makālikaṃ.

Behold this, good sirs, the power of the Great Sage; unthinkable, wondrous, visible here and now, and immediate.

2.

2.

Pasannamanasā buddhe, dinnaṃ pānīyakaṃ mayā;

Vipaccati idāneva, taṃ dānaṃ munivāhasā.

The drink given by me with a gladdened mind to the Buddha; that gift ripens right now, by the power of the Sage.

3.

3.

Ojavantaṃ [M 0.0039] sukhannaṃva, sītalaṃ madhuro dakaṃ;

Dibbapānaṃva devānaṃ, jātamabbhutamakkhayaṃ.

Potent like delicious food, cool, sweet water; like the divine drink of the gods, a wondrous and inexhaustible thing has arisen.

4.

4.

Sīlavantesu ko nāma, na dadeyya vicakkhaṇo;

Idha loke paratteca, sukhadaṃ dāna muttamaṃ.

What discerning person, indeed, would not give to the virtuous? Giving is supreme, bringing happiness both in this world and the next.

5.

5.

Yathi cchitaṃ gahetvāna, pivantu madhuro dakaṃ;

Bhājanānica pūretvā, yantu sabbe yathicchitanti.

Having taken as much as they desire, let them drink the sweet water; and having filled their vessels, let all depart as they wish.

Evañca pana vatvā sabbe manusseca balīvaddeca muddikaraseneva santappesi. Tato tato āgatāpi pānīyaṃ pivantoca pānīyaṃ akkhayaṃ ahosi. Tato vāṇijo satthavāhehi saddhiṃ vāṇijjaṃ payojetvā sakanagaraṃ āgacchanto bhagavantaṃ passitvā gamissāmīti veḷuvanaṃ gantvā satthāraṃ vanditvā katānuñño ekamante nisīdi. Satthāpi tena saddhiṃ madhurapaṭisanthāramakāsi. Upāsakopi bhante tumhākaṃ pāṭihāriyaṃ disvā pasanno vanditvā gamissāmīti āgatomhi, evañce vañca pāṭihāriyanti vitthārena kathesi. Athassa bhagavā dhammaṃ desesi. So dhammaṃ sutvāna satthāraṃ svātanāya nimantetvā mahādānaṃ datvā attano gehameva agamāsi. So tato paṭṭhāya dānādīni puññāni katvā tato cuto devaloke dvādasayojanike kanakavimāne devaccharāparivuto devissariyasamannāgato nibbatti. Tassa pubbakammapakāsanatthaṃ tattha tattha ratanabhājanesu dibbamayehi muddikapānehi paripuṇṇaṃ ahosi. Pānīyaṃ pivitvā devā naccanti vādenti kīḷantīti.

And having spoken thus, he satisfied all the people and the oxen with grape juice itself. Even those who came from various places, as they drank the drink, the drink became inexhaustible. Then the merchant, having conducted his trade with the caravan and returning to his own city, thinking, "I will go and see the Blessed One," went to the Bamboo Grove, paid homage to the Teacher, and having received permission, sat to one side. The Teacher also engaged him in pleasant conversation. The lay follower said, "Venerable sir, having seen your miracle, I came, gladdened, to pay homage before departing." He then recounted the miracle in detail. Thereupon, the Blessed One taught him the Dhamma. He, having heard the Dhamma, invited the Teacher for the next day, gave a great offering, and returned to his own home. From then on, he performed meritorious deeds such as giving, and after passing away from there, he was reborn in the heavenly world in a golden mansion twelve yojanas in extent, surrounded by celestial nymphs, endowed with divine sovereignty. To reveal his past karma, jeweled vessels filled with divine grape juice appeared here and there. Having drunk the juice, the gods danced, played music, and sported.

6.

6.

Na vipulajinasāraṃ jānamāno janevaṃ,

Labhati vipulabhogaṃ toyamattassa dānā;

Viditaguṇagaṇā bho tīsu vatthūsu tumhe,

Labhatha khalu visesaṃ sīlavantesu dānāti.

A person not knowing the vast essence of the Conqueror gains abundant wealth from a mere gift of water; but you, O good sirs, knowing the array of virtues in the three objects, truly attain distinction by giving to the virtuous.

Buddhavammavāṇijakassa vatthuṃ navamaṃ.

The Story of Buddhavamma the Merchant, the Ninth.

10. Rūpadeviyā vatthumhi ayamānupubbīkathā

10. Herein, the sequential account in the story of Rūpadevī.

Atīte [M 0.0040] kira vipassissa bhagavato kāle tasmiṃ nagare ekā gāmadārikā vihāre āhiṇḍantī ekaṃ gilānabhikkhuṃ disvā kampamānahadayā upasaṅkamitvā vanditvā bhante kote ābādho sarīraṃ pīḷetīti pucchi. Tenāpi bhagini kharābādho me pīḷetīti vutte sā tenahi bhante ahaṃ taṃ rogaṃ vūpasamessāmīti nimantetvā gehaṃ gantvā taṃ pavattiṃ mātāpitunnaṃ kathetvā tehi anuññātā puna divase nānaggarasena bhesajjāhāraṃ sampādesi, tato so bhikkhu punadivase cīvaraṃ pārupitvā bhikkhāya caranto tassā gehaṃ gantvā aṭṭhāsi. Sā theraṃ āgataṃ disvā somanassajātā pattaṃ gahetvā āsanaṃ paññāpetvā adāsi. Tattha nisinnaṃ taṃ āhārena sādhukaṃ parivisitvā sakkāra makāsi. Athassā saddhābalena bhuttamatteyeva so ābādho vūpasami. Tato so vūpasantarogo dutiyadivase tassā gehaṃ nā gamāsi. Atha sā vihāraṃ gantvā taṃ vanditvā kasmānāgatatthāti pucchitvā tena me bhagini byādhi vūpasami, tasmā nāgatosmīti vutte sā sādhu bhanteti somanassajātā gehameva agamāsi. Sā tena puññakammena kālaṃ katvā devaloke nibbatti. Tassā tattha dvādasayojanikaṃ kanakavimānaṃ nibbatti. Sā tattha devissariyaṃ anubhavantī chabuddhantaraṃ khepetvā amhākaṃ bhagavato kāle jambudīpe devaputtanagare udiccabrahmaṇakule jeṭṭhabrāhmaṇassa bhariyāya kucchimhi paṭisandhiṃ gaṇhi, sā paripāka manvāya mātukucchito nikkhami. Tassā mātukucchito nikkhantakālato paṭṭhāya divase divase aṭṭhaṭṭhanālimattaṃ taṇḍulaṃ nibbattati. Tassā rūpasampattiṃ disvā pasannā mātāpitaro rūpadevīti nāma makaṃsu. Pacchā taṃ patirūpena dārakena niyojesuṃ. Athassā taṇḍula- nālimattaṃ [M 0.0041] gahetvā pacituṃ āraddhe icchiticchitamaṃsādibyañjanañja sappinavanītadadhikhīrādigorasañca jīramaricādikaṭukabhaṇḍañca kadalipanasamadhuguḷādiupakaraṇañca bhājanāni pūretvā nibbattati, tāya hatthena gahitaṃ kiñci khādanīyaṃ bhojanīyaṃvā pūtibhāvaṃ na gacchati, bhattukkhaliṃ gahetvā sakalanagaravāsino bhojentiyāpi ekakaṭacchumattaṃ bhattaṃ gahitaṭṭhānaṃ na paññāyati. Evaṃ aparikkhayapuññā ahosi, sakaladevaputtanagare candasuriyāva pākaṭā ahosi, atha sā pañcasatabhikkhū nimantetvā niccaṃ sakanivesaneyeva bhojeti, tadā tesaṃ antare paṭisambhidāpatto mahāsaṅgharakkhitattheronāma imissā puññānubhāvaṃ dibbacakkhunā disvā na jānāti esā atthanā pubbe katakammaṃ. Yannūnāhaṃ assā pakāseyyanti ekadivasaṃ tassā nivesane bhuñjitvā anumodanaṃ karonto jānāsi bhagini tayā pubbe katakammanti pucchi, na jānāmi bhante. Sotumicchāmīti, athassā so pubbakammaṃ pakāsento.

It is said that in the past, during the time of the Blessed One Vipassī, in that city, a village girl, wandering in the monastery, saw a sick monk. Moved with a trembling heart, she approached, paid respects, and asked, "Venerable sir, what illness afflicts your body?" When he replied, "Sister, a severe illness afflicts me," she said, "Then, venerable sir, I will cause this illness to subside." Inviting him, she went home, told her parents about the matter, and with their permission, the next day she prepared medicinal food with various excellent flavors. The next day, that monk, having put on his robe, while walking for alms, went to her house and stood there. Seeing the elder arrive, she joyfully took his bowl, prepared a seat, and offered it. Seated there, she carefully served him with food and showed him respect. Then, by the power of her faith, as soon as he had eaten, his illness subsided. After his illness was cured, the next day he did not go to her house. Then she went to the monastery, paid respects to him, and asked, "Why did you not come?" He replied, "Sister, my illness has subsided, therefore I did not come." She said, "Good, venerable sir," and joyfully returned home. Due to that meritorious deed, after her death, she was reborn in the heavenly realm. There, a golden mansion twelve yojanas in extent arose for her. Enjoying divine sovereignty there, she passed through six Buddha-intervals. In the time of our Blessed One, in Jambudīpa, in the city of Devaputta, in a northern brahmin family, she was conceived in the womb of the chief brahmin's wife, and when her time came, she emerged from her mother's womb. From the time she emerged from her mother's womb, eight nāḷis of rice arose daily. Seeing her beauty, her delighted parents named her Rūpadevī. Later, they arranged for her to marry a suitable young man. When she started to cook, taking a nāḷi of rice, various desired curries of meat and so forth, ghee, fresh butter, curd, milk, and other dairy products, spices like cumin and pepper, and accompaniments like bananas, jackfruit, honey, and sugar, filled the pots. Whatever edible or drinkable thing she took with her hand did not become putrid. Even when, taking a pot of rice, she fed all the inhabitants of the city, the place where a single spoonful of rice was taken showed no sign of depletion. Thus, she was of inexhaustible merit. In the entire city of Devaputta, she was as well-known as the sun and moon. Then she invited five hundred monks and regularly fed them in her own dwelling. At that time, among them was a great elder named Mahāsaṅgharakkhita, who had attained the analytical knowledges. He saw her power of merit with his divine eye, but did not know what deed she had done in the past to bring this about. Thinking, "I should reveal it to her," one day, after eating at her home, while giving the blessing, he asked, "Sister, do you know the deed you did in the past?" She replied, "I do not know, venerable sir. I wish to hear." Then, he began to reveal her past deed to her.

1.

1.

Ekanavute ito kappe,

Vipassīnāma nāyako;

Ahosi loke lokeka,

Nāyako chinnabandhano.

Ninety-one aeons ago, the leader named Vipassī arose in the world, the one leader of the world, who had cut all bonds.

2.

2.

Tadā tasmiṃ pure ramme, āsi tvaṃ gāmadārikā;

Āhiṇḍantī vihārasmiṃ, addakkhi jinasāvakaṃ.

Then, in that lovely city, you were a village girl. Wandering in the monastery, you saw a disciple of the Conqueror.

3.

3.

Rogāturaṃ kisaṃ paṇḍuṃ, assasantaṃ muhuṃ muhuṃ;

Disvāna kampitā cittā, nimantetvāna taṃ muniṃ.

Seeing that sage—sick, frail, and pale, breathing heavily again and again—your heart was shaken, and you invited him.

4.

4.

Bhesajjañceva bhattañca, adā tvaṃ tena so yati;

Abyābādho anīghoca, ahosi anupaddavo.

You gave him medicine and food; by that, that ascetic became free from illness, free from affliction, and free from trouble.

5.

5.

Tato tvaṃ tena kammena, sukatena tato cutā;

Jātāsi devalokasmiṃ, sabbakāmasamiddhinī.

Then, by that well-performed deed, passing away from there, you were reborn in the heavenly world, endowed with all sense-pleasures.

6.

6.

Tattha [M 0.0042] te puññatejena, pāsādo ratanāmayo;

Maṇithūpisatākiṇṇo, kūṭāgārehi laṅkato.

There, by the power of your merit, a palace made of gems arose, filled with hundreds of jeweled pinnacles and embellished with gabled mansions.

7.

7.

Nekagabbhasatākiṇṇo, sayanāsanamaṇḍito;

Accharāsatasaṃkiṇṇo, naccagītādisaṃkulo.

It was filled with many hundreds of apartments, adorned with couches and seats, thronged with hundreds of celestial nymphs, and abounding with dancing, singing, and so forth.

8.

8.

Rambhāmbajambusannīra, pūgapunnāgapāṭalī;

Nāgāditarusaṇḍehi, maṇḍituyyānapantihi.

It was adorned with rows of pleasure gardens, with groves of plantain, ambaja, rose-apple, sannīra, areca, punnāga, trumpet flower, ironwood, and other trees.

9.

9.

Padumuppalakaḷāra, kundakānanamaṇḍite;

Madhumattālipālīhi, sārasīsarasaṃkule.

It was adorned with clusters of lotus and blue lotus and with groves of jasmine, humming with rows of bees intoxicated with honey, and filled with the sounds of cranes.

10.

10.

Devaputtehi nekehi, tathā devaccharāhica;

Niccussave mahābhoge, vimāne mananandane.

In that mind-delighting mansion were many sons of the gods and celestial nymphs, with constant festivities and great enjoyments.

11.

11.

Tvamevaṃ devalokamhi, vindamānā mahāyasaṃ;

Addhānaṃ vītināmetvā, nibbute gotame jine.

Thus you, gaining great fame in the heavenly world, passed a long time, until the time of the Victor Gotama, the Liberated One.

12.

12.

Jambudīpe idāni tvaṃ, nibbattā udite kule;

Puññapaññāguṇāvāsā, rūpenaggā piyaṃvadā.

Now, in Jambudīpa, you are reborn in a noble family, a dwelling place of merit, wisdom, and virtue, foremost in beauty, and sweet in speech.

13.

13.

Etaṃ te devalokasmiṃ, devissariyamabbhutaṃ,

Imaṃ te idha lokasmiṃ, sabbaṃ mānusikaṃ sukhaṃ.

That was your marvelous divine sovereignty in the heavenly realm; and this, in this world, is all your human happiness.

14.

14.

Vipassimunino kāle, tvaṃ tassekassa bhikkhuno;

Adā dānaṃ gilānassa, tassa taṃ phalamīdisaṃ.

In the time of the Sage Vipassī, you gave a gift to that one sick monk; this is the fruit of that deed.

15.

15.

Kātabbañhi sadā puññaṃ, icchantena sukhappadaṃ;

Tasmā tvaṃ sabbadā bhadde, ussukkā kusale bhavāti.

Indeed, one who desires what brings happiness should always perform meritorious deeds. Therefore, O auspicious one, always be zealous in what is wholesome.

Evaṃ so tassa purimattabhāve katakammaṃ pakāsetvā idāni puññakamme appamādā bhavāti anusāsi. Sā therassa dhammadesanaṃ sutvā paramasomanassā tato paṭṭhāya dānādīsu niratā puññāni karontī teneva somanassena sotāpannā ariyasāvikā ahosīti.

Having thus revealed to her the deed done in her previous existence, he then advised her, saying, "Now, be diligent in meritorious actions." Upon hearing the Elder's Dhamma teaching, she was filled with supreme joy. From that time onward, she was devoted to meritorious deeds such as giving and, through that very joy, became a noble disciple, a stream-enterer.

16.

16.

Iti [M 0.0043] taruṇakumārī puññakammesu sāraṃ,

Aviditaguṇamattā datva bhikkhussa dānaṃ;

Divimanujasukhaṃ sālattha tumhe bhavantā,

Viditakusalapākā kiṃ na labbhetha santiṃ.

Thus the young maiden, though unaware of the measure of its virtue, gave a gift to the monk, finding the essence in meritorious deeds; she attained celestial and human happiness. You, knowing the result of wholesome deeds, why should you not attain peace?

Rūpadeviyā vatthuṃ dasamaṃ.

The Tenth Story: The Story of Rūpadevī.

Dhammasoṇḍakavaggo pathamo.

The First Chapter: The Chapter on Dhammasoṇḍaka.

Nandiyarājavaggo

The Chapter on King Nandiya

11. Nandiyarājassa vatthumhi ayamānupubbīkathā

11. This is the introductory discourse in the story of King Nandiya.

Ito [M 0.0045] kira kappasatasahassamatthake padumuttaro nāma satthā loke udapādi sadevakaṃ lokaṃ saṃsārakantārā uttārento. Tasmiṃ kira samaye eko kuṭumbiko satthu dhammadesanaṃ sutvā pasannamānaso buddhapamukhaṃ bhikkhusaṅghaṃ nimantetvā mahādānaṃ sajjetvā attano bhavanaṃ devabhavanamiva alaṅkaritvā buddhārahaṃ mahāsanaṃ paññāpetvā gantvā bhagavantaṃ yāci kāloyaṃ bhante bhagavato bhattaggassa upasaṅkamanāyāti. Atha bhagavā bhikkhusaṅghaparivuto mahatā buddhānubhāvena gantvā nisīdi paññattavarabuddhāsane, tato kuṭumbiko haṭṭho udaggo sapariso bhagavantaṃ parivisati anekehi madhurannapānādīhi. Tadā tassa bhagavato sāsane dhutaṅgadharānaṃ aggo vasabhattheronāma mahāsāvako sapadānavattena piṇḍāya caramāno tassa kuṭumbikassa gehadvāre aṭṭhāsi, atha so theraṃ disvā bhante satthā antogehe nisinno, tumhepi pavisathāti yāci, thero apavisitvāva agamāsi, kuṭumbiko bhagavantaṃ upasaṅkamitvā tamattaṃ vatvā kiṃ bhante sadevake loke bhagavatāpi uttaritaro guṇena saṃvijjatīti āha. Athassa satthā puttopamaṃ dassetvā therassa guṇe vaṇṇento evamāha.

It is said that a hundred thousand eons ago, the Teacher named Padumuttara arose in the world, guiding the world with its devas across the wilderness of saṃsāra. At that time, a certain householder, hearing the Teacher’s Dhamma discourse, became serene in mind and invited the Sangha of monks headed by the Buddha. He prepared a great offering, adorned his home like a divine mansion, and arranged a magnificent seat worthy of the Buddha. Then he approached the Blessed One and requested, "Venerable sir, it is time for the Blessed One to approach the meal." Then the Blessed One, surrounded by the Sangha of monks, went with great Buddha-power and sat on the prepared supreme Buddha-seat. The householder, delighted and elated with his retinue, served the Blessed One with various sweet foods and drinks. At that time, in the dispensation of that Blessed One, the foremost among those who observed the ascetic practices was the great disciple, the Elder Vasabha by name, who was going for alms from house to house. He stood at the householder’s door. Seeing the elder, the householder said, "Venerable sir, the Teacher is seated inside the house. Please enter as well." But the elder, without entering, departed. The householder approached the Blessed One and, relating that matter, asked, "Venerable sir, is there anyone in the world with its devas who surpasses the Blessed One in virtue?" Then the Teacher, illustrating with a simile of a son and praising the elder’s virtues, spoke thus:

1.

1.

Pālenti nimmalaṃ katvā, pātimokkhādisaṃvaraṃ;

Samādinnadhutaṅgāca, appicchā munisūnavo.

Having made it pure, they maintain the restraint of the Pātimokkha and so forth; and having undertaken the ascetic practices, the sons of the Sage are of few wishes.

2.

2.

Nicca mantakayuddhamhi, naddhā yodhāva dappitā;

Puññānaṃ vatthubhūtā te, devamānusakā dinaṃ.

In the constant mental battle, they are like bold warriors girded for it; they are a basis of merit for gods and humans day by day.

3.

3.

Dhāremahaṃ vaṇṇavantaṃ, sīveyyampica cīvaraṃ;

Buddhaputtā mahānāgā na dhārenti tathāvidhaṃ.

I wear a colorful and fine Sīveyyaka robe; but the Buddha's sons, the great nāgas, do not wear such a kind.

4.

4.

Dhārenti [M 0.0046] te paṃsukūlaṃ, saṅghāṭetvā pilotike;

Vaṇacchādanacolaṃva, icchālobha vivajjitā.

They wear rag-robes, having stitched together worn-out cloths; like a cloth covering a wound, they are free from desire and greed.

5.

5.

Sādiyāmi sadā hambho, upāsakanimantanaṃ;

Neva sādenti sambuddha, putto pāsakayācanaṃ.

I always accept, good man, the invitation of a lay devotee; but the son of the Sambuddha does not accept the request of a lay follower.

6.

6.

Sapadānena yaṃ laddhaṃ, lūkhaṃvāpi paṇītakaṃ;

Tena tussanti me puttā, rasagedhavivajjitā.

Whatever is obtained by going from house to house, whether coarse or fine, with that my sons are content, being devoid of craving for flavors.

7.

7.

Nipajjāmi ahaṃ sādhu, santhate sayane subhe;

Na te seyyaṃ pakappenti, saṃsārabhayabhīrukā.

I lie down well on a well-spread, beautiful bed; but they, who are fearful of the danger of saṃsāra, do not arrange a lodging.

8.

8.

Ṭhānā sanagamanena, kappenti iriyāpathaṃ;

Nekabhūmisamākiṇṇa, pāsādesu vasāmahaṃ.

Through standing, sitting, and walking, they maintain their deportment; I dwell in palaces of many stories.

9.

9.

Buddhaputtā tathācchannaṃ, na kadā pavisanti te;

Rukkhamūle susānasmiṃ, abbhokāse ramanti te.

The Buddha's sons never enter such a sheltered place; they delight at the foot of a tree, in a charnel ground, or in the open air.

10.

10.

Bhāvetvā bhavanāsāya, hetuṃ bhāvanamuttamaṃ;

Ahaṃ gāme vasissāmi, pāpento janataṃ sivaṃ.

Having cultivated the supreme development, the cause for the destruction of existence, I will dwell in the village, leading the people to security.

11.

11.

Ramanti mama puttā te, pantasenāsane kakā;

Tesaṃ mahattaro santo, theroyaṃ vasabho mahā;

Dhutapāpo dhutaṅgaggo, ñātoyaṃ mama sāsaneti.

Those sons of mine delight in secluded dwellings, alone; among them, this great Elder Vasabha is greater, one who has shaken off evil, foremost in the ascetic practices, known in my dispensation.

Evaṃ bhagavā hatthaṃ ukkhipitvā candamaṇḍale paharanto viya therassa guṇe pakāsesi, tato so tassa guṇakathaṃ sutvā sayampitaṃ ṭhānantaraṃ kāmayamāno yannūnāhaṃ anāgate aññatarassa sammāsambuddhassa sāsane dhutaṅgadharānaṃ aggo bhavissāmīti taṃ ṭhānantaraṃ patthento bhagavato pādamūle nipajji, satthā taṃ kāraṇaṃ upaparikkhitvā ito kappasatasahassamatthake gotamo nāma satthā uppajjissati. Tvaṃ tadā dhutaṅgadharānaṃ aggo hutvā kassa poti paññāyissatīti byākaraṇa [M 0.0047] madāsi. Tato paṭṭhāya so somanasso puññakammaṃ katvā tato cuto devamanussesu devissariyaṃ anubhavanto vipassīsammāsambuddhakāle ekasāṭako nāma brāhmaṇo hutvā mahādānaṃ adāsi. Tato cuto kassapasammāsambuddhe parinibbute bārāṇasīnagare bārāṇasīseṭṭhi hutvā nibbatto dānādīni puññāni katvā tato cavitvā saṃsāre saṃsaranto dasavassasahassā yukesu manussesu bārāṇasiyaṃ eko kuṭumbiko hutvā nibbatti. So panāyaṃ kuṭumbiko araññe jaṅghāvihāraṃ anuvicaranto paccantime janapade araññāyatane aññataraṃ paccekabuddhaṃ addasa. So ca paccekabuddho tattha cīvarakammaṃ karonto anuvāte appahonte saṃharitvā ṭhapetuṃ āraddho. Kuṭumbiko taṃ disvā bhante kiṃ karothāti pucchi. So paccekabuddho appicchatāya tena puṭṭho na kiñci vutto hoti. So cīvaradussaṃ nappahotīti ñatvā attano uttarasāṭakaṃ paccekabuddhassa pādamūle ṭhapetvā agamāsi. Paccekabuddho taṃ gahetvā anuvātakaṃ āropento cīvaraṃ katvā pārupi. Kuṭumbiko jīvitapariyosāne kālaṃ katvā tāvatiṃsabhavane nibbattitvā tattha yāvatāyukaṃ dibbasampattiṃ anubhavitvā tato cavitvā bārāṇasito tiyojanamattake ṭhāne aññatarasmiṃ nagare nibbatti, tassa mātāpitaro nandiyoti nandītināmaṃ itisabbattha nāmaṃ akaṃsu, tassa satta bhātaro ahesuṃ, sesā cha bhātaro nānākammantesu byāvaṭā mātāpitunnaṃ posenti. Nandiyo pana akammasīlo geheyeva vasati. Tasmā tassa sesā kujjhanti. Mātāpitaropi nandiyaṃ āmantetvā ovadanti. So tuṇhī hoteva. Athāparasmiṃ samaye gāme nakkhattaṃ saṅghuṭṭhaṃ, tadā so mātaraṃ āha. Amma sāṭakaṃ dehi, nakkhattaṃ kīḷissāmīti, sā dhota- vatthaṃ [M 0.0048] nīharitvā adāsi, amma thūlaṃ idanti, sā aññaṃ nīharitvā adāsi, tampi paṭikkhipi. Atha naṃ mātā āha. Tāta yādise mayaṃ gehe jātā, natthi ito sukhumatarassa paṭilābhāya puññanti, labhanaṭṭhānaṃ gamissāmi ammāti. Putta ahaṃ ajjeva tava bārāṇasīnagare rajjapaṭilābhaṃ icchāmīti āha. So sādhu ammāti mātaraṃ vanditvā padakkhiṇaṃ katvā pakkāmi. Mātuyā panassa evaṃ ahosi. Kahaṃ so gamissati, pubbeviya idhavā etthavā gehe nisīditvā āgacchatīti, so pana puññaniyāmena codiyamāno gāmato nikkhamitvā bārāṇasiṃ gantvā senaguttassa gehe paṭivasati, athekadivasaṃ so tassa kammakārehi saddhiṃ sallapanto nisīditvā pacalāyanto supinaṃ addasa. Mukhena antaṃ nikkhamitvā sakalajambudīpe pattharitvā antokucchimeva pāvisi. Pabuddho so bhīto mahāsaddamakāsi. Atha naṃ mahāsenagutto pucchi. Nandiyo supinaṃ addasanti āha. Atha tena kīdisanti puṭṭho kathesi, tato senagutto taṃ attano kulūpagaṃ paribbājikaṃ pucchi ko tassa vipākoti. Paribbājikā yadi bho itthī passati. Sattadivasabbhantareyeva abhisekaṃ labhati, yadi puriso passati, tatheva rājā hotīti kathesi. Senagutto tassā taṃ kathaṃ sutvā imaṃ mama ñātiṃ karomīti attano satta dhītare pakkositvā paṭipāṭiyā pucchi. Nandiyassa santike vasathāti, sesā sabbā na icchiṃsu, na mayaṃ jānāma etaṃ adhunāgataṃ kulavantaṃ vā dukkulavantaṃvāti. Atha kaṇiṭṭhikaṃ pucchi. Sā yassa maṃ mātāpitaro dassanti. Tesaṃ vacanaṃ na bhindissāmīti sampaṭicchi, atha senagutto nandiyaṃ pakkositvā attano dhītaraṃ datvā tassa mahāsampatti madāsi. Tato sattame divase nandiyo tattha tattha āhiṇḍanto rañño maṅgaluyyānaṃ passissāmīti gantvā dhaṅgalasilāpaṭṭesasīsaṃ pārupitvā nipajji, so ca bārāṇasīrañño kālaṅkatassa sattamo [M 0.0049] divaso hoti. Amaccāca purohitoca rañño sarīrakiccaṃ kāretvā rājaṅgaṇe nisīditvā mantayiṃsu. Rañño ekāva dhītā atthi, putto panassa natthi. Arājikaṃ rajjaṃ na tiṭṭhati. Phussarathaṃ vissajjessāmāti. Te kumudapattavaṇṇe cattāro sindhave yojetvā setacchattapamukhaṃpañcavidharājakakudhabhaṇḍaṃ rathasmiṃyeva ṭhapetvā rathaṃ vissajjetvā pacchato turiyāni paggaṇhāpesuṃ. Ratho pācīdvārena nikkhamitvā uyyānābhimukho ahosi. Paricayena uyyānābhimukho gacchati. Nivattemāti keci āhaṃsu. Purohito mā nivārayitthāti āha. Ratho kumārakaṃ padakkhiṇaṃ katvā ārohaṇasajjo hutvā aṭṭhāsi. Purohito pārupaṇakaṇṇaṃ apanetvā pādatalāni olokento tiṭṭhatu ayaṃ dīpo. Dvisahassadīpa parivāresu catūsu mahādīpesu ekarajjaṃ kātuṃ samatthotivatvā tassa dhitiṃ upadhāretuṃ tikkhattuṃ turiyāni paggaṇhāpesi. Atha kumāro mukhaṃ vivaritvā oloketvā kena kāraṇena āgatatthāti āha, deva tumhākaṃ rajjaṃ pāpuṇātīti, rājā kahanti, devattaṃ gato sāmīti, kati divasā atikkantāti, ajja sattamo divasoti. Putto vā dhītā vā natthīti, dhītā atthi deva. Putto na vijjatīti, tenahi karissāmi rajjanti, te tāvadeva abhisekamaṇḍapaṃ kāretvā rājadhītaraṃ sabbālaṅkārehi alaṅkāritvā uyyānaṃ netvā kumārassa abhisekaṃ akaṃsu, athassa katābhisekassa satasahassagghanakaṃ vatthaṃ ānesuṃ, so kimidaṃ tātāti āha, nivāsanavatthaṃ devāti, nanu tātā thūlaṃti, manussānaṃ paribhogavatthesu ito sukhumataraṃ natthi devāti, tumhākaṃ rājā evarūpaṃ nivāsesīti, āma devāti. Na maññe puññavā tumhākaṃ rājāti vatvā handa suvaṇṇabhiṅkāraṃ āharatha, labhissāma vatthanti, te suvaṇṇabhiṅkāraṃ āhariṃsu, so uṭṭhāya hatthe dhovitvā mukhaṃ vikkhāletvā hatthena udakaṃ ādāya puratthimadisāya abbhukkiri, [M 0.0050] tāvadeva ghanapathaviṃ bhinditvā soḷasakapparukkhā uṭṭhahiṃsu, puna udakaṃ hatthena gahetvā dakkhiṇaṃ pacchimaṃ uttaranti evaṃ catassopi disā abbhukkiri, sabbadisāsu soḷasa soḷasa hutvā catusaṭṭhikapparukkhā uṭṭhahiṃsu, so ekaṃ dibbadussaṃ nivāsetvā ekaṃ pārupitvā nandiyarañño vijite suttakantikā itthiyo mā suttaṃ kantantūti bheriṃ carāpethāti vatvā chattaṃ ussāpetvā alaṅkatapaṭiyatto hatthikkhandhavaragato nagaraṃ pavisitvā pāsādamabhiruyha mahāsampattiṃ anubhavi. Aho tadā paccekabuddhassa dinnānuvātakassa vipāko. Tenāhu porāṇā.

Thus, the Blessed One, raising his hand as if striking the moon’s orb, proclaimed the Elder’s virtues. Hearing this praise of his virtues, and desiring that position for himself, he thought, “May I become the foremost among those who uphold the ascetic practices in the dispensation of a future Fully Enlightened One!” Aspiring to that position, he lay down at the feet of the Blessed One. The Teacher, having examined the matter, made a prediction: “A hundred thousand aeons from now, a Teacher named Gotama will arise. You will then be the foremost among those who uphold the ascetic practices and will be known as Kassapa.” From that time onward, filled with joy, he performed meritorious deeds. After passing away from that existence, he enjoyed divine sovereignty among gods and humans. During the time of the Fully Enlightened One Vipassī, he was born as a Brahmin named Ekasāṭaka and gave great alms. After passing away from there, when the Fully Enlightened One Kassapa had attained final Nibbāna, he was reborn as the chief merchant of Bārāṇasī. There, he performed meritorious deeds such as giving, and after passing away from there, he wandered through the cycle of rebirths. He was reborn as a householder in Bārāṇasī among humans with a lifespan of ten thousand years. This householder, wandering on foot in the forest, saw a Paccekabuddha in a wilderness area in a border region. The Paccekabuddha was doing robe-work, but as the windbreak was insufficient, he had begun to gather it up to put it away. Seeing this, the householder asked, “Venerable sir, what are you doing?” The Paccekabuddha, being of few desires, said nothing when asked. Realizing that the cloth for the windbreak was insufficient, the householder placed his upper garment at the Paccekabuddha’s feet and left. The Paccekabuddha took it, set it up as a windbreak, made his robe, and wore it. At the end of his life, the householder passed away and was reborn in the Tāvatiṃsa heaven, where he enjoyed divine bliss for his entire lifespan. After passing away from there, he was reborn in a city three yojanas from Bārāṇasī. His parents named him Nandiya. He had six brothers. The other six brothers engaged in various trades to support their parents, but Nandiya, being of lazy habit, stayed at home. Therefore, the others were angry with him. His parents also admonished Nandiya, but he remained silent. On a later occasion, a festival was announced in the village. He then said to his mother, “Mother, give me a garment; I will go and enjoy the festival.” She brought out a washed cloth and gave it to him. “Mother, this is too coarse,” he said. She brought out another, and he rejected that one as well. Then his mother said to him, “Son, given the family we are born into, we have no merit for obtaining anything finer than this.” “I will go to a place where one may be obtained, mother,” he replied. “Son,” she said, “I wish for you to obtain the kingdom in the city of Bārāṇasī this very day!” Saying, “Very well, mother,” he paid homage to his mother, circumambulated her respectfully, and departed. His mother thought, “Where will he go? As before, he will just sit somewhere here or there in the house and then come back.” But driven by the certainty of his merit, he left the village and went to Bārāṇasī, where he stayed in the house of Senagutta. One day, while sitting and chatting with Senagutta's workers, he dozed off and had a dream: his entrails emerged from his mouth, spread over all of Jambudīpa, and then re-entered his belly. Awakened and frightened, he cried out loudly. Then Senagutta asked him, “Nandiya, did you have a dream?” When asked what kind of dream it was, he related it. Then Senagutta asked a female wandering ascetic who was an attendant of his family, “What is the result of this?” The female ascetic said, “If a woman sees this, she will receive the royal consecration within seven days. If a man sees it, he will likewise become king.” Hearing her words, Senagutta thought, “I will make this man my relative.” He called his seven daughters and asked them in turn, “Will you live with Nandiya?” All the others refused, saying, “We do not know this newcomer, whether he is of good family or bad.” Then he asked the youngest. She consented, saying, “To whomever my parents give me, I will not break their word.” Then Senagutta summoned Nandiya, gave him his daughter, and bestowed great wealth upon him. On the seventh day, Nandiya was wandering about and went to see the king’s auspicious park. There, he covered his head and lay down on the auspicious stone slab. That day was the seventh since the king of Bārāṇasī had passed away. The ministers and the royal priest, having performed the king’s funeral rites, sat in the royal courtyard and deliberated: “The king has only one daughter, but no son. A kingdom without a king cannot stand. Let us release the Phussa chariot.” They yoked four Sindh horses, the color of white water lily petals, to the chariot, placed the five royal insignia headed by the white parasol upon it, and released it, having musical instruments played behind it. The chariot left through the eastern gate and headed toward the park. It headed toward the park out of familiarity. Some said, “Let us turn it back.” The priest said, “Do not stop it.” The chariot circumambulated the young man and stopped, ready for him to ascend. The priest moved aside the corner of his covering, looked at the soles of his feet, and declared, “Set this one island aside! He is capable of ruling a single kingdom over the four great continents with their two thousand surrounding islands.” To ascertain his fortitude, he had the musical instruments sounded three times. Then the young man uncovered his face, looked around, and asked, “For what reason have you come?” “Lord, the kingdom has come to you.” “Where is the king?” “He has gone to the state of a deva, sire.” “How many days have passed?” “Today is the seventh.” “Has he no son or daughter?” “There is a daughter, lord, but no son.” “In that case, I will rule the kingdom.” They immediately had a consecration pavilion built, adorned the king’s daughter with all her ornaments, brought her to the park, and performed the young man’s consecration. After his consecration, they brought him a garment worth a hundred thousand. He asked, “My dear, what is this?” “A lower garment for you, lord.” “Is it not coarse, my dear?” “Lord, among the garments used by humans, there is none finer than this.” “Did your king wear such a garment?” “Yes, lord.” “I do not think your king was a man of great merit,” he said. “Come, bring a golden water pot. We shall obtain a garment.” They brought a golden water pot. He rose, washed his hands, rinsed his mouth, took water in his hand, and sprinkled it toward the eastern direction. At that very moment, sixteen wish-fulfilling trees arose, splitting the solid earth. Again he took water in his hand and sprinkled it toward the southern, western, and northern directions. Thus he sprinkled it in all four directions, and in every direction sixteen trees arose, making sixty-four wish-fulfilling trees in total. He put on one divine garment and draped another over himself. Then he had a drum beaten throughout the kingdom of King Nandiya with the proclamation: “Let the women who spin thread not spin thread!” Having raised the white parasol, adorned and ready, he mounted a magnificent elephant, entered the city, ascended to the palace, and enjoyed great prosperity. Ah, such was the result of the windbreak given to the Paccekabuddha! Thus have the ancients spoken.

12.

12.

Yathā sāsapamattamhā, bījā nigrodhapādapo;

Jāyate satasākhaḍḍho, mahānīlambudopamo.

Just as from a seed the size of a mustard grain, a banyan tree arises, rich with a hundred branches, like a great blue cloud.

13.

13.

Tatheva puññakammamhā, aṇumhā vipulaṃ phalaṃ;

Hotīti appapuññanti, nāvamaññeyya paṇḍitoti.

Even so, from a small meritorious deed, abundant fruit arises; therefore, a wise person should not despise a small merit.

Evaṃ gacchante kāle ekadivasaṃ devī rañño sampattiṃ disvā aho tapassīti kāruññākāraṃ dassesi. Kimidaṃ devīti ca puṭṭhā atimahatī te deva sampatti. Atītamaddhānaṃ kalyāṇaṃ katattā. Idāni anāgatassatthāya kusalaṃ karothāti āha. Kassa dassāma. Sīlavantā natthīti. Asuñño deva jambudīpo arahantehi, tumhe dānaṃ sajjetha, ahaṃ arahante lacchāmīti āha. Punadivase rājā mahārahaṃ dānaṃ sajjāpesi. Devī sace imissā disāya arahanto atthi, idhā gantvā amhākaṃ bhikkhaṃ gaṇhantūti uttarahimavantābhimukhī pupphāni uddhaṃ khipitvā urena nipajji. Atha tāni pupphāni ākāsato gantvā himavantapadese vasantānaṃ padumavatiyā puttānaṃ pañcasatānaṃpaccekabuddhānaṃ jeṭṭhakamahāpadumapaccekabuddhassa pādamūle patiṃsu. Tathāhi.

As time was thus passing, one day the queen, seeing the king’s prosperity, showed a compassionate gesture, thinking, “Alas, the poor man!” When asked, “What is this, queen?” she replied, “Your majesty, your prosperity is exceedingly great because you performed good deeds in the past. Now, for the sake of the future, you should perform wholesome deeds.” “To whom shall we give? There are no virtuous ones,” said the king. “Your majesty, Jambudīpa is not devoid of arahants. You prepare the alms, and I shall find the arahants,” she said. The next day, the king had a magnificent alms-offering prepared. The queen, facing the northern Himavanta, threw flowers upwards and lay down prostrate, declaring, “If there are arahants in this direction, may they come here and accept our almsfood.” Then those flowers, traveling through the sky, fell at the feet of the eldest, the Paccekabuddha Mahāpaduma, one of the five hundred paccekabuddhas, the sons of Padumavatī, who were dwelling in the Himalayan region. For thus it is said:

14.

14.

Aho [M 0.0051] passatha bho dāni, vimhayaṃ puññakammuno;

Acetanāpi pupphāni, dūtakiccesu byāvaṭā.

Ah, behold now, sirs, the wonder of a meritorious deed; even flowers, though insentient, are occupied with the duties of a messenger.

15.

15.

Kattukāmena lokasmiṃ, sakalaṃ attano vasaṃ;

Sabbatthāmena kattabbaṃ, puññaṃ paññavatā sadāti.

In this world, a wise person who desires to bring everything under their own control should always make merit with all their strength.

Tato mahāpadumapaccekabuddho taṃ ñatvā sesabhātare āmantesi. Mārisā nandiyarājā tumhe nimantesi. Adhivāsetha tassa nimantananti. Te adhivāsetvā tāvadeva ākāsenā gantvā uttaradvāre otariṃsu. Manussā pañcasatā deva paccekabuddhā āgatāti rañño ārocesuṃ, rājā saddhiṃ deviyā gantvā vanditvā patte gahetvā paccekabuddhe pāsādaṃ āropetvā tattha tesaṃ dānaṃ datvā bhattakiccāvasāne rājā saṅghattherassa devī saṅghanavakassa ca pādamūle nipajjitvā ayyā paccayehi na kilamissantu, mayaṃ puññena na hāyissāma, amhākaṃ idha vāsāya paṭiññaṃ dethāti paṭiññaṃ kāretvā uyyāne nivāsaṭṭhānādayo kāretvā yāvajīvaṃ paccakabuddhe upaṭṭhahitvā tesu parinibbutesu sādhukīḷanaṃ kāretvā candanā garuādīhi sarīrakiccaṃ kāretvā dhātuyo gahetvā cetiyaṃ patiṭṭhāpetvā evarūpānampi mahānubhāvānaṃ mahesīnaṃ maraṇaṃ bhavissati, kimaṅgaṃ pana mādisānanti saṃvegajāto jeṭṭhaputtaṃ rajje patiṭṭhāpetvā sayaṃ pabbajaṃ pabbaji, devīpi raññe pabbajite ahaṃ kiṃkarissāmīti pabbajitvā dvepi uyyāne vasantā jhānābhiññaṃ nibbattetvā jhānasukhena vītināmentā āyupariyosāne brahmaloke nibbattiṃsu. Te amhākaṃ bhagavato kāle brāhmaṇakule nibbattitvā buddhasāsane pabbajiṃsu, tadā nandiyarājā dhutaṅgadharānaṃ aggo mahākassapattheronāma hutvā cando viya suriyo viyaca loke pākaṭo hutvā bhagavati pari- nibbute [M 0.0052] buddhasāsanaṃ ativiya sobheti. Bhariyāpissa bhaddakāpiḷānī nāma ahosīti.

Then the Paccekabuddha Mahāpaduma, knowing this, addressed the other brothers: “Sirs, King Nandiya invites you. Consent to his invitation.” Having consented, they immediately traveled through the sky and descended at the northern gate. The people informed the king, “Your majesty, five hundred paccekabuddhas have arrived.” The king, together with the queen, went, paid homage, took their bowls, and led the paccekabuddhas up to the palace. There, he offered them almsfood. At the conclusion of the meal, the king prostrated himself at the feet of the eldest of the community and the queen at the feet of the youngest, saying, “Venerable sirs, may you not be troubled for requisites, and may we not fall away from merit. Give us your promise to reside here.” Having obtained their promise, he had dwelling places and so on prepared in the park and attended on the paccekabuddhas for as long as they lived. When they attained final Nibbāna, he had a fitting funeral ceremony performed, had the last rites for their bodies performed with sandalwood, aloe-wood, and so on, collected the relics, and established a cetiya. A sense of spiritual urgency arose in him as he reflected: “If death comes even to such great seers of mighty power, what then of those like me?” He installed his eldest son on the throne and himself went forth into the homeless life. The queen, too, thinking, “Now that the king has gone forth, what shall I do?” went forth. Both dwelling in the park, they developed the jhānas and the supernormal knowledges. Passing their time in the bliss of jhāna, at the end of their lifespan they were reborn in the Brahma-world. During the time of our Blessed One, they were reborn in a brahmin family and went forth into the Buddha’s Dispensation. At that time King Nandiya became the Elder Mahākassapa, the foremost of those who undertake the ascetic practices, and he became renowned in the world like the moon and the sun. After the Blessed One’s final Nibbāna, he greatly illumined the Buddha’s Dispensation. And his wife became the one named Bhaddā Kāpilānī.

16.

16.

Datvā pureko vipine caranto,

Paccekabuddhassanuvātamattaṃ;

Katvā saraṭṭhaṃ kurudīpasobhaṃ,

Mahānubhāvo vasudhā dhiposi.

Having formerly given a mere trifle to a Paccekabuddha wandering in the forest, you made your own kingdom as splendid as the Kuru continent and became a lord of the earth of great power.

17.

17.

Tumheca bhonto khalu sīlavante,

Dadātha dānāni anappakāni;

Taṃ vo patiṭṭhāca bhavantarasmiṃ,

Cintāmaṇiṃ kappataruṃva sāranti.

You, sirs, should indeed give abundant gifts to the virtuous. That will be your support in another existence, the very essence, like a wish-fulfilling gem and a celestial tree.

Nandiyarājassa vatthuṃ pathamaṃ.

The First Story of King Nandiya.

12. Aññataramanussassa vatthumhi ayamānupubbīkathā

12. In the story of a certain man, this is the sequential account:

Amhākaṃ bhagavati parinibbute pāṭaliputtasamīpe aññatarasmiṃ gāme aññataro duggatamanusso vasati, so panekadivasaṃ aññataraṃ gāmaṃ gacchanto dve sāṭake nivāsetvā mahantaṃ aṭaviṃpāpuṇi, tadevaṃ gacchantaṃ disvā etassa vatthaṃ gaṇhissāmīti eko coro anubandhi, so dūratova āgacchantaṃ coraṃ disvā cintesi, ahametasmā palāyituṃ vā tena saddhiṃyujjhituṃ vā na sakkomi, ayamāgantvā avassaṃ anicchantassāpi me vatthaṃ gaṇhissati. Mayāpissa niratthakena harituṃ na sakkā, dānavasenassa dassāmīti sanniṭṭhāna makāsi, atha coro āgantvā vatthakaṃ parāmasi, atha so puriso cittaṃ pasādetvā imaṃ mama vatthadānaṃ bhavabhogasukhatthāya paccayo hotūti vatthaṃ datvā ducchāditattā mahāmaggaṃ pahāya aññena jaṅghāmaggena gacchanto āsivisena daṭṭho kālaṃ katvā himavantappadese [M 0.0053] dvādasayojanike kanakavimāne nekaccharāsahassaparivuto nibbatti. Vimānaṃ panassa parivāretvā tiyojanike ṭhāne kapparukkhā nibbattiṃsu, so mahantaṃ dibbasampattiṃ disvā somanassaṃ pavedento āha.

After our Blessed One had attained final Nibbāna, in a certain village near Pāṭaliputta, there lived a poor man. One day, while going to another village, he put on two cloths and reached a great forest. Seeing him going along, a robber thought, “I will take his cloths,” and followed him. The poor man, seeing the robber approaching from afar, thought: “I cannot flee from him nor can I fight with him. When he comes, he will surely take my cloths even if I am unwilling. I cannot let him take them for nothing. I will give them to him as a gift.” Having made this resolution, when the robber came and grabbed his garment, the man, with a serene mind, gave him the cloth, saying: “May this gift of my cloth be a condition for happiness and enjoyment in future existences.” After giving the garment, because he was poorly clad, he abandoned the main road and took a footpath. There, he was bitten by a venomous snake and died. He was reborn in the Himavanta region in a golden celestial mansion twelve yojanas in size, surrounded by many thousands of celestial nymphs. Around his mansion, on a plot of ground three yojanas in extent, celestial trees arose. Seeing this great divine prosperity, he expressed his joy, saying:

1.

1.

Pariṇāmitamattena, dānassa sakasantakaṃ;

Dadāti vipulaṃ bhogaṃ, dibbamissariyaṃ varaṃ.

The mere dedication of a gift of one's own property yields abundant wealth and excellent divine sovereignty.

2.

2.

Dvādasayojanubbedhaṃ, duddikkhaṃ cakkhumūsanaṃ;

Kūṭā gāravarupetaṃ, sabbasovaṇṇayaṃ subhaṃ.

Twelve yojanas high, dazzling and blinding to the eye, endowed with an excellent gabled mansion, all of gold and splendid.

3.

3.

Mama puññena nibbattaṃ, nekarāgaddhajākulaṃ,

Tatheva parisuddhehi, vitānehi ca laṅkataṃ.

Produced by my merit, it is filled with banners of various colors and likewise adorned with pure canopies.

4.

4.

Pāsādapariyantamhi, dibbavatthāni lambare;

Vāteritā te sobhanti, avhentāva sudhāsino.

At the edge of the palace divine garments hang down; stirred by the wind, they shine, as if inviting the partakers of nectar.

5.

5.

Pāsādassa samantā me, bhūmibhāge tiyojane;

Icchiticchitadātāro, jātāsuṃ surapādapā.

All around my palace, on a plot of ground three yojanas in extent, celestial trees have sprung up, which grant whatever is wished for.

6.

6.

Tattha naccehi gītehi, vādehi turiyehi ca;

Ne kaccharāsahassehi, modāmi bhavane mama.

There, in my mansion, I delight with dances, songs, and instrumental music, together with many thousands of celestial nymphs.

7.

7.

Na sammā dinnavatthassa, akkhette phalamī disaṃ;

Khette sammā dadantassa, ko phalaṃ vaṇṇayissatīti.

The fruit of a gift not properly given in an unworthy field is not like this. But for one who gives properly in a worthy field, who can describe the fruit?

8.

8.

Evaṃ vidhampi kusalaṃ manujo karitvā,

Pappoti dibbavibhavaṃ munivaṇṇanīyaṃ;

Mantvāna bho dadatha dānavaraṃ susīle,

Saddhāya suddhamanasāssa visesabhāgīti.

Having done such a wholesome deed, a person attains divine splendor praised by the sages. Reflecting on this, sirs, give an excellent gift to the virtuous. With faith and a pure mind, you will become a partaker of its distinction.

Aññataramanussassa vatthuṃ dutiyaṃ.

The Second Story of a Certain Man.

13. Visamalomakumārassa vatthumhi ayamānupubbīkathā

13. This is the sequential account in the story of Prince Visamaloma.

Atīte [M 0.0054] kira imasmiṃ jambudīpe kassapo nāma sammāsambuddho pāramiyo pūretvā sabbaññutaṃ patto lokassa dukkhāpanudo sukhāvaho paṭivasati lokaṃ nibbāṇamahānagaravare paripūrento. Tasmiṃ samaye aññataro puriso satthu dhammadesanaṃ sutvā pasanno bhikkhusaṅghassa dānaṃ dento sīlaṃ rakkhanto uposathakammaṃ karonto nānāvidhāni puññakammāni katvā suttappabuddhoviya gantvā devaloke nibbatti sabbaratanamaye dibbavidhāne devaccharāsahassaparivuto. Tattha yāvatāyukaṃ ṭhatvā tato cuto amhākaṃ bhagavati parinibbute jambudīpe pāṭaliputtanagare āṇācakkavattidhammāsokamahānarindassa aggamahesiyā kucchimhi nibbatti. Tassa nāmaṃ karonto sīse lomaṃ visamaṃ hutvā jātatthā visamalomakumāroti sañjāniṃsu. So kamena viññutaṃ patto balasampanno ahosi. Mahāthāmo abhirūpoca ahosi. Dassanīyo pāsādiko yasaparivārasampanno paṭivasati. Tato aparena samayena dhammāsokamahānarindo caturāsītisahassarājaparivuto anantabalavāhano kī ḷā pa ro himavantaṃ gantvā yathābhirantaṃ kīḷitvā āgacchanto candabhāgaṃ nāma gaṅgaṃ sampāpuṇi. Sā pana yojanavitthatā tigāvutagambhīrā ahosi. Tadā sā adhunāgatehi oghehi mahāpheṇasamākulā bahūmiyo ubhokūle uttarantī mahāvegā mahāvegāgacchantī itisabbattha gacchanti. Tadā rājā gaṅgaṃ disvā ko nāmettha puriso evaṃvidhaṃ mahāgaṅgaṃ tarituṃ samattho bhavissatīti āha. Taṃ sutvā visamaloma kumāro āgantvā vanditvā ahaṃ deva gaṅgaṃ taritvā gantuñca āgantuñca sakkomīti āha. Rājā sādhūti sampaṭicchi. Atha kumāro gāḷhaṃ nivāsetvā makaradantiyā [M 0.0055] kese bandhitvā gaṅgākūle ṭhito aṭṭhārasahatthaṃ abbhuggantvā usabhamattaṭṭhāne patitvā taritu mārabhi. Tato caṇḍasotaṃ chinditvā taranto gamanā gamanakāle gaṇhanatthāya āgate caṇḍasuṃsumāre pāṇinā paharitvā cuṇṇavicuṇṇaṃ karonto vīsasataṃ māretvā uttāretvā talamuggamma rājānaṃ vanditvā aṭṭhāsi. Rājā taṃ kāraṇaṃ disvā bhayappatto eso kho maṃ māretvā rajjampi gahituṃ samattho. Etaṃ māretuṃ vaṭṭatīti cintetvā nagaraṃ sampatto kumāraṃ pakkosāpetvā amacce āha. Imaṃ bhaṇe bandhanāgāre karothāti. Te tathā kariṃsu, athassa bandhanāgāre vasantassa cattāro māsā atikkantā. Tato rājā catumāsaccayena dīghato saṭṭhihatthappamāṇe saṭṭhiveḷukalāpe āharāpetvā gaṇṭhiyo sodhāpetvā anto ayosāraṃ pūretvā rājaṅgaṇe ṭhapāpetvā visamalomakumāraṃ bandhanāgārato āhārāpetvā āṇāpetvā itisabbattha amacce evamāha. Bhaṇe svāyaṃ kumāro iminā khaggena ime veḷukalāpe caturaṅgulaṃ katvā chindatu. No ce chindituṃ sakkoti. Taṃ mārethāti āha, taṃ sutvā kumāro ahaṃ bandhanāgāre ci ra vu ttho jighacchāpīḷito āhārena kilamiṃ, yannūnāhaṃ āhāraṃ bhuñcitvā chindeyyanti. Te natthi dāni tuyhaṃ āhāranti āhaṃsu. Tenahi pokkharaṇiyā pānīyaṃ pivissāmīti āha. Te sādhūti pokkharaṇiṃ nesuṃ. Kumāro pokkharaṇiṃ otaritvā nahāyitvā nimuggo yāvadatthaṃ kalalaṃ bhuñcitvā pānīyaṃ pivitvā uṭṭhāya asipattaṃ gahetvā mahājanānaṃ mahājanānaṃpassantameva itisabbattha passantānameva aṭṭhāsītihatthaṭṭhānaṃ ākāsaṃ ullaṅghitvā sabbaveḷukalāpe caturaṅgulamattena khaṇḍākhaṇḍaṃ kurumāno otaritvā mūle thūlaayasalākaṃ patvā kiṇīti saddaṃ sutvā asipattaṃ vissajjetvā rodamāno aṭṭhāsi. Tato rājapurisehi kimattaṃ rodasīti vutte [M 0.0056] ettakānaṃ purisānamantare mayhaṃ ekopi suhado natti. Sace bhaveyya, imesaṃ veḷukalāpānamantare ayo sāraṃ atthibhāvaṃ katheyya, ahaṃ pana jānamāno ime veḷu kalāpe aṅgulaṅgulesu chindeyyanti āha. Tato rājā kumārena katakammaṃ oloketvā pasanno uparājaṭṭhānaṃ bahuñca vibhavaṃ dāpesi, evamassa balasampattilābho nāma na jātigottakulapadesādīnaṃ balaṃ. Na pāṇātipātādiduccaritānaṃ balaṃ.
