Kassetaṃ balanti. Kassapasammāsambuddhakāle bhikkhusaṅghassa dinnadānādisucaritakammavipākaṃ. Tena vuttaṃ.

In the past, it is said, in this Jambudīpa, there dwelt a Perfectly Self-Enlightened Buddha named Kassapa, who had fulfilled the perfections, attained omniscience, and was a remover of suffering and bringer of happiness to the world, thereby filling the world with the supreme great city of Nibbāna. At that time, a certain man, having heard the Teacher’s Dhamma discourse, became faithful. He gave alms to the community of monks, observed moral precepts, kept the Uposatha, and performed various meritorious deeds. Like one awakened from sleep, he was reborn in the heavenly world, in a divine palace made of all kinds of jewels, surrounded by thousands of celestial nymphs. Having lived there for the full lifespan, he passed away from that state and, after the Parinibbāna of our Blessed One, was reborn in the womb of the chief queen of King Dhammāsoka, the righteous wheel-turning monarch of Pāṭaliputta in Jambudīpa. When they named him, because the hair on his head had grown unevenly, they called him Prince Uneven-Hair. Gradually, he grew wise and strong, becoming a great and handsome man, pleasing to behold, surrounded by glory and retinue. Later, King Dhammāsoka, accompanied by eighty-four thousand kings, with an endless army, went to the Himalayas for sport. After enjoying himself as he wished, he returned and reached the river Candabhāgā. This river was a yojana wide and three gāvutas deep. At that time, swollen by recent floods, it was filled with great foam, overflowing both banks, rushing with tremendous force. Seeing the river, the king said, 'Who here is capable of crossing such a mighty river?' Hearing this, Prince Uneven-Hair approached, paid homage, and said, 'Great king, I am able to cross the river and return.' The king approved, saying, 'Good.' Then the prince tightly fastened his clothes, tied his hair with a makara-tooth clasp, stood on the riverbank, leaped eighteen cubits high, landed like a bull, and began to swim. Cutting through the fierce current, as he swam, he struck with his hand the fierce crocodiles that came to seize him during his going and coming, shattering them into pieces, killing two hundred of them. Having crossed, he emerged from the water, paid homage to the king, and stood by. Seeing this deed, the king was terrified, thinking, 'This man could kill me and seize the kingdom. It is fitting to kill him.' Upon returning to the city, he summoned the prince and told his ministers, 'Throw him into prison.' They did so. Four months passed while he remained imprisoned. Then the king, after the four months had elapsed, had sixty bundles of bamboo brought, each sixty cubits long. He had the knots removed, filled them with iron cores, and placed them in the royal courtyard. He then had Prince Uneven-Hair brought from prison and told his ministers, 'Let this prince cut these bamboo bundles with this sword, making four-finger-sized pieces. If he cannot cut them, execute him.' Hearing this, the prince said, 'I have been long imprisoned, afflicted by hunger, exhausted by lack of food. Would that I might eat food and then cut them.' They replied, 'You shall have no food now.' 'Then I will drink water from the lotus pond,' he said. They agreed and led him to the pond. The prince entered the pond, bathed, submerged, ate his fill of mud, drank water, then rose, took the sword, and—before the eyes of the great multitude—leaped into the air to a height of eighty-eight cubits. Cutting all the bamboo bundles into four-finger-sized pieces, he descended. Striking the thick iron rod at the base, he heard a 'kiṇīti' sound, threw the sword away, and stood weeping. When asked by the king’s men, 'Why do you weep?', he replied, 'Among so many men, not one is my friend. If there were, he would have told me that these bamboo bundles had iron cores within them. Knowing that, I would have cut these bamboo bundles at every finger’s length.' Then the king, seeing the deed performed by the prince, was pleased and appointed him to the position of viceroy, granting him much wealth. Thus, his attainment of strength was not due to the power of birth, clan, lineage, or region, nor to the power of evil deeds such as killing living beings. Whose strength was this? It was the result of good deeds such as giving alms to the community of monks during the time of the Perfectly Self-Enlightened Buddha Kassapa. Thus it is said.

1.

1.

Kassapassa munindassa, kāle aññataro naro;

Sambuddhamupasaṃkamma, sutvā dhammaṃ sudesitaṃ.

In the time of Kassapa, the lord of sages, a certain man approached the Perfectly Self-Enlightened One, having heard the well-taught Dhamma.

2.

2.

Paṭiladdhasaddho hutvā, sīlavantāna bhikkhunaṃ;

Madhurannapāne pacure, adāsi sumano tadā.

Having acquired faith, with a gladdened heart, he then gave abundant sweet food and drink to the virtuous monks.

3.

3.

Adāsi cīvare patte, tatheva kāyabandhane;

Adā khīrasalākañca, bahū kattarayaṭṭhiyo.

He gave robes and bowls, likewise waistbands; he gave tickets for milk-rice and many knife handles.

4.

4.

Adā supassayaṃ dānaṃ, mañcapīṭhādikaṃ tathā;

Pāvāra kambalādīni, adā sītanivāraṇe.

He gave gifts of fine bedding, likewise couches and chairs; he gave cloaks, woolen blankets, and other things for protection against the cold.

5.

5.

Adā bhesajjadānāni, ārogyatthāya bhikkhunaṃ;

Evaṃ nānāvidhaṃ puññaṃ, katvāna tidivaṃ gato.

He gave gifts of medicine for the health of the monks; having thus performed various kinds of merit, he went to the heaven of the Thirty-Three.

6.

6.

Tattha dibbavimānamhi, uppanno so mahiddhiko;

Devaccharāparivuto, devasenāpurakkhato.

There, in a divine mansion, he was reborn, mighty; surrounded by celestial nymphs, escorted by hosts of deities.

7.

7.

Dibbehi naccagītehi, dibbavāditatantihi;

Modamāno anekehi, dibbasampattiyā saha.

With celestial dances and songs, with divine music and stringed instruments, rejoicing with many divine accomplishments.

8.

8.

Yāvatāyuṃ tahiṃ ṭhatvā, jambudīpe manorame;

Pure pāṭaliputtamhi, dhammāsokassa rājino.

Having lived there for the full lifespan, in delightful Jambudīpa, in the city of Pāṭaliputta, of King Dhammāsoka.

9.

9.

Putto [M 0.0057] hutvāna nibbatti, mahāthāmo mahābalo;

Mahāyaso mahābhogo, āsi buddhādimāmako.

He was reborn as a son, of great strength and mighty power, of great fame and great wealth, a devotee of the Buddha.

10.

10.

Kātabbaṃ kusalaṃ tasmā, bhavasampatti micchatā;

Pāletabba matho sīlaṃ, bhāvetabbañca bhāvananti.

Therefore, one should do what is wholesome, desiring prosperity in existence. Moreover, one should guard morality and cultivate mental development.

Tato kumāro uparājaṭṭhānaṃ labhitvā sampattiṃanubhavamāno moggaliputtatissattheramādiṃ katvā mahābhikkhusaṅghassa cīvarapiṇḍapātasenāsanagilānapaccayadānā divasena sakkāraṃ katvā sīlaṃ rakkhitvā uposathakammaṃ katvā āyupariyosāne yathākammaṃ gatoti.

Then the prince, having obtained the position of viceroy and enjoying prosperity, honoring the great community of monks, headed by the Elder Moggaliputtatissa, with robes, almsfood, lodging, and medicine as daily offerings, keeping the precepts, and performing the Uposatha observance, at the end of his life, went according to his deeds.

11.

11.

Evaṃvidhaṃ sucaritaṃ sumano karitvā,

Bhāgissa nekavibhavassa bhavābhavesu;

Tumhepi bho sucaritaṃ vibhavānurūpaṃ,

Katvāna nibbutipadaṃ karagaṃ karothāti.

Having performed such good conduct with a joyful mind, may one be a partaker of various prosperity in various existences. You too, O friends, should perform good conduct in accordance with your means, and having done so, make the state of Nibbāna your own.

Visamalomakumārassa vatthuṃ tatiyaṃ.

The story of Prince Uneven-Hair, the third.

14. Kañcanadeviyā vatthumhi ayamānupubbīkathā

14. This is the preliminary discourse on the story of Kañcanadevī.

Jambudīpe kira devaputtanagaraṃ nāma dassanīyaṃ ekaṃ nagaraṃ ahosi. Tasmiṃ samaye manussā yebhuyyena pattamahaṃ nāma pūjaṃ karonti, bhagavatā paribhuttapattaṃ gahetvā katānekapūjāvidhānā ussavaṃ karonti. Taṃ pattamahanti vuccati. Tasmiṃ samaye devaputtanagare rājā sabbaratanamayaṃ rathaṃ sabbālaṅkārehi alaṅkārāpetvā kumudapattavaṇṇe cattāro sindhave yojetvā susikkhitasippācariyehi sattaratanapariniṭṭhite asītihatthaveḷagge satthunā paribhuttaṃ selamayapattaṃ muttājālādīhi alaṅkaritvā veḷaggaṃ āropetvā veḷuṃ [M 0.0058] rathe ṭhapāpetvā nagaraṃ devanagaraṃ viya alaṅkaritvā dhajapatākādayo ussāpetvā toraṇaggha kapantiyoca puṇṇaghaṭadīpamālādayoca patiṭṭhāpetvā anekehi pūjāvidhānehi nagaraṃ padakkhiṇaṃ kāretvā nagaramajjhe susajjitaratanamaṇḍape pattadhātuṃ ṭhapetvā sattame divase mahādhammasavaṇaṃ kārāpesi. Tadā tasmiṃ janapade bahū manussāca devatāca yakkharakkhasanāgasupaṇṇādayoca manussavesena yebhuyyena taṃ samāgamaṃ otaranti, evamacchariyaṃ taṃ pūjāvidhānaṃ ahosi.

In Jambudīpa, it is said, there was a beautiful city named Devaputtanagara. At that time, people for the most part performed a ceremony called the Bowl Festival, making various offerings and holding a festival with the bowl used by the Blessed One. This is called the Bowl Festival. At that time, in the city of Devaputtanagara, the king had a chariot made entirely of jewels and adorned with all kinds of ornaments. He yoked it with four Sindh horses the color of water lily petals and had a bamboo pole eighty cubits high, finished with the seven kinds of gems, prepared by skilled artisans. He had the stone bowl used by the Teacher adorned with nets of pearls and other decorations and placed on top of the pole, and had the pole placed on the chariot. The city was adorned like a city of the devas, with banners and flags raised, and with arched gateways, rows of lamps, and full water pots set up. After having the bowl-relic circumambulate the city with many kinds of offerings, he had it placed in a splendid jeweled pavilion in the city center. On the seventh day, he arranged for a great hearing of the Dhamma. At that time, many humans and deities—yakkhas, rakkhasas, nāgas, supaṇṇas, and others—mostly in human guise, descended to that assembly. So marvelous was that ceremony.

Tadā eko nāgarājā uttamarūpadharaṃ agatapubbapurisaṃ ekaṃ kumārikaṃ dhammaparisantare nisinnaṃ disvā tassā paṭibaddhacitto anekākārehi taṃ yā ci tvā tassā aladdhamāno kujjhitvā nāsāvātaṃ vissajjesi imaṃ māressāmīti. Taṃ tassā saddhābalena kiñci upaddavaṃ kātuṃ samattho nāhosi. Athassā nāgo pādato paṭṭhāya yāvasakalasarīraṃ bhogena veṭhetvā sīse phaṇaṃ katvā bhāyāpento aṭṭhāsi. Anaññavihitāya tāya dhammasavaṇabalena aṇumattampi dukkhaṃ nāhosi. Pabhātāya rattiyā taṃ disvā manussā kimetanti kāraṇaṃ pucchiṃsu, sāpi tesaṃ kathetvā evaṃ saccakiriyamakāsi. Tathāhi.

Then a certain Nāga king, seeing a maiden of supreme and unprecedented beauty seated within the Dhamma assembly, became infatuated with her. Having beseeched her in many ways and failing to win her, he grew angry and released a blast of wind from his nostrils, thinking, 'I will kill her!' But due to the power of her faith, he was unable to cause her any harm. Then the Nāga, coiling his body around her from her feet up to her entire frame and making a hood over her head, stood there to frighten her. Yet for her, whose mind was undistracted, through the power of hearing the Dhamma there was not even the slightest suffering. When the night had dawned, people saw her and asked, 'What is this?' She explained the reason to them and then made this act of truth. Thus:

1.

1.

Brahmacārī ahosāhaṃ, sañjātā idha mānuse;

Tena saccena maṃ nāgo, khippameva pamuñcatu.

I have lived the holy life, born here among humans;

By this truth, may the Nāga quickly release me.

2.

2.

Kāmāturassa nāgassa, nokāsamakariṃyato;

Tena saccena maṃ nāgo, khippameva muñcatu.

Since I gave no opportunity to the Nāga afflicted by lust;

By this truth, may the Nāga quickly release me.

3.

3.

Visavātena khittassa, kupitassoragassahaṃ;

Akuddhā tena saccena, so maṃ khippaṃ pamuñcatu.

Though he was an angry serpent who had released a poison-wind, I was not angry with him;

By this truth, may he quickly release me.

4.

4.

Saddhammaṃ suṇamānāhaṃ, garugāravabhattiyā;

Assosiṃ tena saccena, khippaṃ nāgo pamuñcatu.

I listened to the good Dhamma with deep respect and devotion;

By this truth, may the Nāga quickly release me.

5.

5.

Akkharaṃvā [M 0.0059] padaṃvāpi, avināsetvāva ādito;

Assosiṃ tena saccena, khippaṃ nāgo pamuñcatūti.

Without losing a single letter or word from the beginning, I listened;

By this truth, may the Nāga quickly release me.

Saccakiriyāvasāne nāgarājā tassā atīva pasanno bhogaṃ viniveṭhetvā phaṇasataṃ māpetvā taṃ phaṇagabbhe nisīdāpetvā bahūhi nāgamānavakehi saddhiṃ udakapūjaṃ nāma pūja makāsi, taṃ disvā bahū nagaravāsino acchariyabbhutajātā aṭṭhārasakoṭidhanena pūja makaṃsu. Tathāhi.

At the conclusion of the act of truth, the Nāga king, being exceedingly pleased with her, unwound his coils, magically created a hundred hoods, and having seated her in the chamber of hoods, he performed an offering called the 'water-offering' together with many young Nāgas. Seeing this, many city-dwellers, filled with wonder and amazement, made an offering with wealth of eighteen crores. Thus:

6.

6.

Natthi saddhāsamo loke, suhado sabbakāmado;

Passathassā balaṃ saddhā, pūjentevaṃ naro ragā.

There is no friend in the world like faith, which grants all one’s wishes.

Behold the power of her faith: thus do humans and serpents worship.

7.

7.

Idha lokeva sālattha, bhavabhoga manappakaṃ;

Tasmā saddhena kātabbaṃ, ratanattayagāravanti.

Right here in this world she obtained abundant worldly enjoyments.

Therefore, one should act with faith and reverence for the Three Jewels.

Athevaṃ sā paṭiladdhamahāvibhavā yāvajīvaṃ komāriya brahmacāriṇī hutvā āyupariyosāne kālaṃ katvā tasmiṃyeva nagare rañño aggamahesiyā kucchimhi paṭisandhiṃ gaṇhitvā dasamāsaccayena mātukucchito nikkhami. Nikkhantadivase panassā sakaladevaputtanagare ratanavassaṃ vassi. Tenassā kañjanadevīti nāmaṃ kariṃsu. Samantapāsādikā ahosi. Abhirūpā devaccharapaṭibhāgā. Mukhato uppalagandho vāyati. Sarīrato candanagandho vāyati. Sakalasarīrato bālasuriyo viya raṃsiyo nicchārentī caturatanagabbhe padīpakiccaṃ nāma natthi. Sabbo gabbho sarīrā lokena eko bhāso hoti, tassā rūpasampatti sakalajambudīpe pākaṭā ahosi. Tato sakalajambudīpavāsī rājāno tassā atthāya piturañño paṇṇākārāni pahiṇiṃsu. Sā pana pañcakāme ananulittā pitaraṃ anujānāpetvā bhikkhunū passayaṃ gantvā pabbajitvā vipassanaṃ vaḍḍhetvā saha paṭisambhidāhi arahattaṃ pāpuṇīti.

Thus, having obtained great prosperity, she lived her entire life as a celibate maiden. At the end of her life, she passed away and took rebirth in the womb of the chief queen of the king in that same city. After ten months, she emerged from her mother’s womb. On the day she emerged, a rain of jewels fell over the entire city of Devaputtanagara. Therefore, they named her Kañcanadevī. She was lovely to behold from every side, beautiful, a counterpart to a celestial nymph. The fragrance of blue lotuses wafted from her mouth, and the scent of sandalwood from her body. Emitting rays from her entire body like the morning sun, there was no need for a lamp in her four-jeweled chamber; the whole chamber was a single radiance with the light from her body. The perfection of her beauty became renowned throughout all of Jambudīpa. Then the kings dwelling in all of Jambudīpa sent gifts to her father, the king, for her sake. But she, unattached to the five cords of sensual pleasure, obtained her father's permission, went to a nunnery, and went forth. Having developed insight, she attained Arahantship together with the analytical knowledges.

8.

8.

Sutvāna [M 0.0060] sādaravasena kumārikevaṃ;

Dhammañhi sīlamamalaṃ paripālayantī;

Laddhāna nekavibhavaṃ vibhavaṃ payātā,

Mā bho pamajjatha sadā kusalappayogeti.

Having thus listened with respect, the maiden,

while protecting the stainless Dhamma and virtue,

obtained manifold prosperity and then attained to the state without becoming.

Sirs, do not be heedless! Always apply yourselves to what is skillful.

Kañcanadeviyā vatthuṃ catutthaṃ.

The Story of Kañcanadevī, the Fourth.

15. Byagghassa vatthumhi ayamānupubbīkathā

15. This is the connected story concerning the tiger.

Jambudīpe cūlaraṭṭhā sanne bārāṇasīnagare ekaṃ paṃsupabbataṃ vinivijjhitvā mahāmaggo hoti, tattha vemajjhe eko byaggho attano andhapitaraṃ pabbataguhāyaṃ katvā posento vasati. Tasseva pabbatassa vanadvāre tuṇḍilo nāma eko suvapotako rukkhasmiṃ vasati. Te ubhopi aññamaññaṃ piyasahāyā ahesuṃ, tasmiṃ samaye paccantagāmavāsī eko manusso attano mātugāmena saddhiṃ kalahaṃ katvā bārāṇasiṃ gacchanto taṃ vanadvāraṃ sampāpuṇi. Atha suvapotako parihīnattabhāvaṃ dukkhitaṃ taṃ disvā kampamānahadayo taṃ pakkositvā bho kuhiṃ gacchasīti āha, tena parakhaṇḍaṃ gacchāmīti vutte tuṇḍilo bho imasmiṃ vanakhaṇḍamajjhe eko byaggho vasati. Kakkhalo pharuso sampattasampatte māretvā khādati. Mā tvaṃ tena gacchāti āha. Svāyaṃ dubbhago manusso hitakāmassa tassa vacanaṃ anādiyitvā gacchāmevāti āha. Tuṇḍilo tenahi samma yadi anivattamāno gacchasi. Eso byaggho mama sahāyo. Me vacanaṃ tava santikā sutvā na gaṇhātīti. Tassa taṃ anādiyanto so suvarāje paduṭṭhacitto muggarena paharitvā māretvā araṇiṃ aggiṃ katvā maṃsaṃ khādi. Asappurisasaṃsaggo nāme sa idha lokaparalokesu dukkhāvahoyeva. Tathāhi.

In Jambudīpa, in a small country near the city of Bārāṇasī, a great road passed through a sandy mountain. There, in the middle of it, a tiger lived, supporting his blind father whom he kept in a mountain cave. At the entrance to that same mountain's forest, a young parrot named Tuṇḍila lived in a tree. The two were dear friends. At that time, a man from a border village, having quarreled with his wife, was traveling to Bārāṇasī and reached that forest entrance. Seeing that he was dejected and miserable, the young parrot, with a trembling heart, called out to him, saying: “Sir, where are you going?” When he replied, “I am going to another region,” Tuṇḍila said: “Friend, in the middle of this forest lives a tiger—harsh and cruel, he kills and devours whomever he meets. Do not go that way.” But that unfortunate man, disregarding the words of one who wished him well, said: “I will go anyway.” Tuṇḍilo then said: “Well then, friend, if you go without turning back, that tiger is my friend. When he hears my word from you, he will not seize you.” But the man, disregarding this, with a mind full of malice toward the king of parrots, struck him with a club and killed him, made a fire with a fire-drill, and ate his flesh. Indeed, association with a bad person brings suffering both in this world and in the next. For so it is said:

1.

1.

Mayā [M 0.0061] kataṃ mayhamidaṃ, iti vessānaraṃ naro;

Samāliṅgati sappemo, dahatevassa viggahaṃ.

“‘This was done by me, this is mine’:

Thus with affection a man embraces fire,

And it is his own body that it burns.

2.

2.

Madhukhīrādidānena, pemasā paripālito;

Sorago kupitovassa, ḍasatevassa viggahaṃ.

Though lovingly nurtured with gifts of honey and milk,

an angry serpent bites its benefactor's very body.

3.

3.

Evaṃ nihīnajaccena, pāpena akataññunā;

Narā dhamena dīnena, katopi khaṇasaṅgamo.

Thus, an association, even for a moment, has been made

with a person of low birth, evil, ungrateful, and of wretched character.

4.

4.

Asādhuko ayaṃtevaṃ, jānamānena jantunā;

Muhuttampi na kātabbo, saṅgamo so anatthadoti.

A being who knows, ‘This person is not good,’

should not form an association with him even for a moment, for it is harmful.

Tato so asappuriso maṃsaṃ khāditvā gacchanto vanakhaṇḍamajjhaṃ sampāpuṇi. Atha byaggho taṃ disvā mahānādaṃ karonto gahaṇatthāya uṭṭhāsi. So byagghaṃ disvā bhayappatto tuṇḍilassa vacanaṃ saritvā ahaṃ bho tava sahāyatuṇḍilassa santikā āgatomhīti āha, taṃ sutvā byaggho attamano ehi sammāti taṃ pakkositvā attano vasanaṭṭhānaṃ netvā khāditabbāhārena taṃ santappetvā pitusantike nisīdāpetvā puna vanakhaṇḍa magamāsi. Athassa pitā puttassa gatakāle tena saddhiṃ sallapanto tassa vacanānusārena tuṇḍilaṃ māretvā khāditabhāvaṃ aññāsi. Tato so puttassa āgatakāle tava sahāyo tena māritoti āha. Taṃ sutvā byaggho anattamano vegena tassa vasanaṭṭhānaṃ gantvā samma tuṇḍilāti saddaṃ katvā apassanto luñcitapattaṃcassa disvā nissaṃsayaṃ tena mārito me sahāyoti socanto paridevanto āgañchi. Atha so asappuriso tasmiṃ tattha gate tassa pitaraṃ pāsāṇena paharitvā māretvā byagghaṃca dāni māressāmīti byagghāgamanamaggaṃ olokento nilīno aṭṭhāsi. Tasmiṃ khaṇe byagghopi āgañchi. So tassā gatakāle tassa tejena bhīto gantvā [M 0.0062] jīvitaṃ me sāmi dehīti pādamūle urena nipajji, byaggho pana tena katakammaṃ disvā tasmiṃ cittaṃ nibbāpetvā mama sahāyassa sāsanamādāyāgatassa dubbhituṃ na yuttanti cintento taṃ samassāsetvā gaccha sammāti sukhaṃ pesesi. Evañhi sappurisasamāgamo nāma idha lokaparalokesu sukhāvahoyeva, vuttaṃhi.

Then that wicked man, having eaten the flesh, went on and reached the middle of the forest. Seeing him, the tiger roared loudly and rose up to seize him. But the man, seeing the tiger, was struck with fear. Remembering Tuṇḍila’s words, he said: “Sir, I have come from your friend Tuṇḍila.” Hearing this, the tiger was delighted. Inviting him with the words, “Come, friend,” he led him to his own dwelling, satisfied him with food, and had him sit near his father. Then he went back into the forest. Now, while the tiger was away, his father conversed with the man and understood from his speech that he had killed and eaten Tuṇḍila. So when his son returned, he said: “Your friend was killed by that man.” Hearing this, the tiger was displeased and quickly went to Tuṇḍila’s dwelling place. He called out, “Friend Tuṇḍila!” but did not see him. Seeing his plucked feathers scattered about, he returned, grieving and lamenting, certain that “My friend has been killed by him!” Meanwhile, after the tiger had left, that wicked man struck the tiger’s father with a stone and killed him. Thinking, “Now I will kill the tiger too,” he hid and waited, watching the path for the tiger’s return. At that moment, the tiger arrived. When he approached, the man, terrified by his ferocity, fell at his feet, prostrating himself, and pleaded: “My lord, grant me my life!” But the tiger, seeing what the man had done, extinguished his anger towards him. Thinking, “It is not right for me to harm one who came with a message from my friend,” he reassured the man, said, “Go, friend,” and sent him away in peace. Thus, association with the virtuous brings happiness both in this world and in the next. As it has been said:

5.

5.

Sabbhireva samāsetha, sabbhi kubbetha santhavaṃ;

Sabbattha santhavo tena, seyyo hoti na pāpiyo.

Associate only with the good; make friendship only with the good. In every way, association with them is better, never worse.

6.

6.

Sukhāvaho dukkhanudo, sadā sabbhi samāgamo;

Tasmā sappuriseheva, saṅgamo hotu jantunaṃ.

Association with the good always brings happiness and removes suffering; therefore, may beings associate only with the virtuous.

Tato so byaggho tena mettacittānubhāvena kālaṃ katvā sagge nibbattoti.

Then that tiger, through the influence of that mind of loving-kindness, died and was reborn in heaven.

7.

7.

Evaṃvidhopi pharuso paramaṃsabhojī;

Byaggho dayāyupagato sugatiṃ sumedho;

Tasmā karotha karuṇaṃ satataṃ janānaṃ,

Taṃ vo dadāti vibhavañca bhavesu bhoganti.

Even one so fierce, a voracious eater of flesh, the wise tiger, attaining compassion, reached a happy state. Therefore, constantly show compassion to all beings; it will grant you prosperity and enjoyments in future lives.

Byagghassa vatthuṃ pañcamaṃ.

The Fifth Story: The Tiger.

16. Phalakakhaṇḍadinnassa vatthumhi ayamānupubbīkathā

16. This is the sequential account in the story of the gift of a piece of a plank.

Sāvatthiyaṃ kireko manusso uttarāpathaṃ gacchāmīti addhānamaggapaṭipanno gimhānamāse majjhaṇhe bahalātapena kilanto hutvā rukkhacchāyaṃ pavisitvā tambulaṃ khādanto phalake nisīdi. Atha uttarāpathenāgacchānto eko tatheva ātapena kilanto āgantvā purimassa santike nisīditvā bho pānīyaṃ atthīti pucchi. Itaro pānīyaṃ natthīti āha. Athassa [M 0.0063] so mayhampi bho tambulaṃ dehi pipāsitomhiti vatvāpi na labhi. Catukahāpaṇena ekaṃ tambulapaṇṇaṃ kiṇitvā laddho tattheva nisīditvā khāditvā pipāsaṃ vinodetvā tena upakārena tassa sinehaṃ katvā attano gamanaṭṭhāna magamāsi, athā parabhāge so paṭṭanaṃ gantvā nāvāya vaṇijjatthāya gacchanto samuddamajjhaṃ pāpuṇi. Tato sattame divase nāvā bhijji. Manussā macchakacchapānaṃ bhakkhā jātā. So eva puriso arogo hutvā ekaṃ phalakakhaṇḍaṃ ure katvā samuddaṃ tarati. Athe taropi tatheva nāvāya bhinnāya seso hutvā samuddaṃ taranto purimena samāgami. Atha te sattadivasaṃ samudde tarantā aññamaññaṃ sañjāniṃsu. Tesu kahāpaṇe datvā tambulaṃ gahito ekaṃ phalakakhaṇḍaṃ ure katvā tarati. Itarassetaṃ natthi. Atha so kahāpaṇe gahetvā dinnatambulamattasso pakāraṃ saritvā attano phalakakhaṇḍaṃ tassa adāsi. So tasmiṃ sayitvā taranto taṃ pahāya agamāsi, aparo anādhārakena taranto ossaṭṭhaviriyo udake osīditumārati. Tasmiṃ khaṇe samudde adhivatthā maṇimekhalā nāma devadhītā osīdantaṃ taṃ disvā sappurisoti tassa guṇānussarantī vegenā gantvā taṃ attano ānubhāvena samuddatīraṃ pāpesi. Itaraṃpi sā etasseva guṇānubhāvena tīraṃ pāpesi. Atha phalakenotiṇṇapuriso taṃ disvā vimhito kathaṃ purato ahosi sammāti pucchi. So na jānāmi. Apica kho sukheneva tīraṃ pattosmīti āha. Atha devadhītā dissamānakasarīreneva attanā ānītabhāvaṃ ārocentī āha.

At Sāvatthī, it is said, a man, thinking, 'I will go to the northern region,' set out on a journey. During the hot season, at midday, exhausted by the intense heat, he entered the shade of a tree and sat on a plank, chewing betel. Then another man, also exhausted by the heat, coming from the northern region, sat near the first man and asked, 'Friend, do you have any drinking water?' The other replied, 'There is no drinking water.' Then he said, 'Friend, give me some betel, for I am thirsty,' but he did not receive any. Having bought one betel leaf from him for four kahāpanas, he obtained it, sat right there, chewed it, quenched his thirst, and out of gratitude for that favor, developed an affection for that man and went on his way. Later, that first man went to a port and embarked on a ship for trade. In the middle of the ocean, on the seventh day, the ship broke apart. The people became food for fish and turtles. But that very man, unharmed, placed a piece of a plank on his chest and floated on the sea. The other man, also a survivor of the shipwreck, met the first man while floating on the sea. Then, after seven days at sea, they recognized each other. The first man was holding onto a piece of a plank and floating; the other had none. Then the first man, remembering the small favor of the betel he had sold, gave his own piece of a plank to the other. The second man, lying on it, floated away, leaving him behind. The first man, now without support, his energy depleted, began to sink in the water. At that moment, the goddess Maṇimekhalā, who dwells in the ocean, saw him sinking and, recollecting his virtue as a good man, swiftly went to him and by her power brought him to the shore. By the power of that same man's virtue, she also brought the other to the shore. Then the man who had landed with the plank, seeing him, was astonished and asked, 'Friend, how did you arrive before me?' He replied, 'I do not know. But I reached the shore with ease.' Then the goddess, making her body visible, announced that she had brought them there, and she said:

1.

1.

Yo mātaraṃ pitaraṃvā, dhammena idha posati;

Rakkhanti taṃ sadā devā, samudde vā thalepi vā.

Whoever here righteously supports his mother or father, the deities always protect him, whether at sea or on land.

2.

2.

Yo ce buddhañca dhammañca, saṃghañca saraṇaṃ gato;

Rakkhanti taṃ sadā devā, samudde vā thalepi vā.

Whoever has gone for refuge to the Buddha, the Dhamma, and the Sangha, the deities always protect him, whether at sea or on land.

3.

3.

Pañcavidhaṃ [M 0.0064] aṭṭhavidhaṃ, pātimokkhañca saṃvaraṃ;

Pāleti yo taṃ pālenti, devā sabbattha sabbadā.

Whoever observes the fivefold and eightfold precepts and the restraint of the Pātimokkha, the deities guard him everywhere and always.

4.

4.

Kāyena vācā manasā, sucarittaṃ caratī dha yo;

Pālenti taṃ sadā devā, samudde vā thalepi vā.

Whoever here practices good conduct by body, speech, and mind, the deities always guard him, whether at sea or on land.

5.

5.

Yo sappurisadhammesu, ṭhito dha katavediko;

Pālenti taṃ sadā devā, samudde vā thalepi vā.

Whoever here stands firm in the qualities of a good person and is grateful, the deities always guard him, whether at sea or on land.

Tato so āha.

Then he said:

6.

6.

Neva dānaṃ adāsāhaṃ, na sīlaṃ paripālayiṃ;

Kena me puññakammena, mamaṃ rakkhanti devatā;

Pucchāmi saṃsayaṃ tuyhaṃ, taṃ me akkhāhi devateti.

“I have given no gift, nor have I observed moral virtue. By what meritorious deed of mine do the deities protect me? I ask you about this doubt of mine; please explain it to me, O deity.”

Devatā āha.

The deity said:

7.

7.

Agādhā pārage bhīme, sāgare duritā kare;

Bhinnanāvo taranto tvaṃ, hadaye katvā kaliṅgaraṃ.

“In the unfathomable, fearful ocean, difficult to cross, a source of peril; with your ship broken, you were crossing, holding a plank to your chest.

8.

8.

Ṭhatvā sappurise dhamme, attāna manavekkhiya;

Khaṇasanthavassa purisassa, adāsi phalakaṃ sakaṃ.

Established in the qualities of a good person, without regard for yourself, you gave your own plank to a man of brief acquaintance.

9.

9.

Taṃ tuyhaṃ mittadhammañca, dānañca phalakassa te;

Patiṭṭhāsi samuddasmiṃ, evaṃ jānāhi mārisāti.

That friendly deed of yours, and your gift of the plank, became your support in the ocean. Know this, good sir.”

Evañca vatvā sā te dibbāhārena santappetvā dibbavatthālaṅkārehi alaṅkaritvā attano ānubhāvena sāvatthinagareyeva te patiṭṭhāpesi. Tato paṭṭhāya tameva ārammaṇaṃ katvā te dānaṃ dadantā sīlaṃ rakkhantā uposathakammaṃ karontā āyupariyosāne saggaparāyaṇā ahesuṃ.

Having spoken thus, she satisfied them with divine food, adorned them with divine garments and ornaments, and by her own power established them right in the city of Sāvatthī. From then on, taking that incident as their inspiration, they gave gifts, observed the moral precepts, undertook the observance of the Uposatha, and at the end of their lives, they had heaven as their destination.

10.

10.

Evaṃ parittakusalenapi sāgarasmiṃ,

Sattā labhanti saraṇaṃ khalu devatāhi;

Tumhepi sappurisataṃ na vināsayantā,

Mā bho pamajjatha sadā kusalappayogeti.

Thus, even with a little wholesome action, beings find refuge from the deities in the ocean. You too, without destroying your own good character, should never be heedless, friends, but always apply yourselves to what is wholesome.

Phalakakhaṇḍadinnassa vatthuṃ chaṭṭhamaṃ.

The Sixth Story: The Gift of a Piece of a Plank.

17. Corasahāyassa vatthumhi ayamānupubbīkathā

17. This is the sequential discourse in the story of the thief's companion.

Amhākaṃ [M 0.0065] bhagavati parinibbute jambudīpe devadahanagare eko manusso dukkhito tattha tattha vicaranto paccante aññataraṃ gāmaṃ gantvā tattha ekasmiṃ kulagehe nivāsaṃ kappesi. Tattha manussā tassa yāgubhattaṃ datvā posesuṃ, tato so tattha manussehi mittasanthavaṃ katvā katipāhaṃ tattha vasitvā aññaṭṭhānaṃ gantvā aparabhāge corakammaṃ karonto jīvikaṃ kappeti. Athekadivasaṃ corentaṃ taṃ rājapurisā gahetvā rañño dassesuṃ. Rājā taṃ bandhanāgāre karothāti āṇāpesi, te taṃ bandhanāgāraṃ netvā saṅkhalikāhi bandhitvā ārakkhakānaṃ paṭipādetvā agamaṃsu, bandhanāgāre vasantassa tassa dvādasasaṃvaccharāni atikkantāni. Tato aparabhāge tassa pubbasahāyo paccantagāmavāsī manusso kenaci kammena devadaha māgato tattha tattha āhiṇḍanto bandhanāgāre baddhaṃ taṃ addasa. Disvā tassa hadayaṃ kampi, so roditvā paridevitvā kiṃ te mayā kattabbaṃ sammāti pucchi. Tato tena samma bandhanāgāre vasantassa me idāni dvādasasaṃvaccharāni atikkantāni, ettakaṃ kālaṃ dubbhojanādinā mahādukkhaṃ anubhomi. Yāvāhaṃ āhāraṃ pariyesitvā bhuñjitvā āgamissāmi. Tāva maṃ ito muñcanupāyaṃ jānāhīti vutte so sappuriso.

When our Blessed One had attained final Nibbāna, in Jambudīpa, in the city of Devadaha, a certain man, being afflicted, was wandering about here and there. He went to a certain village in the border region and took up residence in a family's house there. The people there nourished him, giving him rice gruel and food. Then, having established a friendship with the people there, he dwelt there for some days and then went to another place. Later, he made his living by committing theft. Then one day, while he was stealing, the king’s men seized him and presented him to the king. The king ordered: “Put him in the prison-house!” They took him to the prison-house, bound him with chains, handed him over to the guards, and departed. While he was dwelling in the prison-house, twelve years passed. Then, later, a former companion of his, a man dwelling in that border village, came to Devadaha on some business. Wandering about here and there, he saw him bound in the prison-house. Seeing him, his heart trembled. He wept and lamented and asked: “Friend, what can I do for you?” Then the other said: “Friend, twelve years have now passed for me dwelling in the prison-house. For all this time I have experienced great suffering from bad food and the like. Find a way to release me from here until I can seek food, eat, and return.” When this was said, that good man…

1.

1.

Rūpena kintu guṇasīlavivajjitena,

Micchālayassa kitavassa dhiyā kimatthaṃ;

Dānā dicāgavigatena dhanena kiṃ vā;

Mittena kiṃ byasanakālaparammukhenāti.

But what is the use of beauty devoid of virtue and good conduct? What is the use of the intelligence of a deceitful person with a wrong inclination? Or what is the use of wealth devoid of giving and liberality? What is the use of a friend who turns his back in a time of calamity?

Evañca pana vatvā sādhu samma karomi te vacananti ārakkhakānaṃ santikaṃ gantvā bhonto yāveso bhattaṃ bhuñjitvā āgacchati. Tāvāhaṃ tassa pāṭibhogo bhavissāmi. Vissajjetha [M 0.0066] nanti āha, tehi na sakkā bho etaṃ vissajjetuṃ, api ca kho yāvāyaṃ āgacchati. Tāva tvaṃ ayasaṅkhalikāya baddho nisīdissasi, evaṃ taṃ vissajjessāma, no ce na sakkāti āhaṃsu, so evampi hotu sammāti vatvā tassa pādato saṅkhalikaṃ muñcitvā attano pāde katvā bandhanāgāraṃ pavisitvā itaraṃ muñcāpesi. Sopi asappuriso bandhanā mutto na puna taṃ ṭhāna magamāsi, aho akataññuno pakatiṃ ñātuṃ bhāriyaṃ. Yathāha.

And having said, “Very well, friend, I will do as you say,” he went to the guards and said: “Sirs, until this one eats his meal and returns, I will be his guarantor. Please release him.” They said: “Sir, it is not possible to release him. However, until he returns, you must sit bound with an iron chain. Thus we will release him; otherwise, it is not possible.” He said, “So be it, friend,” and having unfastened the chain from the other's foot and placed it on his own feet, he entered the prison-house and had the other released. But that ignoble man, freed from his bonds, did not go to that place again. Alas, how difficult it is to know the nature of the ungrateful! As it is said:

2.

2.

Vāripūre yathā sobbhe, nevanto visamaṃ samaṃ;

Paññāyatevaṃsādhussa, bhāvaṃ manasi sambhavaṃ.

Just as in a pit filled with water, the uneven and the even are perceived, so too is the nature of a good person, arisen in the mind, perceived.

3.

3.

Bhāsanti mukhato ekaṃ, cintenti manasā paraṃ;

Kāyenekaṃ karontevaṃ, pakatāyamasādhunaṃ.

They speak one thing with their mouth, but think another in their mind; with their body they do yet another thing—such is the nature of the ignoble.

4.

4.

Tesaṃ yo bhāvamaññāsi, sova paṇḍitajātiko;

Bahussutopi soyeva, paracittavidūpi so.

Whoever understands their nature, he is a truly wise person. He is the one of great learning; he is the one who knows the minds of others.

Athassa bandhanāgāre vasantassa dvādasasaṃvaccharāni atikkantāni. Ettakaṃ kālaṃ jighacchāpīḷitena tena āhārattāya paro na yācitapubbo, anucchiṭṭhāhāraṃ labhanadivasato alabhanadivasāyeva bahutarā honti, atha dvādasasaṃvaccharātikkame rañño putto nibbatti. Tadā rājā attano vijite sabbabandhanāgārāni vivarāpesi. Antamaso migapakkhinopi bandhanā muñcāpesi. Dvāre vivaṭamatteyeva bandhanāgāre manussā icchiticchitaṭṭhānaṃ agamaṃsu. So panekova tehi saddhiṃ agantvā ohīyi. Ārakkhakehi tvaṃ bho kasmā na gacchasīti vutte ahaṃ bho paññātabhāvena idāni na gamissāmi. Atīva parihīnagattosmi. Andhakāre gamissāmīti vatvā andhakāre āgate nikkhamma antonagare vissāsikānaṃ abhāvena kuto āhāraṃ labhissāmīti cintento nikkhamma rattandhakāre āmakasusāna [M 0.0067] magamāsi. Etthāhāraṃ labhissāmīti. Tattha so adhunā nikkhittamatamanussaṃ disvā manussaṭṭhinā maṃsaṃ chinditvā sīsakapāle pakkhipitvā tīhi manussasīsehi katauddhane ṭhapetvā citakato omukkaalātehi aggiṃ katvā susānaṃ nibbāpanatthāyā bhataudakena manussaṭṭhinā ālolento maṃsaṃ pacitvā otāretvā sākhābhaṅgena hirikopīṇaṃ paṭicchādetvā nivatthapilokikaṃ vātāvaraṇaṃ katvānisīdi. Tasmiṃ khaṇe tattha pippalīrukkhe adhivatthā devatā tassa taṃ kiriyaṃ disvā pucchissāmi tāva nanti taṃ upasaṅkamitvā evamāha. Bho tvaṃ ghanataratimirākule mahārattiyaṃ tattha tattha vikiṇṇanaraṭṭhisamākiṇṇe soṇasigālādikuṇapādakākule manussamaṃsabhakkhayakkharakkhasākule tattha tattha pajjalantānekacitakabhayānake susāne manussamaṃsaṃ pacitvā kiṃkarosīti pucchantī āha.

Then, while he was dwelling in the prison-house, twelve years passed. For all this time, though afflicted by hunger, he had never before begged another for food. The days he did not obtain food were more numerous than the days he obtained proper food. Then, after twelve years had passed, a son was born to the king. At that time, the king had all the prison-houses in his realm opened. He even had beasts and birds released from their bonds. As soon as the doors of the prison-house were opened, the men went to whatever place they wished. But he alone, not going with them, lagged behind. When asked by the guards, “Sir, why do you not go?” he replied: “Sirs, because of my conspicuous condition, I will not go now. My body is extremely wasted. I will go in the darkness.” When darkness came, he went out, thinking: “Since there is no one in the city I can trust, from where will I get food?” He went out into a charnel ground for uncremated bodies in the dark of night, thinking: “I will get food here.” There, seeing a human corpse that had just been discarded, he cut off flesh with a human bone and put it into a skull-cup. Placing it on a stove made of three human skulls, he made a fire with embers taken from a pyre. Stirring with a human bone, he cooked the meat with water that had been brought to extinguish the pyre, and then took it down. Covering his private parts with a broken branch, he made a wind-screen with the tattered rag he was wearing and sat down. At that moment, a devatā dwelling in a pīpal tree there saw his action and, thinking, “I shall question him,” she approached him and said this, asking: “Sir, in the deep of night, thick with darkness, in a charnel ground scattered all over with human bones, teeming with corpse-eaters such as dogs and jackals, teeming with flesh-eating yakkhas and rakkhasas, made frightening by the many funeral pyres blazing here and there—what are you doing, cooking human flesh?”

5.

5.

Rattandhakāre kuṇapādakehi,

Samākule sīvathikāya majjhe;

Manussamaṃsaṃ pacasī dha sīse,

Vadehi kiṃ tena payojanaṃ teti.

In the dark of night, in the middle of a charnel ground teeming with corpse-eaters, you cook human flesh here in a skull. Tell me, what is your purpose with that?

Atha so āha.

Then he said:

6.

6.

Na yāgahetu na ca dānahetu,

Susānamajjhamhi pacāmi maṃsaṃ;

Khudāsamaṃ natthi narassa aññaṃ,

Khudāvināsāya pacāmimambhoti.

Not for the sake of sacrifice, nor for the sake of giving, do I cook this flesh in the middle of the charnel ground. For a person there is no other thing like hunger; to destroy hunger I cook this.

Tato devatā taṃ tathā hotu, iminā pilotikena vātāvaraṇaṃ karosi. Kimatthametanti pucchantī.

Then the devatā said: “So be it. You are making a wind-screen with this rag. For what purpose is this?” she asked.

7.

7.

Nivatthasākho [M 0.0068] hirisaṃvarāya,

Pilotikaṃ tattha pasārayanto;

Karosi vātāvaraṇañca samma,

Kimatthametaṃ vada pucchito meti.

Clad in branches for modesty, spreading out a rag there, you also make a wind-screen, friend. For what purpose is this? Tell me, as I am asking you.

So tassā cikkhanto āha.

Explaining to her, he said:

8.

8.

Subhā subhāmissitasītavāto,

Sayaṃ acittova acittabhāvā;

Dehaṃ phusitvāna asādhukassa,

Akataññuno mittapadhaṃsakassa.

The cool wind is pleasant, mixed with what is pleasant, and is itself without a mind due to its mindless nature. Yet, having touched the body of a wicked, ungrateful man, a destroyer of friendship…

9.

9.

Samāvahanto yadi me sarīre,

Phusāti phusātisāsaṅgatimajjadāni itikattaci taṃ vāyu mamā visitvā;

Dukkhaṃ dadātīti visaṃva taṃ bho,

Parivajjituṃ baddhamimaṃ kucelanti.

…if that wind, blowing along, should enter my space and touch my body, it would give me pain like poison. To avoid that, friend, this wretched cloth is bound.

Devatā āha.

The devatā said:

10.

10.

Ki makāsi bho so katanāsako te,

Dhanañca dhaññaṃ tava nāsayī ca;

Mātā pitā bandhavo khetta vatthū,

Vināsitā tena vadehi kiṃ teti.

What has that ingrate done to you? What wealth or grain of yours has he destroyed? Mother, father, relatives, fields, possessions—tell me, what has he ruined for you?

Tato so āha.

Then he said:

11.

11.

Yaṃ rājato hoti bhayaṃ mahantaṃ,

Sabbassa haraṇādivadhādikañca;

Akataññunā sappurisena hoti,

Ārāva so bho parivajjanīyo.

Whatever great fear comes from a king—the seizing of all, killing, and so on—that comes from an ungrateful man, even a good one. He, friend, should be avoided from afar.

12.

12.

Yamatthi [M 0.0069] corāribhayañhi loke,

Atho dakenāpi ca pāvakena;

Akataññunā taṃ sakalampi hoti,

Ārāva so bho parivajjanīyo.

Whatever fear of thieves and enemies exists in the world, and also from water and from fire—all of that comes from an ungrateful person. He, friend, should be avoided from afar.

13.

13.

Pāṇātipātampi adinnadānaṃ,

Parassa dārūpagamaṃ musā ca;

Majjassa pānaṃ kalahañca pesunaṃ,

Samphaṃ giraṃ dhuttajanehi akkhadhuttādiyogaṃ itikatthaci yogaṃ.

Killing living beings, taking what is not given, sexual misconduct, and false speech; drinking intoxicants, quarreling, and slander, frivolous talk, and association with rogues—

14.

14.

Sabbaṃ anatthaṃ asivaṃ aniṭṭhaṃ,

Apāyikaṃ apāyikaṃ itikatthaci dukkhamananta maññaṃ;

Akataññunā sappurisena hoti;

Ārāva so bho parivajjanīyoti.

all that is harmful, inauspicious, and undesirable, the endless suffering of the plane of misery and other such things, comes from an ungrateful man, even a good one. He, friend, should be avoided from afar.

Vatvā attanā asappurisasaṃsaggenānubhūtaṃ sabbaṃ dukkhaṃ kathesi, tato devatā ahampi bho satthuno maṅgalasuttadesanādivase imasmiṃyeva rukkhe nisinno.

Having spoken thus, he told of all the suffering he had experienced through association with an ignoble person. Then the devatā said: “Friend, I too was seated in this very tree on the day the Teacher taught the Maṅgala Sutta and other discourses.”

Asevanā ca bālānaṃ, paṇḍitānañca sevanā;

Pūjā ca pūjanīyyānaṃ, etaṃ maṅgala muttamanti.

Not associating with fools, associating with the wise, honoring those worthy of honor—this is the highest blessing.

Gāthāya bālassa dose assosiṃti vatvā tassa pasanno taṃ attano vimānaṃ netvā nahāpetvā dibbavatthālaṅkārehi alaṅkaritvā dibbannapānaṃ datvā mahantaṃ sakkārasammānaṃ katvā attano ānubhāvena tasmiṃ nagare rajje abhisiñcāpesi. So tattha rajjaṃ karonto dānādīni puññāni katvā āyupariyosāne yathākammaṃ gatoti.

Saying, “I have heard in the verse the faults of a fool,” the devatā was pleased with him. Taking the man to his own celestial mansion, he had him bathed, adorned him with divine garments and ornaments, gave him heavenly food and drink, and bestowed great honor and respect upon him. By his own power, he had him anointed to the kingship in that city. Ruling there and performing meritorious deeds such as giving, at the end of his life he passed on according to his kamma.

15.

15.

Evaṃ [M 0.0070] asādhujanasaṅgamasannivāsaṃ,

Sañcajja sādhusucisajjanasaṅgamena;

Dānādi nekakusalaṃ paripūrayantā,

Saggā pavaggavibhavaṃ abhisambhunāthāti.

Thus, having abandoned association and co-residence with wicked people, through association with good, pure, and virtuous people, fulfilling the many kinds of wholesome deeds, such as giving, may you all attain the splendor of heaven and the ultimate good.

Corasahāyassa vatthuṃ sattamaṃ.

The Story of the Thief's Companion: The Seventh.

18. Maruttabrāhmaṇassa vatthumhi ayamānupubbīkathā

18. This is the preliminary talk in the story of Marutta the Brahmin.

Jambudīpe candabhāgā nāma gaṅgātīre homagāmaṃ nāma atthi. Tasmiṃ eko marutto nāma brāhmaṇo paṭivasati. Tadā so vohāratthāya takkasīlaṃ gantvā gehaṃ āgacchanto antarāmagge ekāya sālāya kuṭṭharogā turaṃ sunakhaṃ disvā tasmiṃ kāruññena nīlavallitakkambilena madditvā pāyesi. Sunakho vūpasantarogo pākatiko hutvā brāhmaṇena attano katūpakāraṃ sallakkhento teneva saddhiṃ agamāsi. Aparabhāge brāhmaṇassa bhariyā gabbhaṃ paṭilabhi, paripuṇṇagabbhāya tāya vijāyanakāle dārako tiriyampatitvā antogabbheyeva mato. Tadā taṃ satthena khaṇḍākhaṇḍikaṃ chinditvā nīhariṃsu, atha brāhmaṇo taṃ disvā nibbindahadayo gharāvāsaṃ pahāya isipabbajjaṃ pabbajitvā araññe viharati. Athassa bhariyā aññena saddhiṃ saṃvasantī ayaṃ maṃ pahāya pabbajitoti brāhmaṇe paduṭṭhacittā bho brāhmaṇaṃ mārehīti sāmikena saddhiṃ mantesi. Tesaṃ mantanaṃ sunakho sutvā brāhmaṇeneva saddhiṃ carati. Athekadivasaṃ tassā sāmiko tā pa saṃ māressāmīti dhanu kalāpaṃ gahetvā nikkhami, tadā tāpaso phalāphalatthāya araññaṃ gato. Su na kho assameyeva o hī yi. Puriso tāpasassāgamanamaggaṃ olokento gacchantare [M 0.0071] nilīno acchi. Sunakho tassa pamādaṃ oloketvā dhanuno guṇaṃ khāditvā chindi. So puna guṇaṃ pākatikaṃ katvā āropesi. Evaṃ so āropitaṃ āropitaṃ khādateva, atha so pāpapuriso tāpasassāgamanaṃ ñatvā taṃ māressāmīti dhanunā saddhiṃ agamāsi. Athassa sunakho pāde ḍasitvā pātetvā tassa mukhaṃ khāditvā dubbalaṃ katvā bhuṅkāramakāsi, evañhi sappurisā attano upakārakānaṃ paccupakāraṃ karonti. Vuttañhi.

In Jambudīpa, on the bank of the Candabhāgā river, there is a village named Homagāma. In that village lived a brahmin named Marutta. Once, for business purposes, he went to Takkasilā. On his way back home, he saw a dog suffering from leprosy in a roadside hall. Filled with compassion, he ground blue creeper and takkambila fibers and made the dog drink it. The dog, its illness subsided, became healthy and, recognizing the brahmin’s kindness, followed him. Later, the brahmin’s wife became pregnant. When her pregnancy was full-term, at the time for her to give birth, the child turned sideways and died within the womb. They then cut the body into pieces with a knife and removed it. Seeing this, the brahmin, disheartened, abandoned household life and went forth as an ascetic, dwelling in the forest. Meanwhile, his wife, living with another man, grew resentful toward the brahmin, thinking, “He abandoned me and went forth.” She conspired with her new husband, saying, “Kill that brahmin!” The dog, overhearing their plot, stayed close to the brahmin. One day, the woman’s husband, intending to kill the ascetic, took his bow and quiver and set out. At that time, the ascetic had gone into the forest to gather fruits and roots. The dog remained at the hermitage. The man, watching the path for the ascetic’s return, hid in a thicket. The dog, noticing his unguarded moment, chewed through the bowstring and broke it. The man repaired the bowstring and restrung it. Each time he restrung it, the dog chewed through it again. Then, the wicked man, knowing the ascetic was returning, went with his bow, intending to kill him. But the dog bit his leg, knocked him down, and mauled his face, weakening him. Then it barked loudly. Thus, good people repay the kindness done to them. As it is said:

1.

1.

Upakāraṃ karonto so, sunakho katavediko;

Sattūpaghātakaṃ katvā, isino dāsi jīvitaṃ.

That grateful dog, having rendered a service and destroyed the enemy, gave life to the sage.

2.

2.

Tiracchānāpi jānanti, guṇamattani kataṃ sadā;

Iti utvāna medhāvī, kataññū hontu pāṇinoti.

Even animals always know the good done to them. Therefore, knowing this, let the wise and all beings be grateful.

Tato tāpaso sunakhassa saddenā gantvā tassa taṃ vippakāraṃ disvā kāruññena paṭijiggitvā vūpasantavaṇaṃ balappattaṃ posetvā tattheva vasanto jhānābhiññaṃ nibbattetvā āyupariyosāne brāhmalokaparāyaṇo ahosīti.

Then, the ascetic, going on account of the dog's cry and seeing its injury, filled with compassion, tended to it. He nourished it until its wound healed and it regained its strength. Living right there, he developed meditative attainments and psychic powers. At the end of his life, he was destined for the Brahma world.

3.

3.

Sutvāna sādhu sunakhena katūpakāraṃ,

Mettiṃdisassa pakataṃ isinā ca sutvā;

Sammā karotha karuṇañca parūpakāraṃ,

Taṃ sabbadā bhavati vo bhavabhogahetūti.

Having heard of the good service done by the dog, and having heard of the kindness shown by the sage, act rightly, with compassion and helpfulness to others. That will always be for you a cause for the enjoyment of existence.

Maruttabrāhmaṇassa vatthuṃ aṭṭhamaṃ.

The Story of Marutta the Brahmin: The Eighth.

19. Pānīyadinnassa vatthumhi ayamānupubbīkathā

19. This is the preliminary talk in the story of the one who gave water.

Jambudīpe [M 0.0072] aññatarasmiṃ janapade kire ko manusso raṭṭhato raṭṭhaṃ janapadato janapadaṃ vicaranto anukkamena candabhāgānadītīraṃ patvā nāvaṃ abhiruhitvā paratīraṃ gacchati. Athāparā gabbhinitthī tāya eva nāvāya gacchati, atha nāvā gaṅgāmajjhappattakāle tassā kammajavātā caliṃsu. Tato sā vijāyitumasakkontī kilantā pānīyaṃ me detha, pipāsitāmhiti manusse yāci. Te tassā vacanaṃ asuṇantā viya pānīyaṃ nādaṃsu, atha so jānapadiko tassā karuṇāyanto karuṇāyapānīyaṃ, karuṇāyantopānīyaṃ, karuṇāyatoyaṃ iticakatthaci toyaṃ gahetvā mukhe āsiñci, tasmiṃ khaṇe sā laddhassāsā sukhena dārakaṃ vijāyi, atha te tīraṃ patvā katipayadivasena attano attano ṭhānaṃ pāpuṇiṃsu. Athāparabhāge so jānapadiko aññatarakiccaṃ paṭicca tassā itthiyā vasananagaraṃ patvā tattha tattha āhiṇḍanto nivāsanaṭṭhānaṃ alabhitvā nagaradvāre sālaṃ gantvā tattha nipajji. Tasmiṃyeva divase corā nagaraṃ pavisitvā rājagehe sandhiṃ chinditvā dhanasāraṃ gahetvā gacchantā rājapurisehi anubandhā gantvā tāyeva sālāya chaḍḍetvā palāyiṃsu. Atha rājapurisā āgantvā core apassantā taṃ jānapadikaṃ disvā ayaṃ coroti gahetvā pacchābāhaṃ gāḷhaṃ bandhitvā puna divase rañño dassesuṃ. Raññā kasmā bhaṇe corakamma makāsīti pucchito nāhaṃ deva coro, āgantukomhīti vutte rājā core pariyesitvā alabhanto ayameva coro, imaṃ mārethāti āṇāpesi. Rājapurisehi taṃ gāḷhaṃ bandhitvā āghātanaṃ nīte sā itthī taṃ tathā nīyamānaṃ disvā sañjānitvā kampamānahadayā muhuttena rañño santikaṃ gantvā vanditvā deva eso [M 0.0073] na coro āgantuko muñcathetaṃ devāti āha. Rājā tassā kathaṃ asaddahanto yajjetaṃ mocetu micchasi. Tassagghanakaṃ dhanaṃ datvā muñcāpehīti, sā sāmi mama gehe dhanaṃ natthi. Apica mama sattaputtehi saddhiṃ maṃ dāsiṃ karohi, etaṃ muñca devāti āha, atha rājā tvaṃ etaṃ adhunāgatoti vadasi. Etaṃ nissāya puttehi saddhiṃ attānaṃ dāsattaṃ sāvesi. Kimeso te ñāti vā, udāhu upakārakoti pucchanto āha.

In a certain region of Jambudīpa, it is said, a certain man traveled from country to country, from region to region, and eventually reached the bank of the Candabhāgā river. Boarding a boat, he crossed to the other shore. Now, a pregnant woman was also traveling on that same boat. When the boat reached the middle of the river, her labor pains began. Then, unable to give birth and exhausted, she begged the people, “Give me water, I am thirsty!” But they, as if not hearing her words, did not give her water. Moved by compassion for her, that man from the country took water and poured it into her mouth. At that moment, having gained relief, she easily gave birth to a child. Then, reaching the shore, after a few days they each arrived at their own places. Some time later, that man from the country, on account of some business, arrived at the city where that woman lived. Wandering here and there and unable to find a place to stay, he went to a hall at the city gate and lay down there. On that very day, thieves entered the city, broke into the royal palace, and stole valuable goods. As they fled, pursued by the king’s men, they abandoned the stolen goods at that same hall and escaped. When the king’s men arrived and did not see the thieves, they saw that man from the country and, thinking, “This is the thief!” seized him. They bound his hands tightly behind his back and presented him to the king the next day. When asked by the king, “Why, fellow, did you commit this act of theft?” he replied, “Your Majesty, I am not a thief—I am a newcomer.” But the king, unable to find the real thieves, commanded, “This is the thief! Execute him!” As the king’s men bound him tightly and led him to the execution ground, that woman saw him being taken away thus. Recognizing him, her heart trembled, and in a moment she went to the king, paid her respects, and said, “Your Majesty, this man is not a thief—he is a newcomer. Please release him, Your Majesty!” The king, not believing her words, said, “If you wish to free him, give wealth equal to his value and have him released.” She replied, “My lord, I have no wealth in my home. But make me and my seven sons your slaves, and release him, Your Majesty!” Then the king, questioning her, said, “You say he has just arrived. For his sake, you offer yourself and your sons into slavery. Is he your relative, or perhaps a benefactor?”

1.

1.

Kiṃte bhoti ayaṃ poso, tuvaṃ pucchāmi saṃsayaṃ;

Bhātā vā te pitā hoti, pati vā devaro tava.

What is this man to you, lady? I ask you, for I am in doubt. Is he your brother or your father, your husband or your brother-in-law?

2.

2.

Ñāti sālohito kinnu, udāhu iṇadāyako;

Athopakārako kinnu, kasmāssa desi jīvitaṃti.

Is he a kinsman or a blood relative, or perhaps a creditor? Or is he a benefactor? Why do you give your life for him?

Tato sā āha.

Then she said:

3.

3.

Eso me puriso deva, katapubbopakārako;

Atāṇamekikaṃ attānamekikaṃ itikatthaci ceso, dukkhitaṃ maraṇe ṭhitaṃ.

This man, O king, is one who did me a service in the past, when I was helpless, alone, suffering, and at the point of death.

4.

4.

Vijāyitu masakkontiṃ, gabbhiniṃ dukkhavediniṃ;

Toyena maṃ upaṭṭhāsi, tenāhaṃ sukhitā tadā.

When I, a pregnant woman suffering pain, was unable to give birth, he attended to me with water—because of that, I was relieved then.

5.

5.

Bhaṅgakallolamālāya, uttarantaṃ mahaṇṇavaṃ;

Pahāya pātuṃ kūpassa, yāti loko pipāsito.

Having abandoned the great ocean, surging with garlands of breaking waves, a thirsty world goes to drink from a well.

6.

6.

Tatheva vijjamānesu, janesu manujādhipa,

Ekasseva manasmiṃhi, guṇaṃ tiṭṭhati sādhukaṃ.

Just so, O lord of men, among existing people, virtue truly remains steadfast in the mind of one alone.

7.

7.

Pahatvāna mataṃ hatthiṃ, maṃsatthī keci jantuno;

Anubandhanti maṃsatthaṃ, sasaṃ dhāvanta mekakaṃ.

Some creatures, craving meat, having abandoned a dead elephant, pursue a single fleeing hare for meat.

8.

8.

Tatheva [M 0.0074] vijjamānesu, janesu manujādhipa;

Guṇavanta manubandhanti, sappurisaṃ katavedikaṃ.

Just so, O lord of men, among existing people, they follow the virtuous, noble, and grateful person.

9.

9.

Tasmā sappurise dhamme, patiṭṭhāsmi narādhipa;

Anussaranti etena, katapubbū pakārakaṃ.

Therefore, O king, I am established in the Dhamma of the virtuous; by this, they remember a kindness previously done.

10.

10.

Ahañca mama puttā ca, etenamha sukhāpitā;

Jīvitampi pariccajja, muccanīyo ayaṃ mayāti.

Both I and my children were made happy by him; even by sacrificing my life, this one must be freed by me.

Tato rājā dovārikaṃ pakkositvā tampi pucchitvā adhunāgatabhāvaṃ ñatvā tassā sappurisadhamme santuṭṭho tesaṃ ubhinnampi mahantaṃ yasaṃ anuppadāsi. Te laddhayasā ta to paṭṭhāya dānādīni puññakammāni katvā saggaparāyaṇā ahesunti.

Then the king, having summoned the gatekeeper and questioned him too, and having understood that he had just arrived, was pleased with her virtuous qualities. He bestowed great fame upon both of them. From then on, having obtained fame, they performed meritorious deeds such as giving and were destined for heaven.

11.

11.

Dhamme patiṭṭhitamanā api mātugāmā,

Evaṃ labhanti vibhavañca pasaṃsanañca;

Dhammañca sādhucaritaṃ manasīkaronto,

Dhammesu vattatha sadā sucisajjanāti.

Even women, with minds established in Dhamma, thus gain both wealth and praise. Bearing in mind the Dhamma and good conduct, always conduct yourselves according to the Dhammas, O pure and good people.

Pānīyadinnassa vatthuṃ navamaṃ.

The ninth story: The Giver of Water.

20. Sahāyassa pariccattajīvitakassa vatthumhi ayamānupubbīkathā

20. This is the sequential account concerning the story of the friend who sacrificed his life.

Bhagavati parinibbute sāvatthiyaṃ somabrāhmaṇo somadattabrāhmaṇoti dve brāhmaṇā vasanti. Tattha somadattabrāhmaṇena saddhiṃ somabrāhmaṇo yebhuyyena dūtaṃ kīḷati. Athekadivasaṃ somadatto somabrāhmaṇaṃ tena parājetvā tassa uttarāsaṅgañca lañchanamuddikañca gahetvā attano gehaṃ gacchanto somabrāhmaṇassa ehi gehaṃ gacchā- māti [M 0.0075] āha. Tato somo nāhaṃ samma ekasāṭako hutvā antaravīthiṃ otarituṃ sakkomi. Gamanato ettheva me ṭhānaṃ varataranti āha, somadattena evaṃ sati samma imaṃ uttarāsaṅgaṃ gaṇhāti tassa taṃ datvā idāni samma ehīti vuttopi nāgacchati. Puna tena bho kasmā nāgacchasīti puṭṭho samma mama hatthe muddikaṃ apassantā me puttadārādayo mayā saddhiṃ kalahaṃ karontīti āha, atha so evaṃ sante yadā te pahoti. Tadā mayhaṃ dehīti muddikampi datvā taṃ gahetvā gehaṃ agamāsi. Atha te ettakena sahāyā ahesuṃ. Aparabhāge somadattabrāhmaṇaṃ ayaṃ paradārakammaṃ akāsīti manussā gahetvā rañño dassesuṃ. Rājā tassa rūpasampattiṃ disvā rājāṇaṃ akatvā mā bho puna evamakāsīti ovaditvā vissajjesi. Rājā naṃ yāvatatiyavāraṃ ovadanto vissajjetvā catutthevāre gacchathetaṃ āghātanaṃ netvā mārethāti āṇāpesi. Evaṃ pāpakamme niratā anekākārena ovadantāpi na sakkā nivāretuṃ. Tathāhi.

When the Blessed One had attained Parinibbāna, two brahmins, Soma and Somadatta, lived in Sāvatthī. There, the brahmin Soma often played a game of dice with the brahmin Somadatta. Then one day, Somadatta defeated the brahmin Soma and, taking his upper robe and signet ring, was going to his own house when he said to the brahmin Soma, “Come, let us go home.” Then Soma replied, “Friend, I cannot go down into the street wearing only a single garment. It is better for me to stay right here than to go.” Thereupon Somadatta said, “Friend, if that is so, take this upper robe,” and gave it to him. But even after being told, “Now, friend, come,” he still did not go. When asked again by him, “Sir, why do you not come?” he replied, “Friend, not seeing the signet ring on my hand, my wife, children, and others will quarrel with me.” Then the other said, “If that is so, give it back to me when you are able,” and having given him the ring as well, he took him and went home. Through this, they became friends. At a later time, people seized the brahmin Somadatta, accusing him, “This man has committed adultery,” and brought him before the king. The king, seeing his handsome appearance, did not impose the royal punishment but admonished him, saying, “Sir, do not do such a thing again,” and released him. The king admonished and released him up to the third time, but on the fourth occasion, he ordered, “Go, take him to the execution ground and kill him.” Thus, those who are devoted to evil deeds cannot be restrained, even when admonished in many ways. As it is said:

1.

1.

Soṇā ceva sigālā ca, vāyasā nīlamakkhikā;

Iccete kuṇape sattā, na sakkā te nisedhituṃ.

Dogs and jackals, crows and blue flies; these beings attached to a corpse—they cannot be restrained.

2.

2.

Tathā pāṇātipātesu, paradāre surāya ca;

Musāvādesu theyyesu, sattasattā na vāriyāti.

Likewise, beings attached to the destruction of life, to the wives of others and to intoxicants, to false speech and to theft, cannot be restrained.

Tato rājapurisā taṃ bandhitvā pakkamiṃsu. Tadā somabrāhmaṇo somadattaṃ tathā nīyamānaṃ disvā kampamānahadayo rājapurisānaṃ santikaṃ gantvā imaṃ bho muhuttaṃ mā māretha. Yāva rājānaṃ jānāpessāmīti vatvā rañño santikaṃ gantvā vanditvā ṭhito deva mama jīvitaṃ somadattassa brāhmaṇassa dassāmi. Etaṃ muñcatha. Yadi māretukāmā, maṃ mārethāti āha. Rājā tuṇhī tuṭṭho itikatthaci ahosi, rājapurisā somadattaṃ muñcitvā [M 0.0076] somabrāhmaṇaṃ āghātanaṃ netvā māresuṃ, aho kataññuno kataveditā.

Then the king’s men bound him and departed. At that time, the brahmin Soma, seeing Somadatta being led away in that manner, with a trembling heart approached the king’s men and said, “Sirs, do not kill him for a moment, until I inform the king.” Having said this, he went to the king, paid homage, and stood there, saying: “Your Majesty, I will give my life for the brahmin Somadatta. Release him. If you wish to kill, kill me.” The king remained silent. The king’s men released Somadatta, took the brahmin Soma to the execution ground, and killed him. Oh, the gratitude and thankfulness of the grateful!

Hoti cettha.

And here it is said:

3.

3.

Kakūpakāra mattānaṃ, sarantā keci mānusā;

Jīvitaṃ denti somova, somadattassa attanoti.

Remembering even a trifling service done to them, some people give their own lives, just as Soma did for Somadatta.

So tena jīvitadānena devaloke nibbattitvā mahante kanakavimāne devaccharāsahassaparivuto dibbasampattimanubhonto paṭivasati. Tadā somadattabrāhmaṇo eso maṃ maraṇappattaṃ mocesīti vatvā tassatthāya dānaṃ datvā pattiṃ adāsi. Tāvadevassa tato bahutaraṃ devissariyaṃ ahosi devānubhāvañca. Tato so somadevo attano devissariyaṃ olokento sahāyassa attano jīvitadānaṃ addasa. Disvā attabhāvaṃ vijahitvā māṇavakavaṇṇena somadattabrāhmaṇaṃ upasaṅkamitvā paṭisanthāraṃ katvā attānaṃ devaloke nibbattabhāvaṃ pakāsetvā taṃ gahetvā attano ānubhāvena devalokaṃ netvā yathākāmaṃ sampatti manubhavāti vatvā sattāhaṃ devassariyaṃ datvā sattame divase netvā tassa geheyeva patiṭṭhāpesi. Tattha hi dibbasampatti manubhūtassa manussasampatti paṭikkulā hoti. Tato so dibbasampattimanussaranto kiso dubbalo uppaṇḍuppaṇḍukajāto ahosi. Athekadivasaṃ devaputto taṃ olokento tathā dukkhappattaṃ disvā na sakkā manussena dibbasampattimanubhavitunti icchiticchitasampattidāyakaṃ ekaṃ cintāmaṇiṃ datvā tassa bhariyampi attano ānubhāvena rūpavanthaṃ yasavantaṃ vaṇṇavantaṃ atikkantamanussitthivaṇṇaṃ akāsi, aparabhāge te jayampatikā paccakkhato diṭṭhadibbasampattivibhavā dānaṃ datvā sīlaṃ rakkhitvā sahāyadevaputtassa santikeyeva nibbattiṃsūti.

By that gift of his life, he was reborn in the deva world. He dwells there in a great golden mansion, surrounded by a thousand celestial nymphs, enjoying divine prosperity. Then the brahmin Somadatta, thinking, “He freed me when I had reached the point of death,” gave alms for his sake and transferred the merit. At that very moment, his divine splendor and power became far greater. Then that Somadeva, surveying his own divine splendor, perceived his friend’s gift of his life. Seeing this, he left his own form and, in the guise of a young man, approached the brahmin Somadatta. After exchanging friendly greetings, he revealed that he had been reborn in the deva world. Taking him by his own power to the deva world, he said, “Enjoy this prosperity as you wish.” He granted him the enjoyment of divine splendor for seven days, and on the seventh day, he brought him back and established him in his own house. For one who has experienced divine prosperity, human prosperity becomes repulsive. Then, as he recalled the divine prosperity, he grew thin, weak, and very pale. One day, the devaputta, observing him and seeing him so afflicted with suffering, thought, “It is not possible for a human to experience divine prosperity.” So he gave him a wish-fulfilling gem that provides whatever prosperity one desires. By his power, he also made his wife beautiful, glorious, and radiant, surpassing the beauty of human women. In time, that husband and wife, having directly witnessed the splendor of divine prosperity, gave alms, observed the moral precepts, and were reborn in the very presence of their friend, the devaputta.

4.

4.

Mandena [M 0.0077] nanditamanā upakārakena,

Pāṇampi denti sujanā iti cintayitvā;

Mittaddu mā bhavatha bho upakārakassa;

Pāsaṃsiyā bhavatha sādhujanehi niccaṃti.

Virtuous people, with minds delighted by even a small service from a benefactor, give even their own lives, having reflected thus: ‘Sirs, do not be treacherous to a benefactor! Be ever praised by the good.’

Sahāyassa pariccattajīvitakassa vatthuṃ dasamaṃ.

The tenth story: The One Who Sacrificed His Life for a Friend.

Nandiyarājavaggo dutiyo.

The Second Chapter: The Chapter on King Nandiya.

Yakkhavañcitavaggo

The Chapter of the Deceived Yakkha.

21. Yakkhavañcita vatthumhi ayamānupubbīkathā

21. This is the sequential account concerning the story of the Deceived Yakkha.

Bhagavati [M 0.0079] parinibbutamhi kosalarañño kira janapade tuṇḍagāmonāma ahosi. Tattheko buddhadāso nāma manusso “yāvajīvaṃ buddhaṃ saraṇaṃ gacchāmi. Buddho me saraṇaṃ tāṇaṃ leṇaṃ parāyaṇanti” evaṃ jīvitapariyantaṃ buddhaṃ saraṇaṃ gato paṭivasati, tasmiṃ samaye eko janapadavāsiko tattha tattha āhiṇḍanto taṃ tuṇḍagāmaṃ patvā tasseva ghare nivāsaṃ kappesi, tassa pana jānapadikassa sarīre eko yakkho āvisitvā pīḷeti, tadā tassa gāmassa pavisanakāle yakkho buddhadāso pāsakassa guṇatejena tassa gehaṃ pavisituṃ asakkonto taṃ muñcitvā bahigāme sattāhaṃ aṭṭhāsi tassāgamanaṃ olokento. Tato so jānapadiko sattadivasaṃ tattha vasitvā sattame divase sakaraṭṭhaṃ gantukāmo gāmā nikkhami. Atha taṃ tathā nikkhantaṃ disvā yakkho aggahesi, atha so taṃ ettakaṃ kālaṃ kuhiṃgatosīti pucchi. Yakkho bho tavatthāya ettha vasantassa me sattāhaṃ atikkantanti. Tato so ko te mayā attho, kiṃ te dammīti, atha yakkhena bho ahaṃ khudāya pīḷito bhattena me atthoti vutto so evaṃ sati kasmā maṃ antogehe vasantaṃ na gaṇhīti āha. Yakkhena bho tasmiṃ ghare buddhaṃ saraṇaṃ gato eko upasako atthi, tassa sīlatejena gehaṃ pavisitumasakkonto aṭṭhāsinti vutto jānapadiko saraṇaṃ nāma kinti ajānanto kinti vatvā so saraṇaṃ aggahesīti yakkhaṃ pucchi. Yakkho “buddhaṃ saraṇaṃ gacchāmī”ti vatvā saraṇaṃ aggahesīti āha. Taṃ sutvā jānapadiko idāni imaṃ vañcessāmīti cintetvā tenahi yakkha ahampi buddhaṃ saraṇaṃ gacchāmīti āha. Evaṃ vuttamatteyeva yakkho mahāsaddaṃ karonto bha ye na bhamanto [M 0.0080] palāyi, evaṃ sammāsambuddhassa saraṇaṃ idhaloke bhayo paddava nivāraṇatthaṃ hoti, paraloke saggamokkhāvahaṃ. Tathāhi.

When the Blessed One had passed into final Nibbāna, it is said that in a district of the Kosalan kingdom there was a village named Tuṇḍagāma. There lived a man named Buddhadāsa who had gone for refuge for life, saying: 'For as long as I live, I go to the Buddha for refuge. The Buddha is my refuge, my protection, my shelter, and my final destination.' At that time, a certain man from the countryside, wandering about, arrived at Tuṇḍagāma and took up lodging in that very house. Now, a yakkha had entered that countryman's body and was tormenting him. But when he was about to enter that village, the yakkha, unable to enter the house of the lay follower Buddhadāsa because of the power of his virtue, released the man and waited outside the village for seven days, watching for his departure. Then that countryman, having stayed there for seven days, on the seventh day left the village, intending to go to his own country. Seeing him leave thus, the yakkha seized him. The man then asked him: 'Where have you been all this time?' The yakkha replied: 'Sir, I have been waiting here for seven days for your sake.' Then the man asked: 'What do you need from me? What shall I give you?' When the yakkha said, 'Sir, I am afflicted with hunger and need food,' the man asked: 'If so, why did you not seize me while I was staying inside the house?' The yakkha replied: 'Sir, in that house there is a lay follower who has gone to the Buddha for refuge. Because of the power of his virtue, I was unable to enter the house, so I had to wait.' The countryman, not knowing what 'refuge' was, asked the yakkha: 'What is this refuge? How did he take refuge?' The yakkha said: 'He took refuge by saying, "I go to the Buddha for refuge."' Hearing this, the countryman thought, 'Now I will deceive him,' and said: 'Well then, yakkha, I too go to the Buddha for refuge.' As soon as this was said, the yakkha let out a great cry and fled, spinning in fear. Thus, taking refuge in the Perfectly Self-Enlightened Buddha serves to ward off fear and disaster in this world, and in the world to come it leads to heaven and liberation. So it is.

1.

1.

Buddhoti vacanaṃ etaṃ, amanussānaṃ bhayāvahaṃ;

Buddhabhattikajantūnaṃ, sabbadā mudamāvahaṃ.

This word 'Buddha' brings fear to non-humans; for beings devoted to the Buddha, it always brings joy.

2.

2.

Sabbopaddavanāsāya, paccakkhadibbamosadhaṃ;

Dibbamantaṃ mahātejaṃ, mahāyantaṃ mahabbhutaṃ.

For the destruction of all calamities, it is a divine medicine directly visible; a divine mantra of great power, a great talisman, a great marvel.

3.

3.

Tasmā so dāruṇo yakkho,

Disvā taṃ saraṇe ṭhithaṃ;

Ubbiggo ca bhayappatto,

Lomahaṭṭho ca chambhīto.

Therefore that fierce yakkha, seeing him established in the refuge, became agitated and struck with fear, his hair standing on end, terrified.

4.

4.

Bhamanto dhāvitaṃ disvā, timirova suriyuggate,

Simbalitūlabhaṭṭhaṃva, caṇḍavātena khaṇḍitaṃ.

Seeing him, he fled spinning about, like darkness at the rising of the sun, like a tuft of silk-cotton torn apart by a fierce wind.

5.

5.

Yaṃ dukkhaṃ rājacorāri, yakkhapetā disambhavaṃ;

Nicchantena manussena, gantabbaṃ saraṇattayaṃti.

A person who wishes to be free from suffering arising from kings, thieves, enemies, yakkhas, petas, and the like, should go to the Three Refuges.

Tato jānapadiko saraṇāgamane mahāguṇaṃ mahānisaṃsaṃ oloketvā buddhe sagāravo sappemo “jīvitapariyantaṃ buddhaṃ saraṇaṃ gacchāmī”ti saraṇaṃ gantvā teneva saraṇāgamanānubhāvena jīvitapariyosāne suttappabuddhoviya devaloke nibbattīti.

Then the countryman, seeing the great virtue and great benefit in going for refuge, with reverence and love for the Buddha, went for refuge, declaring: 'For the duration of my life, I go to the Buddha for refuge.' By the power of that very act of going for refuge, at the end of his life, as if awakening from sleep, he was reborn in the world of the devas.

6.

6.

Disvāna evaṃ saraṇaṃ gataṃ taṃ,

Apenti yakkhāpi mahabbhayena;

Pāletha sīlaṃ saraṇañca tasmā,

Jahātha duritaṃ sugatiṃ bhajavhoti.

Seeing one who has thus gone for refuge, even yakkhas flee in great fear. Therefore, protect your virtue and the refuge; abandon wrongdoing and resort to a good destination.

Yakkhavañcitavatthuṃ pathamaṃ.

The first story, concerning the deceived yakkha.

22. Micchadiṭṭhikassa vatthumhi ayamānupubbīkathā

22. In the story of the one with wrong view, this is the sequential account.

Bhagavati [M 0.0081] dharamāne rājagahanagare kira eko brahmabhattiko micchādiṭṭhiko paṭivasati, tattheva sammādiṭṭhikopi. Tesaṃ ubhinnampi dve puttā ahesuṃ. Te ekato kīḷantā vaḍḍhanti. Athāparabhāge guḷakīḷaṃkīḷantānaṃ sammādiṭṭhikassa putto “namo buddhayā”ti vatvā guḷhaṃ khipanto divase divase jināti. Micchādiṭṭhikassa putto “namo brahmuno”ti vatvā khipanto parājeti, tato micchādiṭṭhikassa putto niccaṃ jinantaṃ sammādiṭṭhikaṃ kumāraṃ disvā samma tvaṃ niccameva jināsi, kiṃ vatvā guḷaṃ khipasīti pucchi. Sohaṃ samma “namo buddhāyā”ti vatvā khipāmīti āha. Sopi tato paṭṭhāya “namo buddhāyā”ti vatvā khipati, atha te yebhuyyena dūte samasamāva honti. Aparabhāge micchādiṭṭhikassa putto pitarā saddhiṃ dārūnamatthāya vanaṃ gantvā sakaṭena dāruṃ gahetvā āgacchanto nagaradvārasamīpe sakaṭaṃ vissajjetvā tiṇe khādanatthāya goṇe vissajjesi, goṇā tiṇaṃ khādanto aññehi gorūpehi saddhiṃ antonagaraṃ pavisiṃsu. Athassa pitā goṇe pariyesanto sakaṭaṃ olokehīti puttaṃ nivattetvā nagaraṃ paviṭṭho ahosi, atha sāyaṇhe jāte manussā nagaradvāraṃ pidahiṃsu, tato kumāro bahinagare dārusakaṭassa heṭṭhā sayanto niddūpagato ahosi. Atha tassā rattiyā sammādiṭṭhiko ca micchādiṭṭhiko cāti dve yakkhā gocaraṃ pariyesamānā sakaṭassa heṭṭhā nipannaṃ kumāraṃ addasaṃsu, tesu micchādiṭṭhiko imaṃ khādāmīti āha. Athāparo mā eva makāsi, “namo buddhāyā”ti vācako esoti, khādāmevetanti vatvā itarena yāvatatiyaṃ vāriyamānopi gantvā tassa pāde gahetvā ākaḍḍhi. Tasmiṃ khaṇe dārako pubbaparicayena “namo buddhāyā”ti āha taṃ sutvā yakkho bhayappatto lomahaṭṭho [M 0.0082] hatthaṃ vissajjetvā paṭikkamma aṭṭhāsi. Aho acchariyaṃ buddhānubhāvaṃ abbhutaṃ, evaṃ attaṃ anīyyātetvā paricayena “namo buddhāyā”ti vuttassapi bhayaṃ chambhitattaṃ upaddavaṃ vā na hoti. Pageva attaṃ nīyyātetvā yāvajīvaṃ buddhaṃ saraṇaṃ gatassāti. Vuttañhi.

While the Blessed One was still living, it is said that in the city of Rājagaha there lived a devotee of Brahmā who held wrong view, and also one who held right view. Each of them had a son. These two grew up playing together. Later, when they were playing a game with balls, the son of the one with right view would say, 'Homage to the Buddha!' before throwing his ball, and he would win every day. The son of the one with wrong view would say, 'Homage to Brahmā!' before throwing, and he would lose. Then the son of the one with wrong view, seeing the other boy always winning, asked him: 'Friend, you always win. What do you say when you throw the ball?' He replied: 'Friend, I say, "Homage to the Buddha!" and then I throw.' From then on, he too started saying, 'Homage to the Buddha!' before throwing, and after that they were mostly equal in the game. On a later occasion, the son of the one with wrong view went with his father to the forest for firewood. Having loaded the cart with wood, they were returning. Near the city gate, they left the cart and released the oxen to eat grass. While grazing, the oxen entered the city with other cattle. His father, telling his son to watch the cart, went into the city to look for the oxen. When evening came, the people closed the city gates. The boy, outside the city, lay down under the firewood cart and fell asleep. That night, two yakkhas—one with right view and one with wrong view—were roaming in search of food when they saw the boy sleeping under the cart. The one with wrong view said: 'I will eat him.' The other said: 'Do not do so! He is one who says, "Homage to the Buddha!"' But the first one said, 'I will eat him anyway!' and though the other warned him up to three times, he went and grabbed the boy’s feet and pulled. At that moment, out of previous habit, the boy cried out: 'Homage to the Buddha!' Hearing this, the yakkha was struck with fear, his hair stood on end, and releasing his hand, he stepped back. Oh, how wonderful, how astonishing is the power of the Buddha! Even for one who utters 'Homage to the Buddha!' merely out of habit, without having dedicated himself, no fear, terror, or danger arises. How much more so for one who has dedicated himself and gone for refuge to the Buddha for life! As it is said:

1.

1.

Yathāpi sikhino nādaṃ, bhujaṅgānaṃ bhayāvahaṃ;

Evaṃ buddhoti vacanaṃ, amanussānaṃ bhayāvahaṃ.

Just as the cry of a peacock brings fear to snakes, so the word 'Buddha' brings fear to non-humans.

2.

2.

Yathā mantassa jappena, vilayaṃ yāti kibbisaṃ;

Evaṃ buddhoti vacanena, apayanti pahāyanti itisabbattha pisācakā.

Just as by the chanting of a mantra evil is destroyed, so by the word 'Buddha' evil spirits depart.

3.

3.

Aggiṃ disvā yathā sitthaṃ, dūratova vilīyati;

Disvānevaṃ saraṇagataṃ, petā pentiva petāpentāva itikatthaci dūrato.

Just as wax melts from afar upon seeing a fire, so petas flee from afar upon seeing one who has gone for refuge.

4.

4.

Pavaraṃ buddhaicceta, makkharadvayamabbhutaṃ;

Sabbo paddavanāsāya, thirapākāra muggataṃ.

This supreme word 'Buddha,' these two wonderful syllables, are a firm and lofty rampart for the destruction of all calamities.

5.

5.

Sattaratanapāsādaṃ, tameva vajiraṃ guhaṃ;

Tameva nāvaṃ dīpaṃ taṃ, tameva kavacaṃ subhaṃ.

It is a palace of seven treasures, a diamond cave; it is a boat, an island, and a beautiful suit of armor.

6.

6.

Tameva sirasi bhāsantaṃ, kirīṭaṃ ratanāmayaṃ;

Lalāṭe tilakaṃ rammaṃ, kappūraṃ nayanadvaye.

It is a radiant jeweled crown upon the head, a lovely mark upon the forehead, and camphor for the two eyes.

7.

7.

Tāḍaṅkaṃ kaṇṇayugale, soṇṇamālā gale subhā;

Ekāvaḷi tārahāra, bhārā jattusu laṅkatā.

It is an earring for the pair of ears, a beautiful golden garland for the neck, a single-strand pearl necklace, and a heavy ornament adorning the collarbones.

8.

8.

Aṅgadaṃ bāhumūlassa, karagge valayaṃ tathā;

Aṅgulisvaṅguliyañca, khaggaṃ maṅgalasammataṃ.

It is an armlet for the upper arm and a bracelet for the wrist, a ring for the fingers and a sword deemed auspicious.

9.

9.

Soṇṇā tapatta muṇhīsaṃ, sabāṇaṃva sarāsanaṃ;

Tameva sabbālaṅkāraṃ, tameva duritāpahaṃ.

It is a golden parasol and a turban, a bow complete with arrows; it is all these ornaments, and it is the dispeller of evil.

10.

10.

Tasmā [M 0.0083] hi paṇḍito poso,

Lokalocanasatthuno;

Saraṇaṃ tassa ganteva,

Guṇanāmaṃ ehipassikaṃ.

Therefore, a wise person should go for refuge to him, the Teacher, the Eye of the World, whose qualities and name are an invitation to 'come and see'.

11.

11.

Namoti vacanaṃ pubbaṃ, buddhāyeti giraṃ tadā;

Supantena kumārena, micchādiṭṭhikasūnunā.

The word 'Namo' first, and then the utterance 'to the Buddha,' was spoken by the sleeping boy, the son of the man with wrong view.

12.

12.

Sutvā vuttaṃ pisā cāpi, manussakuṇape ratā;

Na hiṃsanti aho buddha, guṇasāramahantatāti.

Hearing what was said, even the pisācas, who delight in human corpses, did not harm him, thinking: 'Ah, how great is the essence of the Buddha's virtues!'

Atha sammādiṭṭhikayakkho micchādiṭṭhikassa yakkhassa evamāha, ayuttaṃ bho tayā kataṃ. Buddhaguṇe pahāro dinno, daṇḍakammaṃ tayā kātabbaṃti, tena kiṃmayā samma kātabbanti vutte bubhukkhitassa āhāraṃ dehīti āha. Tato so sādhūti vatvā yāvāhaṃ āgacchāmi, tvaṃ tāvettha vacchāhīti vatvā bimbisārarañño kañcanataṭṭake vaḍḍhitaṃ rasabhojanaṃ āharitvā kumārassa pituvaṇṇena dārakaṃ bhojetvā puna kumārena vuttabuddhavacanañca attanā katavāyāmaṃ cāti sabbaṃ taṭṭake likhitvā idaṃ raññoyeva paññāyatūti adhiṭṭhāya agamaṃsu, atha pabhātāya rattiyā rañño bhojanakāle rājapurisā tattha taṭṭakaṃ adisvā nagaraṃ upaparikkhantā sakaṭe dārakañca taṭṭakañca disvā taṭṭakena saddhiṃ taṃ gahetvā rañño dassesuṃ. Rājā taṭṭake akkharādīni disvā vācetvā tassa guṇe pasanno mahantena yasena saddhiṃ seṭṭhiṭṭhānamadāsi.

Then the yakkha with right view said to the yakkha with wrong view: “Friend, what you have done is improper. A blow has been struck against the qualities of the Buddha; a penalty must be inflicted on you.” When the other asked, “Friend, what should I do?” he replied, “Give food to the hungry one.” Then the first yakkha said, “Very well,” and telling him, “You wait here until I return,” he brought King Bimbisāra’s delicious meal, served on a golden plate. Assuming the appearance of the boy’s father, he fed the child.
