Disvā taṃ upasaṅkamitvā dhammaṃ sutvā aniccasaññaṃ [M 0.0111] paṭilabhitvā tattha nisinnova arahattaṃ pāpuṇi, tato so pattapaṭisambhido attanā katapuññakammaṃ olokento taṃ pubbacariyaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ majjhe pakāsento āha. Tasmā.

It is said that in the time of the Sammāsambuddha Nārada, this island was known by another name. At one time, it suffered from a famine and a poor harvest; the great danger of starvation afflicted its beings. At that time, a disciple in the Dispensation of the Blessed One Nārada, after going for alms in a certain village, left with his bowl as if it had just been washed. Now in a certain house, the people were surviving by tying a nāḷi measure of rice in a cloth, boiling it in water, and then drinking the water. When they saw the elder, they paid him homage, took his bowl, cooked a meal with that rice, placed it in his bowl, and gave it to him. Then, by the power of their faith, that pot became full of rice. Seeing this marvel, they became joyful, thinking, 'Today we have seen for ourselves the result of our gift to the noble one!' They gathered a large crowd, fed them the rice, and only afterwards ate themselves. And whatever place the rice was taken from, it was immediately replenished. From then on, they gave alms to the great crowd that had gathered, and at the end of their lives, they were reborn in a deva world. Then that elder, having taken the alms food, went and sat down at the foot of a certain tree to begin his meal. It seems a devaputta who had been reborn in that tree was afflicted by hunger. Seeing the elder eating, he gave up his own form and stood near him in the guise of an old man. The elder ate on without noticing him. When the elder had eaten and set aside his last morsel, the devaputta coughed to make his presence known. Seeing him, the elder felt remorseful and placed the last morsel of rice in his hand. Taking that morsel of rice, the devaputta stood there and thought: 'It must be that in the past I never gave any food to ascetics and brahmins, or to the poor and destitute, or even to crows and dogs. That is why, though I have been reborn as a deva, I cannot get any food. Come now, I will give this morsel of rice to the elder himself. This will be for my long-term welfare and happiness.' Having thought thus, he gave up his desire for the morsel of rice, approached the elder, and said: 'Master, support in this world is enough for your servant. Please grant me support for the next world!' So saying, he poured it into the elder's bowl. As soon as that morsel of rice fell into the bowl, divine food containers appeared in a space of three gāvutas. The devaputta prostrated himself before the elder with the five-point prostration, took that divine food, first gave alms, and then ate himself. From the second day on, that devaputta gave great alms to the elder and to the great crowd that had gathered. At the end of his life, he was reborn in a deva world. For thirty aeons he enjoyed divine bliss, passing back and forth among the six desire-sphere heavens. In the time of the Blessed One Padumuttara, he passed away from there and was reborn in Bārāṇasī, in the family of a householder of immense wealth who held wrong view. They gave him the name Devatissa. Later, after he had reached maturity, his parents passed away. Looking at the storehouses filled with gold, gems, pearls, and so on, he thought: 'Because my parents held wrong view, they did no deeds of giving and the like. When they went to the next world, they went without taking with them so much as a small coin. But I will take my wealth with me!' Having made this resolution, he had a drum beaten to gather the poor, destitute, and beggars, and within seven days he gave away all his property by way of alms. Then he entered the wilderness, went forth into the ascetic life, practiced the kasiṇa meditation, and produced the five direct knowledges and the eight meditative attainments, becoming one who could travel through the air. At that time, the Blessed One named Padumuttara was residing in the city of Haṃsavatī, surrounded by devas and brahmās, teaching the Dhamma connected with the Four Noble Truths. As that ascetic was traveling through the air, he saw the great gathering of people and the six-hued rays issuing from the Blessed One’s body. Astonished, he descended from the sky and, seeing the Blessed One teaching the Dhamma seated with the great majesty of a Buddha, his mind became confident. He sat down within the assembly, listened to the Dhamma, and, after paying homage to the Blessed One, went back to his own hermitage. He remained there for the rest of his life and at its end was reborn in the Tāvatiṃsa heaven. For thirty aeons he enjoyed divine bliss, wandering back and forth among the six desire-sphere heavens. In fifty-one existences he was Sakka, king of the devas. In twenty-one existences he was a wheel-turning monarch, enjoying human felicity. During this Buddha-epoch, after the Blessed One had attained final Nibbāna, he was reborn in a certain family in Sāvatthī. At the age of seven, he saw a certain bhikkhu teaching the Dhamma. Approaching him, he listened to the Dhamma, acquired the perception of impermanence, and right there, while still seated, he attained arahantship. Then, having attained the analytical knowledges, he reviewed the meritorious deeds he had done and, revealing his past course of conduct in the midst of the bhikkhus, he said: 'Therefore...'

1.

1.

Nārado kira sambuddho, pubbe āsi naruttamo;

Lokaṃ dukkhā pamocento, dadanto amataṃ padaṃ.

Nārada, it is said, was a Sammāsambuddha,

in the past he was the supreme among men;

freeing the world from suffering,

granting the deathless state.

2.

2.

Tasmiṃ tu samaye tassa, sāvako chinnabandhano;

Bhikkhitvā dīpake laddha, māhāraṃ paribhuñjituṃ.

At that time, a disciple of his, one who had severed his fetters, having gone for alms on the island and obtained food, was about to eat.

3.

3.

Rukkhamūla mupā gañchi, tatthāsiṃ rukkhadevatā;

Bubhukkhitā udikkhantī, aṭṭhāsiṃ tassa santike.

He went to the foot of a tree; there I was, a tree-deity. Hungry and looking on, I stood near him.

4.

4.

Adāsi me bhattapiṇḍaṃ, karuṇāpūritantaro;

Gahetvāna ṭhito piṇḍaṃ, sahamāno khudaṃ tadā.

He gave me a morsel of food, his heart filled with compassion. Taking that morsel, I stood there, enduring my hunger at that time.

5.

5.

Adinnattā mayā pubbe, kiñci dānaṃ supesale;

Jighacchāpīḷito homi, jātopi devayoniyaṃ.

Because in the past I gave no gift at all to a virtuous person, I am afflicted by hunger, though born in a divine realm.

6.

6.

Ajja khettaṃ suladdhaṃme, deyyadhammopi vijjati;

Bījamettha ca ropemi, bhavato parimuttiyā.

Today I have found an excellent field, and a gift to be given is at hand; here I will sow the seed for release from becoming.

7.

7.

Iti cintiya vanditvā, dāsassa sāmi vo alaṃ;

Saṅgahaṃ idha lokasmiṃ, karotha pāralokikaṃ.

Thinking thus, I paid him homage, saying: 'Master, enough for your servant is support in this world. Please grant me support for the next world.'

8.

8.

Iti vatvā adāsāhaṃ, bhuñci sopi dayāparo;

Tenāhaṃ puññakammena, sudhannamalabhiṃ khaṇe.

Having spoken thus, I gave it back, and he, the compassionate one, ate. Through that meritorious deed of mine, I obtained excellent food in an instant.

9.

9.

Tato cuto chadevesu, vindanto mahatiṃsiriṃ;

Cirakālaṃ vasiṃ tattha, deviddhīhi samaṅgitā.

Passing away from there, I was reborn in the six deva realms, experiencing great splendor. For a long time I dwelt there, endowed with divine powers.

10.

10.

Satasahasse ito kappe, padumuttaranāmako;

Uppajji lokanāyako, dhammarājā tathāgato.

A hundred thousand aeons ago from this one, there arose the world-leader named Padumuttara, the King of Dhamma, the Tathāgata.

11.

11.

Mahiddhiko [M 0.0112] tadā āsiṃ, tāpaso kānane vane;

Sampattapañcā bhiññāṇo, ākāsena carāmahaṃ.

At that time I was an ascetic of great power, dwelling in the forest. Having attained the five direct knowledges, I traveled through the sky.

12.

12.

Tadā kāsena gacchanto, ramme haṃsavatīpure;

Buddharaṃsiparikkhittaṃ, ketumālāvilāsitaṃ.

Then, traveling through the sky to the delightful city of Haṃsavatī, I saw the Conqueror surrounded by the Buddha's radiance, resplendent with garlands of banners.

13.

13.

Devasaṅghaparibbūḷhaṃ, desentaṃ addasaṃ jinaṃ;

So taṃ disvāna nabhasā, ṭhitohaṃ parisantare.

I saw the Conqueror teaching, surrounded by a host of devas. Having seen him, I stood in the sky in the midst of the assembly.

14.

14.

Dhammaṃ sutvā udaggohaṃ, kālaṃ katvāna satthuno;

Tato cuto papannosmi, tāvatiṃse manorame.

Having heard the Dhamma, I was uplifted. Having passed away in the Teacher's presence, then, fallen from there, I was reborn in the delightful Tāvatiṃsa heaven.

15.

15.

Tiṃsakappasahassāni, caranto devamānuse;

Duggatiṃ nābhijānāmi, labhāmi vipulaṃ sukhaṃ.

For thirty thousand aeons, while wandering among devas and humans, I have not known a bad destination; I obtained abundant bliss.

16.

16.

Ekapaññāsatikkhattuṃ, devarajjamakārayiṃ;

Athekavīsatikkhattuṃ, cakkavattī ahosahaṃ.

Fifty-one times I ruled as king of devas, and twenty-one times I was a wheel-turning monarch.

17.

17.

Padesarajjaṃ kāsāhaṃ, bahukkhattuṃ tahiṃ tahiṃ;

Imasmiṃ bhaddake kappe, nibbutetu nibbutesu itisabbattha tathāgate.

I exercised regional kingship many times in various places. In this fortunate aeon, when the Tathāgata had attained Nibbāna,

18.

18.

Codito puññakammena, sāvatthipuramuttame;

Uppajjitvā kule seṭṭhe, jātiyā sattavassiko.

Impelled by my meritorious kamma, I was reborn in a noble family in the supreme city of Sāvatthī. I was seven years of age.

19.

19.

Sutvā dhammaṃ kathentassa, bhikkhussaññatarassahaṃ;

Bhavassantaṃ karitvāna, arahattamapāpuṇiṃ.

Having heard the Dhamma being taught by a certain bhikkhu, I made an end to existence and attained arahantship.

20.

20.

Sudinnaṃ me tadā dānaṃ, sussutaṃ dhammamuttamaṃ;

Dukkhassantaṃ akāsāhaṃ, tassa kammassa vāhasāti.

Well given was my gift at that time, and well heard was the supreme Dhamma. I made an end to suffering by the force of that kamma.

Evañca pana vatvā bahū jane kusalakamme niyojesīti.

And having spoken thus, he encouraged many people in wholesome deeds.

21.

21.

Dānenapevaṃ [M 0.0113] carimāya piṇḍiyā,

Savaṇāya dhammassa muhuttakena;

Labhanti sattā tividhampi sampadaṃ,

Phalaṃ vade ko bahudāyakassa bho.

By such a gift of a last morsel, and by listening to the Dhamma for a moment, beings obtain even the threefold success. Sirs, who can speak of the fruit for one who gives much?

Devaputtassa vatthuṃ navamaṃ.

The Story of the Devaputta, the Ninth.

30. Sīvalittherassa vatthumhi ayamānupubbīkathā

30. This is the sequential account of the story of the Elder Sīvali.

Ito kira kappasatasahassamatthake padumuttaro nāma satthā loke udapādi dhammadesanāya satte amatamahānibbāṇaṃ pāpento, tasmiṃ samaye bhagavā haṃsāvatiyaṃ sarājikāya parisāya majjhe ekaṃ bhikkhuṃ lābhīnaṃ aggaṭṭhāne ṭhapesi. Tadā rājā taṃ disvā taṃ ṭhānaṃ kāmayamāno buddhapamukhassa saṅghassa mahādānaṃ datvā bhagavato pādamūle sirasā nipajji, tadāssa bhagavā anāgate gotamassa bhagavato sāsane taṃ ṭhānaṃ labhissasīti vatvā byākāsi. Taṃ sutvā mudito rājā puññāni katvā devaloke nibbatti. Tato aparabhāge bārāṇasiyaṃ seṭṭhiputto hutvā paccekabuddhasahassaṃ catupaccayadānena yāvajīvaṃ paṭijaggitvā devaloke nibbatto mahantaṃ sampatti manubhavitvā tato cuto vipassissa bhagavato kāle bandhumatīnagare aññatarasmiṃ kulagehe nibbatti. So tasmiṃ samaye senaguttaṭṭhāne ṭhatvā rañño kammaṃ karoti, tadā nagaravāsino upāsakagaṇā vipassīsammāsambuddhaṃ upasaṅkamma vanditvā bhagavā bhante sasāvako amhākaṃ anuggahaṃ karotūti svātanāya nimantetvā mahādānaṃ datvā sabbe ekacchandā bhagavanta muddissa mahārahaṃ mahāpariveṇaṃ kārāpetvā pariveṇamahe mahādānaṃ dadantā dānagge asukaṃ nāma [M 0.0114] natthīti na vattabbanti vatvā dānaṃ paṭiyādetvā dānaggaṃ olokentā navadadhiñca paṭalamadhuñca apassantā purise pakkositvā sahassaṃ datvā dadhimadhuṃ khippaṃ pariyesitvā ānethāti pesesuṃ, te sahassaṃ gahetvā dadhimadhuṃ upadhāretuṃ tattha tattha vicarantā dvārantare aṭṭhaṃsu, tadā ayaṃ senagutto rañño sabhattaṃ dadhimadhuṃ ādāya gacchanto mahādvāraṃ sampāpuṇi, atha te dadhimadhuṃ disvā bho kahāpaṇaṃ gahetvā imaṃ dehīti yāciṃsu. Tena tenadassāmi itipikattaci na dassāmīti vutte yāvasahassaṃ vaḍḍhetvā yāciṃsu. Tato senagutto imaṃ appagghaṃ sahassena yācatha, ki manena karothāti pucchi, tehi sambuddhatthāyāti vutte tenahi ahameva dassāmīti jīramaricādīhi sakkharamadhuphāṇitādayo yojetvā dānaggaṃ upanāmesi. Taṃ satthu ānubhāvena buddhapamukhassa aṭṭhasaṭṭhibhikkhusatasahassassa pahoṇakaṃ ahosi. Tato so tena puññakammena devamanussalokesu sampatti manubhavitvā aparabhāge amhākaṃ bhagavato kāle koliyanagare mahālilicchavirañño upanissāya suppiyāya nāma aggamahesiyā kucchismiṃ paṭisandhiṃ gaṇhi. So sattamāsasattasaṃvaccharāni mātukucchiyaṃ vasitvā sattadivasāni mūḷhagabbho dukkhamanubhavi. Mātukucchito nikkhamantassa tassa mātāpitaro sīvalīti nāma makaṃsu. Evaṃ mahāpuññassa ettakaṃ kālaṃ mātukucchimhi dukkhānubhavanaṃ attanāva katena pāpabalena ahosi, so kira atīte rājā hutvā attano sapattaraññā saddhiṃ saṅgāmento mātarā saddhiṃ mantesi. Sā nagaraṃ rundhitvā amitte gaṇhituṃ sakkāti upāya madāsi, sopi tassā vacanena nagaraṃ rundhitvā sattame divase aggahesi, tena pāpakammabalena mātāputtānaṃ evaṃ mahantaṃ dukkhaṃ ahosīti. Tato [M 0.0115] sā puttaṃ vijāyanakāle sattame divase bhagavantaṃ anussaritvā sukhena bhāraṃ muñci. Tuṭṭhā sā sattame divase buddhapamukhassa bhikkhusaṅghassa mahādānaṃ adāsi, athassā putto sattavassikakāle gehā nikkhamma satthāraṃ disvā pabbajjaṃ yāci. Satthā sāriputtattherassa niyojesi, tato sāriputtattherena upajjhāyena moggallānamācariyaṃ katvā pabbaji, atha so khuraggeyeva arahattaṃ patvā buddhasāsanaṃ sobhesi, so pubbe katapuññānubhāvena mahāpuñño ahosi lābhīnañca aggo. Athekasmiṃ samaye bhagavā revatattheraṃ dassanāya khadiravanavihāraṃ gacchanto tiṃsabhikkhusahassehi saddhiṃ tiṃsayojanikaṃ chaṭṭitakantāraṃ sampāpuṇi nirūdakaṃ appabhakkhaṃ. Yebhuyyena devatā sīvalitthere pasannā. Tasmā bhagavā sīvalittheraṃ purato cārikaṃ katvā devatāhi kārāpite vihāre vasanto devatāhi sajjitadānaṃ paribhuñjanto revatattheraṃ saṃpāpuṇitvā gatakammaṃ niṭṭhāpetvā jetavanamāgamma lābhīnaṃ aggaṭṭhāne taṃ ṭhapesīti. Tena vuttaṃ apadāne.

It is said that a hundred thousand aeons ago from now, the Teacher named Padumuttara arose in the world, leading beings to the deathless great Nibbāna through his teaching of the Dhamma. At that time, the Blessed One, in the city of Haṃsavatī, in the midst of an assembly with its king, appointed a certain monk to be foremost among those who receive offerings. Seeing this, the king, desiring that position, gave a great offering to the Saṅgha headed by the Buddha and prostrated himself, placing his head at the Blessed One’s feet. Then the Blessed One predicted: 'In the future, during the dispensation of the Blessed One Gotama, you will attain that position.' Hearing this, the joyful king performed meritorious deeds and was reborn in a deva world. Later, in Bārāṇasī, he was born as a banker's son and, throughout his life, attended to a thousand Paccekabuddhas with the four requisites. After passing away, he was reborn in a deva world, experiencing great prosperity. Then, having passed away from there, during the time of the Blessed One Vipassī, he was born in a certain family in the city of Bandhumatī. At that time, holding the position of Senagutta, he performed duties for the king. Then, a group of lay devotees from the city approached the Perfectly Enlightened Vipassī, paid homage, and invited him together with his Saṅgha for the next day, saying, 'Venerable sir, may the Blessed One with his disciples show us compassion.' All with a single purpose, honoring the Buddha, they had a magnificent great monastery built. While giving a great offering for the monastery festival, they said, 'Let it not be said that anything is lacking at the head of the offerings.' Preparing the donation and looking over the chief items, they did not see fresh curd and honey from the comb. They summoned some men, gave them a thousand coins, and sent them out, saying, 'Quickly search for and bring back curd and honey.' Taking the thousand, they wandered about to procure them but stopped at a city gate. At that time, this Senagutta, carrying curd and honey for the king’s meal, arrived at the great gate. Seeing the curd and honey, they begged, 'Sir, take these coins and give us this.' When he refused, they increased their offer up to a thousand. Then Senagutta asked, 'Why do you ask for this inexpensive item for a thousand? What will you do with it?' They replied, 'It is for the sake of the Enlightened One.' Hearing this, he said, 'In that case, I myself will give it.' He mixed the curd and honey with cumin, pepper, sugar, molasses, and other such things and presented it as the chief item of the offering. By the Teacher's power, it sufficed for the Buddha and his assembly of sixty-eight hundred thousand monks. Through this meritorious deed, he experienced prosperity in the human and deva realms. Later, in our Blessed One’s time, he was conceived in the womb of Suppiyā, chief queen of the great Licchavi king of the Koliyan city. He remained in his mother's womb for seven years and seven months, and for seven days he experienced the suffering of an obstructed pregnancy. When he emerged from the womb, his parents named him Sīvali. For one of such great merit, to experience suffering in the womb for so long was due to the power of an evil deed done by himself. It is said that in a past life, as a king warring with a rival king, he conspired with his mother. She gave the advice: 'It is possible to capture the enemies by besieging the city.' Following her words, he besieged the city and captured it on the seventh day. By the power of that evil kamma, such great suffering came to both mother and son. Then, at the time of giving birth, on the seventh day, the mother recollected the Blessed One and was easily relieved of her burden. Delighted, on the seventh day she gave a great offering to the Bhikkhu Saṅgha headed by the Buddha. At seven years of age, her son left home, saw the Teacher, and requested the going forth. The Teacher assigned him to the Elder Sāriputta. He went forth with the Elder Sāriputta as his preceptor and the Elder Moggallāna as his teacher. Then, at the very tip of the razor, he attained arahantship and adorned the Buddha’s dispensation. Due to the power of merit made in the past, he was one of great merit and foremost among recipients of offerings. On one occasion, the Blessed One, accompanied by thirty thousand bhikkhus, was traveling to the Khadiravana Monastery to visit the Elder Revata. They reached a thirty-yojana wilderness that was afflicted by famine, waterless, and with little food. The devas had great confidence in the Elder Sīvali. Therefore, the Blessed One had the Elder Sīvali go on tour in front. Dwelling in monasteries constructed by the devas and partaking of offerings prepared by them, they reached the Elder Revata. Having completed their business, they returned to Jetavana, where the Blessed One appointed him to the foremost position among recipients of offerings. Thus it is said in the Apadāna:

1.

1.

Padumuttaro nāma jino, sabbadhammesu cakkhumā;

Ito satasahassamhi, kappe uppajji nāyako.

The Victor named Padumuttara, the one with the eye for all things, the Leader, arose in the world a hundred thousand aeons ago from now.

2.

2.

Sīlaṃ tassa asaṅkhyeyyaṃ, samādhi vajirūpamo vajirūpamā itikatthaci;

Asaṃkhiyaṃ ñāṇavaraṃ, vimutti ca anopamā.

His virtue is incalculable, his concentration is like a diamond; his supreme knowledge is incalculable, and his liberation is incomparable.

3.

3.

Manujāmaranāgānaṃ, brahmānañca samāgame;

Samaṇabrāhmaṇākiṇṇe, dhammaṃ deseti nāyako.

In an assembly of humans, devas, nāgas, and brahmās, crowded with ascetics and brahmins, the Leader teaches the Dhamma.

4.

4.

Sasāvakaṃ mahālābhiṃ, puññavantaṃ jutindharaṃ,

Ṭhapesi etadaggamhi, parisāsu visārado.

Skilled in the assemblies, he appointed a disciple of his—a great recipient of offerings, meritorious, and radiant—to this foremost position.

5.

5.

Tadāhaṃ [M 0.0116] khattiyo āsiṃ, pure haṃsavatīvhaye haṃsāvatavhaye itisabbattha;

Sutvā jinassa taṃ vākyaṃ, sāvakassa guṇaṃ bahuṃ.

At that time I was a khattiya in the city called Haṃsavatī. Having heard that utterance of the Victor concerning his disciple’s many virtues,

6.

6.

Nimantayitvā sattāhaṃ, bhojayitvā sasāvakaṃ;

Mahādānaṃ daditvāna, taṃ ṭhānamabhipatthayiṃ.

Having invited the Buddha with his disciples for seven days and provided them with food, and having given a great gift, I aspired for that position.

7.

7.

Tadā maṃ vinataṃ pāde, disvāna purisāsabho;

So sarena mahāvīro, imaṃ vacanamabravī.

Then the bull among men, the great hero, seeing me bowed at his feet, spoke this utterance:

8.

8.

Tato jinassa vacanaṃ, sotukāmā mahājanā;

Devadānavagandhabbā, brahmānoca mahiddhikā.

Then, desiring to hear the utterance of the Victor, the great populace—devas, titans, gandhabbas, and brahmās of great psychic power—

9.

9.

Samaṇabrāhmaṇā cāpi, namassisuṃ katañjalī;

Namo te purisājañña, namo te purisuttama.

Ascetics and brahmins also, with hands folded in reverence, paid homage: 'Homage to you, O thoroughbred of men! Homage to you, O supreme among men!'

10.

10.

Khattiyena mahādānaṃ, dinnaṃ sattāhakampi sattāhakaṃpito itipikatthaci no;

Sotukāmā phalaṃ tassa, byākarohi mahāmune.

'A great gift has been given by this khattiya for a full week; we desire to hear its fruit. Declare it, O great sage!'

11.

11.

Tato avoca bhagavā, suṇotha mama bhāsitaṃ;

Appameyyamhi buddhasmiṃ, guṇamhi suppatiṭṭhitā.

Then the Blessed One said: 'Listen to my speech. This gift is well established in the immeasurable qualities of the Buddha.'

12.

12.

Dakkhiṇā dāyakaṃ patvā, appameyyaphalāvahā;

Api ce sa mahābhogo, ṭhānaṃ pattheti muttamaṃ.

A gift brings immeasurable fruit to the giver; and though he is one of great wealth, he aspires to the supreme position.

13.

13.

Lābhī vipulalābhīnaṃ, yathā bhikkhu sudassano;

Tathāhaṃpi bhaveyyanti, lacchate taṃ anāgate.

'Just as the bhikkhu Sudassana is the foremost recipient among those with abundant gains, so too may I be.' He will obtain that in the future.

14.

14.

Satasahassito kappe, okkākakulasambhavo;

Gotamonāma nāmena, satthā loke bhavissati.

A hundred thousand aeons from now, one born of the Okkāka clan, a Teacher named Gotama, will be in the world.

15.

15.

Tassa dhammesu dāyādo, oraso dhammanimmito;

Sīvali nāma nāmena, hessati satthusāvako.

An heir to his Dhamma, his legitimate son, fashioned by the Dhamma, a disciple of the Teacher named Sīvali he will be.

16.

16.

Tena [M 0.0117] kammena sukatena, cetanāpaṇidhīhi ca;

Jahitvā mānusaṃ dehaṃ, tāvatiṃsūpago ahaṃ.

By that well-done deed and by my volitions and aspirations, having abandoned the human body, I went to the Tāvatiṃsa heaven.

17.

17.

Tatoparasmiṃsamaye, bārāṇasipuruttame;

Seṭṭhiputto ahaṃ āsiṃ, aḍḍhappatto mahādhano.

Then, at a later time, in the supreme city of Bārāṇasī, I was a banker's son, affluent and very wealthy.

18.

18.

Sahassamatte pacceka, nāyake ca nimantiya;

Madhurenannapānena, santappesiṃtadādaro.

Having invited about a thousand of those leaders, Paccekabuddhas, I respectfully satisfied them with sweet food and drink.

19.

19.

Tato cuto chakāmagge, anubhosiṃmahāyasaṃ;

Devaccharāparivuto, pāsāde ratanāmaye.

Having passed away from there, I experienced great glory in the six desire-sphere heavens, surrounded by celestial nymphs in a jeweled palace.

20.

20.

Evaṃ acintiyā buddhā, buddhadhammā acintiyā;

Acintiye pasannānaṃ, vipākopi acintiyo.

Thus, Buddhas are inconceivable, the qualities of a Buddha are inconceivable; for those with confidence in the inconceivable, the result too is inconceivable.

21.

21.

Ekanavutito kappe, vipassīnāma nāyako;

Uppajji cārunayano, sabbadhammavipassako.

In the ninety-first aeon from now, the Leader named Vipassī arose, one with lovely eyes, who sees into all phenomena.

22.

22.

Tadāhaṃ bandhumatiyā, kulassaññatarassa ca;

Dayito patthito putto, āsiṃ kammantabyāvaṭo.

At that time in Bandhumatī, I was the beloved and desired son of a certain family, engaged in my work.

23.

23.

Tadā aññataro pūgo, vipassissa mahesino;

Pariveṇaṃ akāresi, mahanta miti vissutaṃ.

At that time a certain group of people had a monastery built for the great seer Vipassī, one that was renowned as 'the great'.

24.

24.

Niṭṭhite ca mahādānaṃ, dadaṃ khajjakasaṃyutaṃ;

Navaṃ dadhi madhuñceva, vicinaṃ na ca maddasa.

When the great offering was completed, while giving food accompanied by delicacies, they searched for but did not find fresh curd and honey.

25.

25.

Tadāhaṃ taṃ gahetvāna, navaṃ dadhi madhumpica,

Kammasāmigharaṃ gacchaṃ, tamenaṃ tamesaṃ itisabbattha dāna maddasaṃ.

Then I, taking that fresh curd and honey and going to my employer's house, saw that group giving the offering.

26.

26.

Sahassampi ca datvāna, na latiṃsu ca taṃ dvayaṃ;

Tato evaṃ vicintesiṃ, netaṃ hessati orakaṃ.

Though they offered even a thousand, they did not obtain those two things. Then I reflected thus: 'This cannot be for some inferior purpose.'

27.

27.

Yathā [M 0.0118] ime janā sabbe, sakkaronti tathāgataṃ;

Ahampi kāraṃ kassāmi, sasaṅghe lokanāyake.

'Just as all these people are honoring the Tathāgata, I too will pay homage to the Leader of the World with his Saṅgha.'

28.

28.

Tadāhamevaṃ cintetvā, dadhiṃmadhuñca ekato;

Yojetvā lokanāthassa, sasaṅghassa adāsahaṃ.

Then, having reflected thus, I prepared the curd and honey together and gave it to the Protector of the World with his Saṅgha.

29.

29.

Tena kammena sukatena, cetanāpaṇidhīhi ca;

Jahitvā mānusaṃ dehaṃ, tāvatiṃsa magañchahaṃ.

By that well-done deed, and by my intention and aspiration, having abandoned the human body, I went to the Tāvatiṃsa heaven.

30.

30.

Punāhaṃ bārāṇasiyaṃ, rājā hutvā mahāyaso;

Sattukassa tadā ruddho, dvārarodhaṃ akārayiṃ.

Again in Bārāṇasī, having become a king of great fame, I was then enraged at an enemy and had the gates barred.

31.

31.

Tato sapattino sampattino; sapattāno iticakatthaci ruddhā, ekāhaṃ rakkhitā ahuṃ;

Tato tassa vipākena, pāpuṇiṃ nirayaṃ bhusaṃ.

Then my enemies were confined, guarded for one day. Then, as a result of that deed, I reached a terrible hell.

32.

32.

Pacchime ca bhave dāni, jātohaṃ koliye pure;

Suppavāsā ca me mātā, mahāli licchavī pitā.

Now in my final existence, I was born in the city of the Koliyans. My mother was Suppavāsā, and my father was the Licchavi Mahāli.

33.

33.

Khattiye puññakammena, dvārarodhassa vāhasā;

Sattamāse sattavasse, vasiṃkucchimhi dukkhito.

As a khattiya due to my merit, yet because of obstructing the gate, I dwelt in the womb for seven years and seven months, greatly afflicted.

34.

34.

Sattāhaṃ dvāramūḷhohaṃ, mahādukkhasamappito;

Mātā me chandadānena, eva māsi sudukkhitā.

For seven days I was obstructed at the birth passage, afflicted with great suffering. My mother, too, was thus in great pain.

35.

35.

Suvatthitohaṃ nikkhanto, buddhena anukampito;

Nikkhantadivaseyeva, pabbajiṃ anagāriyaṃ.

In good health I emerged, shown compassion by the Buddha. On the very day I came forth, I went forth into homelessness.

36.

36.

Upajjhā sāriputto me, moggallāno mahiddhiko;

Kese oropayanto me, anusāsi mahāmati.

My preceptor was Sāriputta; Moggallāna was of great psychic power. While shaving my hair, the greatly wise one instructed me.

37.

37.

Kesesu chijjamānesu channamanesu itipikatthaci, arahattamapāpuṇīṃ;

Devo nāgā manussā ca, paccayānu panenti me.

While my hair was being cut, I attained arahantship. Devas, nāgas, and humans provide me with requisites.

38.

38.

Padumuttaranāmañca, vipassiṃca vināyakaṃ;

Saṃpūjayiṃ pamudito, paccayehi visesato.

I joyfully honored the Buddha named Padumuttara and the Leader Vipassī with special requisites.

39.

39.

Tato [M 0.0119] tesaṃ vipākena, kammānaṃ vipuluttamaṃ;

Lābhaṃ labhāmi sabbattha, vane gāme jale thale.

As the abundant and supreme result of those deeds, I obtain gain everywhere—in the forest and village, on water and on land.

40.

40.

Revataṃ dassanatthāya, yadā yāti vināyako;

Tiṃsabhikkhusahassehi, saha lokagganāyako.

When the Leader, the foremost guide of the world, went to see Revata, he was together with thirty thousand bhikkhus.

41.

41.

Tadā devo panītehi paṇītehi itisabbattha, mamatthāya mahāmati;

Paccayehi mahāvīro, sasaṅgho lokanāyako.

Then, for my sake, a deva honored the Great Hero, the world-leader with his Sangha, with excellent requisites.

42.

42.

Upaṭṭhito mayā buddho, gantvā revatamaddasa;

Tato jetavanaṃ gantvā, etadagge ṭhapesimaṃ.

The Buddha was attended upon by me. Having gone, he saw Revata. Then, having gone to Jetavana, he placed me in this foremost position.

43.

43.

Lābhīnaṃ sīvali aggo, mama sissesu bhikkhavo;

Sabbelokahito satthā, kittayī parisāsumaṃ.

The Teacher, the benefactor of the whole world, praised me in the assembly, saying: ‘Bhikkhus, among my disciples who are recipients of offerings, Sīvali is the foremost.’

44.

44.

Kilesā jhāpitā mayhaṃ, bhavā sabbe samūhatā;

Nāgova bandhanaṃ chetvā, viharāmi anāsavo.

My defilements are burnt up; all states of existence are eradicated. Like a great elephant that has broken its bonds, I dwell free from taints.

45.

45.

Svāgataṃ vata me āsi, buddhaseṭṭhassa santikaṃ;

Tisso vijjā anuppatto, kataṃ buddhassa sāsanaṃ.

A good coming indeed was my coming to the presence of the supreme Buddha. The three true knowledges have been attained; the Buddha’s teaching has been done.

46.

46.

Paṭisambhidā catassopi, vimokkhā pica aṭṭhime;

Chaḷabhiññā sacchikatā, kataṃ buddhassa sāsananti.

The four analytical knowledges, and also the eight liberations, and the six higher knowledges have been realized; the Buddha’s teaching has been done.

Itthaṃ sudaṃ āyasmā sīvalitthero imā gāthāyo abhāsitthāti.

Thus indeed the Venerable Elder Sīvali spoke these verses.

47.

47.

Sutvāna etaṃ caritaṃ mahabbhutaṃ,

Puññānubhāvañca siriṃ sirīmataṃ;

Hitvā kusītaṃ kusalaṃ karotha,

Kāmāttha kāmaṃ bhavabhoganibbutiṃ.

Having heard this great and wonderful account, and the power of merit and glory of the glorious one, abandon laziness and perform wholesome deeds, you who desire sensual pleasures, existence, enjoyments, and Nibbāna.

Sīvalittherassa vatthuṃ dasamaṃ.

The Tenth Story: The Elder Sīvali.

Yakkhavañcitavaggo tatiyo.

The Third Chapter: Deceived by a Yakkha.

Mahāsenavaggo

The Mahāsena Chapter

31. Mahāsenarañño vatthumhi ayamānupubbīkathā

31. This is the sequential narrative in the story of King Mahāsena.

Bhagavati [M 0.0121] parinibbute pāṭaliputtanagare mahāseno nāma rājā rajjaṃ kāresi dhammiko dhammarājā. So pana pitupitāmahādīnaṃ dhanarāsiṃ oloketvā ime imaṃ sāpateyyaṃ pahāya maccuno mukhamupagatā. Aho saṃsārikānaṃ aññāṇatā. Dhanaṃ ṭhapetvā attano vināsañca attānaṃ ṭhapetvā dhanavināsañca na jānantīti sampattiyā adhigamañca vināsaṃ cāti sabbaṃ cintetvā dhammañca sutvā paṭiladdhasaddho divase divase dasasahassānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ madhurena annapānena santappento anekāni puññāni upacinanto ekadivasaṃ raho paṭisallino evaṃ cintesi. Evaṃ rājaniyogena janassa pīḷanaṃ katvā dinnadānato sahatthena kammaṃ katvā laddhena dinnadānaṃ mahapphalaṃ mahānisaṃsaṃ bhavissati, evaṃ mayā kātabbanti so suhadā maccassa rajjaṃ nīyyātetvā attano kaṇiṭṭhikaṃ ādāya kiñci ajānāpetvā aññataravesena nagarā nikkhamma uttaramadhuraṃ nāma nagaraṃ agamāsi, tattha mahāvibhavo eko seṭṭhi paṭivasati, te seṭṭhino samīpa mupagamma ṭhitā. Tena kimatthāyāgatāti vutte rājā tava gehe bhatiyā kammaṃ karissāmīti vatvā tenānuññāto tīṇi vassāni kamma makāsi, tato ekadivasaṃ seṭṭhi te disvā pakkositvā atīva tumhe sukhumālatarā. Tathāpi imasmiṃ gehe kammakaraṇena ciraṃ vasittha, ettakaṃ kālaṃ kismiñci kammepi kusītattaṃ na paññāyati. Pageva anācārampi, yāgubhattaṃ ṭhapetvā aññaṃ upakārampi mama santikā natthi, kenatthena kammaṃ karothāti pucchi. Rājā tassa vacanaṃ sutvā imasmiṃ janapade sālino manāpabhāvo bahuso sūyati. Tasmā sālīnamatthāya idhā gatamhāti āha, taṃ sutvā tesaṃ tuṭṭho seṭṭhi sālīnaṃ sakaṭasahassaṃ adāsi, rājā sāliṃlabhitvā seṭṭhino [M 0.0122] evamāha, bho imaṃ amhākaṃ nagaraṃ pāpethāti, taṃ sutvā seṭṭhi sādhūti vatvā sāliparipuṇṇasakaṭasahassaṃ rañño nagaraṃ pāpesi, rājā nagaraṃ gantvā seṭṭhissa nānāvaṇṇavatthahiraññasuvaṇṇādīhi sakaṭe pūretvā paṭipesetvā mettiṃ thiraṃ katvā ābhatavīhayo rājagehe sannicayamakāsi, atha rājā katipāhaccayena musalaṃ paggayha sahattheneva vīhiṃ koṭṭeti, koṭṭitakoṭṭitaṃ kaṇiṭṭhikā papphoṭeti. Evaṃ ubhopi taṇḍulānaṃ mahantaṃ rāsiṃ katvā dārūdakādayo āharitvā ambilabhattaṃ pacitvā rājagehe pañcasataāsanāni paññāpetvā kālaṃ ugghosesuṃ, āgacchantu ayyā anukampaṃ upādāya mama gehe bhuñjantūti. Taṃ sutvā pañcasatā bhikkhū ākāsena āgamiṃsu, rājā te yāvadatthaṃ parivisi, tato tesaṃ antare piyaṃgudīpavāsī eko mahāsivatthero nāma bhattaṃ gahetvā ete maṃ passantūti adhiṭṭhahitvā ākāsena piyaṃgudīpaṃ gantvā bhattaṃ pañcasatānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ datvā paribhuñci. Taṃ tassānubhāvena sabbesaṃ yāvadatthaṃ ahosi. Evaṃ appakenāpi deyyadhammena sappurisā dāyakānaṃ manaṃ pasādetvā patiṭṭhaṃ karontīti. Vuttañhi.

After the Blessed One had attained final Nibbāna, a righteous king named Mahāsena, a king of Dhamma, was ruling the kingdom in the city of Pāṭaliputta. Having observed the heaps of wealth left by his father, grandfather, and other ancestors, he thought: 'These men abandoned this property and went into the mouth of death. Oh, the ignorance of beings in saṃsāra! They do not understand the destruction of the self while leaving wealth behind, nor the destruction of wealth while leaving the self behind.' Having reflected on the acquisition and loss of all possessions, and having heard the Dhamma, he became established in faith. Day by day he would satisfy ten thousand bhikkhus with sweet food and drink, accumulating numerous merits. One day, while in solitary retreat, he reflected thus: 'A gift given from what is obtained by working with one’s own hands will be of great fruit and great benefit, more so than a gift given after oppressing the people by royal decree. This is what I should do.' So, having entrusted the kingdom to a friendly minister, he took his youngest sister and, without letting anyone know, left the city in disguise and went to a city named Uttarāmadhura. A very wealthy merchant lived there. They approached the merchant and stood there. When asked by him why they had come, the king said, 'I will work for wages in your house.' Having been given permission by him, he worked for three years. Then one day, the merchant saw them, called them over, and said: 'You are extremely delicate and refined, yet you have lived in this house for a long time doing manual labor. In all this time, no laziness has been apparent in any work, let alone any misconduct. Apart from gruel and rice, you have had no other support from me. For what reason are you doing this work?' Hearing his words, the king said: 'In this country, the fine quality of the rice is much spoken of. It is for that rice that we have come here.' Hearing that, the merchant was pleased with them and gave them a thousand cartloads of rice. Having received the rice, the king said to the merchant: 'Sir, please have this transported to our city.' Hearing that, the merchant said, 'Very well,' and had the thousand cartloads full of rice sent to the king’s city. The king, having gone to his city, filled the carts with cloths of various colors, gold, silver, and so on, and sent them back, making his friendship with the merchant firm. He had the transported rice stored in the royal palace. Then, after some days, the king took up a pestle and with his own hands pounded the paddy. As he pounded, his youngest sister winnowed it. In this way, they both made a great pile of rice. Then, having brought firewood, water, and so on, they cooked a sour-milk rice gruel and prepared five hundred seats in the royal palace. They had the time announced: 'Let the noble ones come! Out of compassion, let them eat in my house.' Hearing this, five hundred bhikkhus came through the air. The king served them as much as they wanted. Then, among them, a great elder named Mahāsivatthera, a resident of Piyaṅgu Island, took his food and, having made the resolution, 'Let these people see me,' he went through the air to Piyaṅgu Island, gave the food to five hundred bhikkhus, and partook of it. Through his spiritual power, it was sufficient for all of them. Thus, even with a small gift-object, virtuous persons inspire confidence in the minds of donors and establish them in merit. For it has been said:

1.

1.

Appakenapi medhāvī, dāyakānaṃ manaṃ pati;

Saddhaṃ vaḍḍhenti candova, raṃsinā khīrasāgaraṃ.

Even with a little, the wise one gladdens the mind of the donors; they increase their faith, as the moon with its rays swells the ocean of milk.

2.

2.

Aṭṭhāne na niyojentā, karontā neva sannidhiṃ;

Paribhoga makatvāna, neva nāsenti paccayaṃ.

They do not engage in what is inappropriate, nor do they hoard anything. Not making personal use of them, they do not waste the requisites.

3.

3.

Na pāpentāca theyyassa, na karontā tatheviṇaṃ;

Vibhajanti susīlesu, sayaṃ bhutvāna sīlavāti.

Not committing theft, nor likewise incurring debt, the virtuous one, having partaken himself, distributes among the virtuous.

Atha rājā kaṇiṭṭhikāya saddhiṃ thattheva ṭhito piyaṃgudīpe paribhuñjante pañcasatabhikkhū disvā haṭṭho udaggo ahosi. Atha [M 0.0123] te aparabhāge attanā kataṃ dānavaraṃ anussarantā na cireneva ubhopi sotāpannā ahesuṃti.

Then the king, standing right there with his youngest sister, saw the five hundred bhikkhus partaking of the food on Piyaṅgu Island and became joyful and elated. Then later, recollecting the excellent gift they had given, it was not long before they both became stream-enterers.

4.

4.

Na gaṇentāttano dukkhaṃ, vihāya mahatiṃ siriṃ;

Āyatiṃbhavamicchantā, sujanevaṃ subhe ratā.

Not reckoning their own hardship, having given up great fortune, desiring a good future state, good people thus delight in what is auspicious.

5.

5.

Āyāsena kataṃ puññaṃ, mahantaphaladāyakaṃ;

Iti mantvāna medhāvī, sahattheneva taṃ kareti.

Merit made with effort yields great fruit; thus reflecting, the wise person makes it with their own hands.

Mahāsenarañño vatthuṃ pathamaṃ.

The First Story: King Mahāsena.

32. Suvaṇṇatilakāya vatthumhi ayamānupubbīkathā

32. This is the sequential account in the story of Suvaṇṇatilakā.

Laṅkādīpe kira anurādhapuranagare eko mātugāmo ekāmātugāmātivā ekāmātugāmotivā katthaci saddhāsampannā niccaṃ abhayuttaracetiye pupphapūjaṃ karoti, athekadivasaṃ sā attano dhītuyā saddhiṃ tasmiṃ cetiye pupphapūjanatthāya pupphapūjatthāya itikatthaci gantvā pupphāsanasālāya udakaṃ apassantī dhītu hatthe pupphacaṅgoṭakaṃ ṭhapetvā ghaṭa mādāya pokkharaṇiṃ agamāsi, tato sā dārikā mātari anāgatāyayeva anāgateyeva itisabbattha adhotāsane pupphamuṭṭhiṃgahetvā maṇḍalaṃ katvā pūjetvā evaṃ patthanamakāsi. Tathā hi.

It is said that on the island of Laṅkā, in the city of Anurādhapura, there was a woman endowed with faith who always offered flowers at the Abhayuttara Cetiya. One day, she went to that cetiya with her daughter to offer flowers. Not seeing any water in the hall of the flower-offering stand, she placed the flower basket in her daughter’s hand, took a pot, and went to the pond. While her mother was away, the young girl took a handful of flowers, arranged them in a circle on the unwashed offering stand, made an offering, and made an aspiration thus:

1.

1.

Mahāvīrassa dhīrassa, sayambhussa mahesino;

Tilokaggassa nāthassa, bhagavantassa satthuno.

To the Great Hero, the steadfast one, the Self-become, the Great Seer; to the Foremost in the Three Worlds, the Protector, the Blessed One, the Teacher.

2.

2.

Ya mahaṃ pūjayiṃ pupphaṃ, tassa kammassa vāhasā;

Rūpīnaṃ pavarā hessaṃ, ārohapariṇāhavā.

By the power of this deed, this flower offering I have made, may I become the foremost among the beautiful, endowed with a well-proportioned body.

3.

3.

Maṃ [M 0.0124] disvā purisā sabbe, mucchantu kāmamucchitā;

Niccharantu sarīrā me, raṃsimālīva raṃsiyo.

May all men who see me become infatuated with sensual desire; may rays stream from my body like the rays of the sun.

4.

4.

Hadayaṅgamā kaṇṇasukhā, mañjubhāṇī subhā mama;

Kinnarānaṃ yathā vāṇī, evameva pavattatūti.

May my speech be heart-pleasing, sweet to the ear, lovely, and melodious, just like the voice of the Kinnaras.

Athassā mātā āgamma adhotāsane pūjitāni pupphāni disvā kasmā caṇḍālī adhotāsane bhagavato pupphāni pūjesi, ayuttaṃ tayā katanti āha, taṃ sutvā sā mātuyā kujjhitvā tvaṃ caṇḍālīti akkosi, sā ettakaṃ puññāpuññaṃ katvā aparabhāge tato cutā jambudīpe uttaramadhurāyaṃ ekassa caṇḍālagandhabbabrāhmaṇassa dhītā hutvā nibbatti, uttamarūpadharā ahosi, tassā sarīrato meghamukhato vijjullatāviya raṃsiyo niccharanti. Samantā catuhatthaṭṭhāne sarīrappabhāya andhakāre vidhamati. Mukhato uppalagandho vāyati, kāyato candanagandho, tassā dvinnaṃ thanāna mantare suvaṇṇavaṇṇaṃ ekaṃ tilakaṃ ahosi, tena bālasuriyassa viya pabhā niccharati. Diṭṭhadiṭṭhā yebhuyyena ummattā viya kāmamadena visaññino honti, aho kusalākusalānaṃ ānubhāvo. Tathā hi.

Then her mother arrived, and seeing the flowers offered on the unwashed stand, she said: 'Why, you outcaste, did you offer flowers to the Blessed One on an unwashed stand? What you have done is improper!' Hearing this, the girl became angry with her mother and reviled her, saying, 'You are the outcaste!' Having performed this much merit and demerit, in a later time she passed away from that existence and was reborn in Jambudīpa, in Uttaramadhurā, as the daughter of a certain outcaste musician-brahmin. She possessed supreme beauty, and from her body rays shone forth like lightning from a cloud. All around, for a radius of four cubits, the radiance of her body dispelled the darkness. The fragrance of blue lotuses wafted from her mouth, and the scent of sandalwood from her body. Between her two breasts was a single golden-colored mark, from which light shone like the morning sun. For the most part, those who saw her became like madmen, rendered senseless by the intoxication of desire. Oh, the power of wholesome and unwholesome deeds! For it is said:

5.

5.

Yena sā kodhasāmātu, caṇḍālī iti bhāsitā;

Tena sā āsi caṇḍālī, jegucchā hīnajātikā.

Because with anger she called her mother an 'outcaste,' by that she became an outcaste herself, despised and of low birth.

6.

6.

Sallakkhetvāna sambuddha, guṇaṃ pūjesi yaṃ tadā;

Tena puññānubhāvena, sā bhirūpī manoramā.

But having marked well the virtues of the Sambuddha, she then offered worship; through the power of that merit, she became beautiful and delightful.

7.

7.

Yena yena pakārena, puññapāpāni yo kare;

Tassa tassānurūpena, morova labhate phalaṃ.

In whatever way one performs meritorious and demeritorious deeds, one reaps results accordingly, just as a peacock obtains its fruit.

8.

8.

Pāpena ca tiracchāne, jāyanti kusalena te;

Vaṇṇapokkharatā hoti, morānaṃ kamma mīdisanti.

Through evil deeds, they are born among beasts; through wholesome deeds, they come to possess beautiful plumage. Such is the kamma of peacocks.

Tato [M 0.0125] tassā mātāpitaro suvaṇṇatilakāti nāma makaṃsu. Tasmiṃkira nagare manussā tassā rupadassanenaca savaṇenaca sampattāpi caṇḍāladhītā ayanti paribhavabhayena āvāhaṃ na karonti. Atha tasmiṃ nagare jeṭṭhacaṇḍālabrāhmaṇassa putto etamatthāya tassā mātāpitunnaṃ santikaṃ vatthābharaṇagandhamālādayo pesesi suvaṇṇatilakaṃ amhākaṃ dadantūti, te taṃ pavattiṃ tassā ārocesuṃ. Sāssa jigucchantī parihāsa makāsi. Tato brāhmaṇassa putto lajjito rañño santikaṃ gantvā vīṇaṃ muñcetvā gāyamāno evamāha.

Thereafter, her parents named her Suvaṇṇatilakā. It is said that in that city, although people were captivated by her beauty and by hearing her, they would not arrange a marriage out of fear of disgrace, thinking, 'She is an outcaste’s daughter.' Then the son of the chief outcaste-brahmin in that city, desiring her, sent gifts of clothes, ornaments, perfumes, and garlands to her parents, with the request, 'Give Suvaṇṇatilakā to us.' When they informed her of this, she, being disgusted, mocked him. Then the brahmin’s son, humiliated, went before the king and, playing his lute, sang thus:

9.

9.

Lalanā nanānī calalocanānī,

Taruṇā ruṇānī calitādharāṇī;

Manujo hi yo nettapiyaṃ karoti,

Sa tu nīcajātiṃ api no jahāti.

Women with playful eyes, young and lovely, with trembling lips—indeed, a man who takes delight in the sight of them would not abandon one even of low birth.

Kimidanti raññā puṭṭho āha.

When asked by the king, 'What is this?' he replied:

10.

10.

Sameti kiṃ deva chamāya mattikā,

Kadāci cāmīkarajātikāya;

Sigāladhenu api nīcajātikā,

Sameti kiṃsīhavarena devāti.

O Deva, does clay on the ground ever unite with pure gold? Does a she-jackal, being of low birth, ever mate with a noble lion, O Deva?

Evañca pana vatvā deva imasmiṃnagare suvaṇṇatilakā nāmekā caṇḍāladhītā atthi, sā samānajātikehi pesitapaṇṇākāraṃ na gaṇhāti, kulavanteyeva pattheti, kadā nāma kākī suvaṇṇahaṃsena samāgacchati devāti. Rājā taṃ sutvā tassā pitaraṃ pakkosāpetvā tamatthaṃ vatvā saccaṃ bhaṇeti pucchi, sopi saccaṃ deva, sā jātisampannameva kāmetīti āha. Rājā evaṃ sati bhaṇe pañcamadhuranagare uddāḷabrāhmaṇo nāma atthi, so jātisampanno mātitoca pititoca anupakkuṭṭho[M 0.0126], jegucchā paṭikkūlā etāti mātugāmena saddhiṃ na saṃvasati. Attano gehato rājagehaṃ gacchantoca āgacchanto ca soḷasakhīrodakaghaṭehi magge siñcāpesi. Mātugāme disvā kālakaṇṇī mayā diṭṭhāti khīrodakena mukhaṃ dhovati. Tava dhītā sakkontī tena saddhiṃ saṃvasatu, etamatthaṃ tava dhītaraṃ kathehīti āha, sopi taṃ sutvā gehaṃ gantvā dhītaraṃ pakkositvā raññā vuttaniyāmeneva tassā kathesi. Tāya taṃ sutvā sakkontī ahaṃ uddāḷabrāhmaṇena saddhiṃ vasissāmi, mā tumhe cintetha, papañcampi mā karotha, pātova gamissāmīti vutte pitā panassā sādhūti sahassagghanakacittakambalakañcukena dhītu sarīraṃ pārupāpetvā vīṇādituriyabhaṇḍāni gāhāpetvā dhītuyā saddhiṃ addhānamaggaṃ paṭipajji. Gacchanto antarāmagge aññatarasmiṃ nagare rañño gandhabbaṃ karonto dhītaraṃ piṭṭhipasse nisīdāpetvā gandhabbamakāsi. Athassa piṭṭhipassanisinnā suvaṇṇatilakā nayanakoṭiyā diṭṭhiṃ pāpentī sarasena taṃ oloketvā pārutakañcukaṃ kiñci apanetvā sarīrappabhaṃ paññāpesi, rājā panassā sarīrappabhaṃca rūpasampadaṃca disvā kāmāturo vigatasañño sammūḷho hutvā muhuttena paṭiladdhassāso tassā sassāmikāssāmikabhāvaṃ pucchitvā caṇḍāladhītāti sutvā paribhavabhayena taṃ ānetu masakkonto evarūpaṃ vaṇṇapokkharasampannaṃ itthiratanaṃ alabhantassa me ko attho jīvitenāti socanto paridevanto kāmamucchito kattabbā kattabbaṃ ajānanto asiṃ gahetvā attano sīsaṃ sayameva chinditvā kāla makāsi. Evameva antarāmagge pañcarājāno tassā rūpasampattimadamattā asinā chinnasīsā jīvitakkhayaṃ pāpuṇiṃsu. Tathā hi sattā hiraññasuvaṇṇadāsidāsa puttadārādīsu puttadārādīhi itisabbattha piyaṃ nissāya kāmena mucchitā anayabyasanaṃ pāpuṇantīti. Vuttañhetaṃ bhagavatā.

And having said this, he continued: 'O Deva, in this city there is an outcaste’s daughter named Suvaṇṇatilakā. She does not accept the gifts sent by those of her own caste and desires only those of high birth. When, indeed, will a crow consort with a golden swan, O Deva?' Hearing this, the king had her father summoned and, relating the matter, asked him, 'Tell the truth.' He replied, 'It is true, O Deva. She desires only one who is well-born.' The king then said: 'If that is so, listen. In the city of Pañcamadhurā, there is a brahmin named Uddāḷa. He is of pure birth on both his mother’s and father’s side, unblemished and irreproachable. Thinking women are loathsome and repulsive, he does not associate with them. When going from his house to the royal palace and returning, he has the path sprinkled with sixteen pots of milk-water. If he sees a woman, he exclaims, “I have seen an inauspicious sight!” and washes his mouth with milk-water. Let your daughter, if she is able, live with him. Tell this matter to your daughter.' Hearing this, the father went home, called his daughter, and told her exactly what the king had said. Upon hearing it, she said: 'If I am able, I will live with Uddāḷa the brahmin. Do not you worry or make a fuss. I will go at dawn.' Her father said, 'Very well.' He then had his daughter dressed in an embroidered woolen cloak worth a thousand, had musical instruments such as the lute taken along, and set out on the road with his daughter. While traveling, in a certain city, as he was performing music for the king, he had his daughter sit behind him. Then Suvaṇṇatilakā, seated behind him, cast a passionate glance at the king from the corner of her eye, and removing her cloak a little, she displayed the radiance of her body. The king, seeing her bodily radiance and perfection of form, became afflicted by desire, lost his senses, and was bewildered. After a moment, having regained his breath, he inquired whether she had a husband or not. Hearing she was an outcaste’s daughter, he was unable to have her brought to him for fear of disgrace. Grieving and lamenting, he thought: 'For me who cannot obtain such a jewel of a woman, endowed with such beauty and excellence, what is the use of life?' Infatuated with desire, not knowing what should or should not be done, he took a sword, cut off his own head, and died. In the very same way, on the road, five kings, intoxicated by the madness of her perfection of form, cut off their own heads with a sword and met their end. For indeed, beings, on account of attachment to what is dear—gold and silver, male and female slaves, sons and wives, and so on—become infatuated with desire and meet with calamity and disaster. This was stated by the Blessed One:

11.

11.

Piyato [M 0.0127] jāyate soko,

Piyato jāyate bhayaṃ;

Piyato vippamuttassa,

Natthi soko kuto bhayaṃ.

From what is dear, sorrow is born, from what is dear, fear is born; for one who is released from what is dear, there is no sorrow, so how can there be fear?

12.

12.

Pemato jāyate soko,

Pemato jāyate bhayaṃ;

Pemato vippamuttassa,

Natthi soko kuto bhayaṃ.

From affection, sorrow is born, from affection, fear is born; for one who is released from affection, there is no sorrow, so how can there be fear?

13.

13.

Ratiyā jāyate soko,

Ratiyā jāyate bhayaṃ;

Ratiyā vippamuttassa,

Natthi soko kuto bhayaṃ.

From delight, sorrow is born, from delight, fear is born; for one who is released from delight, there is no sorrow, so how can there be fear?

14.

14.

Kāmato jāyate soko,

Kāmato jāyate bhayaṃ;

Kāmato vippamuttassa,

Natthi soko kuto bhayaṃ.

From sensual desire, sorrow is born, from sensual desire, fear is born; for one who is released from sensual desire, there is no sorrow, so how can there be fear?

15.

15.

Taṇhāya jāyate soko,

Taṇhāya jāyate bhayaṃ;

Taṇhāya vippamuttassa,

Natthi soko kuto bhayaṃ.

From craving, sorrow is born, from craving, fear is born; for one who is released from craving, there is no sorrow, so how can there be fear?

Tato so anukkamena pañcamadhuranagaraṃ gantvā attano āgatabhāvaṃ raññā kathāpetvā tena anuññāto gantvā rājānaṃ addasa. Tadā uddāḷabrāhmaṇo rañño avidūre kambalabhaddapīṭhe nisinno hoti, gandhabbabrāhmaṇopi dhītuyā saddhiṃ gandhabbaṃ kurumāno nisīdi. Tasmiṃkhaṇe pitu piṭṭhipasse nisinnā suvaṇṇatilakā uddāḷabrāhmaṇo katamoti pucchitvā etasmiṃ bhaddapīṭhe nisinnoṃ esoti sutvā nilāmalalocanehi taṃ olokanti dasanaraṃsinā sambhinnasurattādharena mandahasitaṃ [M 0.0128] karontī taṃ oloketvā pārutakañcukaṃ apanetvā sarīrappabhaṃ vissajjesi. Taṃ disvā brāhmaṇo ummatto sokena paridaḍḍhagatto uṇhavātena pūritamukhanāso assunā kilinnanetto visaññī ahosi. Tato so muhuttena laddhassāso rogīviya rañño sakāsā apasaranto attano gehaṃ gantvā suhade pakkositvā tesaṃ evamāha. Bhavantettha.

Then he, having proceeded in due course to the city of Pañcamadhurā, had his arrival announced to the king. Having received permission, he went and saw the king. At that time, the brahmin Uddāḷa was seated not far from the king on a fine woolen seat, and the musician-brahmin also sat down with his daughter, performing music. At that moment, Suvaṇṇatilakā, seated behind her father, asked, 'Which one is the brahmin Uddāḷa?' Hearing, 'It is he, seated on that fine seat,' she gazed at him with her flawless blue eyes. Making a gentle smile with her naturally red lips mingled with the radiance of her teeth, she looked at him, then removed her cloak and released the radiance of her body. Seeing this, the brahmin became like a madman; his body was scorched with grief, his mouth and nose were filled with hot breath, his eyes were wet with tears, and he became unconscious. Then, after a moment, having regained his breath like a sick person, he withdrew from the king’s presence, went to his home, summoned his friends, and said to them:

16.

16.

Yo āpade samuppanne,

Upatiṭṭhati santike;

Sukhadukkhe samo hoti,

So mitto soca ñātako.

He who, when calamity arises, stands by close at hand; who is the same in happiness and suffering—he is a friend and he is a kinsman.

17.

17.

Yo guṇaṃ bhāsate yassa, aguṇañca nigūhati;

Paṭisedhetya paṭisedhetikattabbā itisabbattha kattabbā, so mitto soca ñātako.

He who speaks of one's virtues and conceals one's faults, and prevents what should not be done; such a one is a friend and a kinsman.

18.

18.

Suvaṇṇatilakānāma, lalanā kāmalālayā;

Nīlakkhicaṇḍakaṇḍehi, vikhaṇḍesi mano mama.

A woman named Suvaṇṇatilakā, an abode of sensual desire, has shattered my mind with the fierce darts from her dark eyes.

19.

19.

Tassā mukhambuje sattā, mama nettamadhubbatā,

Appampina sarantāmaṃ, tatthevā bhiramanti te.

My eyes, like bees attached to the lotus of her face, delight right there, not remembering me even a little.

20.

20.

Saheva tehi me cittaṃ, gataṃ ullaṃghiyuddhato;

Lajjāgambhīraparikhaṃ, dhitipākāramuggataṃ.

Together with them, my mind has gone, having leaped over the deep moat of shame and the high rampart of fortitude.

21.

21.

Sammuyhāmi pamuyhāmi, sabbā muyhanti me disā;

Tassa me saraṇaṃ hotha, karotha mama saṅgahanti.

I am utterly confused, I am deluded; all my directions are confounded. Be a refuge for me, grant me your assistance.

Taṃ sutvā te evamāhaṃsu.

Having heard that, they said thus:

22.

22.

Yaṃ tvamācariya patthesi, caṇḍālī sā asaṅgamā;

Kinnu mīḷhena saṃyogo, candanassa kadā siyā.

O teacher, she whom you desire is a Caṇḍāla woman, unfit for association. How could there ever be a union of sandalwood with excrement?

23.

23.

Agammagamanā [M 0.0129] yāti, narānaṃ dūrato sirī;

Kitticāyu balaṃ buddhi, ayasaṃca sa gacchatīti.

For one who goes where he should not go, good fortune departs far away; fame, lifespan, strength, and wisdom wane, and he comes to disgrace.

Atha tesaṃ brahmaṇo āha.

Then the brahmin said to them:

24.

24.

Na pariccajati lokoyaṃ, amejjhe maṇimuttamaṃ;

Thīratanaṃ yuvāṇī ca, dukkulā api gāhiyāti.

This world does not discard the finest jewel even if it is in filth. And a young woman, a jewel of a woman, is accepted even if she is of low birth.

Evañca pana vatvā tassā sassāmikāssāmikabhāvaṃ ñatvā ānethāti āha, te tathā akaṃsu. Tato brāhmaṇo tāya gehaṃ āgatakālato paṭṭhāya cattāro māse rañño upaṭṭhānaṃ neva agamāsi. Tassa pana brahmaṇassa santike pañcasatarājakumārā nānāvidhāni sippāni uggaṇhanti. Te taṃ kāraṇaṃ ñatvā suvaṇṇatilakāya vijjamānāya amhākaṃ sippuggahaṇassa antarāyo bhavissati, yena kenaci upāyena etaṃ māretuṃ vaṭṭatīti, cintetvā te hatthā cariyaṃ pakkositvā lañjaṃ datvā evamāhaṃsu, hatthiṃ surāya mattaṃ katvā suvaṇṇatilakaṃ mārehīti. Tato te sabbe rājaṅgaṇe sannipatitvā dūtaṃ pāhesuṃ, ācariyaṃ daṭṭhukāmamhāti. Tato brāhmaṇena āgantvā nisinnena paṭisanthāraṃ katvā ācariya ācariyāniṃ passitukāmamhāti āhaṃsu. Atha so suvaṇṇatilakaṃ gahetvā āgacchathāti manusse pesesi. Te tathā kariṃsu, atha tassā vīthimajjhaṃ sampattakāle hatthiṃ vissajjāpesuṃ. So soṇḍāya bhūmiyaṃ paharanto paharantoupadhāvitvā itikatthaci gacchanto upadhāvitvā soṇḍena taṃ ukkhipitvā kumbhe nisīdāpesi. Tato rājāno tathā taṃ mārāpetu masakkontā puna divase manusse payojetvā rattiyaṃ mārāpesuṃ. Brāhmaṇopi evarūpaṃ itthiṃ alasitvā jīvanato matameva mataṃmeseyyo itikatthaci seyyoti socanto paridevanto rājaṅgaṇe dārucitakaṃ kārāpetvā aggiṃpavisitvā [M 0.0130] matoti. Evaṃ mātugāmavasaṅgatā mahantaṃ anayabyasanañca maraṇañca pāpuṇantīti. Vuttañhetaṃ bhagavatā.

Having spoken thus, and having ascertained that she was without a husband, he said, 'Bring her.' They did so. Then, from the time she came to the brahmin's house, for four months he did not go to attend on the king. Now, five hundred princes were learning various arts from that brahmin. Knowing the reason for this, they thought: 'As long as Suvaṇṇatilakā is here, there will be an obstacle to our learning. By some means or other she must be killed.' Having thought this, they summoned an elephant trainer, gave him a bribe, and said: 'Make an elephant drunk with liquor and have it kill Suvaṇṇatilakā.' Then they all gathered in the royal courtyard and sent a messenger, saying: 'We wish to see the teacher.' When the brahmin came and sat down, they exchanged greetings and said: 'Teacher, we wish to see the teacher's wife.' So he sent men, saying: 'Go and bring Suvaṇṇatilakā.' They did so. When she reached the middle of the street, they had the elephant released. Striking the ground with its trunk as it went, it rushed up, lifted her with its trunk, and set her on its frontal globes. Then the princes, being unable to have her killed in that way, employed men on another day and had her killed at night. The brahmin, too, grieving and lamenting, thinking, 'Death is better than living without such a woman,' had a funeral pyre made of wood in the royal courtyard, entered the fire, and died. Thus, those who come under the sway of womenfolk meet with great misfortune, disaster, and death. This was said by the Blessed One:

25.

25.

Māyāvesā māyācesā itikatthaci marīcīva,

Soko rogo cupaddavo;

Kharāva bandhanā cesā,

Maccupāso guhāsayo;

Tāsu yo vissase poso,

So naresu narādhamoti.

An embodiment of illusion, like a mirage, a source of sorrow, disease, and calamity. She is like harsh fetters, the hidden snare of Death. Whatever man places his trust in them, he is the lowest of men.

26.

26.

Ayoniso sā purimāya jātiyā,

Puññaṃ karitvā alabhīdisaṃ gatiṃ;

Dhīsampayuttaṃ dhitisampayuttaṃ itikatthaci kusalaṃ karontā,

Nibbāṇamevābhimukhaṃ karothāti.

Unwisely in a former birth, she performed merit and obtained such a destination. But you, performing wholesome actions associated with wisdom, should make Nibbāna your very goal.

Suvaṇṇatilakāya vatthuṃ dutiyaṃ.

The Second Story: Suvaṇṇatilakā.

33. Kapaṇāya vatthumhi ayamānupubbīkathā

33. This is the sequential discourse on the story of the poor woman.

Bhagavati parinibbute jambudīpe tattha tattha bhikkhubhikkhuṇiyo ca upāsakaupāsikāyo ca jayamahābodhiṃ vandissāmīti yebhuyyena gantvā vandanti. Athā parabhāge bahū bhikkhū saṅgamma mahābodhiṃ vandanatthāya gacchantā aññatarasmiṃ gāmake bhikkhāya caritvā āsanasālaṃ gantvā katabhattakiccā thokaṃ vissamiṃsu, tadā tattha ekā kapaṇā duggatitthī tathā nisinnabhikkhū disvā upasaṅkamitvā pañcapatiṭṭhitena vanditvā ekamante ṭhitā ayyā kuhiṃ gacchantīti pucchi. Bhikkhū taṃ sutvā jayamahābodhissa ānubhāvañca taṃ vandanatthāya attānaṃ gamanañca kathentā evamāhaṃsu.

After the Blessed One had attained Parinibbāna, in Jambudīpa, monks, nuns, laymen, and laywomen, for the most part, would go to pay homage to the victorious Great Bodhi tree in various places. Then, on a later occasion, many monks gathered and, while traveling to pay homage to the Great Bodhi tree, they walked for alms in a certain small village. Afterwards, they went to a rest house, and after finishing their meal, they rested for a while. At that time, a poor, destitute woman saw the monks seated thus, approached them, paid homage with the five-point prostration, and, standing to one side, asked, 'Venerable sirs, where are you going?' Hearing this, the monks spoke of the power of the victorious Great Bodhi tree and of their own journey to pay homage to it, saying:

1.

1.

Yatthāsīno [M 0.0131] jino jesi, sasenaṃ makaraddhajaṃ;

Hantvā kilesasenañca, buddho āsi niruttaro.

There the Victor sat and conquered Māra of the sea-monster banner with his army; and having vanquished the army of defilements, he became the unsurpassed Buddha.

2.

2.

Yaṃ pūjesi mahāvīro, ṭhito pada makopayaṃ;

Sattarattindivaṃ netta, nīlanīrajaraṃsinā.

Which the Great Hero honored, standing with unmoving feet for seven days and nights, with eyes like the rays of blue lotuses.

3.

3.

Surāsuranarādīnaṃ, nettāli pāḷipātanā;

Mecakākārapattehi, sikhaṇḍīviya bhāti yo.

It, which shines like a peacock with its dark-hued leaves, is gazed upon in homage by gods, asuras, humans, and others.

4.

4.

Surapādapova sattānaṃ, yaṃ tiṭṭhati mahītale;

Iha loke paratte ca, dadanto icchiticchitaṃ.

Like a celestial wish-fulfilling tree for beings, it stands on the earth, granting whatever is wished for in this world and in the next.

5.

5.

Yassa purāṇapaṇṇampi, patitaṃ yo naro idha;

Pūjeti tassa so deti, bhavabhogaṃ mahīruho.

If any person here honors even a fallen old leaf of this great tree, it bestows upon them existence and enjoyments.

6.

6.

Gandhamālehi salilehi, yamupāsati sadā naro;

Ajjhattañca bahiddhā ca, duritaṃ so nihaññati.

When a person always worships it with perfumes, garlands, and water, they destroy their own wrongdoing, both internal and external.

7.

7.

Yo deti ihalokatthaṃ,

Yo deti pāralokikaṃ,

Sampadaṃ jayabodhiṃtaṃ,

Bhoti gacchāma vandituṃ.

It gives benefit in this world; it gives the blessing pertaining to the next. O lady, we are going to pay homage to that victorious Bodhi tree.

Taṃ sutvā udaggā somanassajātā bhikkhūnaṃ evamāha. Ahaṃ bhante parakule bhatiyā kammaṃ karontī dukkhena kasirena jīvikaṃ kappemi. Svātanāya me taṇḍulanālipi bhaṇḍulanāmpa itisabbattha natthi, pageva aññaṃ dhanaṃ, imaṃ vinā aññaṃ sāṭakampi natthi, kasmā pubbe akatapuññattā, tasmā imaṃ bhante sāṭakaṃ mamānuggahāya bodhimhi dhajaṃ bandhathāti yācitvā sāṭakaṃ dhovitvā tesaṃ adāsi. Bhikkhūpi tassānuggahāya taṃ gahetvā agamaṃsu. Sā sāṭakaṃ datvā pītipāmojjamānasā gehaṃ gantvā tadaheva rattiyā majjhimayāme satthakavātena upahatā kālaṃ katvā tesaṃ [M 0.0132] bhikkhūnaṃ gamanamagge ekasmiṃ ramaṇīye vanasaṇḍe bhummadevatā hutvā nibbattī, athassā puññānubhāvena tiyojanike ṭhāne dibbakapparukkhā pāturahaṃsu, tatta tatta nānāvirāgadhajapatākā olambanti. Devaputtā ca devadhītaro ca sabbābharaṇavibhūsitā tatheva dhajapatākādayo gahetvā kīḷanti. Naccagītādinekāni acchariyāni payojenti. Atha dutiyadivase tepi bhikkhū bodhimaṇḍalaṃ gacchantā sāyaṇhe taṃ ṭhānaṃ patvā ajja imasmiṃ vanasaṇḍe vasitvā gamissāmāti tattha vāsaṃ upagamiṃsu, tato te rattibhāge nānāvaṇṇadhaje ca devatāhi payojiyamānā gītavāditādayo ca tiyojanaṭṭhāne kapparukkhāni ca idaṃ sabbaṃ devissariyaṃ tassānubhāvena nibbattabhāvaṃ disvā vimhitamānasā devadhītaraṃ āmantetvā tvaṃ kena kammena idha nibbattāti pucchiṃsu. Sā bhikkhū vanditvā añjaliṃ paggayha ṭhitā bhante maṃ na sañjānitthāti āha. Bhikkhūhi na mayaṃ sañjānāma bhaginīti vutte sā attano sabhāvaṃ kathentī evamāha.

Hearing this, she became elated and joyful and said to the monks: 'Venerable sirs, I work for wages in another’s family, making a living with difficulty and hardship. For tomorrow I do not even have a measure of rice, let alone other wealth. I have no other garment apart from this one. This is because I made no merit in the past. Therefore, venerable sirs, as a favor to me, please tie this garment as a banner on the Bodhi tree.' Having requested this, she washed the garment and gave it to them. The monks, too, accepted it for her benefit and departed. After giving the garment, with a mind full of joy and gladness, she went home. That very night, in the middle watch, she was afflicted by a 'knife-wind' and passed away. She was reborn as a terrestrial deity in a pleasant grove on the path those monks were traveling. Then, through the power of her merit, celestial wish-fulfilling trees appeared in an area of three yojanas, from which various colorful banners and flags were hanging. Male and female deities, adorned with all their ornaments, played there, holding banners and flags. They produced many marvels, such as dancing and singing. The next day, those monks, traveling to the platform of the Bodhi tree, reached that place in the evening. Thinking, 'Today we will stay in this grove and go tomorrow,' they took up lodging there. Then, in the night, they saw the multi-colored banners, the songs and music produced by the deities, and the wish-fulfilling trees in the three-yojana area. Seeing that all this divine splendor had arisen through her power, they were astonished. They addressed the female deity and asked: 'By what kamma were you reborn here?' She paid homage to the monks, stood with her palms joined in reverence, and said: 'Venerable sirs, do you not recognize me?' When the monks said, 'No, sister, we do not recognize you,' she told them her own story, saying:

8.

8.

Hīyyo āsanasālāya, nisīdittha samāgatā;

Tumhakaṃ santikaṃ gamma, yā varākībhivādayi.

Yesterday in the seating hall, you sat down together; I am that wretched woman who came into your presence and paid homage.

9.

9.

Yā bodhiṃ pūjanatthāya, vatthakaṃ paṭipādayi;

Sāhaṃ hīyyo cutā āsiṃ, rattiyaṃ byādhipīḷitā.

I am she who provided a cloth to honor the Bodhi tree; afflicted by sickness, I passed away last night.

10.

10.

Nānāsampattisaṃyuttā, nānābhūsanabhūsitā;

Vimāne ratanā kiṇṇe, jātāhaṃ ettha kānane.

Endowed with various fortunes, adorned with diverse ornaments, I have been born here in this forest, in a celestial mansion strewn with jewels.

11.

11.

Hīyyo passittha me gattaṃ, rajojallehi saṃkulaṃ;

Ajja passatha me gattaṃ, vaṇṇavantaṃ pabhassaraṃ.

Yesterday you saw my body, covered with dust and grime; today see my body, beautiful and radiant.

12.

12.

Hīyyo passittha me bhante, nivatthaṃ malinambaraṃ;

Ajja passatha me bhante, dibbamambaramuttamaṃ.

Yesterday, venerable sirs, you saw me wearing a soiled garment; today, venerable sirs, see me in the finest celestial cloth.

13.

13.

Vikiṇṇaphalitaggehi[M 0.0133], kesehi viralā kulaṃ;

Ūkāgūthapaṭikkūlaṃ, hīyyo āsisiraṃ mama.

Yesterday my head had scattered, sparse, grey-tipped hair, and was disgusting with lice and nits.

14.

14.

Ajja taṃ parivattitvā, mama puññānubhāvato;

Sunīlamududhammillaṃ, kusumā bharaṇabhūsitaṃ.

Today, through the power of my merit, that has been transformed into a very dark, soft coiffure, adorned with flowers and ornaments.

15.

15.

Purā me sakasīsena, vahitaṃ dārū dakādikaṃ;

Puññenāhaṃ ajja mālā, bhāraṃ sīse samubbahe.

Formerly, I carried wood, water, and so on upon my own head; today, through merit, I bear the weight of garlands on my head.

16.

16.

Dhajatthāya mayā hīyyo, padinnaṃ thūlasāṭakaṃ;

Ajja nibbatti me bhonto, mahantaṃ dibbasampadaṃ.

Yesterday a coarse cloth was given by me for a banner; today, venerable sirs, great divine fortune has arisen for me.

17.

17.

Jānamānena kattabbaṃ, dānādīsu mahapphalaṃ;

Devaloke manussesu, sukhadaṃ dāna muttamanti.

One who knows should perform acts such as giving, which have great fruit. In the world of gods and among humans, giving is the supreme giver of happiness.

Taṃ sutvā sabbe bhikkhū acchariyabbhutacittā ahesuṃ. Sā devatā tassā rattiyā accayena bhikkhū nimantetvā dibbehi khajjabhojjehi sahatthā santappetvā tehi saddhiṃ āgacchantī antarāmagge dānaṃ dadamānā mahābodhiṃ gantvā sabbehi dhajapatākā dīhi ca nānāvidhavaṇṇagandhasampannapupphehi ca dīpadhūpehi ca bodhiṃ pūjetvā bhikkhūnaṃ cīvaratthāya dibbavatthāni datvā āgamma tasmiṃyeva vanasaṇḍe vasantī nānāvidhāni puññāni katvā tāvatiṃsabhavane nibbatti. Bhikkhūpi taṃ acchariyaṃ tattha tattha pakāsentā bahūjane puññakamme niyojesuṃti.

Hearing this, all the bhikkhus were filled with wonder and amazement. After that night had passed, that deity invited the bhikkhus and, having satisfied them with her own hands with divine hard and soft foods, she went with them. Giving alms along the way, she went to the great Bodhi tree. There she paid homage to the Bodhi tree with all kinds of banners, flags, flowers of various colors and fragrances, lamps, and incense. Having given divine cloths to the bhikkhus for their robes, she returned and dwelt in that same forest grove. Having performed various kinds of meritorious deeds, she was reborn in the Tāvatiṃsa heaven. The bhikkhus, too, proclaimed this wonder here and there, thereby encouraging many people to perform meritorious deeds.

18.

18.

Evaṃ vidhāpi kapaṇā jinasāsanamhi,

Katvā pasāda matha thūlakucelakena;

Pūjetva dibbavibhavaṃ alabhīti ñatvā,

Pūjāparā bhavatha vatthusu tīsu sammāti.

Knowing that even a wretched person like this, having generated faith in the Victor’s teaching and made an offering with a coarse, dirty rag, obtained divine splendor, you should rightly be devoted to making offerings to the three objects.

Kapaṇāya vatthuṃ tatiyaṃ.

The Third Story: The Destitute Woman.

34. Indaguttattherassa vatthumhi ayamānupubbīkathā

34. This is the sequential narrative in the story of the Elder Indagutta.

Amhākaṃ bhagavato parinibbāṇato orabhāge jambudīpe kira pāṭaliputtaṃ nāma nagaraṃ ahosi. Tattha dhammāsoko nāma mahiddhiko mahānubhāvo āṇācakkavatti rājā rajjaṃ kāreti. Uddhaṃ ākāsato heṭṭhā pathaviyā yojanappamāṇe sakalajambūdīpe tassa āṇā pavattati, tadā sakalajambudīpavāsino ca caturāsīti nagarasahasse rājāno ca attano attano balavāhane gahetvā āgamma dhammāsokamahārañño upaṭṭhānaṃ karonti. Tasmiṃ samaye devaputtanagare devaputto nāma mahārājā attano balavāhanaṃ gahetvā rañño upaṭṭhānaṃ agamāsi. Dhammāsoko devaputtamahārājānaṃ disvā madhurapaṭisanthāraṃ katvā tumhākaṃ raṭṭhe bahussutā āgatāgamā mahāguṇavantā ayyā atthīti pucchi. Taṃ sutvā devaputtarājā atthi deva tasmiṃ nagare sīhakumbhakaṃ nāma mahāvihāraṃ. Tattha anekasahassabhikkhū viharanti sīlavantā appicchā santuṭṭhā vivekakāmino. Tesu sāṭṭhakathātipiṭakadharo indaguttatthero nāma tesaṃ pāmokkho ahosi. So anekapariyāyena sanarāmarānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ dhammaṃvaṇṇeti. Guṇavā attano guṇaṃ nissāya loke pākaṭoti. Taṃ sutvā rājā tuṭṭhamānaso theraṃ passitukāmo hutvā samma tvameva gantvā theraṃ yācitvā idhā nehīti āha. Taṃ sutvā devaputtarājā attano hatthassabalavāhanā dimahā senaṅgaparivuto indaguttattherassa santikaṃ gantvā vanditvā ayya ayyaṃ dhammāsokamahārājā daṭṭhukāmoti āha. Therena sādhūti sampaṭicchite rājā therena sampaṭicchitabhāvaṃ dhammāsokamahārājino pesesi. Tato dhammāsokamahārājā somanassappatto attano āṇāpavattitaṭṭhāne rājūnaṃ sāsanaṃ pesesi. Sabbeva therāgamanamaggaṃ [M 0.0135] alaṅkarontūti. Atha te rājāno tuṭṭhapahaṭṭhā attano attano nagare bheriṃ carāpetvā devaputtanagarato yāva pāṭaliputtanagaraṃ etthantare pañcapaṇṇāsayojanikaṃ maggaṃ visamaṃ samaṃ karonto devatānaṃ dibbavīthimiva alaṅkaritvā dhammāsokamahānarindassa evaṃ sāsanaṃ pesesuṃ. Bhavantettha.

They say that in the time after our Blessed One’s Parinibbāna, there was a city in Jambudīpa named Pāṭaliputta. There, a wheel-turning monarch named Dhammāsoka, of great psychic power and great might, ruled the kingdom. His command extended over the whole of Jambudīpa, a yojana high up into the sky and a yojana deep down into the earth. At that time, the inhabitants of all Jambudīpa and the kings of eighty-four thousand cities, taking their own armies and vehicles, came to attend upon the great king Dhammāsoka. On that occasion, a great king named Devaputta from the city of Devaputtanagara took his army and vehicles and went to attend upon the king. Seeing the great king Devaputta, Dhammāsoka gave him a friendly greeting and asked: 'In your country, are there any noble ones who are learned, masters of the traditions, and highly virtuous?' Hearing this, King Devaputta replied: 'There are, Your Majesty. In that city there is a great monastery named Sīhakumbhaka. Many thousands of bhikkhus dwell there—virtuous, of few wishes, content, and fond of seclusion. Among them, their chief is the Elder Indagutta, a bearer of the Tipiṭaka with its commentaries. He explains the Dhamma in many ways to bhikkhus, humans, and devas. Being virtuous, he is famous in the world on account of his own virtue.' Hearing this, the king, with a delighted mind, became desirous of seeing the elder and said: 'Friend, you yourself go, invite the elder, and bring him here.' Hearing this, King Devaputta, surrounded by the great divisions of his army—elephants, horses, and so on—went to the Elder Indagutta, paid homage, and said: 'Venerable sir, the great king Dhammāsoka wishes to see you.' When the elder consented, saying, 'Good,' the king sent a message to the great king Dhammāsoka about the elder's consent. Then the great king Dhammāsoka, filled with joy, sent an order to the kings in the places under his command: 'Let everyone adorn the path for the elder's arrival.' Then those kings, joyful and elated, had a drum beaten in their own cities and, from the city of Devaputtanagara to the city of Pāṭaliputta, they made the uneven fifty-five yojana path even, adorning it like a celestial path of the devas. They sent this message to the great king Dhammāsoka: 'It is done.'

1.

1.

Indaguttamahāthera, sāmino gamanāya no;

Maggaṃ alaṅkarontūti, mahārājena pesitaṃ.

The great king sent the command: 'Let them adorn the path for the journey of our master, the great elder Indagutta.'

2.

2.

Tato te apanetvāna, pāsāṇakaṇṭakādikaṃ;

Visamaṃ samaṃ karitvāna, sammajjitvāna sādhukaṃ.

Then, having removed the stones, thorns, and so on, having made the uneven level, and having swept it well,

3.

3.

Dhotamuttā samābhāsā, okiritvāna vālukā;

Ussāpitā tattha tattha, dussatoraṇapantiyo.

They scattered sand shining like washed pearls, and here and there they erected rows of cloth archways.

4.

4.

Kaladhotahemarambhādi, nānātoraṇapantiyo;

Tathā pupphamayā neka, toraṇūparitoraṇā.

There were various rows of archways with silver and golden plantain trees and the like; likewise, many archways made of flowers, and arches built upon arches.

5.

5.

Tesu tesuca ṭhānesu, saṅkhatā kusumagghikā;

Tatheva gandhatelehi, dīpitā dīpapantiyo.

In those various places, festoons of flowers were prepared; similarly, rows of lamps were lit with fragrant oils.

6.

6.

Padumuppalasannīra, pupphapallava laṅkatā;

Ṭhapitā ghaṭamālāyo, puṇṇā sogandhavārihi.

Rows of pots were placed, filled with fragrant water and adorned with lotuses, blue water lilies, flowers, and tender leaves.

7.

7.

Nilapītā disambhinna, patākāhi dhajehi ca;

Maggassa ubhato passe, vanamāsi samākulaṃ.

On both sides of the path, the forest was filled with blue, yellow, and variegated banners and flags.

8.

8.

Ketavo uggatā tattha, mandamandasamīraṇā;

Avhayantāva sobhanti, brahmoragasurādayo.

Banners raised there, stirred by a very gentle breeze, shone as if inviting the Brahmās, nāgas, suras, and others.

9.

9.

Nāgacampapunnāga, ketakīvakulādihi;

Padumuppalā dijalajehi, mālatī kusumā dihi.

The path was adorned with nāgakesara, campaka, punnāga, ketakī, and bakula flowers; with lotuses, blue water lilies, and other aquatic flowers; and with jasmine and other blossoms.

10.

10.

Mālādāmehi [M 0.0136] nekehi, maggamāsi vicittakaṃ;

Pattharitvā pādapaṭe, sittasammattabhūmiyaṃ.

The path was made beautiful with many garlands and wreaths; on the ground that was sprinkled and swept, foot-cloths were spread.

11.

11.

Lājādipañcapupphāni, vikiriṃsu manoramaṃ;

Alaṅkaritvā hattassā, kusumā bharaṇādihi.

They delightfully scattered the five kinds of flowers beginning with roasted rice, and adorned the elephants and horses with flowers, ornaments, and the like.

12.

12.

Maggālaṅkaraṇatthāya, ṭhapitāsuṃ tato tato;

Tesu tesu ca ṭhānesu, bherimaṇḍalamajjhagā.

For the adornment of the path, they were stationed here and there; and in those various places, drum ensembles stood in the center of circles.

13.

13.

Naccanti cāturā nārī, rasabhāvanirantarā;

Kaṃsavaṃsādipaggayha, vajjentānekatantiyo.

Skillful women, continuously filled with aesthetic sentiment and emotion, danced; holding cymbals, flutes, and the like, they played many stringed instruments.

14.

14.

Gāyanti madhuraṃ gītaṃ, gāyantettha layānvitaṃ;

Maggoso sādhuvādehi, bheritantinadehica.

They sang sweet songs, they sang there endowed with rhythm; the path resounded with shouts of approval and the sounds of drums and strings.

15.

15.

Karīnaṃ koñcanādehi, hayānaṃ hesitehi ca;

Nekaviṭaṅkasaṅghehi, so karīhi samākulo.

It was filled with the trumpeting of elephants and the neighing of horses, and crowded with flocks of many pigeons and with those elephants.

16.

16.

Maggassa ubhato passe, devakaññūpamā subhā;

Mālākalāpe paggayha, tiṭṭhanti tuṭṭhamānasā.

On both sides of the path stood beautiful women like celestial maidens, holding bunches of garlands with delighted minds.

17.

17.

Tathā puṇṇaghaṭe gayha, padumuppalasaṃkule,

Aṭṭhamaṅgalamuggayha, tiṭṭhanti pamadā tahiṃ.

Likewise, women stood there holding full pots filled with lotuses and blue water lilies, and holding aloft the eight auspicious symbols.

18.

18.

Sītalūdakasampanna, papāhi samalaṅkatā;

Sinānatthaṃ khatā āsuṃ, pokkharañño tahiṃtahiṃ.

Here and there, ponds for bathing were dug, endowed with cool water and well-adorned with water pavilions.

19.

19.

Tahiṃtahiṃkatā āsuṃ, dānasālā manoramā;

Nicitāsumanekāni, dānopakaraṇā tahiṃ.

Delightful alms-halls were built here and there; many provisions for giving were stocked in them.

20.

20.

Evaṃ nekavidhā pūjā, amhehi paṭipāditā;

Ṭhapetvāna mahāgaṅgaṃ, taṃ jānātu mahīpatīti.

“Thus many kinds of homage have been prepared by us. Except for the great Gaṅgā, let the lord of the earth know this.”

Taṃ sutvā asokamahārājā gaṅgaṃ alaṅkarothāti soḷasayakkhe pesesi, te saparivārā tattha gantvā attano [M 0.0137] ānubhāvena gaṅgāya anto tigāvutaṭṭhāne udukkhalapāsāṇe ṭhapesuṃ. Ṭhapetvā thambhe ussāpetvā tulāsaṃghāṭe datvā himavantato rattacandanasāre āharitvā padare santharitvā anekehi pūjāvidhānehi alaṅkaritvā rañño evaṃ sāsanaṃ pesesuṃ. Bhavantettha.

Hearing this, the great king Asoka sent sixteen yakkhas, saying, “Adorn the Gaṅgā!” They went there with their retinues and, by their own power, placed mortar-stones within a three-gāvuta stretch of the Gaṅgā. Having done so, they erected pillars, set up beams and rafters, brought heartwood of red sandalwood from the Himālaya, spread it as flooring, and adorned it with many kinds of offerings. Then they sent this message to the king:

21.

21.

Yamatthāya mayaṃ sabbe, mahārājena pesitā;

Amhehi dāni taṃ sabbaṃ, katameva suṇotha taṃ.

“For what purpose we all were sent by the great king—all that has now been done by us. Listen to this.”

22.

22.

Gāvutattayagambhīraṃ, gaṅgaṃ yojanavitthataṃ;

Thambhe patiṭṭhapetvāna, anagghaṃ rattacandanaṃ.

“The Gaṅgā is three gāvutas deep and a yojana wide. Having established pillars in it, and having brought priceless red sandalwood from the Himālaya,

23.

23.

Himavantato haritvāna, setuṃ tattha sumāpitaṃ;

Toraṇā ca ubho passe, ratanehi sunimmitā.

a bridge was well-built there. On both sides are archways, beautifully fashioned with jewels.

24.

24.

Puṇṇakumbhaddhajā ceva, padīpāvaliyo tathā;

Ubho passesu ratanāni, māpetvālambanānica.

There are banners with full pots and rows of lamps; on both sides we have fashioned jewels and pendants.

25.

25.

Suvaṇṇamaṇimuttādi, dāmehi samalaṅkatā;

Vālukatthāya okiṇṇā, dhotamuttā pabhassarā.
