It is adorned with garlands of gold, gems, pearls, and the like; for sand, radiant washed pearls have been strewn.

26.

26.

Tesu tesu ca ṭhānesu, ṭhapitāsuṃ mahāmaṇī;

Nānārāgavitānehi, sobhitā setuno pari.

In various places great gems have been set; the area above the bridge is made beautiful with canopies of various colors.

27.

27.

Olambitāsuṃ tattheva, dibbādikusumādayo;

Niṭṭhitaṃ idha kātabba, yuttaṃ pūjāvidhiṃtu no;

Devotaṃ paṭijānātu, iti vatvāna pesayuṃti.

“Divine flowers and the like were hung right there. What was to be done here is finished. But the proper method of worship is not for us; let the deva acknowledge it.” Having said this, they sent the message.

Tampi sutvā asoko mahārājā tumheva theraṃ idhānethāti tesaṃyeva sāsanaṃ paṭipesesi, te sādhūti indaguttattherassa santikaṃ gantvā vanditvā bhante pāṭaliputtanagarassa gamanāya kāloti āhaṃsu, tato thero saṭṭhisahassamattehi [M 0.0138] bhikkhusaṅghehi parivuto pañcapaṇṇāsayojanamaggaṃ paṭipajji. Athāparaṃ devaputtanagaravāsino anekavidhamālāgandhavāsacuṇṇaddhajapatākādīhi anekehi tālāvacarehi naccagītavāditehi pūjetvā agamaṃsu. Atha thero mahantena pūjāvidhānena jambudīpavāsīhi pūjiyamāno anukkamena candabhāgāya gaṅgāya setuṃ patvā tattha mahantaṃ pūjāvidhānaṃ olokento evaṃ cintesi, evaṃ uḷāraṃ pūjāvidhānaṃ idāni jambudīpe nāññassa hoti, mayhamevetaṃ kataṃ. Ahamevetta uttamo appaṭimoti evaṃ seyyassa seyyohamasmīti mānaṃ uppādetvā aṭṭhāsi. Tasmiṃ khaṇe eko khīṇāsavatthero taṃ mānenupatthaddhacetasā ṭhitaṃ dibbacakkhunā disvā upasaṅkamitvā vanditvā therassa ovadanto evamāha. Tasmā.

Hearing that, King Asoka sent back a message to them, saying, “You yourselves bring the elder here.” They replied, “Very well,” went to the Elder Indagutta, paid homage, and said, “Venerable sir, it is time to go to the city of Pāṭaliputta.” Then the elder, surrounded by about sixty thousand monks, set out on the fifty-five yojana journey. Then the inhabitants of Devaputtanagara escorted him, honoring him with various kinds of garlands, perfumes, fragrant powders, banners, and flags, and with much dancing, singing, and music. Then the elder, being honored with a great ceremony by the inhabitants of Jambudīpa, gradually reached the bridge over the river Candabhāgā. Observing the great ceremony there, he thought thus: “Such a magnificent ceremony does not exist for anyone else in Jambudīpa now; this was done for me alone. I alone am the supreme here, the incomparable.” Thus, thinking, “I am better than the best,” he generated conceit and stood there. At that moment, an elder who was an arahant, seeing with the divine eye that he was standing there with a mind stiffened by conceit, approached, paid homage, and admonishing the elder, said this:

28.

28.

Mā mānassa vasī hotha, māno bhante vasaṃgataṃ mānaṃbhantevasīkataṃ itisabbattha;

Anatthado sadā hoti, pātetvāna bhavā vaṭe.

“Do not fall under the sway of conceit; venerable sir, one overcome by conceit is always brought to ruin, cast down into the round of existence.”

29.

29.

Māno palālito satto, taṇhāpaṭighasaṅgato;

Makkaṭoragasoṇādi, hutvā jāyati jātisu.

A being who cherishes conceit, attached to craving and aversion, is born in various births, becoming a monkey, a snake, a dog, and the like.

30.

30.

Mā mānaṃ sāmi pūrehi, attānaṃ parisodhaya;

Aparisuddhā sayo bhikkhu, dāyakaṃ na paritosati.

“Lord, do not fill yourself with conceit; purify yourself. A monk who is himself impure does not gratify the donor.”

31.

31.

Dadantānaṃ sarantānaṃ, pūjentānaṃ sace tuvaṃ;

Mahapphalaṃ mahābhūtiṃ, kāmattha hotha nibbaṇāti nimmanā itikattaci.

“If you wish for there to be great fruit and great prosperity for those who give, remember, and honor you, then you should be without defilements.”

Taṃ sutvā thero saṃsāre nibbindo tattheva ṭhito tilakkhaṇaṃ paṭṭhapetvā karajakāyaṃ sammasanto sahapaṭi sambhidāhi arahattaṃ patvāva nikkhami. Tato dhammāsokamahārājā balavāhanaparivuto mahantena pūjāvidhānena paṭimaggaṃ āgamma vanditvā [M 0.0139] tato diguṇaṃ pūjāsakkāraṃ kurumāno mahābhikkhusaṅghena saddhiṃ theraṃ attano nagaraṃ netvā tassa dhammakathaṃ sutvā pasannamānaso pañcasīle patiṭṭhāya mahantaṃ vihāraṃ kāretvā therena sahāgatānaṃ saṭṭhisahassānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ catupaccayehi upaṭṭhāna makāsi, atha thero sāṭṭhakathaṃ piṭakattayaṃ pakāsento tasmiṃ ciraṃ vasitvā tattheva parinibbāyi. Tato rājā sapariso tassa sarīranikkhepaṃ kāretvā dhātuyo gahetvā mahantaṃ cetiyaṃ kārāpesīti.

Hearing that, the elder became disenchanted with saṃsāra. Standing right there, he established the three characteristics and, contemplating the body born of action, attained arahantship together with the analytical knowledges, and then proceeded. Then King Dhammāsoka, surrounded by his army and vehicles, came to meet him on the way with a great ceremony. After paying homage, he made the offerings and honors twice as great and led the elder together with the great Saṅgha of bhikkhus to his own city. Having heard his Dhamma talk, with a confident mind, the king became established in the five precepts and had a great monastery built. He provided the four requisites for the sixty thousand bhikkhus who had come with the elder. Then the elder, proclaiming the Tipiṭaka with its commentaries, dwelt there for a long time and attained final Nibbāna in that very place. Then the king with his retinue had the elder's bodily remains cremated, and taking the relics, he had a great cetiya built.

32.

32.

Purātanānaṃ bhuvi puññakamminaṃ,

Guṇānubhāvena mahenti evaṃ;

Sadevakā naṃ manasīkarontā,

Puññaṃ karothā yatane sadā darāti.

Thus are those of ancient meritorious deeds on earth honored by the power of their virtue. Therefore, paying attention to them along with the devas, you should always make merit with care and respect.

Indaguttattherassa vatthuṃ catutthaṃ.

The fourth story: the Elder Indagutta.

35. Sākhamālapūjikāya vatthumhi ayamānupubbīkathā

35. This is the preliminary discourse in the story of the woman who offered the branch-garland.

Amhākaṃ bhagavā dasapāramiyo pūretvā anukkamena tusitabhavane nibbatto devehi ārādhito sakkarājakule paṭisandhiṃ gahetvā mātukucchito nikkhanto anukkamena paramābhisambodhiṃ patvā tato paṭṭhāya pañcacattālīsasaṃvaccharāni ṭhatvā caturāsītidhammakkhandhasahassāni desetvā gaṇanapathamatīte satte bhavakantārato santāretvā sabbabuddhakiccāni niṭṭhāpetvā kusinārāyaṃ upavattane mallānaṃ sālavane yamakasālāna mantare uttarasīsakaṃ paññatte mañcake vesākhapuṇṇadivase dakkhiṇena passena sato sampajāno anuṭṭhānaseyyāya nipanno pacchimayāme bhikkhū ovaditvā balavapaccū-sa [M 0.0140] samaye mahāpathaviṃkampento anupādisesāya nibbāṇadhātuyā parinibbāyi, nibbute pana bhagavati lokanāthe ānandatthero mallarājūnaṃ etaṃ pavattiṃ ārocesi. Tato kosinārakā ca devabrahmādayo ca sannipatitvā naccagītavāditehi mālāgandhādīhi ca sakkarontā garukarontā mānentā pūjentā celavitānādayo karontā bhagavato sarīraṃ nagaramajjhe yattha makuṭabandhanaṃ nāma mallānaṃ cetiyaṃ, tattha netvā cakkavattissa sarīraṃ viya ahatena vatthena veṭhetvā tato vihatena kappāsena veṭhetvāti evaṃ pañcadussayugasatehi veṭhetvā āyasāya teladoṇiyā pakkhipitvā aññissāya āyasāya doṇiyā paṭikujjitvā sabbagandhānaṃ citakaṃ karitvā bhagavato sarīraṃ citakaṃ āropesuṃ. Atha mahākassapattherena bhagavato pāde sirasā vandite devatānubhāvena citako samantā ekappahāreneva pajjali. Bhagavato pana sarīre daḍḍhe sumanamakuḷasadisā dhātuyo avasissiṃsu. Tasmiṃ kira samaye kosalarañño janapade aññatarā gāmavāsikā itthī bhagavati parinibbute sādhukīḷhaṃ āgacchamānā antarāmagge attano sarīre uppannavātarogena upaddutā sādhukīḷhaṃ sampāpuṇituṃ asakkontī satthu āḷāhanaṃ gantvā bhagavato dhātusarīre tīṇi sākhapupphāni pūjetvā pasannamānasā pañcapatiṭṭhitena vanditvā gatā tāya eva rattiyā majjhimayāme kālaṃ katvā tāvatiṃsabhavane tiṃsayojanike kanakavimāne nibbatti. Tassā pubbakammapakāsanatthaṃ cakkamattāni sākhapupphāni tatta tattha olambanti. Teheva sabbaṃ vimānaṃ ekobhāsī ekobhāsi tato itisabbattha ahosi. Tato sugandhakaraṇḍakaṃ viya ca ahosi, sā pana attano sobhaggappattaṃ attabhāvañca vimānasampadañca parivārasampattiyo ca disvā vimhitamānasā pubbe katena pubbekissame. pubbekatename iticakatthaci [M 0.0141] kena me puññakammenā yaṃ laddhāti olokantī bhagavato dhātusarīrasmiṃ pūjitāni tīṇi sākhapupphāni disvā pasannamānasā mahācakkappamāṇaṃ sākhamālaṃ hatthena dhārentī dhātupūjanatthāya agamāsi. Tadā tattha sannipatitā manussā tassā rūpasampadañca hatte mahantaṃ sākhamālañca disvā vimhitamānasā amma tvaṃ kattha vāsikā. Kattha panimaṃ pupphaṃ paṭiladdhanti pucchiṃsu, taṃ sutvā devadhītā attanā bhagavato dhātusarīrassa pūjitasākhamālattayānubhāvena paṭiladdhasampattiyo ca dibbavimānañca pubbe matakalevaraṃ cāti sabbaṃ tesaṃ dassetvā dhammadesanāvasāne āha.

Our Blessed One, having fulfilled the ten perfections, was in due course reborn in the Tusita heaven. At the request of the devas, he took conception in the royal family of the Sakyans, emerged from his mother’s womb, and gradually attained supreme full enlightenment. From then on, for forty-five years, he taught the eighty-four thousand aggregates of the Dhamma, guiding countless beings across the wilderness of existence and completing all the duties of a Buddha. At Kusinārā, in the Mallas’ Sāla Grove at Upavattana, between a pair of sāla trees, he lay down on a prepared couch with his head to the north. On the full-moon day of Vesākha, he lay on his right side, mindful and fully aware, in the posture from which he would not rise again. During the last watch of the night, he admonished the bhikkhus, and at the approach of dawn, causing the great earth to quake, he attained Parinibbāna with the Nibbāna-element without residue remaining. When the Blessed One, the Lord of the World, had attained Parinibbāna, the Venerable Ānanda informed the Malla kings of this event. Then the people of Kusinārā, along with devas and brahmās, gathered and paid homage to the Blessed One’s body with dance, song, music, garlands, perfumes, and the like, honoring, revering, and worshiping it, and making canopies of cloth. They carried the Blessed One’s body into the center of the city to the Mallas’ shrine named Makuṭabandhana. There they wrapped it in five hundred pairs of cloths—first with new cloth, then with teased cotton—like the body of a universal monarch. They placed it in an iron trough of oil, covered it with another iron trough, built a pyre of all kinds of fragrant wood, and lifted the Blessed One’s body onto it. Then, when the Venerable Mahākassapa had venerated the Blessed One’s feet with his head, the pyre ignited all at once by the power of the devas. When the Blessed One’s body was consumed, relics resembling jasmine buds remained. At that time, a woman from a village in the Kosala country, while on her way to a festival, was afflicted by a wind-disease that arose in her body. Unable to reach the festival, she went to the Master’s cremation ground, offered three branch-flowers to the Blessed One’s bodily relics with a confident mind, paid homage with the five-point prostration, and departed. That very night, in the middle watch, she passed away and was reborn in the Tāvatiṃsa heaven in a golden celestial mansion thirty yojanas in extent. To reveal her past kamma, branch-flowers the size of chariot wheels hung here and there. By these the entire mansion was made radiant, and it became like a casket of perfume. Seeing her own splendid new body, the opulence of her mansion, and her magnificent retinue, she was amazed and wondered, “By what meritorious deed of mine was this obtained?” Looking back, she saw the three branch-flowers she had offered to the Blessed One’s bodily relics. With a confident mind, holding a branch-garland the size of a great wheel in her hand, she went to pay homage to the relics. The people gathered there, amazed at her beautiful form and the great branch-garland in her hand, asked, “Lady, where do you live? And where did you get this flower?” Hearing this, the devadhītā showed them everything: the fortune she had obtained through the power of the three branch-flowers she had offered to the Blessed One’s bodily relics, her divine mansion, and her former dead body. At the conclusion of her Dhamma talk, she said:

1.

1.

Samāgatā bhavantā bho, passantu mama sampadaṃ;

Katamappena kārena, sammāsambuddhadhātuyā.

“Good sirs who have assembled here, behold my good fortune, obtained by a small deed done to the relic of the Perfectly Enlightened One.”

2.

2.

Sākhamālāni tīṇeva, hīyyohaṃ munidhātuyā;

Pūjayitvāna santuṭṭhā, nivattā tāya rattiyā.

“Yesterday I offered just three branch-flowers to the Sage’s relic; having worshipped, content, I departed that night.”

3.

3.

Marantī kharavātena, tamahaṃ sucaritaṃ sariṃ;

Tenāhaṃ puññakammena, tāvatiṃsūpagāahuṃ.

“As I was dying from a harsh wind, I remembered that good deed. By that meritorious kamma, I have come to Tāvatiṃsa.”

4.

4.

Tattha me āsi pāsādo, tiṃsayojanamuggato;

Kūṭāgāravarākiṇṇo, sākhamālāti sādhamālo itipikatthaci, vissuto.

“There I have a palace, thirty yojanas high, filled with excellent gabled chambers, renowned as ‘Sākhamālā’.”

5.

5.

Yathā sabbasugandhehi, karaṇḍaṃ paribhāvitaṃ;

Tathā dibbasugandhehi, gandhitaṃ bhavanaṃ mama.

“Just as a casket is pervaded with all perfumes, so my abode has been scented with divine perfumes.”

6.

6.

Cakkamattā sākhapupphā, tattha tatthūpalambare tatavatthupalabbare itipikatthaci;

Dibbagandhā pavāyanti, madhubbabhanisevitā.

“Chariot-wheel-sized branch-flowers are found here and there; divine fragrances waft, frequented by bees.”

7.

7.

Bhassanti ekapupphasmā, tumbamattā hi reṇavo;

Tehi piñjaritā devā, kīḷanti ca lalantica.

“From a single flower falls pollen the size of gourds; made tawny-gold by it, the devas sport and play.”

8.

8.

Pīḷandhitvāna [M 0.0142] mālādhayā, sudibbā bharaṇānica;

Sahaccharā devaputtā, naccagītādibyāvaṭā.

“Wearing garlands and fine divine ornaments, a thousand celestial youths attend on me, engaged in dancing, singing, and the like.”

9.

9.

Passathemaṃ bhujaṅgā bho, sattā mohena pārutā;

Hīyyo mataṃ paviddhaṃ me, pūtibhūtaṃ kalevaraṃ.

“Sirs, see this: beings are cloaked in delusion. See my corpse, which died and was cast aside yesterday, now putrid.”

10.

10.

Puḷavehi samākiṇṇaṃ, makkhikāgaṇakīḷitaṃ;

Kākasoṇādisattāna, māhāraṃ kuṇapālayaṃ.

“It is overrun with worms, a playground for swarms of flies, a foul carcass, food for beings such as crows and dogs.”

11.

11.

Patthenti purisā pubbe, anekopāyanena taṃ;

Daṭṭhumpidāni nicchanti, taṇhāyaññāṇatā aho.

“Formerly men desired it with many gifts, but now they do not even wish to see it. Alas, such is the ignorance born of craving!”

12.

12.

Lokapajjotakassāhaṃ, vimalassa yasassino;

Dhātuṃ hīyyo mahiṃ sammā, ajja sagge patiṭṭhitā.

“Yesterday I properly honored on earth the relic of the world’s illuminator, the stainless and renowned one. Today I am established in heaven.”

13.

13.

Hitvāne tādisaṃ kāyaṃ, laddhaṃdāni mamedisaṃ;

Dibbattabhāvaṃ sobhāhi, bhāsamāna mudikkhatha.

“Having abandoned a body such as that, I have now obtained this divine existence. Behold me shining forth, radiant with splendor.”

14.

14.

Bhāsamānāya me vācaṃ, suṇothettha samāgatā;

Natthevākatapuññassa, aṇumattaṃ bhave sukhaṃ.

“You who are gathered here, listen to my words as I speak. For one who has not made merit, there is not even an atom’s measure of happiness in this existence.”

15.

15.

Bindumattampi yo puñña, bījaṃ ropeti sāsane;

Na hā natthaphalaṃ nahapanantaphalaṃ itipikatthaci hoti, yāva nibbāṇapattiyāti.

“Whoever plants a seed of merit in the Teaching, even if only a drop, it is never fruitless until the attainment of Nibbāna.”

Evaṃ sā devatā attanā paṭiladdhadibbavibhavaṃ dassetvā janakāyaṃ ovaditvā dibbasākhapupphehi jinadhātuṃ pūjetvā manussānaṃ passantānaṃyeva saddhiṃ vimānena devalokameva agamāsi. Taṃ disvā mahājano dānādīni puññāni katvā devalokaṃ pūresīti.

Thus that deity, having displayed the divine splendor she had obtained, admonished the assembly of people, and worshipped the Victor’s relic with divine sākha flowers, departed with her mansion to the deva world right before the eyes of the human beings. Seeing this, the great crowd performed meritorious deeds such as giving and thereby filled the deva world.

16.

16.

Evañhi [M 0.0143] sā pupphamattena dhātuṃ,

Pūjetva devesu alattha bhūtiṃ;

Tumhepi bhonto tidivesu sātaṃ,

Kāmattha ce kattha puññāni sādhuṃti.

Thus indeed, by worshipping the relic with just a flower, she attained glory among the gods. You too, good sirs, if you desire pleasure in the heavens, should perform meritorious deeds well.

Sākhamālapūjikāya vatthuṃ pañcamaṃ.

The fifth story is that of the Sākha-Garland Worshipper.

36. Moriyabrahmaṇassa vatthumhi ayamānupubbīkathā

36. This is the introductory account in the story of the Moriya brahmin.

Amhākaṃ bhagavati parinibbute magadharaṭṭhe macalaṃ nāma mahāgāmaṃ ahosi. Tattha moriyonāma brāhmaṇo paṭivasati saddho pasanno, tassa senānāme kā bhariyā atthi. Sāpi saddhā pasannā ratanattayesu. Te ubhopi samaggā sammodamānā bhikkhusaṅghaṃ nimantetvā niccaṃ dānaṃ pavattentā cīvarādicatupaccayehi upaṭṭhahantā sīlaṃ rakkhantā uposathakammaṃ karontā divasaṃ vītināmenti. Athassa gehe vibhavaṃ yebhuyyena dānādīsu parikkhayamagamāsi. Tato brāhmaṇī sāmi no gehe dhanaṃ parikkhīṇaṃ. Kathaṃ dānaṃ pavatteyyāmāti brāhmaṇassa ārocesi, tato brāhmaṇo mā bhadde cintesi. Yenakenaci upāyena dānaṃ patiṭṭhapessāmāti vatvā tasmiṃyeva attano saussāhataṃ pakāsonto āha.

After our Blessed One had attained final Nibbāna, there was a great village named Macala in the country of Magadha. A brahmin named Moriya lived there, a man faithful and devout. He had a wife named Senā, who was also faithful and devout toward the Three Jewels. The two of them lived together in harmony and concord. They passed their days regularly inviting the Saṅgha of bhikkhus and providing alms, attending on them with the four requisites such as robes, observing the moral precepts, and performing the Uposatha observance. Then their household wealth became mostly exhausted on account of their giving and other meritorious deeds. The brahmin’s wife then informed the brahmin: “Master, our wealth at home is exhausted. How shall we continue our giving?” The brahmin replied: “Do not worry, my good lady. By some means or other I will re-establish our giving.” Then, displaying his own zeal, he spoke:

1.

1.

Jānamāno hi lokasmiṃ, dānassedaṃ phalaṃ iti;

Na dajjā ko susīlesu, appampi divasampati.

Knowing in the world that 'this is the fruit of giving,' who would not give even a little every day to the virtuous?

2.

2.

Saggalokanidānāni, dānāni matimā idha;

Kohināma naro loke, na dadeyya hite ratoti.

What wise person in this world, delighting in his own welfare, would not give gifts, which are the very cause of the heavenly world?

Evañca pana vatvā bhadde vanaṃ pavisitvā anekavidhāni paṇṇāni ca phalāni ca pacchipūraṃ āharitvā vikkiṇitvāpi dānaṃ na upacchindissāmāti [M 0.0144] vatvā tato paṭṭhāya vanaṃ gantvā paṇṇāni ca phalāni ca āharitvā vikkiṇitvā dānaṃ dento paṭivasati. Athekadivasaṃ brāhmaṇo vanaṃ paviṭṭho paṇṇehi ca phalehi ca pacchiṃ pūretvā sīsenā dāya gehaṃ āgacchanto pupphaphalapallavehi vinataṃ nekatarugaṇanicitaṃ sammattānekacātakacatuppadanisevitaṃ vippakiṇṇānantapupphapattakiñjakkhacchannavāḷukātalaṃ sandamānasītalā malajalappavāhaṃ akaddamāninnasupatittehi sundaraṃ kandaraṃ disvā pacchiṃ tīre ṭhapetvā otiṇṇo nahāyati, tasmiṃ khaṇe tattha ekasmiṃ rukkhe adhivattho devaputto taṃ tattha nahāyantaṃ disvā kinnu kho esa kalyāṇajjhāsayo vā udāhu pāpajjhāsayo sattoti dibbacakkhunā upadhāranto acchariyapuriso eso duggatopi hutvā attano dānappaveṇiyā upacchijjanabhayena vanaṃ gantvā paṇṇāni ca phalāni ca āharitvā dukkhena kasirena jīvikaṃ kappento dānadhammaṃ na upacchindatīti cintetvā tassa guṇādayo paṭicca pacchiyaṃ ṭhapitapaṇṇāni ca phalāni ca sabbāni suvaṇṇāni hontūti adhiṭṭhāsi. Athassānubhāvena sabbaṃ suvaṇṇaṃ ahobhi, atha so suvaṇṇapuṇṇapacchiyaṃ upari suvaṇṇarāsimatthake sabbakāmadadaṃ mahantaṃ maṇiratanaṃ ṭhapetvā antarahito paṭikkamma aṭṭhāsi, tato brāhmaṇo nahātvā uttiṇṇo pacchiyaṃ sampuṇṇasuvaṇṇavaṇṇaraṃsinā sambhinnavijjotamānamaṇiratanaṃ disvā kimetaṃti āsaṅkitaparisaṅkito pacchisamīpaṃ gantvā hatthaṃ pasāretuṃ avisahanto aṭṭhāsi. Taṃ disvā devaputto dissamānasarīrena ṭhatvā mā tvaṃ bhāyi brāhmaṇa. Mayā etāni nimmitāni, gahetvā gacchāhīti āha, atha brāhmaṇo devaputtassa kathaṃ sutvā ayaṃ devaputto imaṃ mayā nimmitaṃ, gahetvā gacchāti vadati. Kinnu kho so attano ānubhāvena deti, udāhu mayā katapuññenāti pucchissāmi taṃti pañjalikova devaputtaṃ pucchanto āha.

And having said this, he added: 'My good lady, I will enter the forest, bring back a basket full of various leaves and fruits, sell them, and not interrupt our giving.' From then on, he would go to the forest, bring back leaves and fruits, sell them, and continue to give alms. Then one day, the brahmin entered the forest, filled his basket with leaves and fruits, and was returning home carrying it on his head. He saw a beautiful ravine, adorned with flowers, fruits, and tender leaves, dense with various kinds of trees, frequented by many kinds of birds and quadrupeds, with a sandy floor covered with countless scattered petals and pollen, with a stream of cool, clear water flowing by, and with lovely, mud-free, gently sloping banks. Placing his basket on the bank, he went down to bathe. At that moment, a deity dwelling in a tree there saw him bathing and wondered: 'Is this being of good or evil disposition?' Observing with his divine eye, he thought: 'This is a wonderful man! Though destitute, for fear that his tradition of giving might be interrupted, he goes to the forest, gathers leaves and fruits, and makes his living with hardship and difficulty, yet he does not cease his practice of giving.' Reflecting on his virtues, the deity made a determination: 'May all the leaves and fruits placed in his basket turn to gold!' By the deity's power, everything became gold. Then, on top of the heap of gold in the gold-filled basket, he placed a great wish-fulfilling jewel that grants all desires, and then he disappeared and stood aside. When the brahmin had bathed and come out of the water, he saw the wish-fulfilling jewel shining, its brilliance mingling with the golden radiance of the full basket. Thinking, 'What is this?' he was filled with doubt and suspicion. He approached the basket but stood there, unable to stretch out his hand. Seeing this, the deity appeared in visible form and said: 'Do not be afraid, brahmin. I have created these. Take them and go.' Hearing the deity’s words, the brahmin thought: 'This deity says he created this for me and that I should take it and go. Is he giving it by his own power, or is it because of merit I have made?' Then, with his hands raised in reverence, he asked the deity:

3.

3.

Pucchāmi [M 0.0145] pañjalī dāni, devaputta mahiddhika;

Dadāsi me suvaṇṇañca, kāmadaṃ maṇimuttamaṃ.

I ask you now with hands clasped, O mighty deity: you give me gold and this best of wish-fulfilling gems.

4.

4.

Nāpi ko no tuvaṃ ñāti,

Na mitto nopakārako;

Kiṃ tvaṃ atthavasaṃ disvā,

Mama dajjāsimaṃ dhanaṃ.

You are no relative of mine, nor a friend, nor a benefactor. Seeing what advantage for yourself do you give me this wealth?

5.

5.

Kena tapena sīlena, kenācāraguṇena ca;

Yena dajjāsi me deva, kiṃ me sucaritaṃ citaṃ.

By what asceticism, what virtue, or what quality of conduct do you give this to me, O deity? What good action of mine has been accumulated?

6.

6.

Kinnu purātanaṃ kammaṃ, kena kammena dassasi;

Athavā taviddhiyā desi, taṃ me akkhāhi pucchitoti.

Is it because of some ancient kamma? By what deed of mine do you give this? Or do you give it by your own power? Since I ask, please explain this to me.

Tato devaputto na kho panāhaṃ brāhmaṇa devoti paresaṃ kiñci dātuṃ sakkomi, tayā pubbe katasucaritānubhāvena nibbattatīti vatvā dibbacakkhunā tassa pubbakammaṃ disvā tassa pakāsento āha.

Then the deity said: 'Brahmin, it is not that I, as a deity, am able to give anything to others. This has arisen through the power of the good deeds you yourself performed in the past.' Then, having seen the brahmin’s past kamma with his divine eye, he revealed it to him, saying:

7.

7.

Kassape lokapajjote, sambuddhe parinibbute;

Sabbattha patthaṭaṃ āsi, tassa buddhassa sāsanaṃ.

When Kassapa, the Light of the World, the Perfectly Enlightened One, had attained final Nibbāna, the Dispensation of that Buddha was spread everywhere.

8.

8.

Tadā paccantime gāme, tvamāsi kuladārako;

Saddho āsi pasanno ca, dāyako kusale rato.

At that time, in a frontier village, you were the son of a good family. You were faithful and devout, a generous donor who delighted in the wholesome.

9.

9.

Tadā pabbajito eko, gacchanto antarāpathe;

Corehi anubaddhosi, acchinnapattacīvaro.

At that time a certain monk, traveling along the road, was pursued by thieves and robbed of his bowl and robes.

10.

10.

Sākhābhaṅgaṃ nivāsetvā, pārupitvā tatheva taṃ;

Antogāmaṃ paviṭṭhosi, esamāno pilotike.

Having put on broken branches as a lower garment and covered himself with the same, he entered the village seeking scraps of cloth.

11.

11.

Tato tvaṃ caramānaṃ taṃ, disvā kampitamānaso;

Vatthayugaṃ adāsi tvaṃ, saddahaṃ dānato phalaṃ.

Then you, seeing him wandering about, were moved with compassion and gave him a pair of cloths, having faith in the fruit of giving.

12.

12.

Patthodanena [M 0.0146] taṃ bhikkhuṃ, parivisitvā yathābalaṃ;

Pesesi abhivādetvā, saddhāya suddhamānaso.

Having served that bhikkhu with a bowl of food according to your ability, you paid homage to him and sent him on his way, with a pure mind and with faith.

13.

13.

Imaṃ tvaṃ akarī puññaṃ, tuyhetaṃ caritaṃ imaṃ;

Tassa te puññakammassa, amukhyaphala mīdisaṃti.

This was the merit you made, this was your conduct. This here is just a preliminary fruit of that meritorious deed of yours.

Evañca pana vatvā idaṃ te brāhmaṇa dhanaṃ rājādīhi mayā anāharaṇīyaṃ kataṃ, tvaṃ aparisaṅkanto gahetvā yathādhippāyaṃ karohi, imaṃ kho pana maṇiratanaṃ icchiticchitaṃ pasavati, tenāpi ānubhāvena tava dānaṃ anupacchindanto puttadārādayo posehīti anusāsi, taṃ sutvā brāhmaṇo tena vuttaniyāmeneva bhikkhusaṅghassa mahādānaṃ dadanto sīlaṃ rakkhanto ciraṃ vasitvā aparabhāge tato cuto devaloke nibbattīti.

Having spoken thus, the deity said: 'Brahmin, I have made this wealth of yours unable to be confiscated by kings and others. Take it without any doubt and do as you wish. Moreover, this wish-fulfilling jewel produces whatever you desire. By its power, continue your giving without interruption and support your wife and children.' Having heard this, the brahmin, in accordance with the deity's instruction, gave great gifts to the Saṅgha of bhikkhus and observed the moral precepts. He lived for a long time and, at the end of his life, he passed away and was reborn in the deva world.

14.

14.

Evaṃ nihīnāpī dhanena santo,

Dānanvayaṃ neva pariccajanti;

Tasmā hi bhonto sati deyyadhamme,

Mā kattha dānesu pamādabhāvaṃti.

Thus the good, even when poor in wealth, never abandon the tradition of giving. Therefore, good sirs, when there is something to be given, do not be negligent in matters of giving.

Moriyabrāhmaṇassa vatthuṃ chaṭṭhamaṃ.

The sixth story is that of the Moriya Brahmin.

37. Puttavatthumhi ayamānupubbīkathā

37. This is the introductory narrative to the Story of the Son.

Ekasmiṃ kira samaye laṅkādīpavāsino saṭṭhimattā bhikkhū jayamahābodhiṃ vanditukāmā ekato mantetvā mahātitthena nāvaṃ āruyha jambudīpaṃ patvā tāmalittipaṭṭhene tamalittapaṭṭane itikatthaci otaritvā anukkamena pāṭaliputtanagaraṃ pāpuṇiṃsu. Atha tasmiṃ [M 0.0147] nagare piṇḍāya carante te bhikkhū eko duggatamanusso disvā cirena cirenāhaṃdiṭṭhā itisabbattha mayā diṭṭhā buddhaputtāti somanasso bhariyaṃ pakkositvā bhadde imesaṃ ayyānaṃ dānaṃ dātukāmomhi, pubbe no akatapuññattā idāni duggatā jātā, imesu puññakkhettesu bījaṃ no ce ropessāma, punapi evameva bhavissāmāti vatvā kiṃ me gehe deyyadhammaṃ atthīti pucchi. Sā taṃ sutvā ghare no sāmi ayyānaṃ kiñci dātabbaṃ na passāmi. Api ca mama puttaṃ māretvā dānaṃ dātuṃ sakkāti. So tassā kathaṃ sutvā bhadde puttaṃ māretvā kiṃ dānaṃ demāti āha. Tāya taṃ sutvā sāmi kiṃ na jānāsi, putte no mate sandiṭṭhasambhattā ñātimittasuhajjā ca amhākaṃ santikaṃ āgacchāntā kiñci paṇṇākāraṃ gahetvā āgacchanti. Mayaṃ tena paṇṇākārena dānaṃ dassāmāti vutte upāsako sādhu tathā karohīti mātuyā eva bhāramakāsi, sā puttaṃ māretuṃ avisahantī āha. Tathā hi.

Once, it is said, about sixty monks dwelling on the island of Laṅkā, wishing to pay homage to the Jayamahābodhi tree, consulted together and boarded a ship at Mahātittha. Reaching Jambudīpa, they disembarked at the port of Tāmalitti and in due course arrived at the city of Pāṭaliputta. While those monks were wandering for alms in that city, a poor man saw them and, filled with joy, thought: “After a long time, I have seen the sons of the Buddha!” He called his wife and said: “Good lady, I wish to give alms to these venerable ones. Because we made no merit in the past, we have now been born poor. If we do not sow a seed in these fields of merit, we will be just like this again.” Then he asked: “What gift-worthy item do we have in our house?” Hearing this, she replied: “Master, I see nothing in our house suitable to offer to the venerable ones. However, it is possible to give alms by killing our son.” Hearing her words, he said: “Good lady, what alms can we give by killing our son?” She replied: “Master, do you not know? When our son is dead, our kinsmen, friends, and companions who are well-acquainted and intimate with us will come to us bringing some presents. With those presents, we can give alms.” When she said this, the layman replied: “Good, do so,” and placed the burden on the mother. But she, unable to kill her son, said: “For:”

1.

1.

Kicchā laddhaṃ piyaṃ puttaṃ, ammammāti piyaṃ vadaṃ;

Sunīlanettaṃ subhamuṃ, ko pakkamitumicchati.

“A beloved son obtained with difficulty,

who sweetly says, ‘Mama, mama’;

with lovely dark eyes and a beautiful face—

who would wish to do away with him?”

2.

2.

Mātarā māriyantopi, mātarameva rodati;

Māretuṃ taṃ na sakkomi, hadayaṃ me pavedhatīti.

“Even while being killed by his mother,

he would cry out only for his mother.

I cannot kill him;

my heart trembles.”

Evañca pana vatvā ahaṃ sāmi na sakkomi puttaṃ māretuṃ. Tvaṃ mārehīti puttaṃ pitusantikaṃ pesesi. Atha sopi taṃ māretu masakkonto evamāha. Vuttañhi.

And having said this—“Master, I cannot kill my son. You kill him!”—she sent the son to his father. Then he too, being unable to kill him, said this. For it has been said:

3.

3.

Tāyanti pituno dukkhaṃ, puttā puttāti kittitā;

Pitu dukkhaṃ sukhaṃ puttā, dāyādā honti sabbadā.

“Because they protect from a father’s suffering,

children are acclaimed as ‘puttā.’

Children are a father’s sorrow and his joy;

they are always his heirs.”

4.

4.

Tasmā [M 0.0148] me sadisaṃ puttaṃ, pillakaṃ mañjubhāsanaṃ;

Na sakkomi ahaṃ bhadde, jīvitā taṃ viyojituṃ.

“Therefore this son of mine, this child,

this sweet-spoken boy,

I cannot, good lady,

deprive him of his life.”

5.

5.

Ayasañca akittiñca, pappoti puttaghātako;

Pāṇātipātakammampi, kāmaṃ so phusate naroti.

“A child-killer gains

disgrace and ill-repute;

that man certainly incurs

the kamma of destroying life.”

Evañca pana vatvā so tvameva tava puttaṃ mārehīti pesesi, evaṃ tena vutte putthassa mārapāṇūpāyaṃ pariyesantā evamāhaṃsu, amhe panimaṃ māretuṃ na sakkomi, amhākaṃ pacchāgehe mahanto vammiko atthi, tasmiṃ eko nāgarājā paṭivasati. Kumāraṃ tattha pesessāma, so taṃ ḍasitvā māressatīti. Iccete eso kho upāyo evāti cintetvā kumāraṃ pakkositvā añcanā valivalayādīhi maṇḍetvā tassa hatthe geṇḍuṃ teṇḍuṃ itisabbattha ṭhapetvā tāta pacchāgehe vammikasamīpaṃ gantvā kīḷāti pesesuṃ. Tato dārako gantvā geṇḍukena kīḷanto vammikabile geṇḍukaṃ pātesi. Atha so geṇḍukaṃ gaṇhissāmīti vammikasusire hatthaṃ pavesesi. Tato sappo kujjhitvā susūtisaddaṃ karonto mahantaṃ phaṇaṃ katvā bilato sīsaṃ ukkhipitvā olokento aṭṭhāsi kumārassa hatthato parigalitapāsaṃviya. Athassa kumāro kiñci ajānanto sappassa gīvaṃ daḷhaṃ gaṇhi. Athassa mātāpitunnaṃ saddhābalena nāgarājā kumārassa karatale aṭṭhaṃsaṃ icchādāyakaṃ kaṇṭhamaṇiratanaṃ pātesi. Kumārassa mātāpitaro dvāraṃ nissāya ṭhitā tassa kiriyaṃ olokento taṃ maṇiratanaṃ disvā sīghaṃ gantvā puttaṃ ukkhipitvā hatthato maṇiratanaṃ gaṇhiṃsu. Tato te taṃ maṇiratanaṃ parisuddhāsane ṭhapetvā upacāraṃ katvā amhākaṃ idañcidañca dethāti abbhukkiriṃsu. Atha te maṇiratanānubhāvena gehadvāre mahantaṃ maṇḍapaṃ kāretvā vitānādinā maṇḍapaṃ [M 0.0149] alaṅkaritvā bhikkhūnaṃ āsanāni paññāpetvā te saṭṭhimatte bhikkhū nisīdāpetvā mahādānaṃ adaṃsu. Tato nagaravāsino maṇiratanānubhāvaṃ sutvā sannipatiṃsu. Atha te tesaṃ majjhe attano saddhābalena maṇiratanassa lābhaṃ pakāsetvā imañhi dānatthāyeva pariccajjāmāti ekasmiṃ ṭhāne patiṭṭhāpetvā tenānubhāvena yāvajīvaṃ dānaṃ dadantā sīlaṃ rakkhantā āyupariyosāne devaloke nibbattiṃsūti.

And having said this, he sent word: “You yourself kill your son!” When this was said, seeking a means to kill the boy, they said to each other: “We cannot kill him. But in the back of our house there is a large anthill where a nāga king dwells. We will send the boy there; he will bite him and kill him.” Thinking, “This is indeed the stratagem!”, they called the boy, adorned him with kohl, bracelets, and the like, placed a ball in his hand, and sent him off, saying: “My dear, go and play near the anthill in the back of the house.” The child went and, while playing with the ball, dropped it into the anthill’s hole. Then, thinking, “I will get the ball,” he put his hand into the opening of the anthill. The serpent, enraged, making a hissing sound, spread its great hood, raised its head from the hole, and stood looking, like a noose that had slipped from the boy’s hand. Then the boy, not knowing anything, firmly grasped the serpent’s neck. Then, by the power of his parents’ faith, the nāga king dropped an eight-faceted, wish-fulfilling neck-jewel into the boy’s palm. The boy’s parents, who were standing by the door watching his actions, saw the jewel, ran quickly, picked up their son, and took the jewel from his hand. Then they placed the jewel on a clean seat, paid homage to it, and sprinkled it, saying: “Give us this and that!” Then, by the power of the jewel, they had a great pavilion built at their house door, adorned it with canopies and so on, prepared seats for the monks, seated the sixty-odd monks, and gave a great offering of alms. Then the city-dwellers, hearing of the power of the jewel, gathered together. In their midst, the couple proclaimed that they had obtained the jewel through the power of their own faith, and establishing it in one place, they said: “We dedicate this for the purpose of giving alms.” By its power, they gave alms and observed the moral precepts for the rest of their lives, and at the end of their lifespan, they were reborn in the world of the devas.

6.

6.

Chetvāna pemaṃ api atrajesu,

Dadanti dānaṃ idha mānusevaṃ;

Na dadāti ko nāma naro samiddho,

Dānañhi dānassa phalaṃ sarantoti.

“Having cut off love even for their own children,

thus do people here give gifts.

What prosperous person, then, would not give,

remembering the fruit of giving?”

Puttavatthuṃ sattamaṃ.

The Story of the Son: The Seventh.

38. Tebhātikamadhuvāṇijakānaṃ vatthumhi ayamānupubbīkathā

38. This is the sequential narrative in the story of the three brother honey merchants.

Atīte kira bārāṇasiyaṃ te bhātikā ekato hutvā madhuṃ vikkiṇantā puttadāre posenti. Tato tesu eko paccantaṃ gantvā malayavāsīnaṃ malavāsīnaṃ itipikatthaci hatthato madhuṃ kiṇitvā vakkiṇitvā itisabbattha gaṇhāti, eko gahitagahitamadhuṃ nagaraṃ āharati. Eko tena āhaṭāhaṭamadhūni bārāṇasiyaṃ nisīditvā vikkiṇāti. Tasmiṃ samaye gandhamādanapabbate eko paccekabuddho vaṇarogenā turo ahosi. Athaññataro paccekabuddho tassa madhunā phāsu bhavissatīti ñatvā gandhamādanapabbateyeva ṭhito [M 0.0150] cīvaraṃ pārupitvā ākāsenā gantvā nagaradvāre otaritvā kattha madhuṃ labhāmīti labbhati itisabbattha olokento aṭṭhāsī, tadā tasmiṃparakule bhatiṃ katvā jīvamānā ekā ceṭikā ghaṭamādāya udakatthaṃ titthaṃ gacchantī maggā okkamma ghaṭaṃ ṭhapetvā vanditvā ekamantaṃ aṭṭhāsi, tadā paccekabuddho bhaginī ettha bhikkhāya carantānaṃ katarasmiṃ ṭhāne madhu labbhatīti pucchi. Sā tassa kathaṃ sutvā madhuāpaṇassa paññāyanaṭṭhāne ṭhatvā hatthaṃ pasāretvā esa bhante madhuāpaṇoti dassetvā yajjāyaṃ paccekabuddho āpaṇato madhuṃ na labhati. Mama nivatthavatthakaṃ datvāpi madhuṃ dassāmīti cintetvā olokentī tattheva aṭṭhāsi, atha paccekabuddho anupubbena vicaranto caranto itipikatthaci madhuāpaṇaṃ sampāpuṇi, tato kuṭimbiko kuṭimba ko itipikatthaci taṃ disvā hatthato pattaṃ gahetvā ādhārake ṭhapetvā madhughaṭaṃ ādāya pattassa upanāmento sahasā nikkujji. Tato madhu pattaṃ pūretvā uttaranto puna bhūmiyaṃ pagghari. Taṃ disvā somanasso vāṇijo evaṃ patthanamakāsi.

In the past, it is said, in Bārāṇasī, three brothers, having come together, sold honey and supported their wives and children. Then one of them went to the border region and bought honey from the inhabitants of Malaya. Another carried the honey that had been acquired to the city. The third sat in Bārāṇasī and sold the honey that had been brought there. At that time, on Mount Gandhamādana, a paccekabuddha was afflicted with a skin disease. Then another paccekabuddha, knowing that he would find relief with honey, while standing right there on Mount Gandhamādana, put on his robe and traveled through the sky. Descending at the city gate, he stood looking around, thinking, 'Where can I obtain honey?' At that time, a certain maidservant who made a living by working for hire in another family took a water pot and was going to the bathing place for water. Stepping aside from the path, she set down the pot, paid homage, and stood to one side. Then the paccekabuddha asked her, 'Sister, in which place can honey be obtained by those wandering for alms?' Hearing his words, she stood at a place from which the honey shop was visible, stretched out her hand, and said, 'Venerable sir, this is the honey shop,' pointing it out. Thinking, 'If this paccekabuddha does not obtain honey from the shop, I will give him honey even if it means giving my lower garment,' she stood there watching. Then the paccekabuddha, wandering in due course, reached the honey shop. Seeing him, the householder took the bowl from his hand, placed it on a stand, and took the honey pot. As he was bringing it to the bowl, it suddenly overturned. Then the honey, filling the bowl and overflowing, spilled onto the ground again. Seeing this, the delighted merchant made this aspiration:

Vuttañhi mahāvaṃse.

For it is said in the Mahāvaṃsa:

1.

1.

Tattha pattassa buddhassa, vāṇijo so pasādavā;

Vissandayanto mukhato, pattapūraṃ madhuṃ adā.

To the Buddha who had arrived, that faithful merchant gave a bowlful of honey, letting it stream from the mouth of the pot.

2.

2.

Puṇṇañca uppatītañca, patitañca mahītale;

Disvā madhuṃ pasanno so, evaṃ paṇidahī tadā.

Seeing the honey that filled the bowl, that which had overflowed, and that which had fallen on the ground, he was pleased and then made this aspiration:

3.

3.

Jambudīpe ekarajjaṃ, dānenānena hotu me;

Ākāse yojane āṇā, bhūmiyaṃ yojanepiti.

By this gift, may I have sole sovereignty in Jambudīpa; may my command extend for a yojana in the sky and for a yojana upon the earth.

Evañca pana vatvā pattaṃ adāsi, paccekabuddho pattaṃ paṭiggahetvā tattheva ṭhito.

And having spoken thus, he gave the bowl. The paccekabuddha, having accepted the bowl, stood right there.

4.

4.

Icchitaṃ [M 0.0151] patthitaṃ tuyhaṃ, khippameva samijjhatu;

Pūrentu citthasaṃkappā, cando paṇṇaraso yathā.

May what you wish and long for be quickly accomplished; may the resolves of your mind be fulfilled, like the moon on the fifteenth day.

5.

5.

Icchitaṃ patthitaṃ tuyhaṃ, sabbameva samijjhatu;

Pūrentu cittasaṃkappā, maṇijotiraso yathāti.

May everything you wish and long for be accomplished; may the resolves of your mind be fulfilled, like the wish-fulfilling gem.

Vatvā maṅgalaṃ vaḍḍhetvā agamāsi. Athantarāmagge ṭhitā ghaṭaceṭikā paccekabuddhābhimukhaṃ gantvā madhuṃ labhittha bhanteti pucchi. Tena laddhaṃ bhaginīti vutte kiṃ vatvā so adāsīti pucchi. Paccekabuddho sabbaṃ kathesi. Sā taṃ sutvā thokaṃ bhante idheva hotha dāsiyā anuggahatthāyāti sīghaṃ gehaṃ gantvā nivatthapiḷotikā attano sāṭakaṃ dhovitvā āharitvā cumbaṭakaṃ katvā paccekabuddhassa adāsi, yadā so bhante madhudāyako sakalajambudīpe ekarajjaṃ kāreti. Tadāhaṃ tassa aggamahesī bhaveyyaṃti vatvā patthanaṃ karontīevamāha.

Having spoken auspicious words and pronounced a blessing, he departed. Then the maidservant with the pot, who was standing on the way, approached the paccekabuddha and asked, 'Venerable sir, did you obtain honey?' When he replied, 'I obtained it, sister,' she asked, 'What was said that he gave it?' The paccekabuddha recounted everything. Hearing this, she said, 'Venerable sir, please wait here a moment for the sake of helping this servant.' Quickly going home, she washed her own outer garment, brought it back, made it into a folded pad, and gave it to the paccekabuddha. Making an aspiration, she said: 'Venerable sir, when that giver of honey exercises sole sovereignty over all of Jambudīpa, may I then become his chief queen.' She spoke thus:

6.

6.

Yadā te madhudo bhante, bhūbhujo hoti bhūtale;

Tassa hessaṃ tadā bhante, piyā aggamahesikā.

Venerable sir, when that honey-giver becomes a ruler on the earth, then, venerable sir, may I be his beloved chief queen.

7.

7.

Surūpāca suvāṇīca, suyasā subbatā subhā;

Assaṃ tassa piyācātha, manāpā icchadā icchidā itipikatthaci sadāti.

Beautiful in form, sweet of voice, of good repute, virtuous, and lovely, may I be dear and agreeable to him, always granting his wishes.

Tassāpi tadā paccekabuddho tatheva hotūti maṅgalaṃ vatvā ākāsena gandhamādanameva agamāsi, athāparabhāge te tayopi ekato hutvā madhulokanaṃ karontā taṃ madhughaṭaṃ kuhiṃti pucchiṃsu, so tenattanā katakammaṃ vatvā sace tumhe tasmiṃ pattiṃ anumodeyyātha, taṃ sādhu. No ce. Madhuagghanakaṃ mama hatthato gaṇhathāti vatvā tehi tato na no attho nanohattho itisabbattha madhunā, kīdisassetaṃ adāsīti vutte taṃ sutvā itaro paccekabuddhā nāme te gandhamādane vasanti kāsāvaṃ [M 0.0152] pārupitvā kule kule bhikkhaṃ caranti, santo ete sīlavantāti kathesi, atha tesu jeṭṭho brāhmaṇacaṇḍālakāpi kāsāvaṃ paridahitvā caranti. Nūnāyaṃ caṇḍālakoti maññāmīti āha, majjhimo kujjhitvā tava paccekabuddhaṃ parasamudde khipāhīti avoca, atha tesaṃ kathaṃ sutvā madhudāyako mā bho tumhe ariyānaṃ mahesakkhānaṃ mahānubhāvānaṃ paccekabuddhānaṃ pharusaṃ kathetha. Nirayadukkhā na bhāyathātiādinā anekā kārena nivāretvā tesaṃ guṇaṃ pakāsesi, taṃ sutvā te ubhopi sādhūti pasannācittā anumodiṃsu, aparabhāge te kālaṃ katvā devamanussesu saṃsarantā tattha tattha mahāsampattiyo anubhavitvā amhākaṃ satthu parinibbāṇato dvinnaṃ vassasatānaṃ accayena attano attano sampattaṭṭhāne nibbattiṃsu. Tena vuttaṃ.

Then to her too the paccekabuddha, after speaking the auspicious words, 'May it be so,' departed through the sky to Mount Gandhamādana. Later, the three brothers, coming together and looking for the honey, asked, 'Where is that honey pot?' He told them the deed he had done, saying, 'If you rejoice in the merit of this, that is good. If not, take the price of the honey from my hand.' When they said, 'We have no need for the honey. To what sort of person did you give it?', he heard this and explained: 'They are called paccekabuddhas. They dwell on Mount Gandhamādana, wear saffron robes, and wander for alms from house to house. They are peaceful and virtuous.' Then the eldest among them said, 'Brahmins and outcastes also wear saffron robes and wander about. I think this one was surely an outcaste.' The middle one became angry and said, 'Throw your paccekabuddha into the ocean across!' Hearing their words, the honey-giver, restraining them in many ways, praised the virtues of the paccekabuddhas, saying: 'Sirs, do not speak harshly of the noble, mighty, and powerful paccekabuddhas. Do you not fear the suffering of hell?' Hearing this, both of them, with pleased minds, rejoiced in the merit, saying, 'Good!' Later, after they passed away, they wandered among gods and humans, experiencing great prosperity in various places. Two hundred years after our Teacher’s Parinibbāna, they were reborn in their respectively attained stations. Therefore it was said:

8.

8.

Asoko madhudo sandhi, mittādevī tu ceṭikā;

Caṇḍālavādī nigrodho, tisso so pāravādikoti.

The connection is as follows: Asoka was the honey-giver, and Mittādevī the maidservant; Nigrodha was the one who spoke of an outcaste, and Tissa was the one who spoke of the far shore.

Tesu caṇḍālavādī jeṭṭhavāṇijo bindusārarañño jeṭṭhaputtassa sumanarājakumārassa putto hutvā nibbatti. Tassāyamānupubbīkathā. Bindusārarañño kira dubbalakāleyeva asokakumāro attanā laddhaṃ ujjeniyā rajjaṃ pahāya āgantvā sabbanagaraṃ attano hatthagataṃ katvā sumanarājakumāraṃ aggahesi. Taṃ divasameva sumanassa rājakumārassa sumanā nāma devī paripuṇṇagabbhā ahosi. Sā aññātakavesena nikkhamitvā avidūre aññataraṃ caṇḍālagāmaṃ sandhāya gacchantī jeṭṭhakacaṇḍālassa gehato avidūre nigrodho atthi, tasmiṃ rukkhe adhivatthāya devatāya ito ehi sumaneti vadantiyā saddaṃ sutvā tassā samīpaṃ gatā, devatā attano ānubhāvena ekaṃ sālaṃ nimmiṇitvā ettha vasāhīti pādāsi. Sā [M 0.0153] taṃ sālaṃ pāvisi, gatadivaseyeva sā puttaṃ vijāyi. Sā tassa nigrodhadevatāya pariggahitattā nigrodhotveva nāmaṃ akāsi. Jeṭṭhakacaṇḍālo diṭṭhadivasatoppabhuti taṃ attano sāmidhītaraṃ viya maññamāno nibaddhavattaṃ paṭṭhapesi. Rājadhītā tattha sattavassāni vasi, nigrodhakumāropi sattavassiko jāto, tadā mahāvaruṇatthero nāma eko arahā dārakassa hetusampadaṃ disvā viharamāno sattavassiko dāni dārako. Kālo naṃ pabbājetuṃti cintetvā rājadhītāya ārocāpetvā nigrodhakumāraṃ pabbājesi, kumāro khuraggeyeva arahattaṃ pāpuṇi. Tena vuttaṃ mahāvaṃse.

Among them, the eldest merchant, the one who spoke of an outcaste, was reborn as the son of Prince Sumanā, the eldest son of King Bindusāra. This is his sequential narrative. It is said that during the time of King Bindusāra’s illness, Prince Asoka, abandoning the viceroyalty of Ujjenī that he had received, came and took the entire city into his own hands and had Prince Sumanā killed. On that very day, Prince Sumanā’s consort, the lady Sumanā, was at the end of her pregnancy. She left in the guise of an unknown person and, not far away, went towards a certain outcaste village. Near the house of the chief outcaste there was a banyan tree. Hearing the voice of a deity dwelling in that tree saying, 'Come here, Sumanā!', she went near it. The deity, by its own power, created a hall and gave it to her, saying, 'Dwell here.' She entered that hall, and on the very day she arrived, she gave birth to a son. Because he was protected by the banyan-tree deity, she gave him the name Nigrodha. From the day he saw her, the chief outcaste, regarding her as his master’s daughter, established a regular service for her. The princess lived there for seven years, and Prince Nigrodha also became seven years old. At that time, an arahant named the Elder Mahāvaruṇa, seeing the boy’s endowment with the conditions for attainment, reflected: 'The boy is now seven years old. It is time to ordain him.' Having informed the princess, he ordained Prince Nigrodha. The prince attained arahantship at the very touch of the razor. Thus it is said in the Mahāvaṃsa:

9.

9.

Taṃ mahāvaruṇo thero, tadā disvā kumārakaṃ;

Upanissayasampannaṃ, arahā pucchi mātaraṃ;

Pabbājesi khuragge so, arahattamapāpuṇīti.

The Elder Mahāvaruṇa, an arahant, seeing the boy at that time endowed with supporting conditions, asked his mother. He ordained him, and at the very touch of the razor, he attained arahantship.

So kira ekadivasaṃ pātova sarīraṃ paṭijaggitvā ācariyupajjhāyavattaṃ katvā pattacīvara mādāya mātuupāsikāya gehadvāraṃ gacchāmīti nikkhami. Mātunivāsaṭṭhānañcassa dakkhiṇadvārena nagaraṃ pavisitvā nagaramajjhena gantvā pācīnadvārena nikkhamitvā gantabbaṃ hoti. Tena ca samayena asoko dhammarājā pācīnadisābhimukho sīhapañjare caṅkamati. Taṃ khaṇaṃyeva nigrodho rājaṅgaṇaṃ sampāpuṇi santindriyo santamānaso yugamattaṃ pekkhamāno, tena vuttaṃ ekadivasaṃ sīhapañjare ṭhito addasa nigrodhaṃ sāmaṇeraṃ rājaṅgaṇena gacchantaṃ dantaṃ guttaṃ santindriyaṃ iriyāpathasampannanti. Disvā panassa etadahosi. Ayaṃ jano sabbopi vikkhittacitto bhantamigapaṭibhāgo. Ayaṃ pana daharako avikkhitto ativiyassa ālokitavilokitaṃ sammiñjanapasāraṇañca sobhati. Addhā etassabbhantare lokuttaradhammo bhavissatīti rañño saha [M 0.0154] dassaneneva sāmaṇere cittaṃ pasīdi, pemaṃ saṇṭhahi, kasmā. Pubbe kira puññakaraṇakāle rañño jeṭṭhakabhātā vāṇijakoyaṃ.

It is said that one day, early in the morning, having taken care of his body and performed his duties towards his teachers and preceptor, he took his bowl and robe and set out, thinking, 'I will go to the house of my mother, the lay devotee.' His mother's residence was to be reached by entering the city through the south gate, going through the middle of the city, and leaving by the east gate. At that time, the righteous king Asoka was walking back and forth in the lion-windowed chamber, facing east. At that very moment, Nigrodha arrived at the royal courtyard, with tranquil faculties, with a peaceful mind, looking a yoke's length ahead. Thus it was said: 'One day, while standing in the lion-windowed chamber, he saw the novice Nigrodha walking through the royal courtyard, tamed, guarded, with tranquil faculties, and endowed with perfect deportment.' Having seen him, this thought occurred to the king: 'All these other people have distracted minds, like startled deer. But this young boy is undistracted; his looking ahead and looking around, his bending and stretching, are very graceful. Surely, within him there must be the supramundane Dhamma.' As soon as the king saw the novice, his mind became confident and affection for him was established. Why? It is said that in the past, at a time of making merit, this one had been the king's elder brother, a merchant.

10.

10.

Pubbeva pubbena itikatthaci sannivāsena, paccuppannahitena vā;

Evaṃ taṃ jāyate pemaṃ, uppalaṃva yathodaketi.

From having lived together in the past, or from a benefit in the present, thus is affection born, like a lotus from the water.

Atha rājā sañjātapemo sabahumāno sāmaṇeraṃ pakkosathāti amacce pesesi, te aticirāyantīti puna dve tayo pesesi turitaṃ āgacchatūti. Sāmaṇero attano pakatiyā eva agamāsi. Rājā patirūpāsanaṃ ñatvā nisīdathāti āha. So itocito ca viloketvā natthi dāni añño bhikkhūti samussitasetacchattaṃ rājapallaṅkaṃ upasaṅkamitvā pattaṃ gaṇhanatthāya rañño ākāraṃ dassesi. Rājā taṃ pallaṅkasamīpaṃ gacchantaṃ eva disvā cintesi ajjeva dāni ayaṃ sāmaṇero imassa gehassa sāmiko bhavissatīti, sāmaṇero rañño hatthe pattaṃ datvā pallaṅkaṃ abhiruhitvā nisīdi. Rājā attano atthāya sampāditaṃ sabbaṃ yāgukhajjakabhattavikatiṃ upanāmesi. Sāmaṇero attano yāpanamattameva sampaṭicchi. Bhattakiccāvasāne rājā āha satthārā tumhākaṃ dinnaovādaṃ jānāthāti. Jānāmi mahārāja ekadesenāti. Tāta mayhampī naṃ kathehīti. Sādhu mahārājāti rañño anurūpaṃ dhammapaeda appamādavaggaṃ anumodanatthāya abhāsi. Rājā pana appamādo amatapadaṃ, pamādo maccuno padaṃti sutvāva aññātaṃ tāta, pariyosāpehīti āha. Sāmaṇero anumodanā vasāne dvattiṃsadhuvabhattāni dvittiṃsadhurabhattāni itisabbattha labhitvā puna divase dvattiṃsabhikkhū gahetvā rājantopuraṃ pavisitvā bhattakicca makāsi. Rājā aññepi dvattiṃsabhikkhū tumhehi saddhiṃsve bhikkhaṃ gaṇhantūti eteneva upāyena divase [M 0.0155] divase vaḍḍhāpento saṭṭhisahassānaṃ brāhmaṇaparibbājakānaṃ bhattaṃ upacchinditvā antonivesane saṭṭhisahassānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ niccabhattaṃ paṭṭhapesi nigrodhattheragateneva pasādena. Nigrodhattheropi rājānaṃ saparisaṃ tīsu saraṇesu pañcasu sīlesu patiṭṭhāpetvā buddhasāsane pothujjanikena pasādena acalappasādaṃ katvā patiṭṭhāpesi. Rājāpi asokārāmaṃ nāma mahāvihāraṃ kārāpetvā saṭṭhisahassānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ niccabhattaṃ paṭṭhapesi. Sakalajambudīpe caturāsītiyā nagarasahassesu caturāsītivihārasahassāni kārāpesi. Tena vuttaṃ.

Then the king, filled with affection and great respect, sent his ministers to summon the novice. When they were taking too long, he sent two or three more, urging, 'Let him come quickly!' The novice, however, went at his own natural pace. The king, perceiving a suitable seat, said, 'Please sit down.' The novice looked around and, seeing no other bhikkhu, approached the royal throne with its raised white parasol and made a sign to the king to take his bowl. Seeing the novice approaching the throne, the king thought, 'Today this novice will become the master of this house.' The novice, having given his bowl into the king's hands, ascended the throne and sat down. The king offered him all the various kinds of gruel, hard food, and rice that had been prepared for himself. The novice accepted only as much as was sufficient for his sustenance. At the conclusion of the meal, the king asked, 'Do you know the instruction given by the Teacher?' 'I know it in part, great king,' he replied. 'Dear one, tell it to me too.' 'Very well, great king,' he said, and as an expression of gratitude, he recited a Dhamma passage from the chapter on heedfulness, suitable for the king. But the king, upon hearing, 'Heedfulness is the path to the Deathless, heedlessness is the path of death,' said, 'I have understood, dear one. Please finish it.' At the end of the expression of gratitude, the novice received thirty-two permanent food portions. The next day, taking thirty-two bhikkhus, he entered the royal palace and took his meal. The king said, 'Let another thirty-two bhikkhus take alms with you tomorrow.' In this way, increasing the number day by day, he discontinued the meals for sixty thousand brahmins and wanderers and established a regular meal for sixty thousand bhikkhus inside the palace, through the confidence inspired by the Elder Nigrodha. The Elder Nigrodha also established the king and his retinue in the three refuges and the five precepts, making their ordinary person's faith into an unshakable faith in the Buddha's Dispensation. The king, too, had the great monastery called the Asokārāma built and established a regular meal for sixty thousand bhikkhus. Throughout all of Jambudīpa, in eighty-four thousand cities, he had eighty-four thousand monasteries built. Thus it is said.

11.

11.

Caṇḍālavādidosena,

Jāto caṇḍālagāmake;

Pattānumodanā pākā,

Āse so aseso itipikatthaci hi anāsavoti.

Due to the fault of abusing an outcaste, he was born in an outcaste village; but through the fruit of rejoicing in an offering, he became one without taints.

Ayaṃ nigrodhattherassa kathānayo.

This is the course of the story of the Elder Nigrodha.

Madhudāyako pana vāṇijo devalokato cavitvā pupphapure rājakule uppajjitvā piyadāso nāma kumāro hutvā chattaṃ ussāpetvā sakalajambudīpe ekarajjaṃ akāsi. Kathaṃ.

But the merchant, the honey-giver, having passed away from the deva world, was reborn in the royal family in Pupphapura. Becoming the prince named Piyadāsa and raising the white parasol, he exercised sole sovereignty over all of Jambudīpa. How so?

Bindusārarājassa ekasataputtā ahesuṃ. Te sabbe asoko attanā saddhiṃ ekamātikaṃ tissakumāraṃ ṭhapetvā ghātesi. Ghātento cattāri vassāni anabhisittova rajjaṃ kāretvā catunnaṃ vassānaṃ accayena tathāgatassa parinibbāṇato dvinnaṃ vassasatanaṃ upari aṭṭhārasame vasse sakalajambudīpe ekarajjābhisekaṃ pāpuṇi. Atha taṃ sakalajambudīpe caturāsītinagarasahasse rājāno āgantvā upaṭṭhahissanti. Tiṇṇaṃ utūnaṃ anucchavikā tayo pāsādā ahesuṃ. Eko mahāsappiko[M 0.0156], eko moragīvo, eko maṅgalo nāma, tesu nekanāṭakasahassaparivuto paṭivasati. Yāssa madhuāpaṇaṃ dassesi, sā asandhimittā nāma devaccharaparibhāgā rājadhītā hutvā saṭṭhisahassānaṃ itthīnaṃ jeṭṭhikā dhammāsokarañño aggamahesī ahosi. Abhisekānantaraṃ tassa imā rājiddhiyo āgatā, pathaviyā ca heṭṭhā yojanappamāṇe āṇā pavattati. Tathā upari ākāse. Anotattadahato aṭṭhahi kājehi soḷasapānīyaghaṭe divase divase devatā āharanti. Yato sāsane uppannasaddho hutvā aṭṭhaghaṭe bhikkhusaṅghassa adāsi. Dve ghaṭe saṭṭhimattānaṃ tepiṭakabhikkhūnaṃ dve ghaṭe aggamahesiyā asandhimittāya. Cattāro ghaṭe attanā paribhuñci devatā devatāevaṃ itisabbattha, eva himavantato nāgalatādantakaṭṭhaṃ siniddhaṃ mudukaṃ rasavantaṃ divase divase āharanti, tena rañño ca mahesiyā ca soḷasannaṃ nāṭakasahassānañca saṭṭhimattānañca bhikkhusahassānaṃ devasikaṃ dantapoṇakiccaṃ nipphajjati. Devasikamevassa devatā agadāmalakaṃ agadaharīṭakaṃ suvaṇṇavaṇṇañca gandharasasampannaṃ ambapakkaṃ āharanti. Chaddantadahato pañcavaṇṇaṃ nivāsanapārupanaṃ pītakavaṇṇaṃ hatthapuñchanakapaṭaṃ hatthapucchanakapaṭṭaṃ itisabbattha dibbañca pānaṃ āharanti. Devasikamevassa anulepanagandhaṃ pārupanatthāya asuttamayikaṃ sumanapupphapaṭaṃ mahārahañja añjanaṃ nāgabhavanato nāgarājāno āharanti. Chaddantadahatoyeva uṭṭhitassa sālino navavāhasahassani divase divase suvā āharanti. Te mūsikā nitthusakāni karonti. Ekopi khaṇḍataṇḍulo nāhosi. Rañño sabbaṭṭhānesu ayameva taṇḍulo paribhogaṃ gacchati. Madhumakkhikā madhuṃ karonti. Kammārasālāsu acchā kūṭaṃ paharanti. Karavīkasakuṇā āgantvā madhurassaraṃ vikūjentā rañño balikammaṃ karonti. Imāhi iddhīhi samannāgato rājā ekadivasaṃ suvaṇṇasaṅkhalikabandhanaṃ [M 0.0157] pesetvā catunnaṃ buddhānaṃ adhigatarūpadassanaṃ kappā yukaṃ mahākālanāgarājānaṃ ānayitvā setacchattassa heṭṭhā mahārahe pallaṅke nisīdāpetvā anekasata vaṇṇehi jalajathalajapupphehi suvaṇṇapupphehi ca pūjaṃ katvā sabbā laṅkārapatimaṇḍitehi soḷasahi nāṭakasahassehi samantato parikkhipitvā anantañāṇassa tāva me saddhammavaracakkavattino sammāsambuddhassa sammāsambuddharūpaṃ itisabbattha rūpaṃ imesaṃ akkhīnaṃ āpāthaṃ karohīti vatvā tena nimmitaṃ sakalasarīre vippakiṇṇapuññappabhāvanibbattāsīti anubyañjanapatimaṇḍita dvattiṃsa mahāpurisalakkhaṇa sassirīkatāya vikacakamaluppalapuṇḍarīkapatimaṇḍitamiva salilatalaṃ tārāgaṇaraṃsijālavisaravipphuritasobhāsamujjalamiva gaganatalaṃ nīlapītalohitā dibhedaṃ vicittavaṇṇaraṃsivinaddhabyāmappabhāparikkhepavilāsitāya sañcyāppabhānurāgaindadhanuvijjullatāparikkhittamiva kaṇakagirisikharaṃ nānāvirāgavimalaketumālāsamujjalitacārumatthakasobhanaṃ nayanarasāyanamiva ca brahmadevamanujanāgayakkhagaṇānaṃ buddharūpaṃ passanto sattadivasāni akkhipūjaṃ nāma akāsi. Rājā kira abhisekaṃ pāpuṇitvā tīṇiyeva saṃvaccharāni bāhirakapāsaṇḍaṃ parigaṇhi, catutthe saṃvacchare buddhasāsane pasīdi, tassa pana pitā bindusāro brāhmaṇabhatto ahosi. So brāhmaṇānañca brāhmaṇajātipāsaṇḍānaṃ paṇḍaraṅgaparibbājakānañca saṭṭhisahassamattānaṃ niccabhattaṃ paṭṭhapesi. Asokopi pitarā pavattitaṃ dānaṃ attano antopure antepure itisabbattha tatheva dadamāno ekadivasaṃ sīhapañjare ṭhito te upasamaparibāhirena ācārena bhuñjamāne asaṃyatindriye avinītairiyāpathe disvā cintesi, īdisaṃ dānaṃ upaparikkhitvā yuttaṭṭhāne dātuṃ vaṭṭatīti. Evaṃ cintetvā amacce āha, gacchatha bhaṇe attano attano sādhusammate samaṇabrāhmaṇe antopuraṃ abhiharatha dānaṃ dassāmīti, amaccā sādhu devāti rañño [M 0.0158] paṭissutvā te te paṇḍaraṅgaparibbājakā jīvaka nigaṇṭhādayo ānetvā ime mahārāja amhākaṃ arahantoti āhaṃsu, atha rājā antopure uccāvacāni āsanāni paññāpetvā āgacchantūti vatvā āgatāgate āha attano anurūpe āsane nisīdathāti, ekacce bhaddapīṭhakesu ekacce phalakapīṭhakesu nisīdiṃsu, taṃ disvā rājā natthi etesaṃ anto sāroti ñatvā tesaṃ anurūpaṃ khādanīyabhojanīyaṃ datvā uyyojesi. Evaṃ gacchante kāle ekadivasaṃ sīhapañjare ṭhito nigrodhasāmaṇeraṃ disvā tasmiṃ gatena pasādena buddhasāsane pasanno saṭṭhisahassamatte pāsaṇḍiye apanetvā saṭṭhisahassamatte bhikkhū bhojento buddhasāsane pasīditvā asokārāmaṃ kāretvā tattha te vasāpento ekadivasaṃ asokārāme saṭṭhisahassabhikkhūnaṃ dānaṃ datvā tesaṃ majjhe nisajja saṅghaṃ catūhi paccayehi pavāretvā imaṃ pañhaṃ pucchi, bhante bhagavatā desitadhammo nāma kittako hotīti, mahārāja nava aṅgāni, khandhato caturāsītidhammakkhandhasahassānīti. Rājā dhamme pasīditvā ekekaṃ dhammakkhandhaṃ ekekavihārena pūjessāmīti ekadivasameva channavutikoṭikhanaṃ vissajjetvā amacce āṇāpesi, ekamekasmiṃ nagare ekamekaṃ vihāraṃ kārentā caturāsītiyā nagarasahassesu caturāsītivihārasahassāni kārāpethāti, sayañca asokārāme asokamahāvihāratthāya sayaṃcaasokahovihāratthāya itipikatthaci kammaṃ paṭṭhapesi, saṅgho indaguttattheraṃ nāma mahiddhikaṃ mahānubhāvaṃ khīṇāsavaṃ navakammādhiṭṭhāyakaṃ adāsi, thero yaṃ yaṃ na niṭṭhāti, taṃ taṃ attano ānubhāvena niṭṭhāpesi, evaṃpitīhi saṃvaccharehi vihārakammaṃ niṭṭhāpesi, ekadivasameva sabbanagarehi paṇṇāni āgamiṃsu, amaccā rañño ārocesuṃ niṭṭhitāni deva caturāsītimahāvihārasahassānīti. Atha rājā [M 0.0159] bhikkhusaṅghaṃ upasaṅkamitvā bhante mayā caturāsītivihārasahassāni kāritāni, dhātuyo kuto lacchāmīti pucchi, mahārāja dhātunidhānaṃ nāma atthīti suṇoma, na pana paññāyati asukaṭṭhāneti. Rājā rājagahe cetiyaṃ bhindāpetvā dhātuṃ apassanto paṭipākatikaṃ kārāpetvā bhikkhubhikkhuṇiyo upāsakaupāsikāyoti catasso parisā gahetvā vesāliyaṃ gato. Tatrāpi alabhitvā kapilavatthuṃ gato, tatrāpi alabhitvā rāmagāmaṃ gato, rāmagāme nāgā cetiyaṃ bhindituṃ nādaṃsu. Cetiye nipatitakuddālo khaṇḍāvaṇḍaṃ hoti, evaṃ tatrāpi alabhitvā allakappaṃ pāvaṃ kusināraṃti sabbacetiyāni bhinditvā dhātuṃ alabhitvā paṭipākatikāni katvā rājagahaṃ gantvā catasso parisā sannipātāpetvā atthi kenaci sutapubbaṃ asukaṭṭhāne dhātunidhānaṃti pucchi. Tattheko vīsaṃvassa satiko thero asukaṭṭhāne dhātunidhānaṃti na jānāmi, mayhaṃ pana pitāmahatthero mayi sattavassikāle mālācaṅgoṭakaṃ gāhāpetvā ehi sāmaṇera asukagacchantare pāsāṇathūpo atthi. Tattha gacchāmāti gantvā pūjetvā imaṃ ṭhānaṃ upadhāretuṃ vaṭṭati sāmaṇerāti āha. Ahaṃ etameva jānāmi mahārājāti āha. Rājā etadeva ṭhānaṃti vatvā gacche harāpetvā pāsāṇathūpaṃ paṃsuṃca apanetvā heṭṭhā sudhābhūmiṃ addasa, tato sudhā ca iṭṭhakāyo ca harāpetvā anupubbena pariveṇaṃ oruyha sattaratanavālikaṃ asihatthāni ca kaṭṭharūpakāni samparivattantāni addasa, so yakkhadāsake pakkosāpetvā balikammaṃ kārāpetvāpi neva antaṃ passanto devatā namassamāno ahaṃ imā dhātuyo gahetvā caturāsītivihārasahasse nidahitvā sakkāraṃ karomi. Mā me devatā antarāyaṃ karontūti āha, sakko devarājā cārikaṃ caranto taṃ disvā vissakammaṃ āmantetvā tāta asokadhammarājā dhātuyo [M 0.0160] nīharissāmīti pariveṇaṃ otiṇṇo. Gantvā kaṭṭharūpāni nīharāpehīti. So pañcacūlakagāmadārakavesena gantvā rañño purato dhanukahattho ṭhatvā hāremi mahārājāti āha, hara tātāti saraṃ gahetvā sandhimhiyeva vijjhi, sabbaṃ vippakirīyittha, atha rājā āviñjane aviñcine itipikatthaci bandhakuñcikamuddikaṃ gaṇhi, maṇikkhandhaṃ passitvā anāgate daḷiddarājāno imaṃ maṇiṃ gahetvā dhātūnaṃ sakkāraṃ karontūti paṇṇe akkharāni disvā kujjhitvā mādisānaṃ pana rājūnaṃ rājānaṃ itisabbattha daḷiddarājāti vattuṃ ayuttaṃti punappunaṃ ghaṭetvā dvāraṃ vivaritvā antogehaṃ paviṭṭho aṭṭhārasavassādhikānaṃ dvinnaṃ vassasatānaṃ upari āropitadīpā tatheva pajjalanti, nīluppalapupphāni taṃkhaṇaṃyeva āharitvā āropitāni viya pupphasanthāro taṃ khaṇaṃ santhato viya gandhā taṃ muhuttaṃ piṃsitvā ṭhapitā viya ahesuṃ. Rājā suvaṇṇapaṭṭaṃ gahetvā anāgate piyadāso nāma kumāro chattaṃ ussāpetvā asoko nāma dhammarājā bhavissati, so imā dhātuyo vitthāritā karissatīti vācetvā diṭṭhohaṃ ayyena mahākassapattherenāti vatvā vāmahatthaṃ ābhujitvā dakkhiṇahatthena apphoṭesi. So tasmiṃ ṭhāne paricaraṇakadhātumattameva ṭhapetvā sesadhātuyo sabbā gahetvā dhātugharaṃ pubbe pihitanayeneva pidahitvā sabbā yathā pakatiyāyeva kāretvā uparipāsāṇacetiyaṃ patiṭṭhāpetvā caturāsītiyā vihārasahassesu dhātuyo patiṭṭhāpesi. Athekadivasaṃ rājā vihāraṃ gantvā bhikkhusaṃghaṃ vanditvā ekamante nisinno yadi bhante channavutikoṭidhanaṃ vissajjetvā caturāsītivihārasahassānisacetiyāni kārāpetvāpi ahaṃ na dāyādo. Añño ko dāyādoti, paccayadāyako nāma tvaṃ mahārāja, yo pana attano puttañca dhītarañca pabbājeti, ayaṃ sāsane dāyādo nāmāti evaṃ vutte [M 0.0161] asoko rājā sāsane dāyādabhāvaṃ patthayamāno avidūre ṭhitaṃ mahindakumāraṃ disvā sakkhissasi tāta tvaṃ pabbajituṃti āha. Kumāro pakatiyā pabbajitukāmo rañño vacanaṃ sutvā ativiya pāmojjajāto pubbajāmi pabbajjāmi itisabbattha deva. Maṃ pabbājetvā sāsane dāyādo hothāti āha. Tena ca samayena rājadhītā saṃghamittāpi tasmiṃ ṭhāne ṭhitā hoti. Taṃ disvā āha tvampi amma pabbajituṃ sakkhissasīti, sādhu tātāti sampaṭicchi. Rājā puttānaṃ manaṃ labhitvā pahaṭṭhacitto bhikkhusaṃghaṃ upasaṅkamitvā bhante ime dārake pabbājetvā maṃ sāsane dāyādaṃ karothāti. Saṅgho rañño vacanaṃ sampaṭicchitvā kumāraṃ moggaliputtatissattherena upajjhāyena mahādevattherena ācariyena pabbajāpesi pabbajjāpesi itisabbattha. Majjhantikattherena ācariyena upasampādesi, so upasampadāmālakeyeva saha paṭisambhidāhi arahattaṃ pāpuṇi. Saṅghamittāyapi rājadhītāya ācariyāṇī āyupālattherī nāma acāriyā āyupālattherīnāma itisabbattha. Upajjhāyā dhammapālattherī nāma ahosi. Rājā pana anekākārena buddhasāsanaṃ sobhetvā moggaliputtatissattherassa sahāyena sahayyena. sāhāyyena. sāhayyena itipikatthaci saṭṭhisahassamatte dussīle titthiye buddhasāsanā uppabbājetvā tatiyadhammasaṃgītiṃ niṭṭhāpesi. Tasmiṃ kira samāgame bhikkhubhikkhuṇiyo kittakāti kittakānīti itisabbattha, vuttañhi.

King Bindusāra had one hundred and one sons. Asoka killed all of them, setting aside only Prince Tissa, his brother by the same mother. While killing them, he ruled the kingdom for four years without being consecrated. After the lapse of four years, in the year two hundred and eighteen after the Tathāgata’s final Nibbāna, he attained the consecration of sole sovereignty over all of Jambudīpa. Then kings from the eighty-four thousand cities in all of Jambudīpa came and attended upon him. He had three palaces suitable for the three seasons: one named Mahāsappika, one Moragīva, and one Maṅgala. He resided in them, surrounded by many thousands of female dancers. The princess who had shown him the honey shop, named Asandhimittā, who had the splendor of a celestial nymph, became the chief queen of King Dhammāsoka, the foremost among sixty thousand women. Immediately after his consecration, these royal supernormal powers came to him: his command prevailed for a yojana below the earth and likewise in the sky. Every day, deities brought sixteen pitchers of water from Lake Anotatta on eight carrying-poles. After he gained faith in the Dispensation, he gave eight pitchers to the Saṅgha of bhikkhus, two pitchers to the sixty bhikkhus who were masters of the Tipiṭaka, and two pitchers to his chief queen Asandhimittā. He himself used the remaining four pitchers. Likewise, every day deities brought from the Himalayas nāgalatā tooth-sticks that were smooth, soft, and flavorful. With these, the daily task of cleaning the teeth was accomplished for the king, the queen, the sixteen thousand female dancers, and the sixty thousand bhikkhus. Daily, deities also brought for him medicinal myrobalan and medicinal harītakī fruits, and ripe mangoes that were golden in color and endowed with fragrance and taste. From Lake Chaddanta they brought five-colored under-robes and outer-robes, yellow cloth for wiping the hands, and a divine beverage. Every day, from the Nāga realm, the Nāga kings brought for him fragrances for anointing, a priceless cloth of sumana flowers not woven from thread to be worn as a robe, and valuable collyrium. Daily, parrots brought nine thousand vāhas of sālī rice that grew by Lake Chaddanta. Mice would husk this rice without producing a single broken grain. This very rice was used by the king on all occasions. Honeybees made honey. In the forges, bears wielded the hammers. Karavīka birds would come and, warbling with sweet voices, make an offering to the king. Endowed with these supernormal powers, the king one day sent a golden chain and had the great Nāga king Mahākāla, who had a lifespan of a kappa and had seen the forms of the four Buddhas, brought to him. Having had him seated on a priceless couch beneath a white parasol, he made offerings with many hundreds of kinds of aquatic and terrestrial flowers and with golden flowers, and had him surrounded on all sides by sixteen thousand female dancers adorned with every kind of ornament. Then he said: “Please make the form of the Perfectly Enlightened One—the unsurpassed wheel-turner of the good Dhamma, the one of infinite knowledge—come into the range of these my eyes!” Seeing the Buddha-form created by the Nāga king—adorned with the thirty-two marks of a great man and the eighty minor characteristics, produced by the radiance of merit diffused throughout his entire body; like a water surface adorned with blooming lotuses, blue water-lilies, and white lotuses; like the expanse of the sky, brilliant with the beauty of the spreading net of rays from the host of stars; graced by the charm of its surrounding fathom-wide halo interwoven with variegated rays of blue, yellow, and red; like a golden mountain peak encircled by a rainbow and flashes of lightning; with a beautiful crest resplendent with a garland of diverse and immaculate banners; and like an elixir for the eyes of the hosts of Brahmās, devas, humans, nāgas, and yakkhas—the king performed what is called the “eye-offering” for seven days. It is said that after attaining his consecration, the king supported the outside sects for only three years; in the fourth year, he gained faith in the Buddha’s Dispensation. His father, Bindusāra, had been a devotee of brahmins. He had established a perpetual food offering for sixty thousand brahmins, heretics of brahmin birth, and Paṇḍaraṅga wanderers. Asoka, too, continued to give in his inner palace the same donation established by his father. One day, standing at a lion-window, he saw them eating with conduct devoid of peacefulness, with unrestrained sense faculties and undisciplined deportment. He thought: “It is proper to investigate a gift like this and give it in a worthy place.” Having thought thus, he said to his ministers: “Go, sirs, and bring to the inner palace the ascetics and brahmins that you yourselves hold in high esteem; I will give a donation.” Replying, “Very well, Your Majesty,” the ministers brought Paṇḍaraṅga wanderers, Ājīvakas, Nigaṇṭhas, and others, and said: “These, great king, are our arahants.” Then the king had high and low seats prepared in the inner palace and said, “Let them come.” To each one who came, he said: “Sit on a seat appropriate for you.” Some sat on fine chairs, others on wooden planks. Seeing this, the king knew, “There is no inner substance in these people.” He gave them suitable hard and soft food and then dismissed them. As time was passing, one day, while standing at the lion-window, he saw the novice Nigrodha. Inspired with confidence by the novice’s serene deportment, he became pleased with the Buddha’s Dispensation. He dismissed the sixty thousand heretics and began to feed sixty thousand bhikkhus. Having gained faith in the Buddha’s Dispensation, he had the Asokārāma built and made the bhikkhus reside there. One day, after giving a donation to sixty thousand bhikkhus in the Asokārāma, he sat down in their midst, invited the Saṅgha to request any of the four requisites, and then asked this question: “Venerable sirs, how extensive is the Dhamma taught by the Blessed One?” They replied: “Great king, it has nine divisions. In terms of aggregates, there are eighty-four thousand Dhamma-aggregates.” Pleased with the Dhamma, the king resolved: “I will honor each Dhamma-aggregate with a monastery.” On a single day he disbursed ninety-six crores and commanded his ministers: “Have eighty-four thousand monasteries built in the eighty-four thousand cities, one monastery in each city.” He himself initiated the work at the Asokārāma for the Great Asoka Monastery. The Saṅgha appointed the elder named Indagutta, an arahant of great psychic power and might, to be the supervisor of the new construction. Whatever was not being completed, the elder had it completed by his own power. Thus, the work on the monasteries was completed in three years. On a single day, letters arrived from all the cities, and the ministers informed the king: “Your Majesty, the eighty-four thousand great monasteries are finished.” Then the king approached the Saṅgha of bhikkhus and asked: “Venerable sirs, I have had eighty-four thousand monasteries built. From where will I obtain relics?” They replied: “Great king, we have heard that there is a relic depository, but it is not known where it is located.” The king had a cetiya in Rājagaha broken open, but seeing no relics, he had it restored. Then, taking the fourfold assembly—bhikkhus, bhikkhunīs, laymen, and laywomen—he went to Vesāli. Finding none there either, he went to Kapilavatthu. Finding none there either, he went to Rāmagāma. In Rāmagāma, the Nāgas did not allow the cetiya to be broken open; a pickaxe that fell upon the cetiya shattered into pieces. Finding none there either, he went to Allakappa, Pāvā, and Kusinārā. Having broken open all the cetiyas and found no relics, he had them restored to their original state, went to Rājagaha, had the fourfold assembly convened, and asked: “Has anyone ever heard that there is a relic depository in such-and-such a place?” There, an elder who was a hundred and twenty years old said: “I do not know of a relic depository in any particular place. However, when I was seven years old, my grandfather, a great elder, had me take a flower-basket and said: ‘Come, novice, in a certain thicket there is a stone stūpa. Let us go there.’ After we went and paid homage, he said: ‘Novice, you should remember this place.’ This is all I know, great king.” Saying, “This is the very place,” the king had the thicket cleared. He had the stone stūpa and the soil removed and saw a plastered surface below. Then, having had the plaster and bricks removed, he descended gradually into the chamber and saw sand of the seven kinds of jewels and revolving wooden figures with swords in their hands. He summoned yakkha servants and had them make an offering, but still seeing no end to the machines, he paid homage to the deities and said: “I will take these relics, deposit them in the eighty-four thousand monasteries, and pay homage to them. May the deities not create an obstacle for me.” Sakka, king of the devas, who was wandering on tour, saw this and addressed Vissakamma: “My dear, the Dhamma-king Asoka has descended into the chamber, intending to remove the relics. Go and have the wooden figures removed.” Vissakamma went in the guise of a village boy with five top-knots, stood before the king with a bow in hand, and said: “I will remove them, great king.” The king said, “Remove them, my dear.” Vissakamma took an arrow and shot it right at the mechanism’s joint. Everything was scattered. Then the king took the key-ring that was tied to a fan. He saw a block of jewels and an inscription on a leaf which read: “In the future, poor kings may take this jewel and pay homage to the relics.” He became angry, thinking, “It is improper to call a king like me a ‘poor king’!” Striving again and again, he opened the door and entered the inner chamber. Lamps that had been lit over two hundred and eighteen years ago were still burning just as they were. Blue lotus flowers looked as if they had just been brought and arranged. The carpet of flowers looked as if it had just been spread. The perfumes were as if they had just been ground. The king took a golden plaque and had it read: “In the future, a prince named Piyadāsa will raise the parasol and become the Dhamma-king named Asoka. He will distribute these relics.” Saying, “I have been foreseen by the noble elder Mahākassapa!” he bent his left arm and snapped the fingers of his right hand. Leaving in that place just enough relics for the local worship, he took all the remaining relics. He sealed the relic chamber in the same way it had been sealed before, had everything restored to its original condition, and re-established the stone cetiya on top. Then he had the relics enshrined in the eighty-four thousand monasteries. Then one day the king went to the monastery, paid homage to the Saṅgha of bhikkhus, and sat down to one side. He asked: “Venerable sirs, if I am not an heir to the Dispensation even after spending ninety-six crores of wealth and having eighty-four thousand monasteries with cetiyas built, then who is an heir?” They replied: “You, great king, are a donor of requisites. But one who has his own son and daughter go forth into homelessness, this one is called an heir of the Dispensation.” Hearing this, King Asoka, desiring to become an heir of the Dispensation, saw Prince Mahinda standing nearby and asked: “Dear son, would you be able to go forth?” The prince, who by his nature wished to go forth, was overjoyed on hearing the king’s words and said: “I will go forth, Your Majesty. By having me go forth, you will become an heir of the Dispensation.” At that time the princess Saṅghamittā was also standing there. Seeing her, the king asked: “And you, my dear daughter, would you be able to go forth?” She consented, saying, “Very well, dear father.” The king, having received his children’s consent, approached the Saṅgha of bhikkhus with a delighted mind and said: “Venerable sirs, ordain these children and make me an heir of the Dispensation.” The Saṅgha accepted the king’s request and had the prince go forth with the elder Moggaliputtatissa as his preceptor and the elder Mahādeva as his teacher. He received the higher ordination with the elder Majjhantika as his teacher. Within the ordination boundary itself, he attained arahantship together with the analytical knowledges. For the princess Saṅghamittā, her teacher was the elder nun named Āyupālā, and her preceptor was the elder nun named Dhammapālā. The king, having glorified the Buddha’s Dispensation in many ways, with the assistance of the elder Moggaliputtatissa, had sixty thousand immoral heretics expelled from the Buddha’s Dispensation and brought the Third Dhamma Council to completion. At that gathering, it is said, how many bhikkhus and bhikkhunīs were there? For it has been said:

12.

12.

Tasmiṃ samāgame āsuṃ, asītibhikkhukoṭiyo;

Ahesuṃ satasahassāni, tesu khīṇāsavā yatī.

At that gathering were eighty crores of bhikkhus; among them were a hundred thousand restrained ones who had destroyed the taints.

13.

13.

Navutisatasahassāni, ahū bhikkhuṇiyo tahiṃ;

Khīṇāsavā sikkhuṇiyo, sahassaṃ āsu tāsu cāti.

Ninety hundred thousand bhikkhunīs were present there; among them were a thousand bhikkhunīs who had destroyed the taints.

Evaṃ [M 0.0162] so asoko dhammarājā sakalajambudīpe aggarājā hutvā buddhasāsanaṃ sobhento vihāsi. Ayaṃ panettha saṅkhepo, vitthāro pana mahāvaṃse vuttoti. Vuttañhi.

Thus King Asoka, the righteous ruler, having become the foremost king over all of Jambudīpa, reigned, glorifying the Buddha's dispensation. This is a summary here; the full account, however, is stated in the Mahāvaṃsa. For it has been said:

14.

14.

Sampuṇṇattā ayaṃ tisso, cetanāyo madhuppado;

Sabbattha sabbadā sabba, sampattimabhisambhuṇīti.

Because of their completeness, these three volitions are producers of what is sweet; everywhere, always, in every respect, they bring about complete success.

Majjhimo pana vāṇijo attano pāravādidosena parasamudde laṅkāyaṃ nibbatti. Tassevaṃ kathāpaṭipāṭi veditabbā. Tambapaṇṇidīpe kira muṭasīvo nāma rājā saṭṭhivassāni rajjaṃ kāresi, tassa puññapaññāguṇopetā aññamaññaṃ hitesino dasa puttā ahesuṃ. Dve ca dhītaro. Sabbe te samaggā sammodamānā vasanti. Athāparasmiṃ samaye amaccā muṭasīvaraññe kālakate devānaṃpiyatissakumāraṃ abhisiñciṃsu, abhisekasamakālamevassa anekāni acchariyāni ahesuṃ. Tāni pakāsentā mahāvaṃsakathācariyā āhaṃsu.
