The merchant Majjhima, however, due to his fault of speaking ill of others, was reborn in Laṅkā across the sea. The sequence of his story should be understood thus. It is said that in the island of Tambapaṇṇi, a king named Muṭasīva reigned for sixty years. He had ten sons, endowed with the qualities of merit and wisdom, who were well-wishers of one another, and two daughters. All of them lived together in harmony and joy. Then, on a later occasion, when King Muṭasīva had passed away, the ministers consecrated Prince Devānaṃpiyatissa. At the very time of his consecration, many wonders occurred. The teachers of the Mahāvaṃsa, making these known, said:

15.

15.

Devānaṃpiyatissoti, vissuto dutiyo suto;

Tesu ahosimuṭasīvassa, dasaputtesupuññavā itikatthaci bhātusu sabbesu, puññapaññādhiko ahu.

The second son, renowned as Devānaṃpiyatissa, was foremost among all those brothers in merit and wisdom.

16.

16.

Devānaṃpiyatisso so, rājāsi pituaccaye;

Tassābhisekena samaṃ, bahūnacchariyānahū.

That Devānaṃpiyatissa became king after his father's passing; together with his consecration, many wonders occurred.

17.

17.

Laṅkādīpamhi sakale, nidhayo ratanānica;

Antoṭhitāni uggantvā, pathavītalamāruhuṃ.

Throughout the entire island of Laṅkā, treasures and jewels lying within rose up and ascended to the earth's surface.

18.

18.

Laṅkādīpasamīpamhi, bhinnanāvā gatāni ca;

Tatra jātāni ca thalaṃ, ratanāni samāruhuṃ.

Jewels that had come from shipwrecks near the island of Laṅkā, and those produced there, ascended to the land.

19.

19.

Chātapabbatapādamhi, tisso ca veḷuyaṭṭhiyo;

Jātā rathapatodena, samānā parimāṇato.

At the foot of Mount Chāta, three bamboo stalks arose, equal in size to a chariot-goad.

20.

20.

Tāsu [M 0.0163] ekā latāyaṭṭhi, rajatābhā tahiṃ latā;

Suvaṇṇavaṇṇā rucirā; Dissantetā manoramā.

Among them, one was a creeper-stalk of silvery lustre; on it was a beautiful creeper of golden color, appearing delightful.

21.

21.

Ekā kusumayaṭṭhītu; Kusumāni tahiṃpana;

Nānāni nānāvaṇṇāni; Dissantetiphuṭāni ca.

One was a flower-stalk, and on it were flowers of various kinds and various colors, appearing fully blossomed.

22.

22.

Ekā sakuṇayaṭṭhī tu, tahiṃpakkhimigā bahū;

Nānā ca nānāvaṇṇā ca, sajīvāviya dissare.

And one was a bird-stalk; on it were many birds and beasts of various kinds and various colors, and they appeared as if alive.

23.

23.

Hayagajarathā malakyā, valayaṅguliveṭhakā ceva;

Kakudhaphalā pākatikā, icce tā aṭṭha jātiyā.

Horse-pearls, elephant-pearls, chariot-pearls, āmalaka-pearls, bracelet-pearls, finger-ring-pearls, kakudha-fruit-pearls, and natural pearls—these were the eight kinds.

24.

24.

Muttā samuddā uggantvā; Tīre vaṭṭiviyaṭṭhitā;

Devānaṃpiyatissassa; Sabbaṃ puññavijambhitaṃ.

Pearls, having emerged from the ocean, stood in heaps upon the shore; it was all the full manifestation of the merit of Devānaṃpiyatissa.

25.

25.

Indanīlaṃ veḷuriyaṃ, lohitaṅkamaṇī ci me;

Ratanāni pane tāni, muttā tā tāca yaṭṭhiyo;

Sattāhabbhantareyeva, rañño santikamāharuṃti.

Sapphires, beryls, and rubies; those gems, those pearls, and those stalks—they brought them all into the king's presence within just seven days.

Tena ca samayena devānaṃpiyatissamahārājā ca asoko dhammarājā ca addiṭṭhasahāyā honti. Tasmā so etāni ratanāni ca aññāni bahūni upāyanāni mama sahāyassa dethāti dhammāsokamahānarindassa paṇṇākāratthāya pesesi. Sopi taṃ disvā pasīditvā pañcarājakakudhabhaṇḍāni ca aññañca bahupaṇṇākārañca abhisekatthāya pesesi. Mayhaṃ sahāyaṃ abhisekaṃ karontūti. Na kevalañcetaṃ āmisapaṇṇākāraṃ. Imaṃ kira dhammapaṇṇākārampi pesesi.

At that time, the great king Devānaṃpiyatissa and the Dhamma King Asoka were friends who had not seen each other. Therefore, the former sent these jewels and many other presents to the great king Dhammāsoka as a gift, saying, 'Give these to my friend.' Having seen them, the latter was pleased and sent the five royal insignia and many other gifts for the consecration, saying, 'Let them consecrate my friend.' And it was not only this material gift; it is said he also sent this gift of the Dhamma:

26.

26.

Ahaṃ buddhaṃca dhammaṃca; Saṃghaṃca saraṇaṃ gato;

Upasakattaṃ vedesiṃ; Sakyaputtassa sāsane.

I have gone for refuge to the Buddha, the Dhamma, and the Saṅgha; I have declared myself a lay follower in the Dispensation of the Sakyan Son.

27.

27.

Imesu tīsu vatthūsu; Uttamesu naruttama;

Cittaṃ pasādayitvāna, saddhāya saraṇaṃ vajāti.

O best of men, in these three supreme things, having gladdened your mind, go for refuge with faith.

Amaccā [M 0.0164] puna laṅkamāgamma rājānaṃ abhisiñciṃsu, tena kho pana samayena moggaliputtatissatthero kattha nukho anāgate sāsanaṃ suppatiṭṭhitaṃ bhaveyyāti upaparikkhanto paccantime suppatiṭṭhitaṃ bhavissatīti ñatvā te te there tattha tattha pesetvā mahāmahindattheraṃ gantvā tambapaṇṇidīpaṃ pasādehīti niyojesi, sakko ca devānamindo mahāmahindattheraṃ upasaṃkamitvā kālakato bhante muṭasīvo rājā. Idāni devānaṃpiyatissamahārājā rajjaṃ kāreti. Sammāsambuddhena ca tumhe byākatā anāgate mahindo nāma bhikkhu tambapaṇṇidīpaṃ pasādessatīti. Tasmā tiha kho bhante kālo dīpavaraṃ gamanāya, ahampi sahāyo bhavissāmīti, thero tassa vacanaṃ sampaṭicchitvā attasattamo cetiyapabbatavihārato vehāsaṃ uppatitvā anurādhapurassa puratthimadisāya missakapabbate patiṭṭhahi, imaṃ etarahi cetiyapabbatotipi sañjānanti. Tadā tambapaṇṇiyaṃ ussavadivaso hoti, rājā chaṇaṃ karothāti amacce āṇāpetvā cattāḷīsasahassapurisehi parivārito nagaramhā nikkhamitvā missakapabbataṃ pāyāsi migavaṃ kīḷitukāmo. Atha tasmiṃ pabbate adhivatthā ekā devatā rañño there dassessāmīti rohitamīgavaṇṇena avidūre tiṇapaṇṇāni khādamānā viya carati, rājā ayuttaṃ dāni pamattaṃ vijjhituṃti jiyaṃ poṭhesi, migo ambatthalamaggaṃ gahetvā palāyituṃ ārabhi, rājā taṃ anubandhanto ambatthalameva abhiruhi, migopi therānaṃ avidūre antaradhāyi, mahindatthero rājānaṃ avidūre āgacchanta maṃyeva rājā passatu, mā itareti adhiṭṭhahitvā tissa tissa ito ehīti āha, rājā taṃ sutvā cintesi. Imasmiṃ tambapaṇṇidīpe jāto maṃ tissoti nāmaṃ gahetvā ālapituṃ samattho nāma natthi. Ayaṃ pana chinnabhinnapaṭadharo bhaṇḍukāsāva vasano maṃ nāmenā lapati, ko nukho yaṃ bhavissati manusso amanusso vāti. Thero āha.

The ministers, having returned to Laṅkā, consecrated the king again. Now at that time, the Elder Moggaliputtatissa, investigating where the Dispensation would be well-established in the future, and knowing that it would be well-established in the borderlands, sent various elders to various places. Approaching the great Elder Mahinda, he enjoined him, saying, 'Go and gladden the island of Tambapaṇṇi.' And Sakka, lord of the devas, approached the great Elder Mahinda and said: 'Venerable sir, King Muṭasīva has passed away. Now the great king Devānaṃpiyatissa rules. And you have been predicted by the Perfectly Self-Enlightened One: "In the future a bhikkhu named Mahinda will gladden the island of Tambapaṇṇi." Therefore, venerable sir, now is the time to go to that excellent island. I too will be your companion.' The elder, having accepted his word, as the seventh of his group, rose into the air from the Cetiyapabbata monastery and alighted on Missaka mountain to the east of Anurādhapura. This they now know as Cetiyapabbata. At that time, it was a festival day in Tambapaṇṇi. The king, having commanded his ministers, 'Hold a festival!', surrounded by forty thousand men, departed from the city and went to Missaka mountain, desiring to play at hunting deer. Then a deity dwelling on that mountain, thinking, 'I will show the elders to the king,' in the form of a rohita deer, wandered about as if eating grass and leaves not far away. The king, thinking, 'It is not right to shoot one who is heedless,' twanged his bowstring. The deer, taking the path to Ambatthala, began to flee. The king, pursuing it, ascended Ambatthala itself. The deer then vanished not far from the elders. The Elder Mahinda, having resolved, 'Let the king see only me, not the others,' called out, 'Tissa, Tissa, come here!' Hearing this, the king pondered: 'In this island of Tambapaṇṇi, there is no one who is able to address me by the name "Tissa." But this one, a wearer of torn and tattered robes, a shaven-headed man in saffron garments, addresses me by name. Who can this be? A human or a non-human?' The elder said:

28.

28.

Samaṇā [M 0.0165] mayaṃ mahārāja, dhammarājassa sāvakā;

Taveva anukampāya, jambudīpā idhāgatāti.

We are recluses, O great king, disciples of the Dhamma King; out of compassion for you alone, we have come here from Jambudīpa.

Rājā dhammāsokanarindena pesitasāsanānusārena anussaramāno ayyā nukho āgatāti tāvadeva āyudhaṃ nikkhipitvā ekamantaṃ nisīdi sammodanīyaṃ kathaṃ kathayamāno sāraṇīyaṃ sammodanīyaṃ-itisabbattha kathaṃ kurumāno. Tasmiṃ tānipi cattāḷīsapurisasahassāni āgantvā taṃ parivāresuṃ, tadā thero itarepi jane dassesi, rājā disvā ime kadā āgatāti pucchi, mayā saddhiṃyeva mahārājāti. Idāni pana jambudīpe aññepi evarūpā samaṇā santīti. Mahārāja etarahi jambudīpo kāsāvapajjoto isivātapaṭivāto, tasmiṃ.

The king, recollecting in accordance with the message sent by King Dhammāsoka, thought, 'Surely the noble ones have come!' He at once laid down his weapon and sat down to one side, exchanging friendly and courteous greetings. Then those forty thousand men also came and surrounded him. The elder then revealed the other people. Seeing them, the king asked, 'When did these come?' 'With me, great king.' 'But are there now other such recluses in Jambudīpa?' 'Great king, Jambudīpa now shines with saffron robes, swept by the wind of the seers. In it...'

29.

29.

Tevijjā iddhippattā ca, cetopariññakovidā;

Khīṇāsavā arahanto, bahū buddhassa sāvakāti.

Many are the Buddha's disciples who possess the three true knowledges, have attained psychic power, are skilled in penetrating the minds of others, and are arahants with their taints destroyed.

Rājā taṃ sutvā pasanno ahosi, atha thero rukkhopamādinā tassa paññāveyyattiyaṃ ñatvā dhammaṃ desesi sanarāmarehi sādhukāraṃ kārayamāno. Tena vutthaṃ.

Hearing that, the king was pleased. Then the elder, discerning his intellectual acuity by means of the simile of the tree and others, taught the Dhamma, eliciting cries of 'Well said!' from humans and devas. Thus it was said:

30.

30.

Paṇḍitoti viditvāna, cullahatthipadopamaṃ;

Suttantaṃ desayī thero, mahīpassa mahāmati mahīmatī itipikatthaci.

The greatly wise elder, having recognized that the king was wise, taught him the Cūḷahatthipadopama Suttanta.

Desanāpariyosāne so saddhiṃ tehi narehi cattāḷīsasahassehi saraṇesu patiṭṭhahīti. Athassa rājā sve bhante mama gehe bhikkhaṃ gaṇhāthāti yācitvā gantvā nagarañca rājagehañca alaṅkaritvā there nisīdāpetvā paṇītenāhārena parivisitvā anuḷādevippamukhāhi pañcasataitthīhi saddhiṃ ekamantaṃ nisīdi. Atha thero dhammaratana vassaṃ vassāpesi. Tato [M 0.0166] tā pañcasataitthiyo sotāpattiphalaṃ pāpuṇiṃsu. Tato hatthisālāyaṃ sahassaṃ, nandanavane sahassaṃti evaṃ dutiyadivase aḍḍhateyyāni pāṇasahassāni sotāpattiphale patiṭṭhāpesi. Tatiyadivase aḍḍhanavappamāṇaṃ pāṇasahassaṃti evaṃ anekasatānaṃ anekasahassānaṃ anekasata sahassānaṃ dhammā mataṃ pāyesi. Vuttañhi.

At the conclusion of the discourse, he, together with those forty thousand men, became established in the Refuges. Then the king invited the elder, saying, 'Venerable sir, please accept a meal in my house tomorrow.' Having gone and adorned the city and the royal palace, having had the elders seated and served them with exquisite food, he sat down to one side together with the five hundred women headed by Queen Anulā. Then the elder rained down the jewel of the Dhamma. Thereupon, those five hundred women attained the fruit of stream-entry. On the second day, he established two and a half thousand beings in the fruit of stream-entry: a thousand in the elephant stable and a thousand in the Nandana Grove. On the third day, he established eight and a half thousand beings. In this way, he gave the deathless Dhamma to drink to many hundreds, many thousands, and many hundreds of thousands. For it has been said:

31.

31.

Mahāmahindasuriyo, laṅkāvehāsamajjhago;

Bodhaneyyambuje kāsi, vikāsaṃ dhammaraṃsinā.

The sun of Mahinda, having reached the middle of Laṅkā's sky, caused the lotuses of those ready for awakening to bloom with the rays of the Dhamma.

32.

32.

Mahāmahindacando so, laṅkāvehāsamajjhago;

Bodhesi dhammaraṃsīhi, veneyyakumudākare.

That moon of Mahinda, having reached the middle of Laṅkā's sky, awakened with the rays of the Dhamma the water-lily pond of those ready to be trained.

33.

33.

Mahāmahindamegho so, vassaṃ dhammambuvuṭṭhiyā;

Sādhūnaṃ cittabījesu, janesi kusalaṅkureti.

That cloud of Mahinda, raining with a shower of the water of Dhamma, generated wholesome sprouts in the seed-like hearts of the virtuous.

Atha rājā sumanasāmaṇerena dhammāsokassa hatthato sammāsambuddhaparibhuttapattapūradhātuyo ca sakkassa santikā dakkhiṇakkhakadhātuṃca āharāpetvā āhāritvā-itisabbattha cetiyapabbate thūpaṃ ādiṃkatvā sakalalaṅkādīpe yojane yojane thūpāni kāretvā dakkhiṇakadhātuṃ nidahitvā thūpārāmathūpañca patiṭṭhāpesi. Atha saṅghamittāya theriyā ānītaṃ jayamahābodhino dakkhiṇamahāsākhaṃ patiṭṭhāpetvā pūjaṃ kāresi. Sabbo panettha kathāvittharo mahāvaṃsato veditabbo.

Then the king had the novice Sumana bring the relics that filled the bowl used by the Perfectly Self-Enlightened One from the hand of Dhammāsoka, and the right collar-bone relic from the presence of Sakka. He initiated the construction of a stupa on Cetiyapabbata and then had stupas built at every yojana throughout the entire island of Laṅkā. He enshrined the right collar-bone relic and established the Thūpārāma Stupa. Then he had the great southern branch of the Jaya Mahābodhi, which had been brought by the therī Saṅghamittā, planted and he performed worship. The entire detailed account of this should be understood from the Mahāvaṃsa.

34.

34.

Pāravādikadosena, jātevaṃ parasāgare;

Pattānumodanā evaṃ, laṅkāyaṃ āsi issaro.

By the fault of another's words, he was born, as it were, in a foreign ocean; yet by rejoicing in merit, he thus became lord in Laṅkā.

35.

35.

Pāpampi evaṃ phalatīti mantvā,

Ñatvāna puññassa phalaṃ idanti;

Bho yoniso kubbatha puññakamme,

Gantvāna ye yattha na socayantīti.

Considering that even evil thus bears fruit, and knowing this to be the fruit of merit, sirs, wisely perform meritorious deeds, for having gone to that state, one does not grieve.

Tebhātikamadhuvāṇijakānaṃ vatthuṃ aṭṭhamaṃ.

The Eighth: The Story of the Three Brother Honey Merchants.

39. Bodhirājadhītuyā bodhirājadhītāya itisabbattha vatthumhi ayamānupubbīkathā

39. This is the connected story in the account of the daughter of King Bodhi.

Bhagavati parinibbute laṅkāyaṃ sāsane suppatiṭṭhite tattha hakureḷīti eko gāmo ahosi, tatthekā dārikā gāmādārikāhi saddhiṃ tattha tattha kīḷantī viharati, tadā gāmasamīpe mahāsobbhaṃ hoti. Tattheko maruttharukkho sabbadā pāḷiphullova tiṭṭhati. Manussā buddhapūjanatthaṃ pupphāni viciṇantā udakaṃ ogahetvā rukkhamabhiruyha pupphāni ociṇanti, kumārikā te disvā manussā sukhena gantvā pupphāni ociṇantūti ekaṃ sukkhapālibhaddadaṇḍakaṃ āharitvā setuṃ katvā ṭhapesi, tato paṭṭhāya manussā tenagantvā pupphāni ociṇanti. Atha sā tato cutā teneva kusalakammena jambudīpe pāṭaliputtanagare somadattarañño dhītā hutvā nibbatti, uttamarūpadharā devaccharapaṭibhāgā ahosi. Mātāpitaro panassā bodhirājakumārikāti vohariṃsu. Pubbe katasetuānubhāvena tassā suvīrako nāmeko ākāsagāmī sindhavapotako nibbatti. Rājadhītuyā pana pitā buddhamāmako dhammamāmako saṅghamāmako hutvā mahantaṃ puññaṃ pasavanto sindhavapotakaṃ disvā puññakaraṇassa me sahāyo laddhoti tuṭṭhamānaso assaṃ abhiruhitvā divasassa tikkhattuṃ gantvā mahābodhiṃ [M 0.0168] vandati. Rājadhītā naṃ disvā pitusantikaṃ gantvā abhiṇhaṃ tāta kuhiṃ gacchasīti pucchi. Rājā na kiñci kathesi, atha sā punappunaṃ pitaraṃ nibandhantī pucchi. Tato rājā tassāvi karonto evamāha.

When the Blessed One had attained final Nibbāna and the Dispensation was well established in Laṅkā, there was a village named Hakureḷī. There, a young girl dwelt, playing here and there with the other village girls. At that time, there was a large chasm near the village. There a coral tree stood, always in full bloom. People wishing to worship the Buddha would enter the water, climb the tree, and pick the flowers. Seeing them, the young girl brought a dry branch of a coral tree and, making it into a bridge, set it in place, thinking, 'May people go with ease and gather flowers.' From then on, people used it to cross and gather flowers. After passing away from there, by that very wholesome deed she was reborn in the city of Pāṭaliputta in Jambudīpa as the daughter of King Somadatta. She possessed supreme beauty, like a celestial nymph. Her parents called her Bodhirājakumārī. By the power of the bridge she had made in the past, a sky-faring Sindhu colt named Suvīraka was manifested for her. The princess's father, being devoted to the Buddha, the Dhamma, and the Saṅgha, was accumulating great merit. Seeing the Sindhu colt, he joyfully thought, 'I have found a companion for making merit!' Mounting the horse, he went three times a day to pay homage to the Great Bodhi tree. The princess, seeing this, went to her father and repeatedly asked, 'Father, where do you go?' The king at first said nothing, but as she persisted in asking, he finally explained it to her, saying:

1.

1.

Amhākaṃ bhagavā pubbe, pūrento dasapāramī;

Adāsi sīsarattakkhi, maṃsaṃ jīvitameva ca.

Our Blessed One in the past, while fulfilling the ten perfections, gave his head, his blood, and his eyes, his flesh, and even his very life.

2.

2.

Puttadāre ca rajje ca, patvā datvā itipikatthaci pāramimatthakaṃ;

Anappakappakoṭīnaṃ, khepetvā kapilavuye.

Giving his children, wife, and kingdom, and reaching the perfections’ very peak, he passed countless crores of eons within the lineage of Kapila.

3.

3.

Sakkarājakule jāto, loke appaṭipuggalo;

Sampattacakkavattittaṃ, pahantvāna narādhipo.

Born in the royal Sakyan clan, a person with no peer in all the world, the lord of men renounced the state of a universal monarch he had gained.

4.

4.

Disvā nimitte caturo, nikkhamma abhinikkhamaṃ;

Bodhimūlamupāgamma, nisinno vajirāsane.

Seeing the four signs, he went forth in the great renunciation; approaching the foot of the Bodhi tree, he sat upon the adamantine seat.

5.

5.

Sahassabāhuṃ māpetvā, nānāyudhasamākulaṃ nānāvudhasamākulaṃ-itisabbattha;

Mahābhītikaraṃ vesaṃ, kāḷapabbatasādisaṃ.

Creating a thousand arms, bristling with various weapons, a guise causing great fear, like a black mountain.

6.

6.

Māpetvāna samāruyha, girimekhalavāraṇaṃ;

Mārasenaṃ samānetvā, āgataṃ makaraddhajaṃ.

Having created and mounted the elephant Girimekhala, and having assembled the army of Māra, he with the makara banner approached.

7.

7.

Pāramitābalena taṃ, mārasenaṃ palāpiya;

Yatthāsīno kilesāri, sahassaṃ ghātayī jino.

By the power of his perfections, he put Māra’s army to flight; seated there, the Victor destroyed the thousand foes that are the defilements.

8.

8.

Nayanaṃ sujalasekehi, sattāhaṃ jinasevitaṃ;

Pūjitaṃ devabrahmehi, siddhoraganarādihi;

Vandituṃ jayabodhiṃtaṃ, gacchāmi satataṃ ahaṃ.

Attended by the Victor for seven days with unblinking gaze, worshipped by devas, brahmās, siddhas, nāgas, and men, to that victorious Bodhi tree I always go to pay it homage.

9.

9.

Upāsati sadā gantvā, yo naro bodhipādapaṃ;

Gandhodadīpadhūpādi, nānāpūjāhi sādhukaṃ.

Whatever person constantly goes and reveres the Bodhi tree, with perfumes, water, lamps, incense, and various other proper offerings,

10.

10.

Sa naro nirāmayo hoti, paccatte ca parattha ca;

Pūjito mānito hoti, dīghāyu balavā sukhī.

that person becomes free from affliction, both in this world and the next; he is honored and respected, long-lived, powerful, and happy.

11.

11.

Tadatthaṃ patthayantena, atthakāmena jantunā;

Upāsanīyaṃ saddhāya, niccaṃ taṃ bodhipādapaṃti.

Therefore, a person who desires that benefit, one who seeks what is good, should always with faith venerate that Bodhi tree.

Taṃ sutvā kumārikā pītiyā phuṭasarīrā pitaraṃ vanditvā ahampi tāta gacchāmīti āha. Rājā panassā upaddavabhayena gamanaṃ na icchi. Tato sā yāvatatiyaṃ pitaraṃ yācitvā raññā anuññātā. Tato paṭṭhāya pitarā saddhiṃ sindhavamāruyha bodhiṃ vandituṃ satataṃ gacchati. Athāparabhāge rājā maraṇamañce nipanno cintesi. Dhītā me nirantaraṃ bodhiupaṭṭhānaṃgacchati, etissā anāgate yaṃkiñci bhayaṃ uppajjamānaṃ tato uppajjati. Tattha me kiṃ kātabbanti. Tato sindhavaṃ pakkosāpetvā tassa kaṇṇamūle mantento evamāha, tāta mama dhītā abhiṇhaṃ tava sahāyaṃ katvā bodhiṃ vandituṃ gacchati. Tatthassā yaṃkiñci bhayaṃ bhaveyya. Taṃ nappatirūpaṃ. Tattha gamanāgamane mama dhītaraṃ rakkheyyāsīti tassa dhītaraṃ paṭipādetvā kālamakāsi. Tato rājadhītā pitusarīrakiccaṃ kāretvā divasassa tikkhattuṃ assamabhiruyha bodhiupaṭṭhānaṃ gacchati. Manussā panassā rūpasampattiṃ disvā vimhitamānasā rājārahaṃ vata no idaṃ paṇṇākāraṃ diṭṭhaṃ. Gantvā rañño ācikkhissāma. Appeva nāma rājā so kiñci no dadeyyāti cintetvā rañño santikaṃ gantvā vanditvā ṭhitā evamāhaṃsu.

Hearing that, the princess, her body suffused with joy, paid homage to her father and said, "I too, dear father, shall go." But the king, fearing some misfortune for her, did not wish her to go. Then she entreated her father up to the third time, and the king gave his permission. From then on, she would always go with her father, mounting the Sindhu horse, to pay homage to the Bodhi tree. Later, when the king was lying on his deathbed, he thought, "My daughter constantly goes to attend upon the Bodhi tree. If any danger should arise for her in the future, it would arise from that. What should I do about this?" Then he had the Sindhu horse summoned and, whispering in its ear, said, "Dear one, my daughter frequently goes with you as her companion to pay homage to the Bodhi tree. Should any danger befall her there, it would be unfitting. Therefore, you must protect my daughter on her journeys to and fro." Having entrusted his daughter to it, the king passed away. After performing her father's funeral rites, the king's daughter would go three times a day, mounting the horse, to attend upon the Bodhi tree. People, seeing her accomplishment of beauty, were amazed and thought, "Truly, this gift we have seen is worthy of a king. Let us go and inform the king. Perhaps the king will give us something." So they went to the king, paid homage, and standing there, spoke thus:

12.

12.

Bodhimaṇḍaṃ samāgamma, abhiṇhaṃ tuṭṭhamānasā;

Vandantī yāti kaññekā, vijjūva siriyā jalaṃ.

Having come to the terrace of Awakening, her mind ever filled with delight, a certain maiden goes in worship, blazing with glory like lightning.

13.

13.

Nīladhampillabhārā sā, visālāyatalocanā;

Soṇṇadolābhasavaṇā, sāmā subhapayodharā.

She has a heavy mass of dark braided hair and large, wide eyes; her ears have the splendor of golden pendants, her complexion is dark, and she has beautiful, full breasts.

14.

14.

Sataraṃsīhi sammissa, sañcyāmbudasamādharā;

Tuṅganāsā nīlabhamu, hāsabhāsā manoramā.

Gleaming as if with a hundred rays, like a gathering of rain clouds, she has a prominent nose, dark eyebrows, and a charming smile and speech.

15.

15.

Īdisaṃ [M 0.0170] no mahārājā mahārājadiṭṭhapubba itipikatthaci, diṭṭhapubbaṃ kudācanaṃ;

Ehi tassā siriṃdeva, bodhimaṇḍamhi dakkhasīti.

"Such a one, O great king, has never been seen by us before. Come, O deva, you will see her splendor at the terrace of Awakening."

Taṃ sutvā rājā savaṇasaṃsaggeneva tāya paṭibaddhacitto caturaṅginiṃsenaṃ gahetvā rājadhītaraṃ bodhivandanatthāya āgatakāle bahipākāre senaṃ parikkhipāpetvā gaṇhathetanti manusse niyojesi. Tato senāpi taṃ gahaṇasajjā aṭṭhāsi. Rājadhītā te disvā sīghaṃ sindhavaṃ upasaṅkamitvā tassa piṭṭhiyaṃ nisinnā paṇhiyā saññaṃ adāsi. So taṃ gahetvā vegena ākāsaṃ pakkhandi. Sā pana dunnisinnabhāvena assassa vegaṃ sandhāretumasakkontī assapiṭṭhito parigali. Sindhavo rājadhītaraṃ patamānaṃ disvā rājovādaṃ saramāno vegenāgantvā tassā kese ḍaṃsitvā ukkhipitvā patamānāya tassā piṭṭhiṃdatvā nisīdāpetvā ākāsena taṃ netvā pāṭaliputtanagareyeva patiṭṭhāpesi pātaliputtanagareyevataṃ paviṭhāpesiitipikatthaci.

Hearing this, the king, his heart bound to her merely from the report, took his fourfold army and, when the princess arrived to pay homage to the Bodhi tree, he ordered his men, "Surround the outer enclosure with the troops and seize her!" Then the army stood ready to capture her. Seeing them, the princess quickly approached the Sindhu horse, sat on its back, and gave a signal with her heel. The horse took her and swiftly leapt into the sky. But because she was not seated securely, she was unable to withstand the horse’s speed and slipped from its back. The Sindhu horse, seeing the princess falling and remembering the king’s command, swiftly returned, seized her by the hair with its teeth, lifted her up, and presenting its back to her as she fell, seated her upon it. Then it carried her through the sky and set her down right in the city of Pāṭaliputta.

16.

16.

Tiracchānagatāpevaṃ, sarantā upakārakaṃ;

Na jahantīti mantvāna, kataññū hontu hontipāṇino-itipikatthaci pāṇinoti.

Considering that even animals, remembering a benefactor, do not abandon them, may all living beings be grateful.

Tato paṭṭhāya sā puññakammaṃ katvā saggaparāyanā ahosīti.

From that time onward, having performed meritorious deeds, she had heaven as her destination.

17.

17.

Yo yaṃ dumindaṃyatinandanena;

Sampūjitaṃ pūjayate sa pañño;

Sa bhogavā hoti anītiko ca,

Sabbattha so hoti pasattharūpo.

That wise person who worships the King of Trees, which was honored by the Delight of the Sages, becomes wealthy and free from misfortune, and is praised everywhere for his fine character.

Bodhirājadhītuyā vatthuṃ navamaṃ.

The Ninth: The Story of the Daughter of King Bodhirāja.

40. Kuṇḍaliyā vatthumhi ayamānupubbīkathā

40. This is the gradual exposition concerning Kuṇḍalī.

Laṅkādīpe [M 0.0171] rohaṇajapade mahāgāmo nāma ahosi, tattha tissavihāraṃ nāma anekasatabhikkhūhi samākiṇṇaṃ anekapariveṇapatimaṇḍitaṃ vihāraṃ ahosi. Tattheko tisso nāma sāmaṇero paṭivasati. So ekasmiṃsamaye janapadacārikaṃ caranto pāsāṇavāpigāme bhikkhaṃ caritvā yāpanamattaṃ bhattaṃ sappinā saddhiṃ labhitvā nikkhamma gāmadvāraṃ patvā mahāgāmābhimukho gacchanto manusse udakaphāsukaṭṭhānaṃ pucchi. Tehi bhante tumhākaṃ abhimukhe avidūraṭṭhāne kakubandakandaraṃ nāma sandamānasītalodakaṃ dhavalavālukātalaṃ tattha tumhe gantvā nahātvā sītalacchāyāya vālukātale nisinno bhattakiccaṃ katvā gacchathāti vutte sāmaṇero [M 0.0172] sādhūti vatvā tattha gantvā phāsukaṭṭhāne nisinno bhattaṃ bhuñjitumārabhi. Tadā ekena vanakammikena saddhiṃaraññaṃ gatā ekā sunakhī tasmiṃ kandare ekasmiṃ pabbhāraṭṭhāne dārake vijāyitvā chātajjhattā pavedhamānagattā dārakānaṃ samīpenipannā sāmaṇerassa patte āhāragandhaṃ ghāyitvā nipannaṭṭhānato vuṭṭhāya pavedhamānā tassa samīpaṃ āgamma naṅguṭṭhaṃ cālentī aṭṭhāsi, sāmaṇero taṃ disvā kampitamānaso attano bhojanatthāya vaṭṭitaṃ pathamālopaṃ tassā purato ṭhapesi, tato sā somanassā taṃ bhuñji. Taṃ disvā tuṭṭho punappunaṃ ālopaṃ karonto tassā bhattaṃ datvā pattaṃ dhovitvā thavikāya pakkhipitvā agamāsi. Tato sā sunakhī sāmaṇeragatena pasādena tato cutā jambudīpe devaputtanagare rājānaṃ paṭicca tassa mahesiyā kucchimhi paṭisandhiṃ gaṇhitvā dasamāsaccayena mātukucchito nikkhami, athassā sikhāmaṅgaladivase sampatte mātāpitaro panassā kuṇḍalāvaṭṭakesattā kuṇḍalāti nāma makaṃsu. Sā anukkamena soḷasavassuddesikā ahosi, tasmiṃ kira samaye tisso sāmaṇero mahābodhiṃ vandissāmīti nāvaṃ abhiruyha jambudīpaṃ gantvā anupubbena devaputtanagaraṃ patvā sunivattho supāruto yugamattadaso bhikkhāya caranto mahāvīthiṃ sampāpuṇi. Rājadhītā sīhapañjaraṃ ugghāṭetvā antaravīthiṃ olokentī bhikkhantaṃ sāmaṇeraṃ disvā pubbasinehaṃ paṭilabhi, tasmiṃkhaṇe tassā jātissarañāṇaṃ ahosi. Sā kira pubbe bhikkhuṇī hutvā paṇṇasūciyā saddhiṃ potthakañca padīpiyatelañca datvā jātissarā bhaveyyaṃti patthanaṃ ṭhapesi, tato sājātiṃ anussarantī sāmaṇerena attano katūpakāraṃ ditvā somanassā naṃ pakkosāpetvā rājagehe āsanaṃ paññāpetvā tattha nisinnaṃ nānaggarasa bhojanena parivisitvā onītapattapāṇiṃ sāmaṇeraṃ upasaṅkamitvā vanditvā ekamantaṃ nisinnā tena saddhiṃ sallapantī evamāha.

In the island of Laṅkā, in the Rohaṇa country, there was a great village named Mahāgāma. There, a monastery called Tissavihāra was crowded with many hundreds of monks and adorned with numerous cells. A novice named Tissa was dwelling there. Once, while wandering on alms round through the countryside, he received just enough food with ghee at the Pāsāṇavāpi village. After leaving, he reached the village gate and, heading towards the great village, asked people for a place suitable for water. They told him, "Venerable, not far ahead in your direction is a place called Kakubandakandara, with cool, flowing water and white sandy banks. Go there, bathe, sit in the cool shade on the sandy bank, finish your meal, and then proceed." The novice agreed and went there. Sitting in the pleasant spot, he began to eat his meal. At that time, a female dog who had gone into the forest with a woodcutter gave birth to puppies in a cave within that ravine. Hungry and trembling, she lay near the puppies. Catching the scent of food from the novice’s bowl, she rose from her spot, trembling, approached him, and stood wagging her tail. Seeing her, the compassionate novice placed the first morsel of his meal before her. Delighted, she ate it. Pleased by this, he repeatedly made morsels and gave her food. Then, he washed his bowl, placed it in his bag, and departed. Due to the serene confidence she felt towards the novice, the female dog passed away and was reborn in Jambudīpa, in Devaputtanagara, in the womb of the chief queen of a king. After ten months, she emerged from her mother’s womb. On her hair-tying ceremony day, her parents, because she was born with a curl on her head, named her Kuṇḍalī. Gradually, she reached sixteen years of age. At that time, the novice Tissa, wishing to pay homage to the Great Bodhi Tree, boarded a ship and traveled to Jambudīpa. Eventually, he arrived at Devaputtanagara. Dressed neatly and well-robed, looking only a yoke’s length ahead, he walked for alms along the main street. The king’s daughter, having opened the lion-window, looked down the street and saw the novice on alms round. She regained her former affection, and at that moment, her knowledge of past lives arose. It is said that in a previous life, she had been a nun. Having given a book and lamp oil, together with a leaf-sewing needle, she had made a vow: "May I be reborn with the memory of past lives." Remembering this, and seeing the novice’s kindness to her, filled with joy, she summoned him, prepared a seat in the royal house, and served him various delicious foods. When he had finished his meal, she approached the novice, paid respects, sat to one side, and conversed with him, saying:

1.

1.

Na sañjānāsi maṃ dhīra, pubbehaṃ tava dāsikā;

Tenāhaṃ sukhitā āsiṃ, tasmā tvamasi issaroti.

"Wise one, do you not recognize me? Formerly, I was your female servant. Because of that, I was happy, and therefore you are my master."

Taṃ sutvā sāmaṇero āha.

Hearing this, the novice said:

2.

2.

Natthi me tādisī dāsī, na sañjānāmi taṃ ahaṃ;

Kāsi tvaṃ kassa vā dhītā, taṃ me akkhāhi pucchitāti.

"I have no such female servant, nor do I recognize you. Who are you, or whose daughter are you? Tell me what I ask."

Tato sā taṃ sārāpentī āha.

Then, reminding him, she said:

3.

3.

Sārāpemi tuvaṃ ajja, yathā jānāsimaṃ ise;

Bujjhassu bodhito dāni, mayā jātiṃsarantiyā.

"I will remind you today, so that you may know me, O sage. Understand now from my explanation, as I remember my birth."

4.

4.

Pāsāṇavāpigāmamhi, tambapaṇṇimhi rohaṇe;

Bhikkhitvāna tuvaṃ bhante, yadā kakubandakandare.

"Venerable sir, in Tambapaṇṇi, in Rohaṇa, in the village of Pāsāṇavāpi, when, having begged for alms, you were in the Kakubandakandara ravine,

5.

5.

Nisīditvāna tvaṃ bhattaṃ, bhuttakālaṃ sarissasi;

Tadāhaṃ sunakhī āsiṃ, vijātā laddhagocarā.

"You will remember the time you sat down to eat your meal. Then I was a female dog, having given birth and found a place with food."

6.

6.

Dārake [M 0.0173] khādituṃ mayha, māsannā khudapīḷitā;

Pavedhamānasabbaṅgā, aṭṭhāsiṃ tava santike.

"Tormented by hunger, I was close to devouring my own young; trembling in every limb, I stood in your presence."

7.

7.

Disvā taṃ maṃ tadā bhante, vedhamānaṃ bubhukkhitaṃ;

Chinnabhatto tuvaṃ hutvā, mamaṃ bhattena tosayī.

"Seeing me then, venerable sir, trembling and afflicted by hunger, you, sharing your own meal, satisfied me with food."

8.

8.

Tadāhaṃ muducittena, cittaṃ tayi pasādayiṃ;

Tenāhaṃ puññakammena, dutiye attasambhave.

"Then, with a tender heart, I made my mind serene towards you. Because of that meritorious deed, in a second arising of existence,"

9.

9.

Idha rājakule jātā, sabbakāmasamiddhinī;

Cittappasādamattena, lokanāthassa sāsane.

"I was born here in a royal family, fulfilled in all desires, through the mere serene confidence of mind in the teaching of the Protector of the World."

10.

10.

Tadahupabbajitassāpi, īdisā honti sampadā;

Kīdisaṃ hoti sambuddhe, pasādena phalaṃ aho.

"Even for one who went forth on that very day, such are the accomplishments. What fruit must there be, O wonder, from serene confidence in the Perfectly Enlightened One!"

11.

11.

Aññāni pana kiccāni, pahāyāttahite rato;

Atandito divārattiṃ, sarātu ratanattayaṃti.

"Having abandoned other tasks, delighting in one's own welfare, unwearied day and night, may one remember the Triple Gem."

Evaṃ sāmaṇerena katūpakāraṃ sārāpetvā bhante tava dāsiyā anuggahaṃ paṭicca idheva vasathāti nimantitvā tena sampaṭicchite mahantaṃ vihāraṃ kārāpetvā sāmaṇeraṃ ādiṃ katvā anekabhikkhusate nimantetvā vihāre vasāpetvā sulabhaṃ katvā catupaccayehi upaṭṭhāsi. Sāmaṇeropi sunakhiyā dinnadānaṃ anussaritvā tuṭṭho buddhānussatiṃ manasikaronto na cirenava arahattaṃ patvā tasmiṃyeva vihāre vasanto āyupariyosāne tattheva parinibbāyīti.

Thus, having reminded the novice of the help he had rendered, she invited him, saying, "Venerable, for the sake of your female servant, please dwell right here." When he agreed, she had a large monastery built. Inviting the novice first, along with many hundreds of monks, she had them dwell in the monastery and supported them with the four requisites, making them easy to obtain. The novice, too, recalling the gift given to the female dog, being joyful and attending to the recollection of the Buddha, before long attained arahantship. Dwelling in that very monastery, at the end of his life, he attained final Nibbāna right there.

12.

12.

Tiyaddhesu tilokasmiṃ, natthi vatthuttayaṃ vinā;

Sattāna mañña micchattha, dāyakaṃ surapādapaṃ.

"In the three times and in the three worlds, there is no object of refuge apart from the Triple Gem. Other goals of beings are false; the Triple Gem is the wish-fulfilling tree that gives."

Kuṇḍaliyā vatthuṃ dasamaṃ.

The Story of Kuṇḍalī is the tenth.

Mahāsenavaggo catuttho.

The Mahāsena Chapter is the fourth.

Ettāvatā jambudīpuppattikathā samattā.

Thus concludes the account of the origin of Jambudīpa.
